For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Growth & Development

1. Q: What is the normal pattern of physical growth during infancy?

A: Infants grow rapidly during the first year. Birth weight approximately doubles by 4–6 months and triples by 12 months. Length increases by about 50% during the first year.

2. Q: What is the normal pattern of head circumference growth during infancy?

A: Head circumference increases rapidly during infancy:

~2 cm/month during the first 3 months
~1 cm/month during months 4–6
~0.5 cm/month during months 7–12

3. Q: When does the posterior fontanelle normally close?

A: Usually by 2–3 months of age.

4. Q: When does the anterior fontanelle normally close?

A: Usually by 12–18 months.

5. Q: What is the developmental significance of a persistently open anterior fontanelle?

A: Consider conditions such as hypothyroidism, rickets, Down syndrome, increased intracranial pressure, or skeletal dysplasia.

6. Q: At what age should a healthy infant typically have a social smile?

A: Approximately 2 months.

7. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected at approximately 4 months?

A: Good head control and the ability to roll from prone to supine. The infant should have minimal head lag.

8. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected at approximately 6 months?

A: The infant can typically sit with support, roll in both directions, and bear weight when held upright.

9. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected around 9 months?

A: The infant can typically sit without support and may begin crawling or pulling to stand.

10. Q: What gross motor milestones are expected around 12 months?

A: The child typically pulls to stand, cruises along furniture, and may take independent steps.

11. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected around 18 months?

A: The child should typically walk independently and run, although running may still be somewhat awkward.

12. Q: What gross motor milestones are expected around 2 years?

A: A 2-year-old should typically be able to run, climb stairs with assistance, kick a ball, and jump with both feet.

13. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected around 3 years?

A: The child can typically ride a tricycle, climb stairs using alternating feet, and briefly stand on one foot.

14. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected around 4 years?

A: The child can typically hop on one foot and throw a ball overhand.

15. Q: What gross motor milestone is expected around 5 years?

A: The child can typically skip, hop well, and perform more coordinated complex movements.

16. Q: When does the pincer grasp usually develop?

A: Around 9–12 months.

17. Q: What is the typical progression of grasp development?

A:
Palmar grasp → radial-palmar grasp → inferior pincer → mature pincer grasp.

A mature pincer grasp is generally present by about 12 months.

18. Q: When should an infant begin transferring objects from one hand to the other?

A: Around 6 months.

19. Q: When does a child typically begin using a spoon independently?

A: Around 18 months, although spilling is common.

20. Q: When does a child typically begin scribbling?

A: Around 12–15 months.

21. Q: When can a child typically draw a circle?

A: Around 3 years.

22. Q: When can a child typically draw a square?

A: Around 4 years.

23. Q: When can a child typically draw a triangle?

A: Around 5 years.

24. Q: When does canonical babbling usually begin?

A: Around 6 months.

25. Q: What language milestone is expected around 12 months?

A: The child typically says 1–3 meaningful words in addition to "mama" and "dada."

26. Q: What language milestone is expected around 18 months?

A: Vocabulary is typically around 10–20 words, with rapidly increasing language acquisition.

27. Q: What language milestone is expected around 2 years?

A: The child typically uses two-word phrases and has a vocabulary of roughly 50 or more words.

28. Q: What language milestone is expected around 3 years?

A: The child typically speaks in 3-word or longer sentences, and speech is understandable to strangers approximately 75% of the time.

29. Q: What language milestone is expected around 4 years?

A: Speech is generally understandable to strangers, and the child can typically use 4-word or longer sentences.

30. Q: What is the "rule of fours" for speech intelligibility?

A: A useful approximation is:

Age in years ÷ 4 × 100 ≈ percentage of speech understood by strangers.

Thus:

1 year → ~25%
2 years → ~50%
3 years → ~75%
4 years → ~100%

31. Q: When does stranger anxiety typically develop?

A: Around 6–9 months.

32. Q: When does separation anxiety typically peak?

A: Approximately 9–18 months.

33. Q: When does a child typically develop parallel play?

A: Around 2 years.

Parallel play means children play alongside one another but do not yet engage in organized cooperative play.

34. Q: When does associative play typically develop?

A: Around 3–4 years.

Children interact and share materials but do not necessarily have a common organized goal.

35. Q: When does cooperative play typically develop?

A: Around 4–5 years.

Children begin playing together with shared rules, roles, and goals.

36. Q: What developmental milestone is particularly concerning if absent by 6 months?

A: Poor head control or persistent head lag is concerning and warrants evaluation.

37. Q: What is a major developmental red flag at 9 months?

A: Inability to sit independently should prompt further evaluation.

38. Q: What is a major developmental red flag at 12 months?

A: Not pulling to stand or cruising, especially when accompanied by other developmental delays.

39. Q: What is a major developmental red flag at 18 months?

A: Not walking independently is concerning and requires evaluation.

40. Q: What is a major language red flag at 18 months?

A: No meaningful words should prompt developmental evaluation, including assessment for hearing impairment and autism spectrum disorder.

41. Q: What is a major developmental red flag at 2 years?

A: No two-word spontaneous phrases or significant lack of meaningful language is concerning.

42. Q: What is the most important distinction between developmental delay and developmental regression?

A:
Developmental delay: the child fails to acquire expected milestones.

Developmental regression: the child loses previously acquired skills.

Regression is particularly concerning for neurologic or neurodegenerative disease and requires prompt evaluation.

43. Q: What should be done when a child fails a developmental screening test?

A: Do not simply wait and reassess at the next routine visit. Perform appropriate evaluation, including targeted assessment and referral to early intervention/developmental services when indicated.

44. Q: What is the most important first step when speech delay is suspected?

A: Evaluate hearing.

Hearing impairment can present as delayed speech and language development despite otherwise normal behavior.

45. Q: What is the typical pattern of growth during childhood?

A: Growth is relatively steady during childhood, with approximately 5–7 cm/year of linear growth before puberty.

A persistent decrease in growth velocity is more concerning than a child who is simply small but following an established growth curve.

46. Q: What is constitutional growth delay?

A: A normal variant characterized by short stature and delayed bone age, often with delayed puberty. The child typically has a normal growth velocity and eventually reaches a normal adult height consistent with familial potential.

47. Q: What is familial short stature?

A: A normal variant in which the child is short but has:

Normal growth velocity
Bone age approximately equal to chronological age
Short parents/family members

Adult height is generally consistent with the child's genetic potential.

48. Q: What is the first visible sign of puberty in girls?

A: Breast budding (thelarche) is usually the first sign.

Pubic hair may develop around the same time but is not usually the first event.

49. Q: What is the first visible sign of puberty in boys?

A: Testicular enlargement, typically beginning when testicular volume reaches approximately 4 mL.

50. Q: What is the typical sequence of puberty in girls?

A: A simplified sequence is:

Thelarche → pubarche → peak height velocity → menarche

Menarche generally occurs about 2–3 years after thelarche and usually after peak height velocity.

51. Q: What is the expected birth weight loss in a healthy newborn?

A: A term newborn may lose up to approximately 7–10% of birth weight during the first several days due primarily to fluid loss. Birth weight is usually regained by about 10–14 days.

52. Q: What is the expected weight gain during the first year of life?

A: Birth weight approximately:

Doubles by 4–6 months
Triples by 12 months

Failure to achieve expected weight gain should prompt evaluation for growth faltering.

53. Q: What is growth faltering?

A: Growth faltering is inadequate physical growth, particularly poor weight gain, compared with expected growth for age and sex.

Possible causes include:

Inadequate caloric intake
Malabsorption
Increased metabolic requirements
Chronic systemic disease
Psychosocial/environmental factors

54. Q: In a child with growth faltering, which growth parameter is usually affected first?

A: Weight is generally affected first, followed by length/height, and finally head circumference in severe or prolonged nutritional deprivation.

55. Q: What does crossing downward through multiple major percentile lines on a growth chart suggest?

A: It suggests abnormal growth and warrants evaluation, particularly if accompanied by poor growth velocity or clinical symptoms.

56. Q: What is the most useful measure for assessing linear growth in a child?

A: Growth velocity over time is more informative than a single height measurement.

57. Q: What is mid-parental target height for a girl?

A:

Target height=
2
Father’s height+Mother’s height−13 cm
	​


58. Q: What is mid-parental target height for a boy?

A:

Target height=
2
Father’s height+Mother’s height+13 cm
	​


The expected target range is approximately ±8.5 cm.

59. Q: How does bone age help distinguish familial short stature from constitutional growth delay?

A:
Familial short stature: bone age is approximately equal to chronological age.

Constitutional growth delay: bone age is delayed relative to chronological age.

60. Q: What growth pattern is typical of endocrine causes of short stature?

A: Endocrine disorders often cause decreased height velocity with relatively preserved/increased weight, unlike nutritional disease, in which weight is often affected first.

61. Q: What growth pattern is typical of chronic systemic disease or malnutrition?

A: Weight decreases first, followed by impaired linear growth if the condition persists.

62. Q: What is the typical relationship between head circumference and chest circumference during infancy?

A: At birth, head circumference is larger than chest circumference.

Chest circumference approaches head circumference during the first year of life and generally becomes larger thereafter.

63. Q: What does microcephaly indicate in a developing child?

A: Microcephaly may indicate abnormal brain growth and can be associated with genetic disorders, congenital infections, prenatal exposures, or other neurologic conditions.

64. Q: What is macrocephaly?

A: Macrocephaly is an abnormally large head circumference, generally defined as >2 standard deviations above the mean for age and sex.

65. Q: What should be considered in an infant with rapidly increasing head circumference?

A: Evaluate for causes such as:

Hydrocephalus
Intracranial mass
Subdural collections
Metabolic/storage disorders
Familial macrocephaly

Rapidly crossing percentiles is particularly concerning.

66. Q: What is the significance of a bulging anterior fontanelle?

A: A persistently bulging fontanelle suggests increased intracranial pressure, which can occur with meningitis, hydrocephalus, intracranial hemorrhage, or other intracranial pathology.

67. Q: What is the significance of a sunken fontanelle?

A: A sunken fontanelle suggests dehydration, especially when accompanied by decreased urine output, dry mucous membranes, and poor skin turgor.

68. Q: At what age does an infant typically begin to recognize familiar people?

A: By approximately 2–3 months, infants increasingly recognize familiar caregivers and respond socially to them.

69. Q: What is object permanence, and when does it develop?

A: Object permanence is understanding that an object continues to exist even when it cannot be seen. It develops during infancy, becoming apparent around 8–12 months.

70. Q: What cognitive stage does Piaget assign to infants?

A: Sensorimotor stage: birth to approximately 2 years.

Learning occurs primarily through sensory experiences and motor interactions with the environment.

71. Q: What cognitive stage does Piaget assign to children approximately 2–7 years old?

A: Preoperational stage.

Children develop symbolic thought but have difficulty with logical reasoning and conservation.

72. Q: What is egocentrism in Piaget's preoperational stage?

A: The child has difficulty understanding that other people may have perspectives different from their own.

73. Q: What is conservation in child development?

A: Understanding that a quantity remains the same despite changes in its appearance or arrangement.

It develops during the concrete operational stage, approximately 7–11 years.

74. Q: What cognitive stage corresponds to approximately 7–11 years?

A: Concrete operational stage.

Children develop logical reasoning about concrete objects and understand concepts such as conservation and reversibility.

75. Q: What cognitive stage corresponds to adolescence?

A: Formal operational stage, beginning around 11 years and onward.

It is characterized by abstract reasoning, hypothetical thinking, and systematic problem solving.

76. Q: What is the typical developmental sequence of gross motor control?

A: Motor development generally progresses from head → trunk → extremities and from proximal → distal control.

For example, infants develop head control before sitting and sitting before walking.

77. Q: What is the significance of persistent primitive reflexes?

A: Primitive reflexes normally disappear as the nervous system matures. Persistence beyond the expected age may suggest neurologic dysfunction or cerebral palsy.

78. Q: When does the Moro reflex normally disappear?

A: The Moro reflex usually disappears by approximately 4–6 months.

Persistence beyond this period may suggest neurologic abnormalities.

79. Q: When does the rooting reflex normally disappear?

A: The rooting reflex typically disappears by approximately 4 months.

80. Q: When does the palmar grasp reflex normally disappear?

A: It generally disappears by approximately 4–6 months, allowing development of voluntary grasping.

81. Q: When does the asymmetric tonic neck reflex normally disappear?

A: It generally disappears by approximately 4–6 months.

Persistence may indicate abnormal neurologic development.

82. Q: What is a major developmental concern when a child demonstrates asymmetric motor function?

A: Early hand preference before approximately 18 months can be concerning for unilateral weakness, such as hemiplegic cerebral palsy.

83. Q: Why is early hand dominance concerning?

A: Toddlers normally use both hands relatively equally. Strong preference for one hand before ~18 months may indicate contralateral motor impairment.

84. Q: What is a typical sign of cerebral palsy during early development?

A: Possible findings include:

Persistent primitive reflexes
Abnormal muscle tone
Delayed motor milestones
Scissoring of the legs
Early hand preference
Persistent head lag

85. Q: What developmental pattern is characteristic of autism spectrum disorder?

A: Deficits in:

Social communication/interaction
Restricted, repetitive behaviors or interests

Symptoms begin in early childhood and interfere with functioning.

86. Q: What early social-communication findings should raise concern for autism?

A: Examples include:

Poor eye contact
Lack of response to name
Limited joint attention
Lack of pointing to share interest
Delayed or absent babbling
Reduced social reciprocity

87. Q: What is joint attention?

A: The ability to share attention about an object or event with another person, such as pointing to something interesting and looking back at the caregiver.

Impaired joint attention is an important early sign of autism spectrum disorder.

88. Q: What is the recommended approach when a child loses previously acquired language or social skills?

A: Prompt developmental evaluation is required. Developmental regression is not considered normal and may indicate autism, neurologic disease, metabolic disease, or other pathology.

89. Q: What is the difference between a developmental screening test and developmental surveillance?

A: Surveillance is continuous monitoring of development during routine clinical care.

Screening uses standardized tools at specific ages to identify children who may require further evaluation.

90. Q: At what ages is general developmental screening commonly performed in primary care?

A: Standard developmental screening is commonly performed at approximately:

9 months, 18 months, and 30 months.

91. Q: At what ages is autism-specific screening commonly performed?

A: Autism-specific screening is commonly performed at approximately:

18 months and 24 months.

92. Q: What should be done if a developmental screening test is abnormal?

A: The child should receive further developmental evaluation and appropriate referral, rather than simply being reassured.

Early intervention can improve developmental outcomes.

93. Q: What is the typical age range for toilet training?

A: Toilet training commonly begins around 18–24 months, but there is substantial normal variation.

The child should demonstrate developmental readiness rather than being trained according to a rigid age.

94. Q: What signs indicate readiness for toilet training?

A: Examples include:

Ability to follow simple instructions
Awareness of bladder/bowel sensations
Ability to communicate the need to toilet
Ability to sit on and get off the toilet
Ability to pull clothing up/down

95. Q: At what age is nighttime bladder control typically achieved?

A: There is substantial variation. Many children achieve nighttime continence by approximately 5 years, and occasional bed-wetting can still occur in otherwise healthy children.

96. Q: When is primary nocturnal enuresis considered developmentally abnormal?

A: Persistent nighttime bed-wetting at ≥5 years may be diagnosed as nocturnal enuresis, provided there is no underlying medical explanation.

97. Q: What is primary versus secondary enuresis?

A: Primary enuresis: the child has never achieved sustained nighttime dryness.

Secondary enuresis: bed-wetting returns after at least 6 months of established nighttime continence.

Secondary enuresis should prompt consideration of psychosocial and medical causes.

98. Q: What is the normal age range for onset of puberty in girls?

A: Approximately 8–13 years.

Pubertal development before age 8 is considered precocious and warrants evaluation.

99. Q: What is the normal age range for onset of puberty in boys?

A: Approximately 9–14 years.

Pubertal development before age 9 is considered precocious.

100. Q: What is delayed puberty in girls and boys?

A: Girls: no breast development by approximately 13 years, or prolonged absence of menarche despite otherwise appropriate development.

Boys: no testicular enlargement by approximately 14 years.

Evaluation includes growth pattern, family history, physical examination, bone age, and targeted laboratory testing.

101. Q: A 6-month-old was born at 32 weeks' gestation. How should developmental milestones be assessed?

A: Use corrected age for developmental assessment.

Corrected age:

Corrected age=Chronological age−Weeks premature

For this infant:

6 months−8 weeks≈4 months corrected age

Corrected age is generally used until approximately 2 years of age for premature infants.

102. Q: Why is corrected age important when assessing a premature infant?

A: Premature infants have had less time for postconceptional development than term infants. Using chronological age alone can incorrectly label normal premature development as delayed.

103. Q: A child is at the 5th percentile for height but has maintained that percentile consistently for several years. Is this necessarily abnormal?

A: No. A child who consistently tracks along a low percentile with normal growth velocity may simply have familial short stature or another normal growth variant.

A falling growth curve is more concerning than a stable low percentile.

104. Q: What is the most important difference between familial short stature and pathologic short stature?

A: Growth velocity.

A child with familial short stature usually has a normal growth velocity, whereas many pathologic causes produce decreased growth velocity.

105. Q: What is the typical bone-age finding in endocrine growth disorders?

A: Delayed bone age is common.

For example, growth hormone deficiency and hypothyroidism can cause short stature with delayed skeletal maturation.

106. Q: How does growth hormone deficiency typically affect body proportions?

A: It causes proportionate short stature, often with relatively increased body fat.

107. Q: What additional physical finding may suggest growth hormone deficiency in a child?

A: Delayed dentition, immature facial appearance, increased adiposity, and delayed bone age may occur.

108. Q: What endocrine disorder should be considered in a child with poor linear growth, constipation, fatigue, dry skin, and delayed bone age?

A: Hypothyroidism.

Thyroid hormone is essential for normal skeletal growth and development.

109. Q: A child has short stature, delayed bone age, and normal growth velocity. Which diagnosis is most likely?

A: Constitutional growth delay.

These children often have a family history of delayed puberty and eventually achieve an adult height appropriate for their genetic potential.

110. Q: What is the typical pubertal pattern in constitutional growth delay?

A: Delayed puberty with eventual spontaneous progression.

Bone age is delayed, allowing additional time for growth.

111. Q: What is the key distinction between constitutional growth delay and hypogonadism?

A: Constitutional delay is usually a normal variant with eventual spontaneous puberty.

Hypogonadism represents pathologic gonadal dysfunction and generally requires evaluation for an underlying disorder.

112. Q: What is the most common cause of delayed puberty in otherwise healthy adolescents?

A: Constitutional delay of growth and puberty.

113. Q: What is the first sign of central puberty in boys?

A: Testicular enlargement due to activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis.

114. Q: What is the mechanism responsible for testicular enlargement during puberty?

A: Increased FSH stimulation of Sertoli cells causes seminiferous tubular growth, increasing testicular volume.

115. Q: What hormone primarily stimulates Leydig cells during male puberty?

A: LH stimulates Leydig cells to produce testosterone.

116. Q: What hormone primarily stimulates Sertoli cells during male puberty?

A: FSH stimulates Sertoli cells and supports spermatogenesis.

117. Q: What is the first major event of adrenarche?

A: Increased production of adrenal androgens, particularly DHEA and DHEA-S.

This produces pubic and axillary hair, body odor, and acne but does not itself represent true gonadal puberty.

118. Q: Can isolated pubic hair in a young child represent true puberty?

A: Not necessarily.

Premature adrenarche can cause isolated pubic/axillary hair and body odor without breast development in girls or testicular enlargement in boys.

119. Q: What finding distinguishes central precocious puberty from premature adrenarche?

A: Central precocious puberty causes activation of the HPG axis with progressive secondary sexual characteristics.

Premature adrenarche involves adrenal androgen production without true gonadal activation.

120. Q: Why can untreated precocious puberty result in short adult stature?

A: Early sex-steroid exposure causes rapid initial growth and accelerated epiphyseal maturation, leading to premature epiphyseal closure and reduced final adult height.

121. Q: What is the typical growth pattern during puberty?

A: There is a pubertal growth spurt, followed by slowing of growth as the epiphyseal plates mature and eventually close.

122. Q: Do girls or boys generally reach peak height velocity earlier?

A: Girls generally reach peak height velocity earlier than boys because puberty begins earlier.

123. Q: Why do boys generally have a greater average adult height than girls?

A: Boys typically experience:

Later onset of puberty
Longer prepubertal growth period
Greater magnitude of pubertal growth

124. Q: What is the Tanner staging system used for?

A: Tanner staging assesses sexual maturation based on development of:

Breast development in girls
Genital development in boys
Pubic hair in both sexes

125. Q: What does Tanner stage I represent?

A: Prepubertal development with no significant secondary sexual characteristics.

126. Q: What does Tanner stage V represent?

A: Adult sexual maturation.

127. Q: A 14-year-old boy has small testes, sparse pubic hair, short stature, and no other pubertal development. What is the appropriate next step?

A: Evaluate for delayed puberty, including growth history, family history, physical examination, bone age, and appropriate endocrine testing.

128. Q: Why is growth velocity particularly useful when evaluating short stature?

A: A single height measurement cannot distinguish a normal small child from a child with impaired growth.

Serial measurements allow calculation of growth velocity, which can reveal pathologic growth failure.

129. Q: What is failure of catch-up growth after intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR)?

A: Most infants who are small for gestational age demonstrate catch-up growth, particularly during infancy. Persistent poor linear growth warrants evaluation for genetic, endocrine, nutritional, or systemic causes.

130. Q: What is the difference between small for gestational age and constitutional small size?

A: Small for gestational age (SGA) refers to birth size below the expected range for gestational age.

A constitutionally small child may simply have genetically determined small stature and normal postnatal growth.

131. Q: What is the typical pattern of brain growth compared with overall body growth?

A: Brain growth occurs especially rapidly during early childhood.

Head circumference therefore increases rapidly during infancy and early childhood before growth slows.

132. Q: Why is head circumference routinely measured during early childhood?

A: It provides an indirect measure of brain growth and can help identify disorders such as microcephaly, macrocephaly, and hydrocephalus.

133. Q: A child has a large head circumference but normal development and similarly large-headed parents. What is a possible explanation?

A: Benign familial macrocephaly.

A normal neurologic examination and stable growth trajectory support a benign familial variant.

134. Q: What is the clinical significance of developmental dissociation?

A: A child may have delay in one developmental domain while other domains remain relatively preserved.

For example, isolated language delay may occur with normal motor and social development.

135. Q: A toddler has isolated expressive-language delay but normal receptive language, social interaction, hearing, and motor development. What is a likely diagnosis?

A: Developmental language delay, which may be a transient developmental variation.

However, hearing and broader developmental assessment should still be performed.

136. Q: Why is receptive-language delay generally more concerning than isolated expressive-language delay?

A: Impaired receptive language suggests a broader developmental or neurologic problem, whereas isolated expressive delay can occur in otherwise normally developing children.

137. Q: What is the clinical significance of regression of motor skills?

A: Motor regression is always concerning and requires evaluation for neurologic, metabolic, neuromuscular, or neurodegenerative disease.

138. Q: A previously toilet-trained child begins wetting the bed after a major family stressor. What category of enuresis is this?

A: Secondary enuresis.

Psychosocial stress can contribute, but medical causes such as UTI and diabetes mellitus should also be considered.

139. Q: A previously toilet-trained child develops both daytime and nighttime urinary accidents. What should be considered?

A: Consider:

UTI
Constipation
Diabetes mellitus
Bladder dysfunction
Psychosocial stressors

Daytime symptoms are less typical of uncomplicated monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis.

140. Q: What developmental characteristic is expected in a normal toddler's play?

A: Toddlers commonly engage in parallel play, playing beside other children without sustained cooperative interaction.

141. Q: What is symbolic play?

A: Using one object to represent another or engaging in pretend behavior—for example, using a block as a telephone.

It emerges during the early childhood/preoperational period.

142. Q: What is stranger anxiety developmentally?

A: A normal infant response involving distress or caution around unfamiliar people.

It typically emerges during later infancy as attachment and recognition abilities mature.

143. Q: Why is separation anxiety developmentally normal during infancy?

A: It reflects the development of attachment and object permanence.

The infant recognizes that a caregiver exists even when out of sight and may therefore become distressed when separated.

144. Q: A 3-year-old frequently says "mine," has difficulty sharing, and insists on routines. Is this necessarily pathologic?

A: No. Egocentrism, possessiveness, and difficulty sharing are common during normal early childhood development.

145. Q: What is the expected emotional development of a school-aged child compared with a toddler?

A: School-aged children develop greater:

Independence
Peer relationships
Rule-based behavior
Understanding of social expectations
Ability to consider perspectives beyond themselves

146. Q: What psychosocial developmental task characterizes Erikson's school-age period?

A: Industry vs inferiority.

Children develop competence through school, skills, accomplishments, and peer interactions.

147. Q: What psychosocial developmental task characterizes adolescence?

A: Identity vs role confusion.

The adolescent develops an integrated sense of identity, values, goals, and social role.

148. Q: What psychosocial developmental task characterizes toddlerhood?

A: Autonomy vs shame and doubt.

Toddlers seek independence, control, and mastery of basic activities.

149. Q: What psychosocial developmental task characterizes early childhood?

A: Initiative vs guilt.

Children increasingly initiate activities, explore their environment, and develop a sense of purpose.

150. Q: A pediatrician is concerned about a child's development despite the parent reporting that "everything seems fine." What is the appropriate approach?

A: Perform developmental surveillance, including parental concerns, developmental history, direct observation, physical examination, and clinical judgment. If concerns persist, use a validated developmental screening tool and arrange appropriate evaluation/intervention. AAP recommends surveillance at every health supervision visit and standardized developmental screening at 9, 18, and 30 months.

High-yield distinction: developmental surveillance is ongoing, whereas developmental screening uses standardized tools at specified ages or when concerns arise.


151. Q: A 4-year-old has normal height and weight but has difficulty copying a cross, dressing independently, and using scissors. Which developmental domain is primarily affected?

A: Fine motor/adaptive development.

Fine motor skills involve coordination of small muscles, particularly the hands and fingers, and are distinct from gross motor skills such as running and jumping.

152. Q: A 3-year-old can run and climb normally but cannot speak in sentences or follow two-step commands. Which developmental domain is primarily delayed?

A: Language/cognitive development.

Normal gross motor development does not exclude significant language or cognitive delay.

153. Q: A 2-year-old has delayed speech but demonstrates appropriate eye contact, pointing, pretend play, and social interaction. What is the most appropriate initial consideration?

A: Isolated language delay, while still evaluating hearing and overall development.

Normal social reciprocity makes autism less likely but does not completely exclude it.

154. Q: What is the difference between expressive and receptive language?

A: Expressive language is the ability to communicate thoughts using sounds, words, or sentences.

Receptive language is the ability to understand language spoken by others.

155. Q: A child speaks very few words but follows commands appropriately and communicates with gestures. Is receptive or expressive language more affected?

A: Expressive language is more affected.

Understanding commands indicates relatively preserved receptive language.

156. Q: A toddler cannot follow simple verbal commands and has very limited vocabulary. What should be evaluated in addition to speech development?

A: Evaluate hearing and global development.

Receptive-language impairment may indicate hearing loss, intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorder, or another developmental disorder.

157. Q: What is the developmental significance of pointing?

A: Pointing is an important nonverbal communication and joint-attention skill.

Absence of pointing to share interest can be an early sign of autism spectrum disorder.

158. Q: A 15-month-old points to a toy, looks at the parent, and vocalizes to get the parent's attention. What developmental ability does this demonstrate?

A: Joint attention and social communication.

The child is intentionally directing another person's attention toward an object or event.

159. Q: A 20-month-old rarely makes eye contact, does not point to show objects, and does not respond consistently to his name. What disorder should be suspected?

A: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD).

These are important early abnormalities in social communication.

160. Q: What is the appropriate next step when autism is suspected based on clinical observation?

A: Perform an autism-specific screening assessment and comprehensive developmental evaluation rather than waiting for symptoms to resolve.

AAP recommends ASD screening at 18 and 24 months, in addition to ongoing developmental surveillance.

161. Q: Can a normal autism screening result completely exclude autism?

A: No.

Screening tools identify children at increased risk; they do not establish or definitively exclude the diagnosis.

Persistent clinical concerns require further evaluation.

162. Q: What is the purpose of developmental screening?

A: To identify children who may have developmental delays or disorders requiring further assessment or intervention.

Screening is not the same as making a definitive diagnosis.

163. Q: What is the purpose of developmental surveillance?

A: Surveillance is continuous monitoring of development through parental concerns, developmental history, observation, risk assessment, documentation, and communication.

It occurs at every health supervision visit.

164. Q: A parent expresses concern about a child's development at a visit occurring before the next scheduled screening age. Should the pediatrician wait until the routine screening visit?

A: No.

Developmental concerns should prompt additional standardized screening and/or referral immediately, rather than waiting for the next scheduled screening age.

165. Q: A child misses the 18-month developmental screening visit. What should be done?

A: Perform the developmental screen at the next available opportunity rather than waiting until the next routinely scheduled screening age.

166. Q: Why are developmental screening tools not diagnostic tests?

A: Screening identifies children who are at increased risk and need further evaluation.

Diagnosis requires a comprehensive assessment involving history, examination, developmental testing, and sometimes specialty evaluation.

167. Q: What is the significance of developmental milestones being achieved in a particular sequence?

A: Development generally follows a predictable sequence, although the exact timing varies.

A child who is late but follows the expected sequence may have a normal variant, whereas loss of skills or an abnormal sequence is more concerning.

168. Q: What does the principle of cephalocaudal development mean?

A: Development proceeds generally from head to toe.

For example, infants develop head control before trunk control and eventually lower-extremity motor skills.

169. Q: What does the principle of proximodistal development mean?

A: Motor control generally develops from central/proximal structures toward distal structures.

For example, control of the shoulder and arm develops before refined finger movements.

170. Q: A child can make large arm movements but cannot manipulate small objects with the fingers. Which developmental process is still maturing?

A: Distal fine-motor control.

Fine motor precision develops after more basic proximal motor control.

171. Q: Why is developmental trajectory often more clinically useful than a single milestone?

A: Development is variable, and individual milestones may occur somewhat earlier or later.

A persistent delay, abnormal trajectory, multiple-domain delay, or regression is more concerning than isolated variation.

172. Q: What is global developmental delay?

A: Significant delay in ≥2 developmental domains, such as motor, language, cognition, social-personal, or adaptive functioning, in a young child.

173. Q: A child has delays in gross motor, fine motor, language, and social development. What category of developmental problem should be considered?

A: Global developmental delay.

Multiple affected domains suggest a broader developmental disorder rather than an isolated skill delay.

174. Q: What is intellectual disability?

A: Intellectual disability involves deficits in intellectual functioning and adaptive functioning with onset during the developmental period.

Adaptive functioning includes conceptual, social, and practical skills.

175. Q: What are the three major domains of adaptive functioning?

A:

Conceptual — language, reading, writing, mathematics
Social — communication, relationships, social judgment
Practical — self-care and independent daily activities

176. Q: What is a developmental quotient (DQ)?

A: A developmental quotient estimates developmental performance relative to chronological age:

DQ=
Chronological age
Developmental age
	​

×100

A substantially reduced DQ suggests developmental delay, but formal diagnosis requires comprehensive assessment.

177. Q: A 3-year-old functions developmentally like a 2-year-old. What is the approximate developmental quotient?

A:

DQ=
3
2
	​

×100≈67

So the developmental quotient is approximately 67.

178. Q: What is the purpose of early intervention in children with developmental delays?

A: Early intervention provides speech, occupational, physical, behavioral, educational, and other therapies during a period of rapid neurodevelopment.

Treatment should not be unnecessarily delayed while waiting for a definitive etiologic diagnosis.

179. Q: A toddler has motor delay but normal social interaction and language. What important examination finding should be assessed?

A: Assess muscle tone, strength, reflexes, gait, symmetry, and neurologic examination to distinguish central from peripheral motor disorders.

180. Q: What does hypotonia suggest in a child with motor delay?

A: Hypotonia may result from:

Central nervous system disorders
Neuromuscular disease
Genetic/metabolic disorders

The distribution of weakness and reflex findings help distinguish the cause.

181. Q: What does spasticity in a child with delayed motor milestones suggest?

A: It suggests an upper motor neuron/central nervous system disorder, such as cerebral palsy.

182. Q: A child has progressive weakness and loss of previously acquired motor skills. Is this typical of cerebral palsy?

A: No.

Cerebral palsy is a nonprogressive disorder of the developing brain.

Progressive weakness or regression suggests another neurologic or neuromuscular disorder.

183. Q: What is the developmental significance of a child who cannot bear weight at an age when independent walking should already be established?

A: This represents gross motor delay and warrants evaluation for neurologic, neuromuscular, orthopedic, nutritional, and other causes.

184. Q: A child with delayed motor milestones has enlarged calves and difficulty rising from the floor. What disorder should be suspected?

A: Duchenne muscular dystrophy.

Typical findings include:

Proximal muscle weakness
Gowers sign
Calf pseudohypertrophy
Delayed motor development

185. Q: What does a positive Gowers sign indicate?

A: Difficulty rising from the floor without using the hands to push against the thighs, suggesting proximal muscle weakness.

It is classically associated with Duchenne muscular dystrophy.

186. Q: A child has delayed motor development, hypotonia, and a strong family history of neuromuscular disease. What type of disorder should be considered?

A: Neuromuscular disease, including muscular dystrophy, spinal muscular atrophy, or congenital myopathy.

187. Q: Why is hearing assessment important in a child with developmental delay?

A: Hearing impairment can cause speech, language, social, and academic difficulties and may mimic broader developmental delay.

188. Q: A toddler has delayed speech but normal cognitive, motor, and social development. Hearing testing is normal. What is an appropriate management principle?

A: Provide developmental/language evaluation and early intervention when indicated, rather than adopting a prolonged "wait and see" approach.

189. Q: What is the developmental importance of pretend play?

A: Pretend play reflects development of symbolic thought, imagination, and cognitive/social skills.

Reduced pretend play can be seen in developmental disorders including ASD.

190. Q: What type of play involves children working together toward a common goal?

A: Cooperative play.

It becomes increasingly prominent during the preschool period.

191. Q: A 4-year-old insists that a stuffed animal is "sad" because its toy friend is missing. What cognitive feature does this demonstrate?

A: Animistic thinking — attributing human characteristics, feelings, or intentions to nonliving objects.

This is typical of the preoperational stage.

192. Q: A preschool child believes that getting sick occurred because they were "bad." What developmental concept may explain this?

A: Magical thinking.

Young children may believe thoughts, wishes, or actions can directly cause events.

193. Q: Why should clinicians use concrete explanations with preschool-aged children?

A: Preschool children are still developing logical and abstract reasoning and may interpret language literally or magically.

Simple, concrete explanations are easier to understand.

194. Q: What cognitive ability develops during the concrete operational stage?

A: Children develop logical reasoning involving concrete situations, including conservation, classification, seriation, and reversibility.

195. Q: What is seriation?

A: The ability to arrange objects according to a characteristic, such as ordering sticks from shortest to longest.

It is characteristic of the concrete operational period.

196. Q: What cognitive change allows adolescents to reason about hypothetical situations?

A: Development of formal operational thinking, which allows abstract, hypothetical, and systematic reasoning.

197. Q: What is the significance of an adolescent suddenly developing markedly different academic performance and behavior?

A: Consider developmental, psychosocial, psychiatric, substance-related, sleep-related, and medical causes rather than assuming it represents normal adolescence.

A change from the child's established developmental trajectory warrants assessment.

198. Q: What is the developmental importance of peer relationships during adolescence?

A: Peer relationships become increasingly important for identity formation, autonomy, social development, and emotional development.

199. Q: At what well-child age should clinicians pay particular attention to school readiness?

A: The 4–5-year visit is particularly important for assessing developmental skills relevant to school readiness.

AAP emphasizes developmental surveillance at this visit, with screening when concerns are identified.

200. Q: What is the most important USMLE Step 2 principle when a child has a developmental concern?

A: Do not simply reassure and wait if there is a genuine developmental concern.

The appropriate approach is:

Recognize the abnormality → perform developmental surveillance/screening → evaluate hearing and relevant medical causes → refer for appropriate developmental assessment/early intervention.

201. Q: A 2-year-old child has a normal neurologic examination but has not begun combining words. What should be the next step?

A: Perform formal developmental assessment and hearing evaluation rather than simply reassuring the parents. Language delay warrants evaluation even when other developmental domains appear normal.

202. Q: A 3-year-old understands commands but has unintelligible speech. What should be considered?

A: Speech sound disorder or expressive language disorder should be considered after assessing hearing and overall development.

203. Q: A child has normal intelligence and language but has persistent difficulty coordinating movements such as dressing, using utensils, and handwriting. What disorder should be considered?

A: Developmental coordination disorder (DCD).

It causes impaired motor coordination that interferes with activities of daily living or academic performance and is not explained by another neurologic disorder.

204. Q: What is the key feature distinguishing developmental coordination disorder from cerebral palsy?

A: DCD causes motor coordination impairment without the characteristic neurologic abnormalities of cerebral palsy, such as persistent spasticity or other upper motor neuron findings.

205. Q: A school-aged child has difficulty reading despite normal intelligence, vision, and adequate schooling. What developmental disorder should be suspected?

A: Specific learning disorder, such as dyslexia.

The difficulty is disproportionate to the child's overall intellectual ability and educational opportunity.

206. Q: What is dyslexia?

A: A specific learning disorder involving reading, particularly difficulties with accurate or fluent word recognition, decoding, and spelling.

207. Q: What is dyscalculia?

A: A specific learning disorder involving mathematical reasoning and numerical skills.

208. Q: What is dysgraphia?

A: A learning difficulty involving written expression, such as handwriting, spelling, grammar, or organization of written work.

209. Q: A child performs well verbally but has persistent difficulty with reading and spelling. What developmental pattern does this suggest?

A: Specific learning disorder, rather than global intellectual disability.

A child can have normal or above-average intelligence while having a specific academic deficit.

210. Q: What is the developmental significance of regression in academic performance?

A: A significant decline from previously established functioning warrants evaluation for learning disorders, ADHD, mood disorders, sleep problems, psychosocial stress, substance use, neurologic disease, or other medical conditions.

211. Q: A school-aged child has persistent difficulty maintaining attention and completing assignments across both school and home settings. What should be considered?

A: Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) should be considered after assessing symptoms across settings and excluding alternative explanations.

212. Q: Why must ADHD symptoms be present in more than one setting?

A: ADHD is characterized by persistent impairment across settings, such as home and school. Symptoms confined to a single environment may suggest environmental, educational, family, or other contextual causes.

213. Q: What developmental requirement must be met before diagnosing ADHD?

A: Symptoms must have been present for at least 6 months, be inappropriate for developmental level, cause functional impairment, and have onset during childhood according to diagnostic criteria.

214. Q: A 4-year-old has frequent tantrums but otherwise demonstrates appropriate development and functioning. Is this automatically abnormal?

A: No. Temper tantrums are common during toddler and preschool development because emotional regulation is still developing.

Persistent, extreme, dangerous, or developmentally inappropriate behavior warrants further assessment.

215. Q: Why are tantrums common during toddlerhood?

A: Toddlers have increasing desires for autonomy but limited language, impulse control, and emotional regulation.

Tantrums can therefore be part of normal development.

216. Q: What is an important developmental goal during toddlerhood?

A: Development of autonomy and self-regulation.

Toddlers increasingly attempt independent feeding, dressing, toileting, and decision-making.

217. Q: What is the appropriate parental approach to normal toddler tantrums?

A: Parents should use consistent limits, predictable routines, positive reinforcement, and calm responses while avoiding reinforcement of tantrums.

218. Q: What is the developmental significance of a child's ability to follow increasingly complex commands?

A: It reflects maturation of receptive language, cognition, attention, and executive functioning.

Failure to understand age-appropriate commands should prompt assessment of hearing and development.

219. Q: What is executive function?

A: A group of higher-order cognitive abilities involving:

Planning
Working memory
Inhibitory control
Cognitive flexibility
Organization
Self-regulation

These skills continue developing throughout childhood and adolescence.

220. Q: Why can adolescents demonstrate impulsive decision-making despite having adult-level intellectual abilities?

A: Brain systems involved in reward, emotion, and impulse control mature at different rates. Executive-control networks continue developing through adolescence.

221. Q: What is the developmental significance of imaginary fears during early childhood?

A: Fears of monsters, darkness, or imaginary creatures are often developmentally normal during preschool years because imagination develops before abstract reasoning is fully mature.

222. Q: A 5-year-old is afraid of monsters under the bed but has normal functioning and development. What is the most appropriate interpretation?

A: This can represent a normal developmental fear rather than psychopathology.

Clinical concern increases when fears are severe, persistent, developmentally inappropriate, or significantly impair functioning.

223. Q: What is separation anxiety disorder?

A: Excessive and developmentally inappropriate fear or distress concerning separation from attachment figures that causes significant impairment.

It differs from normal separation anxiety by its severity, persistence, and functional impact.

224. Q: How does normal separation anxiety differ from separation anxiety disorder?

A: Normal separation anxiety occurs during expected developmental periods and generally decreases with maturation.

Separation anxiety disorder is excessive, persistent, and impairing.

225. Q: A child develops age-inappropriate difficulty with social interaction and communication after previously normal development. What important diagnosis should be considered?

A: Autism spectrum disorder with developmental regression should be considered, although neurologic, metabolic, hearing, and other causes must also be evaluated.

226. Q: Is loss of previously acquired language considered a normal developmental variant?

A: No.

Loss of established language or social skills warrants evaluation.

227. Q: What is the significance of a child who stops responding to sounds and subsequently develops speech delay?

A: Hearing impairment should be strongly considered.

Hearing loss can disrupt language acquisition and social communication.

228. Q: A child with recurrent otitis media develops speech delay. What developmental assessment is particularly important?

A: Formal hearing evaluation.

Conductive hearing loss from middle-ear disease can interfere with speech and language development.

229. Q: What developmental domains should be assessed during a pediatric developmental evaluation?

A: Major domains include:

Gross motor
Fine motor
Language
Cognitive
Social-emotional
Adaptive/self-help

230. Q: Why should adaptive functioning be assessed when evaluating developmental delay?

A: Adaptive functioning reflects how effectively the child performs age-appropriate daily activities, such as feeding, dressing, toileting, communication, and safety behaviors.

231. Q: A child has normal motor and language skills but cannot perform age-appropriate self-care tasks. Which developmental domain may be impaired?

A: Adaptive functioning.

232. Q: What is the developmental significance of a child's ability to dress independently?

A: Independent dressing reflects maturation of fine motor coordination, sequencing, planning, and adaptive functioning.

233. Q: A 5-year-old cannot independently use the toilet, dress, or feed despite having normal strength and coordination. What should be considered?

A: Adaptive/developmental delay should be evaluated, particularly if the deficits interfere with age-appropriate functioning.

234. Q: Why should developmental assessment include the child's functional abilities rather than only milestone recall?

A: Developmental disorders are defined by their effect on functioning. A child may technically achieve certain milestones but still have clinically significant impairment in communication, learning, social interaction, or daily activities.

235. Q: What is developmental regression particularly concerning for in a previously healthy child?

A: It raises concern for neurologic, metabolic, neurodegenerative, toxic, or severe developmental disorders and should not be attributed to normal maturation.

236. Q: A previously normal toddler develops loss of language, repetitive behaviors, and social withdrawal. What broad categories of disease should be considered?

A: Consider:

Autism spectrum disorder
Neurodegenerative/metabolic disease
Hearing impairment
Neurologic disease
Psychosocial causes

A comprehensive evaluation is required.

237. Q: What is the significance of a child who has developmental delay in only one domain?

A: Isolated developmental delay may represent a specific disorder or normal variation, depending on the domain and severity.

The child should still undergo appropriate evaluation rather than being automatically reassured.

238. Q: What does developmental "catch-up" mean?

A: Catch-up growth or development refers to a child progressively approaching the expected trajectory after an earlier period of slower growth or development.

It is common in certain normal variants and in some infants born small for gestational age.

239. Q: What does "catch-down" growth mean?

A: Catch-down growth occurs when a genetically larger infant or child moves toward a lower growth percentile during early life before establishing a stable growth trajectory.

This can be normal if growth subsequently stabilizes.

240. Q: Why should clinicians avoid interpreting a single percentile as evidence of growth failure?

A: Children have different genetic growth potentials. A stable trajectory with normal growth velocity may be normal even at a low or high percentile.

241. Q: What is the significance of disproportionate short stature?

A: Disproportionate body proportions suggest disorders affecting skeletal growth, such as skeletal dysplasias, rather than many endocrine or nutritional causes of proportionate short stature.

242. Q: A child has short limbs relative to the trunk and markedly short stature. What broad category of disease should be considered?

A: Skeletal dysplasia.

Assessment should include body proportions, family history, physical examination, and appropriate radiographic/genetic evaluation.

243. Q: What is the significance of an abnormally low sitting-height-to-height ratio in a short child?

A: Abnormal body proportions can help distinguish skeletal dysplasia or other disproportionate growth disorders from proportionate short stature.

244. Q: What is the importance of measuring arm span in a child with abnormal stature?

A: Arm span helps assess body proportions.

Abnormal arm-span-to-height relationships may suggest skeletal dysplasia or connective-tissue disorders.

245. Q: A tall adolescent has disproportionately long limbs and an arm span greater than height. What should be considered?

A: Marfan syndrome or another connective-tissue disorder should be considered, particularly if accompanied by features such as arachnodactyly, lens abnormalities, or aortic root dilation.

246. Q: What is the developmental significance of delayed eruption of primary teeth?

A: Delayed dentition can occur with constitutional variation, but significant delay may be associated with hypothyroidism, nutritional deficiency, or other systemic disorders.

It should be interpreted in the context of overall growth and development.

247. Q: What is the usual order of eruption of primary teeth?

A: Lower central incisors generally erupt first, followed by upper central incisors and subsequent primary teeth.

Primary dentition ultimately consists of 20 teeth.

248. Q: What is the significance of markedly delayed dental eruption accompanied by poor linear growth and constipation?

A: Consider hypothyroidism, which can impair skeletal growth, dentition, and overall development.

249. Q: A child has normal physical growth but significant delays in multiple developmental domains. What does this demonstrate?

A: Normal somatic growth does not exclude developmental disability.

Growth assessment and developmental assessment are separate components of pediatric care.

250. Q: What is the highest-yield distinction between growth assessment and developmental assessment on USMLE Step 2?

A: Growth = physical maturation measured by parameters such as weight, length/height, and head circumference.

Development = acquisition of functional skills involving motor, language, cognitive, social-emotional, and adaptive domains.

A child can have normal growth with abnormal development, or abnormal growth with relatively normal development.

251. Q: What is the difference between growth velocity and growth percentile?

A: Growth percentile describes a child's size relative to peers at a particular time.

Growth velocity describes how rapidly the child is growing over time.

A normal growth velocity is often more reassuring than being at a low percentile.

252. Q: A child drops from the 50th to the 10th height percentile over 2 years. Why is this concerning?

A: Significant downward crossing of growth percentiles suggests decreased growth velocity and warrants evaluation for nutritional, systemic, endocrine, genetic, or other causes.

253. Q: A child remains at the 3rd percentile for height but has normal growth velocity and normal development. What is the most appropriate interpretation?

A: This may represent normal variant short stature, particularly if the child's genetic potential and family heights are consistent with the finding.

254. Q: Which is more concerning: a child consistently at the 5th percentile or a child falling from the 75th to the 25th percentile?

A: The child falling across percentiles is more concerning because it suggests impaired growth velocity.

255. Q: What is the typical effect of chronic malnutrition on growth?

A: Weight is affected first, followed by linear growth and, in severe prolonged malnutrition, head growth.

256. Q: What is the typical effect of growth hormone deficiency on weight?

A: Children with growth hormone deficiency often have relatively increased weight for height, unlike many children with nutritional growth failure.

257. Q: A short child has delayed bone age, increased weight for height, and poor growth velocity. Which category of disease is most likely?

A: Endocrine disease, such as growth hormone deficiency or hypothyroidism.

258. Q: A child has short stature, low weight, chronic diarrhea, and abdominal distension. What broad cause of growth failure should be considered?

A: Malabsorption, such as celiac disease, should be considered.

259. Q: Why can chronic systemic disease impair linear growth?

A: Chronic illness can reduce caloric intake, increase metabolic demands, impair nutrient absorption, or alter endocrine signaling required for normal growth.

260. Q: A child with chronic kidney disease develops growth failure. What contributes to impaired growth?

A: Multiple factors can contribute, including:

Poor nutrition
Chronic inflammation
Metabolic abnormalities
Renal osteodystrophy
Disturbances in the growth hormone/IGF-1 axis

261. Q: What is the insulin-like growth factor responsible for many growth hormone effects on skeletal growth?

A: Insulin-like growth factor 1 (IGF-1), produced primarily by the liver in response to growth hormone.

262. Q: What is the relationship between growth hormone and IGF-1?

A: Growth hormone stimulates production of IGF-1, which mediates many of the growth-promoting effects of GH on cartilage and bone.

263. Q: What laboratory pattern can suggest growth hormone deficiency?

A: Low IGF-1 may support the diagnosis, but IGF-1 is affected by nutrition and other conditions. Diagnosis generally requires growth assessment plus provocative GH testing and appropriate endocrine evaluation.

264. Q: Why is a random growth hormone level generally not useful for diagnosing growth hormone deficiency?

A: GH secretion is pulsatile, so a random measurement may be low even in a healthy child.

265. Q: What is the role of bone age in evaluating short stature?

A: Bone age estimates skeletal maturation and helps distinguish normal variants from pathologic causes.

266. Q: A child's chronological age is 12 years but bone age is 9 years. What does this indicate?

A: Delayed skeletal maturation.

This can occur in constitutional growth delay, endocrine disorders, chronic disease, and some nutritional disorders.

267. Q: What bone-age pattern is typical of familial short stature?

A: Bone age is generally consistent with chronological age.

268. Q: What bone-age pattern is typical of constitutional growth delay?

A: Bone age is younger than chronological age.

269. Q: Why can constitutional growth delay eventually result in normal adult height?

A: Delayed skeletal maturation leaves additional time for linear growth before epiphyseal closure.

270. Q: A boy has short stature and delayed puberty. His father also had delayed puberty but eventually reached normal adult height. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Constitutional delay of growth and puberty.

271. Q: What is the most important family-history question when evaluating delayed puberty?

A: Ask whether parents or siblings experienced late puberty or delayed growth.

A positive family history strongly supports constitutional delay.

272. Q: What is central precocious puberty?

A: Precocious puberty caused by premature activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, resulting in normal pubertal progression occurring too early.

273. Q: What is peripheral precocious puberty?

A: Precocious sexual development caused by sex steroids produced independently of normal HPG-axis activation.

Potential causes include adrenal, gonadal, or exogenous sex-steroid sources.

274. Q: What distinguishes central from peripheral precocious puberty clinically?

A: Central precocious puberty generally follows a normal sequence of puberty.

Peripheral precocious puberty may produce incomplete or atypical pubertal development.

275. Q: What is the typical laboratory pattern in central precocious puberty?

A: Gonadotropins become consistent with pubertal HPG-axis activation, particularly after stimulation testing when needed.

276. Q: Why is central precocious puberty particularly important in boys?

A: Precocious puberty in boys is more likely than in girls to have an underlying pathologic CNS or other cause, so evaluation for an underlying etiology is important.

277. Q: What is one important intracranial cause of central precocious puberty?

A: Hypothalamic lesions, including hypothalamic hamartoma.

278. Q: What is a hypothalamic hamartoma?

A: A benign congenital lesion associated with central precocious puberty and sometimes gelastic seizures.

279. Q: A 6-year-old girl develops breast enlargement and vaginal bleeding. What should be suspected?

A: Precocious puberty.

The child requires evaluation to determine whether puberty is central or peripheral and to identify the underlying cause.

280. Q: Why can vaginal bleeding occur in precocious puberty?

A: Estrogen stimulation causes endometrial proliferation followed by withdrawal bleeding, similar to menstruation.

281. Q: A girl has pubic hair and body odor but no breast development. What endocrine process is most likely?

A: Adrenarche, rather than true gonadal puberty.

Premature adrenarche causes androgenic changes without estrogen-mediated breast development.

282. Q: What finding strongly suggests estrogen exposure rather than isolated adrenarche in a girl?

A: Breast development indicates estrogen effect and suggests activation of gonadal puberty or another estrogen source.

283. Q: What finding indicates true gonadal puberty in a boy?

A: Testicular enlargement is the key physical finding.

284. Q: A boy develops pubic hair and acne but has prepubertal-sized testes. What should be considered?

A: Premature adrenarche or another source of androgen exposure.

Testicular enlargement would be expected with central gonadal puberty.

285. Q: What is the typical sequence of male pubertal development?

A: A simplified sequence is:

Testicular enlargement → pubic hair/penile growth → peak height velocity → facial/axillary hair and voice changes

There is individual variation.

286. Q: When does peak height velocity generally occur in boys relative to genital development?

A: It occurs later in puberty, after initial testicular enlargement and progression of genital development.

287. Q: When does peak height velocity generally occur in girls relative to menarche?

A: Peak height velocity generally occurs before menarche.

Growth velocity has already begun slowing by the time most girls experience menarche.

288. Q: Why does growth slow after puberty?

A: Increasing sex-steroid exposure accelerates epiphyseal maturation, eventually causing growth plates to close.

289. Q: What hormone is primarily responsible for epiphyseal closure?

A: Estrogen plays the central role in epiphyseal maturation and closure in both sexes.

In males, much estrogen is produced through aromatization of testosterone.

290. Q: Why can estrogen exposure cause accelerated skeletal maturation in boys?

A: Testosterone can be converted by aromatase into estradiol, which promotes epiphyseal maturation.

291. Q: What is the major nutritional factor required for normal linear growth?

A: Adequate energy and protein intake, along with essential micronutrients.

Chronic inadequate caloric intake can impair both weight gain and linear growth.

292. Q: What micronutrient deficiency can cause growth impairment and delayed development?

A: Zinc deficiency can cause growth retardation and impaired immune function, among other manifestations.

293. Q: What vitamin deficiency can cause impaired bone mineralization and growth abnormalities?

A: Vitamin D deficiency, which can cause rickets.

294. Q: What physical finding in a child suggests rickets rather than simple short stature?

A: Findings can include:

Widened wrists
Rachitic rosary
Bowed legs
Delayed dentition
Frontal bossing

295. Q: How can severe chronic nutritional deficiency affect puberty?

A: Severe undernutrition can cause delayed puberty because adequate energy availability is required for normal hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal function.

296. Q: What is functional hypothalamic suppression of puberty?

A: Reduced hypothalamic GnRH secretion due to factors such as low energy availability, excessive exercise, chronic illness, or significant psychological stress, resulting in delayed or absent puberty.

297. Q: An adolescent athlete has very low body weight, intense exercise, and delayed menstruation. What mechanism can explain the delayed puberty/reproductive dysfunction?

A: Low energy availability suppresses hypothalamic GnRH secretion, reducing LH and FSH secretion and impairing reproductive function.

298. Q: What is the relationship between chronic disease and pubertal timing?

A: Chronic systemic disease can cause delayed growth and delayed puberty, particularly when associated with malnutrition or chronic inflammation.

299. Q: A child is short, has delayed puberty, and has a chronic inflammatory disease. What two mechanisms should be considered?

A: Both:

Chronic disease/inflammation impairing growth
Delayed hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal activation

Nutritional status and disease control should be assessed.

300. Q: A 13-year-old has delayed puberty, short stature, and no breast development. Her growth velocity is poor and bone age is markedly delayed. Why is distinguishing constitutional delay from chronic disease important?

A: Constitutional delay is a normal variant, whereas chronic disease requires identification and treatment of the underlying condition.

The distinction is made using growth pattern, growth velocity, bone age, family history, physical examination, and targeted investigations.

301. Q: Which growth chart should be used for a healthy child from birth through 2 years in the United States?

A: The WHO Child Growth Standards are recommended from birth to 2 years.

302. Q: Which growth chart should generally be used for children age 2 years and older in the United States?

A: The CDC 2000 Growth Charts are used for children and adolescents age 2 years and older.

303. Q: What growth parameter is used to assess adiposity in children age ≥2 years?

A: BMI-for-age percentile.

BMI is calculated as:

BMI=
[height (m)]
2
weight (kg)
	​


The BMI is then interpreted according to age- and sex-specific percentiles.

304. Q: Why should adult BMI cutoffs not be directly applied to children?

A: Normal body composition changes substantially with age and sex during childhood. Therefore, pediatric BMI must be interpreted using age- and sex-specific percentiles.

305. Q: What BMI-for-age percentile is considered obesity in children and adolescents?

A: ≥95th percentile for age and sex.

306. Q: What BMI-for-age percentile is considered overweight in children and adolescents?

A: ≥85th to <95th percentile for age and sex.

307. Q: What is the clinical significance of a BMI below the 5th percentile in a child?

A: It may indicate underweight or inadequate nutritional status and should be interpreted alongside the child's growth trajectory, diet, medical history, and physical examination.

308. Q: Why is BMI particularly useful after age 2 years?

A: BMI allows assessment of weight relative to height, making it more useful than weight alone for identifying excessive or insufficient adiposity in older children.

309. Q: A child's weight percentile is falling while height remains relatively preserved. What should be evaluated first?

A: Nutritional intake and caloric adequacy should be assessed first, including feeding history, dietary restrictions, food insecurity, vomiting, diarrhea, and feeding difficulties.

310. Q: A child has poor weight gain despite adequate reported caloric intake. What broad categories should be considered?

A: Consider:

Malabsorption
Increased metabolic requirements
Chronic systemic disease
Endocrine disease
Genetic/metabolic disorders

311. Q: What is the significance of a child whose weight is disproportionately low compared with height?

A: This pattern suggests undernutrition or inadequate caloric intake more than an isolated endocrine growth disorder.

312. Q: A child has poor height gain but relatively preserved or increased weight. What broad category should be suspected?

A: Endocrine disease should be considered, particularly hypothyroidism or growth hormone deficiency.

313. Q: What is the typical growth pattern in untreated hypothyroidism?

A: Reduced linear growth velocity with delayed bone age, often accompanied by relatively preserved or increased weight.

314. Q: What is the typical growth pattern in celiac disease?

A: Chronic malabsorption can cause poor weight gain followed by impaired linear growth if untreated.

315. Q: A child with short stature has abdominal pain, chronic diarrhea, iron-deficiency anemia, and delayed puberty. What underlying condition should be suspected?

A: Celiac disease.

It can cause malabsorption, growth failure, and delayed puberty.

316. Q: Why can chronic glucocorticoid therapy impair growth in children?

A: Glucocorticoids can suppress growth plate activity and growth hormone/IGF-1 signaling, resulting in reduced linear growth.

317. Q: A child receiving prolonged systemic corticosteroid therapy develops decreased height velocity. What is the likely mechanism?

A: Glucocorticoid-mediated inhibition of linear growth.

The medication history is therefore crucial when evaluating growth failure.

318. Q: What is the relationship between chronic inflammation and growth?

A: Chronic inflammatory diseases can impair growth through inflammatory cytokines, reduced appetite, increased metabolic demands, and altered GH/IGF-1 signaling.

319. Q: An adolescent with inflammatory bowel disease has delayed puberty and poor linear growth. What factors may contribute?

A: Both chronic inflammation and inadequate nutrition can impair growth and suppress normal pubertal progression.

320. Q: What is the typical effect of untreated hyperthyroidism on growth?

A: Children may initially demonstrate accelerated growth and advanced bone maturation, potentially resulting in reduced final adult height if untreated.

321. Q: How does hypothyroidism differ from hyperthyroidism with respect to skeletal maturation?

A:
Hypothyroidism → delayed skeletal maturation.

Hyperthyroidism → accelerated skeletal maturation.

322. Q: What is the effect of excess glucocorticoids on bone age?

A: Chronic glucocorticoid exposure can cause growth suppression and delayed skeletal maturation.

323. Q: A child has short stature, delayed bone age, and chronic use of prednisone. What is the likely explanation?

A: Glucocorticoid-induced growth suppression should be considered.

324. Q: What is the significance of a very low growth velocity in a child whose parents are both tall?

A: Familial short stature becomes less likely. Pathologic growth failure should be investigated.

325. Q: Why is parental height useful when evaluating short stature?

A: It provides an estimate of the child's genetic growth potential and helps distinguish familial short stature from pathologic causes.

326. Q: A child's projected adult height is substantially below the expected mid-parental target range. What does this suggest?

A: The child may have pathologic short stature or another growth disorder and warrants further assessment.

327. Q: What is the most important distinction between proportionate and disproportionate short stature?

A: Proportionate short stature affects the body relatively uniformly.

Disproportionate short stature involves abnormal relationships between the trunk and limbs and suggests a skeletal growth disorder.

328. Q: A child has short stature, normal intelligence, and disproportionately short limbs with a relatively preserved trunk. What category of disorder should be considered?

A: Skeletal dysplasia, such as achondroplasia.

329. Q: What growth pattern is characteristic of achondroplasia?

A: Disproportionate short stature with rhizomelic limb shortening, relatively preserved trunk length, macrocephaly, and characteristic facial features.

330. Q: What is the genetic basis of achondroplasia?

A: It is usually caused by an activating mutation in the FGFR3 gene, which inhibits endochondral bone growth.

331. Q: Why does achondroplasia primarily affect long-bone growth?

A: FGFR3 abnormalities interfere with endochondral ossification, which is responsible for longitudinal growth of long bones.

332. Q: What type of skeletal growth is primarily responsible for increasing the length of long bones?

A: Endochondral ossification at the growth plates.

333. Q: What type of ossification primarily forms the flat bones of the skull?

A: Intramembranous ossification.

334. Q: Why is the epiphyseal growth plate clinically important?

A: It is the site of longitudinal bone growth. Once the growth plates close after puberty, further longitudinal growth is no longer possible.

335. Q: What happens to growth plates during puberty?

A: Sex steroids initially promote growth acceleration but ultimately cause epiphyseal maturation and closure.

336. Q: Why can early puberty cause tall stature initially but short adult stature eventually?

A: Early sex-steroid exposure causes:

Early growth acceleration → accelerated bone maturation → premature epiphyseal closure → reduced final adult height.

337. Q: A child with precocious puberty is initially much taller than peers. Why should clinicians not assume this predicts tall adult height?

A: Accelerated skeletal maturation can cause premature growth-plate closure, reducing final adult height.

338. Q: What is the role of bone age in suspected precocious puberty?

A: Bone age helps determine whether sex-steroid exposure has caused accelerated skeletal maturation.

An advanced bone age supports significant hormonal exposure.

339. Q: What is the key clinical distinction between constitutional delay and endocrine growth failure?

A: In constitutional delay, growth velocity is generally normal but skeletal maturation and puberty are delayed.

In endocrine growth disorders, growth velocity is often reduced.

340. Q: What is the significance of a child with short stature whose height velocity has progressively decreased?

A: Progressive reduction in growth velocity is concerning for pathologic growth failure and requires evaluation.

341. Q: Why is growth velocity calculated using measurements obtained months apart rather than from a single visit?

A: Growth velocity requires change in height over time:

Growth velocity=
Δtime
Δheight
	​


Serial accurate measurements are therefore necessary.

342. Q: A child grows from 100 cm to 106 cm over one year. What is the growth velocity?

A:

Growth velocity=106−100=6 cm/year

So the growth velocity is 6 cm/year.

343. Q: Why should standing height be used rather than recumbent length in an older child?
