A: Recumbent length is used primarily in younger children who cannot reliably stand. Standing height is used once the child can be measured appropriately while standing.

344. Q: Why can length and standing height differ slightly when switching measurement methods?

A: Recumbent length is generally slightly greater than standing height because of differences in positioning and spinal compression.

Therefore, clinicians should interpret the transition carefully.

345. Q: What is the significance of crossing percentiles during the first 2 years of life?

A: Some percentile adjustment can occur as infants move toward their genetically determined growth trajectory.

Persistent or marked crossing, especially with poor growth velocity or symptoms, requires evaluation.

346. Q: What is the difference between growth monitoring and growth diagnosis?

A: Growth monitoring tracks weight, length/height, head circumference, and BMI over time.

Growth diagnosis requires integration of the growth pattern with history, examination, family height, nutrition, bone age, and targeted testing.

Growth charts should not be used as the sole diagnostic tool.

347. Q: A premature infant is evaluated for developmental milestones. Why should chronological age sometimes be adjusted?

A: Premature birth means the infant had less intrauterine time for maturation. Corrected age helps compare developmental progress more appropriately.

Current CDC milestone guidance advises using corrected age for infants born more than 3 weeks prematurely.

348. Q: How is corrected age calculated for a premature infant?

A:

Corrected age=Chronological age−(40−gestational age at birth)

where gestational age is expressed in weeks.

349. Q: A child born at 28 weeks' gestation is now 8 months old chronologically. What is the approximate corrected age?

A: The infant was:

40−28=12 weeks premature

Therefore:

8 months−12 weeks≈5 months corrected age

Development should be interpreted using approximately 5 months corrected age.

350. Q: What is the key Step 2 principle when a developmental milestone is absent but the child otherwise appears healthy?

A: Do not diagnose a developmental disorder from a single missed milestone.


=======================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Neonatology

1. Q: What are the major physiologic changes that occur immediately after birth?

A: The newborn must transition from placental gas exchange to pulmonary respiration. Major changes include:

First breaths and lung expansion
Decreased pulmonary vascular resistance
Increased pulmonary blood flow
Closure of fetal circulatory pathways
Establishment of independent thermoregulation and glucose homeostasis

2. Q: What causes pulmonary vascular resistance to decrease after birth?

A: Lung expansion and increased alveolar oxygen tension cause pulmonary vasodilation, markedly decreasing pulmonary vascular resistance.

3. Q: What happens to pulmonary blood flow after birth?

A: It increases substantially because pulmonary vascular resistance falls.

4. Q: What happens to systemic vascular resistance after umbilical cord clamping?

A: Systemic vascular resistance increases because the low-resistance placental circulation is removed.

5. Q: What causes functional closure of the foramen ovale after birth?

A: Increased pulmonary blood flow raises left atrial pressure, while removal of placental circulation decreases right-sided pressure.

Thus:

P
LA
	​

>P
RA
	​


The septum primum is pressed against the septum secundum, producing functional closure.

6. Q: What causes closure of the ductus arteriosus after birth?

A: Increased oxygen tension and decreased circulating prostaglandin E₂ (PGE₂) promote ductal constriction.

7. Q: What happens to the ductus arteriosus if oxygen tension remains low?

A: Low oxygen tension promotes ductal patency.

This is particularly important in premature infants and in certain cyanotic congenital heart lesions.

8. Q: What causes closure of the ductus venosus?

A: Umbilical cord clamping eliminates umbilical venous blood flow, leading to closure of the ductus venosus.

9. Q: What is the purpose of the ductus venosus in fetal circulation?

A: It allows oxygenated blood from the umbilical vein to bypass much of the hepatic circulation and enter the inferior vena cava.

10. Q: What is the normal heart rate of a newborn immediately after birth?

A: Approximately 100–160 beats/min.

A heart rate below 100/min in the newborn is clinically important during neonatal resuscitation.

11. Q: What is the normal respiratory rate of a newborn?

A: Approximately 30–60 breaths/min.

12. Q: What is the normal temperature range for a term newborn?

A: Approximately 36.5–37.5°C.

Maintaining normal temperature is an important component of routine newborn care.

13. Q: Why are newborns particularly susceptible to hypothermia?

A: Newborns have:

Large surface-area-to-body-mass ratio
Limited subcutaneous fat
Immature thermoregulation
Limited ability to shiver

They primarily generate heat through nonshivering thermogenesis.

14. Q: What tissue is primarily responsible for nonshivering thermogenesis in newborns?

A: Brown adipose tissue.

It produces heat through mitochondrial uncoupling and increased oxygen consumption.

15. Q: What is the most important initial intervention to prevent neonatal hypothermia?

A: Dry the newborn and provide warmth, typically with skin-to-skin contact and/or a radiant warmer as clinically appropriate.

16. Q: What are the three questions used in the initial assessment immediately after birth?

A: Ask:

Is the baby term?
Is the baby breathing or crying?
Does the baby have good muscle tone?

If all are reassuring, routine newborn care can proceed.

17. Q: What is the Apgar score?

A: A standardized assessment performed at 1 and 5 minutes after birth using five parameters:

A — Appearance
P — Pulse
G — Grimace
A — Activity
R — Respiration

Each is scored 0, 1, or 2.

18. Q: What five components are evaluated in the Apgar score?

A:

Component	0	1	2
Appearance	Blue/pale	Body pink, extremities blue	Completely pink
Pulse	Absent	<100/min	≥100/min
Grimace	None	Weak response	Vigorous cry/withdrawal
Activity	Limp	Some flexion	Active movement
Respiration	Absent	Slow/irregular	Good cry

19. Q: What does an Apgar score of 7–10 generally indicate?

A: Reassuring neonatal adaptation.

Routine care is generally appropriate if the newborn is otherwise clinically well.

20. Q: What does an Apgar score of 4–6 indicate?

A: Moderately depressed neonatal condition, requiring assessment and potentially supportive intervention.

The Apgar score alone does not determine the need for resuscitation.

21. Q: What does an Apgar score of 0–3 indicate?

A: Severely depressed condition requiring immediate assessment and resuscitative support as indicated.

22. Q: Can the Apgar score determine whether neonatal resuscitation should be initiated?

A: No.

Resuscitation decisions are based on the newborn's breathing, heart rate, and clinical condition, not the Apgar score.

23. Q: When is the Apgar score routinely assigned?

A: At 1 minute and 5 minutes after birth.

If the 5-minute score remains <7, additional scores are generally recorded every 5 minutes until 20 minutes.

24. Q: What is the single most important indicator of whether a newborn requires immediate resuscitative intervention?

A: Heart rate, together with assessment of breathing and respiratory effort.

25. Q: A newborn is apneic immediately after delivery. What is the first priority?

A: Provide the initial steps of newborn stabilization:

Warm → dry → position airway → stimulate → clear airway if needed.

If apnea persists or the heart rate is <100/min, positive-pressure ventilation (PPV) is indicated.

26. Q: When is positive-pressure ventilation indicated during neonatal resuscitation?

A: PPV is indicated when the newborn has:

Apnea
Gasping
Heart rate <100/min after initial steps

27. Q: What is the most important intervention for a newborn with persistent apnea?

A: Positive-pressure ventilation.

Ventilation is the cornerstone of neonatal resuscitation because most neonatal arrests are primarily respiratory.

28. Q: A newborn has a heart rate of 80/min after initial stabilization but is breathing inadequately. What should be done?

A: Initiate positive-pressure ventilation.

A heart rate <100/min after initial steps indicates inadequate ventilation until proven otherwise.

29. Q: After 30 seconds of effective PPV, a newborn's heart rate remains below 60/min. What is the next step?

A: Begin chest compressions coordinated with PPV, preferably using an alternative airway such as an endotracheal tube or laryngeal mask when appropriate.

30. Q: What compression-to-ventilation ratio is used during neonatal resuscitation?

A: 3:1

Thus:

3 compressions:1 ventilation

This produces approximately 90 compressions and 30 breaths/min.

31. Q: Where are neonatal chest compressions performed?

A: On the lower third of the sternum, avoiding the xiphoid process.

The preferred technique is the two-thumb encircling-hands technique.

32. Q: What is the target depth for neonatal chest compressions?

A: Compress the chest to approximately one-third of the anterior-posterior diameter of the chest.

33. Q: After effective PPV and chest compressions, the newborn's heart rate remains <60/min. What medication should be given?

A: Epinephrine should be administered.

34. Q: What is the preferred route for epinephrine during neonatal resuscitation?

A: Intravenous access through an umbilical venous catheter (UVC) is preferred.

Intraosseous access may be considered if IV/UVC access cannot rapidly be established.

35. Q: What concentration of epinephrine is used for neonatal resuscitation?

A: The recommended IV concentration is 0.1 mg/mL (1:10,000).

36. Q: What is the major difference between neonatal and adult cardiopulmonary arrest?

A: Neonatal arrest is usually caused by respiratory failure/hypoxia, whereas adult cardiac arrest is more commonly primarily cardiac.

Therefore, effective ventilation is the cornerstone of neonatal resuscitation.

37. Q: When should supplemental oxygen be used during neonatal resuscitation?

A: Oxygen concentration is adjusted according to gestational age and pulse oximetry. Term and late-preterm infants generally begin with room air (21% oxygen), while preterm infants may require a lower supplemental oxygen concentration with titration based on oxygen saturation.

38. Q: Why should excessive oxygen exposure be avoided in premature infants?

A: Hyperoxia can contribute to oxidative injury, including increased risk of retinopathy of prematurity and bronchopulmonary dysplasia.

39. Q: Where should the pulse oximeter be placed during neonatal resuscitation?

A: On the right hand or wrist for preductal oxygen saturation.

40. Q: Why is right-hand pulse oximetry considered preductal?

A: The right upper extremity receives blood from the aortic arch before potential mixing through the ductus arteriosus, providing an estimate of oxygenation before ductal mixing.

41. Q: What is a preterm infant?

A: An infant born before 37 completed weeks of gestation.

42. Q: How is extremely preterm birth defined?

A: <28 weeks gestation.

43. Q: How is very preterm birth defined?

A: 28 to <32 weeks gestation.

44. Q: How is moderate-to-late preterm birth defined?

A: 32 to <37 weeks gestation.

45. Q: What is a term infant?

A: An infant born at 37–41 completed weeks.

46. Q: What is early-term birth?

A: 37 weeks 0 days through 38 weeks 6 days.

47. Q: What is full-term birth?

A: 39 weeks 0 days through 40 weeks 6 days.

48. Q: What is late-term birth?

A: 41 weeks 0 days through 41 weeks 6 days.

49. Q: What is post-term birth?

A: Birth at ≥42 weeks gestation.

50. Q: What are major complications associated with prematurity?

A: Major complications include:

Respiratory distress syndrome
Apnea of prematurity
Intraventricular hemorrhage
Necrotizing enterocolitis
Patent ductus arteriosus
Retinopathy of prematurity
Bronchopulmonary dysplasia
Sepsis
Hypothermia
Hypoglycemia
Feeding difficulties
Long-term neurodevelopmental impairment

51. Q: What is the primary cause of respiratory distress syndrome (RDS) in premature infants?

A: Pulmonary surfactant deficiency, causing increased alveolar surface tension, alveolar collapse, and decreased lung compliance.

52. Q: Which cells produce pulmonary surfactant?

A: Type II pneumocytes.

53. Q: What is the major phospholipid component of pulmonary surfactant?

A: Dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine (DPPC), also called lecithin.

54. Q: What is the function of pulmonary surfactant?

A: It reduces alveolar surface tension, preventing alveolar collapse, particularly at the end of expiration.

55. Q: Why are premature infants particularly susceptible to RDS?

A: Surfactant production increases substantially late in gestation. Therefore, the risk of RDS increases as gestational age decreases.

56. Q: What maternal condition increases the risk of neonatal RDS even when the infant is relatively mature?

A: Maternal diabetes mellitus.

Fetal hyperinsulinemia can delay surfactant maturation.

57. Q: What antenatal medication reduces the risk of respiratory distress syndrome in an anticipated preterm delivery?

A: Antenatal glucocorticoids, such as betamethasone or dexamethasone, accelerate fetal lung maturation and surfactant production.

58. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of neonatal RDS?

A: Diffuse bilateral reticulogranular ("ground-glass") opacities with air bronchograms and low lung volumes.

59. Q: Why does RDS produce low lung volumes on chest x-ray?

A: Surfactant deficiency causes alveolar collapse and decreased pulmonary compliance, resulting in reduced lung expansion.

60. Q: When does respiratory distress from neonatal RDS typically begin?

A: Shortly after birth, often progressively worsening over the first several hours.

61. Q: What are classic clinical findings of neonatal RDS?

A:

Tachypnea
Nasal flaring
Grunting
Intercostal/subcostal retractions
Hypoxemia
Increasing oxygen requirement

These findings reflect increased work of breathing.

62. Q: Why does a newborn with RDS grunt during expiration?

A: Expiratory grunting creates positive end-expiratory pressure, helping maintain alveolar patency.

63. Q: What is the initial respiratory support commonly used for a spontaneously breathing premature infant with RDS?

A: Noninvasive respiratory support, commonly CPAP, with oxygen titrated according to oxygen saturation.

64. Q: What is the role of exogenous surfactant in neonatal RDS?

A: Surfactant replacement improves lung compliance and oxygenation and reduces complications associated with surfactant deficiency.

65. Q: A premature infant has worsening respiratory distress despite noninvasive support. What treatment should be considered?

A: Exogenous surfactant and escalation of respiratory support, depending on the severity and clinical response.

66. Q: What is transient tachypnea of the newborn (TTN)?

A: TTN is delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid, producing transient respiratory distress after birth.

67. Q: Which newborns are at increased risk of TTN?

A: Risk is increased with:

Cesarean delivery without labor
Late-preterm/early-term birth
Maternal diabetes
Male sex

68. Q: When does TTN usually present?

A: Shortly after birth, with tachypnea often being the predominant finding.

69. Q: What is the typical course of TTN?

A: It is generally self-limited, with improvement within approximately 24–72 hours.

70. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding in TTN?

A: Prominent pulmonary vascular markings, interlobar fissure fluid, and hyperinflation.

71. Q: How can TTN be distinguished from neonatal RDS clinically?

A: TTN is more common in term/late-preterm infants, especially after cesarean delivery, and usually resolves quickly.

RDS is more strongly associated with prematurity and surfactant deficiency.

72. Q: A term infant born by elective cesarean delivery develops tachypnea shortly after birth. Chest x-ray shows fluid in the fissures and prominent pulmonary vascular markings. What is the diagnosis?

A: Transient tachypnea of the newborn.

73. Q: What is meconium aspiration syndrome (MAS)?

A: Respiratory disease caused by aspiration of meconium-stained amniotic fluid, resulting in airway obstruction, inflammation, chemical pneumonitis, and potentially air trapping.

74. Q: Which newborns are at greatest risk for meconium aspiration syndrome?

A: It is more common in term and post-term infants, particularly when there is fetal distress and passage of meconium.

75. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of meconium aspiration syndrome?

A: Patchy, coarse, bilateral pulmonary opacities with areas of hyperinflation and atelectasis.

76. Q: Why can meconium aspiration cause a pneumothorax?

A: Meconium can produce partial airway obstruction, creating a ball-valve effect with air trapping and alveolar rupture.

77. Q: What serious pulmonary vascular complication can occur with meconium aspiration?

A: Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN).

78. Q: What is persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?

A: Failure of the normal postnatal fall in pulmonary vascular resistance, causing continued right-to-left shunting through fetal circulatory pathways.

79. Q: What is the key physiologic abnormality in PPHN?

A: Persistently elevated pulmonary vascular resistance:

PVR↑

This causes right-to-left shunting through the ductus arteriosus and/or foramen ovale, producing hypoxemia.

80. Q: What clinical finding suggests PPHN?

A: Severe hypoxemia that may be disproportionate to the radiographic lung findings, sometimes accompanied by differential cyanosis.

81. Q: What is differential cyanosis?

A: Cyanosis affecting the lower extremities more than the right upper extremity, caused by right-to-left ductal shunting into the descending aorta.

82. Q: How can preductal and postductal oxygen saturation help identify PPHN?

A: Compare:

Preductal: right hand
Postductal: one foot

A significant difference suggests ductal-level right-to-left shunting.

83. Q: What is a major treatment principle for PPHN?

A: Reduce pulmonary vascular resistance and optimize oxygenation/ventilation. Treatment may include:

Adequate oxygenation
Respiratory support
Treatment of underlying disease
Inhaled nitric oxide in appropriate term/near-term infants

84. Q: How does inhaled nitric oxide improve PPHN?

A: Nitric oxide causes selective pulmonary vasodilation, reducing pulmonary vascular resistance and improving pulmonary blood flow.

85. Q: What neonatal respiratory disorder is characterized by respiratory distress, hypoxemia, and pulmonary hypertension following meconium aspiration?

A: Meconium aspiration syndrome complicated by PPHN.

86. Q: What is neonatal pneumonia/sepsis an important mimic of?

A: It can mimic RDS, TTN, and other neonatal respiratory disorders, because neonatal infection often presents with nonspecific respiratory findings.

87. Q: A newborn develops respiratory distress, temperature instability, poor feeding, and lethargy. What diagnosis must be considered?

A: Neonatal sepsis, particularly if respiratory findings are accompanied by systemic signs.

Neonatal infection frequently has nonspecific manifestations.

88. Q: What is the major physiologic role of CPAP in neonatal respiratory disease?

A: CPAP provides continuous positive airway pressure, helping prevent alveolar collapse and improving functional residual capacity.

89. Q: Why does CPAP help a premature infant with surfactant deficiency?

A: It helps maintain end-expiratory alveolar patency, partially compensating for reduced surfactant activity.

90. Q: What is bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD)?

A: A chronic lung disease of prematurity characterized by persistent respiratory support/oxygen requirement and abnormal lung development, particularly in very preterm infants.

91. Q: What major factors contribute to bronchopulmonary dysplasia?

A:

Extreme prematurity
Immature lungs
Mechanical ventilation
Oxygen exposure
Inflammation
Infection

92. Q: Why can mechanical ventilation contribute to BPD?

A: Mechanical ventilation can produce barotrauma/volutrauma, damaging immature lung tissue and promoting inflammation and abnormal lung development.

93. Q: Why can excessive oxygen exposure contribute to BPD?

A: Hyperoxia generates reactive oxygen species, causing oxidative injury to developing lung tissue.

94. Q: What is apnea of prematurity?

A: A developmental disorder caused by immature respiratory control, characterized by recurrent episodes of apnea in premature infants.

95. Q: What types of apnea can occur in premature infants?

A:

Central apnea: absent respiratory effort
Obstructive apnea: continued respiratory effort despite upper-airway obstruction
Mixed apnea: combination of both

96. Q: What medication is commonly used to treat apnea of prematurity?

A: Caffeine, a methylxanthine that stimulates the respiratory center and reduces apnea episodes.

97. Q: Why are premature infants particularly prone to apnea?

A: Their respiratory control centers are immature, resulting in an increased tendency toward central and mixed apnea.

98. Q: A 28-week infant has recurrent apnea and bradycardia without evidence of infection or airway obstruction. What is the likely diagnosis?

A: Apnea of prematurity.

99. Q: What important diagnoses should be excluded before attributing apnea in a neonate to prematurity?

A: Consider:

Sepsis
Hypoglycemia
Hypoxemia
Anemia
Seizures
CNS disease
Airway obstruction
Temperature instability

100. Q: What is the major difference between RDS and TTN on a USMLE-style question?

A: RDS: premature infant + respiratory distress soon after birth + surfactant deficiency + low lung volumes/ground-glass appearance.

TTN: term/late-preterm infant, often after cesarean delivery + early tachypnea + retained lung fluid + fissure fluid/hyperinflation + rapid spontaneous improvement.

101. Q: Why do newborns commonly develop physiologic jaundice?

A: Newborns have:

Increased RBC mass
Shorter RBC lifespan
Increased bilirubin production
Immature hepatic bilirubin conjugation

This produces a transient rise in unconjugated bilirubin.

102. Q: What type of bilirubin predominates in physiologic neonatal jaundice?

A: Unconjugated (indirect) bilirubin.

103. Q: When does physiologic jaundice typically become clinically apparent in a term newborn?

A: Usually after the first 24 hours of life, with bilirubin generally peaking around days 3–5.

Jaundice during the first 24 hours is pathologic until proven otherwise.

104. Q: Why is jaundice appearing during the first 24 hours concerning?

A: Early jaundice suggests pathologic bilirubin production, particularly hemolysis, and requires prompt bilirubin measurement and evaluation.

105. Q: What is the major difference between physiologic and pathologic neonatal jaundice?

A: Physiologic jaundice:

Begins after 24 hours
Is usually unconjugated
Follows a predictable course

Pathologic jaundice may:

Begin within the first 24 hours
Rise rapidly
Be associated with hemolysis or illness
Involve conjugated hyperbilirubinemia

106. Q: What enzyme is responsible for conjugating bilirubin in the liver?

A: UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT1A1).

It converts unconjugated bilirubin into water-soluble conjugated bilirubin.

107. Q: Why is bilirubin conjugation less efficient in newborns?

A: Hepatic UGT1A1 activity is immature, particularly in premature infants.

108. Q: What is the biochemical difference between unconjugated and conjugated bilirubin?

A: Unconjugated bilirubin is lipid-soluble and transported bound to albumin.

Conjugated bilirubin is water-soluble and can be excreted in bile.

109. Q: Why can unconjugated bilirubin cross the blood-brain barrier?

A: Unconjugated bilirubin is lipid-soluble. When sufficiently elevated, particularly when unbound to albumin, it can enter the CNS and cause neurotoxicity.

110. Q: What is kernicterus?

A: Chronic bilirubin encephalopathy caused by deposition of unconjugated bilirubin in vulnerable areas of the brain.

Severe hyperbilirubinemia can cause permanent neurologic injury.

111. Q: What are important neurologic manifestations of acute bilirubin encephalopathy?

A: Early findings can include:

Poor feeding
Lethargy
Hypotonia

Progressive disease can cause:

Irritability
Hypertonia
Retrocollis
Opisthotonus
High-pitched cry
Seizures

112. Q: What long-term neurologic problems can result from kernicterus?

A: Possible sequelae include:

Choreoathetoid cerebral palsy
Sensorineural hearing loss
Abnormal upward gaze
Dental enamel abnormalities
Developmental neurologic impairment

113. Q: What is the most important treatment for significant unconjugated neonatal hyperbilirubinemia?

A: Phototherapy when the bilirubin level reaches the appropriate treatment threshold based on gestational age, postnatal age, and neurotoxicity risk factors.

114. Q: How does phototherapy lower unconjugated bilirubin?

A: Blue-green light converts bilirubin into water-soluble photoisomers, particularly lumirubin, which can be excreted without normal hepatic conjugation.

115. Q: Why is blue light particularly effective for neonatal phototherapy?

A: Bilirubin absorbs light strongly in the blue-green wavelength range, allowing efficient photoisomerization.

116. Q: Does phototherapy require normal hepatic bilirubin conjugation?

A: No.

Phototherapy creates water-soluble bilirubin products that can be excreted without requiring normal UGT1A1-mediated conjugation.

117. Q: What determines the phototherapy threshold in a newborn?

A: The threshold depends primarily on:

Gestational age
Postnatal age in hours
Presence of hyperbilirubinemia neurotoxicity risk factors

The current AAP approach uses hour-specific treatment thresholds rather than one universal bilirubin value.

118. Q: What is an important neurotoxicity risk factor that lowers the threshold for treatment?

A: Isoimmune hemolytic disease is an important neurotoxicity risk factor.

Other risk factors include G6PD deficiency and significant clinical instability.

119. Q: Why does lower gestational age increase the risk from hyperbilirubinemia?

A: Premature infants have greater vulnerability to bilirubin neurotoxicity because of immature physiology and increased susceptibility of the developing brain.

120. Q: What is the relationship between bilirubin level and treatment?

A: Treatment is not based on a single universal bilirubin cutoff.

The bilirubin level must be interpreted according to age in hours, gestational age, and neurotoxicity risk factors.

121. Q: What should be done if a newborn's bilirubin approaches the exchange transfusion threshold?

A: This represents a medical emergency requiring escalation of care, intensive phototherapy, urgent evaluation, and preparation for possible exchange transfusion.

122. Q: What is exchange transfusion used for in neonatal hyperbilirubinemia?

A: It rapidly removes:

Unconjugated bilirubin
Circulating antibodies
Antibody-coated RBCs

It is used for severe hyperbilirubinemia when indicated by age- and risk-adjusted thresholds.

123. Q: What is the mechanism of hyperbilirubinemia in ABO incompatibility?

A: Maternal IgG antibodies against fetal RBC antigens cross the placenta and cause hemolysis.

A classic scenario is:

Mother: type O → infant: type A or B

124. Q: Why can ABO incompatibility occur during the first pregnancy?

A: Anti-A and anti-B antibodies in type O mothers can include IgG, which crosses the placenta.

Therefore, clinically significant ABO hemolysis can occur without prior pregnancy sensitization.

125. Q: What is the mechanism of Rh hemolytic disease?

A: An Rh-negative mother becomes sensitized to Rh(D)-positive fetal RBCs, producing IgG anti-D antibodies that cross the placenta during a subsequent Rh-positive pregnancy and cause fetal RBC hemolysis.

126. Q: What test detects antibodies attached to the newborn's RBCs?

A: The direct antiglobulin test (DAT; direct Coombs test).

127. Q: What does a positive direct antiglobulin test in a jaundiced newborn suggest?

A: Immune-mediated hemolysis, such as ABO or Rh incompatibility.

128. Q: What test can identify maternal antibodies circulating in maternal serum?

A: The indirect antiglobulin test (indirect Coombs test).

129. Q: A newborn develops jaundice at 12 hours of life and has a positive DAT. What is the most likely mechanism?

A: Immune-mediated hemolysis, most commonly ABO or Rh incompatibility.

Jaundice during the first 24 hours is particularly concerning for hemolysis.

130. Q: What CBC finding supports significant neonatal hemolysis?

A: Anemia with reticulocytosis supports increased RBC destruction.

131. Q: What peripheral smear finding may support hemolytic disease of the newborn?

A: Polychromasia and increased reticulocytes indicate increased erythropoietic activity.

The morphology can vary depending on the underlying cause.

132. Q: What inherited enzyme deficiency can cause severe neonatal unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia?

A: Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency.

It can cause hemolysis and severe hyperbilirubinemia and is an important cause of kernicterus.

133. Q: Why is G6PD deficiency particularly important in neonatal jaundice?

A: It can cause severe hemolysis and hyperbilirubinemia, sometimes without an obvious family history.

134. Q: What type of inheritance does G6PD deficiency have?

A: X-linked recessive.

135. Q: What type of bilirubin elevation occurs in hemolytic disease?

A: Primarily unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia because accelerated RBC breakdown increases bilirubin production.

136. Q: What is the significance of a rapidly rising bilirubin level?

A: A rapid rise suggests pathologic bilirubin production, particularly hemolysis, and requires prompt evaluation.

The AAP identifies a rapid rise in bilirubin as a risk factor for significant hyperbilirubinemia.

137. Q: What is breastfeeding jaundice?

A: Early neonatal jaundice associated with inadequate milk intake, resulting in dehydration, reduced stooling, and increased enterohepatic bilirubin circulation.

It is often related to suboptimal intake rather than breast milk itself.

138. Q: What is breast milk jaundice?

A: Persistent unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia occurring in an otherwise healthy breastfed infant, usually beginning after the first several days of life and potentially lasting for weeks.

139. Q: What is the key difference between breastfeeding/suboptimal-intake jaundice and breast milk jaundice?

A: Suboptimal-intake jaundice: inadequate milk intake → dehydration/poor weight gain → increased bilirubin.

Breast milk jaundice: persistent unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia despite otherwise adequate feeding and growth.

140. Q: Should breastfeeding routinely be stopped because a newborn develops uncomplicated breast milk jaundice?

A: No.

Breastfeeding should generally be continued while the infant is appropriately evaluated and monitored.

141. Q: Why can inadequate feeding increase unconjugated bilirubin?

A: Reduced enteral intake causes:

Decreased stooling
Increased intestinal bilirubin reabsorption
Increased enterohepatic circulation

This increases serum unconjugated bilirubin.

142. Q: A newborn with jaundice has poor feeding, excessive weight loss, and decreased urine output. What should be suspected?

A: Suboptimal intake/dehydration-associated jaundice should be considered, while also assessing for other causes of hyperbilirubinemia.

143. Q: Why should oral water or dextrose water not be routinely given to prevent neonatal hyperbilirubinemia?

A: It does not adequately prevent significant hyperbilirubinemia and can interfere with effective nutritional intake. Appropriate breastfeeding/formula feeding should be supported instead.

144. Q: What type of jaundice is always considered pathologic in a newborn?

A: Conjugated (direct) hyperbilirubinemia is pathologic and requires evaluation for cholestasis or hepatobiliary disease.

145. Q: What is neonatal cholestasis?

A: Impaired bile formation or bile flow resulting in elevated conjugated/direct bilirubin.

It is not physiologic and requires evaluation.

146. Q: What important surgical cause of neonatal cholestasis must be recognized early?

A: Biliary atresia.

Early diagnosis and surgical treatment are important because outcomes are better when treatment occurs early.

147. Q: What stool finding strongly suggests biliary obstruction in a newborn?

A: Acholic (pale/clay-colored) stools indicate absent or markedly reduced bile pigment reaching the intestine.

148. Q: A 3-week-old infant has persistent jaundice, dark urine, and pale stools. What diagnosis should be strongly suspected?

A: Biliary atresia causing conjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

149. Q: Why does conjugated hyperbilirubinemia cause dark urine?

A: Conjugated bilirubin is water-soluble, so it can be filtered by the kidneys and excreted in urine.

150. Q: A breastfed infant remains visibly jaundiced at 4 weeks. What laboratory evaluation is recommended?

A: Obtain total and direct/conjugated bilirubin levels to evaluate for pathologic cholestasis. The AAP specifically recommends this for breastfed infants who remain jaundiced at 3–4 weeks.

151. Q: What are the two broad categories of neonatal sepsis?

A:

Early-onset sepsis: usually acquired vertically from the mother around delivery
Late-onset sepsis: usually acquired from the environment after birth

152. Q: Which organism is the most important cause of early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Group B Streptococcus (GBS).

153. Q: What is another major cause of early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Escherichia coli, particularly in premature infants.

154. Q: What maternal factor strongly increases the risk of early-onset neonatal GBS infection?

A: Maternal GBS colonization.

155. Q: What is the standard method used to reduce early-onset GBS disease?

A: Intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis for pregnant patients with an indication for prophylaxis.

156. Q: What antibiotic is commonly used for intrapartum GBS prophylaxis in a patient without penicillin allergy?

A: Penicillin G is the preferred agent.

157. Q: What maternal intrapartum condition increases the risk of early-onset neonatal infection?

A: Prolonged rupture of membranes, particularly when prolonged for ≥18 hours.

158. Q: What other maternal condition is an important risk factor for early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Maternal intrapartum fever/chorioamnionitis, indicating possible intra-amniotic infection.

159. Q: What clinical findings can indicate neonatal sepsis?

A: Neonatal sepsis is often nonspecific. Findings include:

Temperature instability
Poor feeding
Lethargy
Apnea
Respiratory distress
Tachycardia or bradycardia
Hypotension
Abdominal distension
Glucose abnormalities

160. Q: Why can neonatal sepsis be difficult to recognize clinically?

A: Newborns often have nonspecific immune and physiologic responses, so infection may initially present as feeding difficulty, respiratory distress, temperature instability, or lethargy rather than focal infection. (msdmanuals.com)

161. Q: What empiric antibiotics are commonly used for suspected early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Ampicillin + gentamicin.

This combination provides coverage for organisms such as GBS, Listeria, and gram-negative bacilli.

162. Q: Why is ampicillin included in empiric neonatal sepsis treatment?

A: It provides coverage against important neonatal pathogens including:

GBS
Listeria monocytogenes
Some enterococci

163. Q: Why is gentamicin included?

A: Gentamicin provides important gram-negative coverage, particularly against organisms such as E. coli, and has synergistic activity with certain β-lactam antibiotics.

164. Q: What organism should be specifically considered in neonatal meningitis and early-onset sepsis because of maternal food exposure?

A: Listeria monocytogenes.

165. Q: Why is ampicillin important when Listeria is suspected?

A: Listeria is intrinsically resistant to cephalosporins, so ampicillin is required for appropriate empiric coverage.

166. Q: A neonate develops fever, poor feeding, lethargy, and respiratory distress at 12 hours of life. What is the most appropriate approach?

A: Treat as possible early-onset neonatal sepsis, obtain appropriate cultures/laboratory evaluation, and begin empiric antibiotics when indicated.

167. Q: What is the major difference between early- and late-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Early-onset: usually vertically acquired from the mother.

Late-onset: commonly acquired from the environment, including healthcare-associated exposure.

168. Q: Which organism is particularly associated with late-onset sepsis in hospitalized premature infants?

A: Coagulase-negative Staphylococcus, especially in infants with central venous catheters.

169. Q: What neonatal infection is strongly associated with prolonged hospitalization and central venous catheter use?

A: Late-onset bloodstream infection, particularly from coagulase-negative staphylococci and other healthcare-associated organisms.

170. Q: A premature infant with a central venous catheter develops temperature instability and lethargy at 3 weeks of life. What should be suspected?

A: Late-onset sepsis, including catheter-associated bloodstream infection.

Neonatal Hypoglycemia

171. Q: Why are newborns vulnerable to hypoglycemia after birth?

A: Placental glucose delivery abruptly stops at birth while the newborn must transition to glycogenolysis, gluconeogenesis, and feeding to maintain glucose levels.

172. Q: Which newborns are at increased risk for hypoglycemia?

A: Important risk groups include:

Infants of diabetic mothers
SGA/IUGR infants
LGA infants
Premature infants
Infants with perinatal stress/asphyxia
Infants with disorders of glucose metabolism

173. Q: Why are infants of diabetic mothers at risk for neonatal hypoglycemia?

A: Maternal hyperglycemia causes fetal hyperglycemia, stimulating fetal pancreatic β-cell hyperplasia and hyperinsulinemia.

After birth, maternal glucose supply stops abruptly while insulin remains elevated.

174. Q: What is the classic sequence in an infant of a diabetic mother?

A: Maternal hyperglycemia →

Fetal hyperglycemia→Fetal hyperinsulinemia→Macrosomia + neonatal hypoglycemia

175. Q: Why can an infant of a diabetic mother be macrosomic?

A: Fetal insulin acts as an important anabolic/growth-promoting hormone, increasing glucose uptake, fat deposition, and protein synthesis.

176. Q: What symptoms can neonatal hypoglycemia cause?

A: Possible manifestations include:

Jitteriness
Irritability
Poor feeding
Lethargy
Hypotonia
Apnea
Cyanosis
Seizures

177. Q: Why can neonatal hypoglycemia cause seizures?

A: The neonatal brain has a high metabolic demand and is particularly dependent on glucose. Severe or prolonged hypoglycemia can cause neuroglycopenic brain injury and seizures.

178. Q: A jittery newborn is suspected of having hypoglycemia. What is the appropriate first investigation?

A: Measure blood glucose promptly.

Jitteriness is a common manifestation of neonatal hypoglycemia.

179. Q: What is the initial treatment for an asymptomatic at-risk newborn with low glucose who can feed?

A: Prompt enteral feeding, with glucose monitoring according to the neonatal protocol.

180. Q: What is the treatment for symptomatic or severe neonatal hypoglycemia?

A: Prompt IV dextrose is indicated.

A common initial regimen is:

D10W2 mL/kg IV bolus

followed by a continuous glucose infusion adjusted according to serial glucose measurements and clinical response.

181. Q: Why is D10W generally used rather than highly concentrated dextrose through a peripheral IV?

A: Highly concentrated dextrose solutions can cause osmotic injury and extravasation-related tissue damage. D10W is commonly used for initial neonatal IV glucose therapy.

182. Q: What is an important cause of persistent neonatal hypoglycemia?

A: Congenital hyperinsulinism should be considered, particularly when hypoglycemia is severe, persistent, or requires unusually high glucose infusion rates.

183. Q: What laboratory pattern is characteristic of hyperinsulinism during hypoglycemia?

A: Inappropriately detectable insulin/C-peptide, suppressed ketones and free fatty acids, and an increased glucose requirement.

184. Q: Why are ketones often low during hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia?

A: Insulin suppresses lipolysis and ketogenesis, preventing adequate production of alternative fuels during hypoglycemia.

Neonatal Temperature Regulation

185. Q: What is neonatal hypothermia?

A: A core temperature below the normal neonatal range, generally <36.5°C.

186. Q: What are the major mechanisms by which a newborn loses heat?

A: Four mechanisms:

Evaporation
Conduction
Convection
Radiation

187. Q: A wet newborn loses heat immediately after delivery. What is the major mechanism?

A: Evaporation.

Drying the newborn promptly reduces evaporative heat loss.

188. Q: A newborn is placed on a cold examination table. What type of heat loss occurs?

A: Conduction.

Heat transfers directly from the newborn to the colder surface.

189. Q: A newborn loses heat to nearby cold air. What mechanism is responsible?

A: Convection.

190. Q: A newborn loses heat while positioned near a cold window even without touching it. What mechanism is responsible?

A: Radiation.

Heat transfers from the newborn to nearby colder surfaces without direct contact.

191. Q: Why can hypothermia worsen neonatal hypoglycemia?

A: Cold stress increases metabolic demand and glucose consumption, potentially exhausting limited neonatal energy stores.

192. Q: Why can hypothermia increase oxygen consumption in a newborn?

A: Nonshivering thermogenesis requires increased metabolic activity and oxygen consumption.

193. Q: What is the clinical significance of neonatal hypothermia?

A: It can contribute to:

Hypoglycemia
Increased oxygen consumption
Metabolic acidosis
Respiratory distress
Increased morbidity, particularly in premature infants
Congenital Infections

194. Q: What does the TORCH group represent?

A: A mnemonic for important congenital/perinatal infections:

T — Toxoplasmosis
O — Other infections
R — Rubella
C — Cytomegalovirus
H — Herpes simplex virus

195. Q: What classic triad is associated with congenital toxoplasmosis?

A:

Chorioretinitis + hydrocephalus + intracranial calcifications

196. Q: What type of intracranial calcifications are classically associated with congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Diffuse intracranial calcifications, often involving the cortex and other regions.

197. Q: What intracranial calcification pattern is classically associated with congenital CMV?

A: Periventricular calcifications.

198. Q: What is the classic clinical triad of congenital CMV infection?

A: Important findings include:

Periventricular calcifications
Sensorineural hearing loss
Petechiae/"blueberry muffin" lesions
Microcephaly
Seizures
Growth restriction

199. Q: What is the most common congenital viral infection?

A: Cytomegalovirus (CMV).

200. Q: A newborn has microcephaly, petechiae, hepatosplenomegaly, thrombocytopenia, and periventricular intracranial calcifications. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Congenital CMV infection.

201. Q: What is the classic triad of congenital rubella syndrome?

A:

Sensorineural hearing loss
Cataracts
Congenital heart disease, classically patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)

Congenital rubella is particularly associated with infection during early pregnancy.

202. Q: What cardiac lesion is classically associated with congenital rubella?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA).

203. Q: What eye abnormality is classically associated with congenital rubella?

A: Cataracts.

204. Q: What auditory abnormality is characteristic of congenital rubella?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss.

205. Q: What skin finding can occur in congenital rubella syndrome?

A: A "blueberry muffin" rash caused by dermal extramedullary hematopoiesis.

206. Q: A newborn has cataracts, PDA, and sensorineural deafness. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Congenital rubella syndrome.

207. Q: What congenital infection is associated with a "blueberry muffin" rash, hepatosplenomegaly, and cataracts?

A: Congenital rubella is a classic cause.

Other congenital infections can also cause extramedullary hematopoiesis, so the associated cataracts + PDA + deafness strongly favor rubella.

208. Q: What is the major neurologic manifestation of congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Hydrocephalus, often accompanied by intracranial calcifications and chorioretinitis.

209. Q: What ocular lesion is classic for congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Chorioretinitis.

210. Q: What maternal exposure is classically associated with congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Exposure to cat feces/soil contaminated with Toxoplasma oocysts or consumption of undercooked meat containing tissue cysts.

211. Q: What is the classic congenital toxoplasmosis pattern on neuroimaging?

A: Diffuse intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus, and brain abnormalities.

212. Q: A newborn has hydrocephalus, chorioretinitis, and intracranial calcifications. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Congenital toxoplasmosis.

213. Q: What congenital infection is particularly associated with sensorineural hearing loss that may develop later in childhood?

A: Congenital CMV.

Most infants with congenital CMV are asymptomatic at birth, but hearing loss can develop later.

214. Q: What is the preferred specimen for confirming congenital CMV infection in a newborn?

A: Urine PCR/NAAT is preferred; saliva can also be used, with confirmation using urine if saliva is positive.

Testing should occur within the first 2–3 weeks of life to establish congenital rather than postnatal infection.

215. Q: Why is timing important when diagnosing congenital CMV?

A: Detection after the first few weeks cannot reliably distinguish congenital infection from infection acquired after birth.

216. Q: What is a major long-term complication of symptomatic congenital CMV?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss is the most common long-term complication. Developmental and motor problems, seizures, and vision abnormalities can also occur.

217. Q: What antiviral may be used for symptomatic congenital CMV?

A: Valganciclovir may be used in selected infants with symptomatic congenital CMV under specialist management.

218. Q: What congenital infection is associated with a characteristic "snuffles" presentation?

A: Congenital syphilis.

219. Q: What is a classic early manifestation of congenital syphilis?

A: Persistent rhinitis ("snuffles"), often accompanied by rash, hepatosplenomegaly, and systemic illness.

220. Q: What type of rash is classically associated with congenital syphilis?

A: A diffuse maculopapular/desquamating rash, often involving the palms and soles.

221. Q: What characteristic bone abnormalities can occur in congenital syphilis?

A: Osteochondritis and periostitis, producing painful long bones and characteristic radiographic abnormalities.

222. Q: What are important late manifestations of congenital syphilis?

A:

Hutchinson teeth
Interstitial keratitis
Sensorineural hearing loss
Saddle nose
Saber shins

223. Q: What dental abnormality is classically associated with congenital syphilis?

A: Hutchinson teeth — small, notched permanent incisors.

224. Q: What is the classic triad of late congenital syphilis?

A: Hutchinson teeth + interstitial keratitis + sensorineural deafness.

225. Q: What congenital infection is associated with limb abnormalities and a characteristic dermatomal scar-like rash?

A: Congenital varicella syndrome.

226. Q: What are important findings in congenital varicella syndrome?

A:

Limb hypoplasia
Cicatricial skin scars
Neurologic abnormalities
Ocular abnormalities
Low birth weight

227. Q: What neonatal infection acquired around delivery can cause skin vesicles, encephalitis, and disseminated disease?

A: Neonatal herpes simplex virus (HSV) infection.

228. Q: What are the three major clinical patterns of neonatal HSV infection?

A:

Skin, eye, and mouth disease
Central nervous system disease
Disseminated disease

229. Q: When is neonatal HSV transmission particularly likely?

A: Risk is highest when the mother has newly acquired genital HSV infection near the time of delivery, because maternal antibodies may be insufficient and viral shedding can be substantial.

230. Q: A neonate develops vesicular skin lesions and conjunctivitis at 10 days of life. What diagnosis should be suspected?

A: Neonatal HSV infection, particularly skin/eye/mouth disease.

Hypoxic-Ischemic Encephalopathy

231. Q: What is hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy (HIE)?

A: Brain dysfunction caused by inadequate oxygen and/or blood flow around the time of birth, resulting in hypoxic-ischemic brain injury.

232. Q: What newborns are at increased risk for HIE?

A: Infants experiencing significant perinatal asphyxia, such as severe placental insufficiency, prolonged hypoxemia, placental abruption, uterine rupture, or severe complications during delivery.

233. Q: What clinical findings suggest moderate-to-severe HIE?

A: Findings may include:

Altered level of consciousness
Abnormal tone
Abnormal reflexes
Poor feeding
Seizures
Abnormal respiratory effort
Multiorgan dysfunction

234. Q: What is an important laboratory finding supporting significant perinatal hypoxia?

A: Metabolic acidosis, particularly significant umbilical arterial acidemia, supports impaired oxygen delivery.

235. Q: What is therapeutic hypothermia?

A: Controlled reduction of core temperature to approximately 33–34°C for a defined period to reduce secondary neuronal injury after qualifying neonatal hypoxic-ischemic injury.

236. Q: Which newborns may benefit from therapeutic hypothermia?

A: Selected term or near-term infants with moderate-to-severe HIE who meet established clinical and biochemical criteria.

237. Q: Why does therapeutic hypothermia help after hypoxic-ischemic injury?

A: Cooling decreases cerebral metabolic demand and secondary neuronal injury, including excitotoxicity, oxidative stress, and apoptosis.

238. Q: When should therapeutic hypothermia ideally be initiated?

A: As soon as possible, generally within 6 hours after birth in eligible infants.

239. Q: A term infant develops seizures, hypotonia, poor consciousness, and evidence of severe perinatal asphyxia at 2 hours of life. What treatment should be urgently considered?

A: Therapeutic hypothermia, provided the infant meets established eligibility criteria.

240. Q: Why should newborns who receive advanced resuscitation be monitored for hypoglycemia?

A: Advanced resuscitation and hypoxic-ischemic injury increase the risk of abnormal glucose levels, which can worsen neurologic injury. Current neonatal-resuscitation guidance specifically emphasizes glucose monitoring after advanced resuscitation.

Neonatal Seizures

241. Q: What are common causes of neonatal seizures?

A: Important causes include:

Hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy
Hypoglycemia
Hypocalcemia
Intracranial hemorrhage
CNS infection
Stroke
Metabolic disorders
Drug withdrawal

242. Q: Why are neonatal seizures sometimes difficult to recognize?

A: Neonates often have subtle or focal seizure manifestations, such as eye deviation, lip movements, bicycling movements, apnea, or abnormal repetitive movements rather than generalized tonic-clonic seizures.

243. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for suspected neonatal seizures?

A: Continuous or prolonged EEG monitoring is important because many neonatal seizures are electrographic without obvious clinical manifestations.

244. Q: What is a common first-line antiseizure medication for clinically significant neonatal seizures?

A: Phenobarbital remains a commonly used first-line medication for many neonatal seizures.

245. Q: A neonate develops seizures after severe birth asphyxia. What underlying condition should be suspected?

A: Hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy.

Intracranial Hemorrhage

246. Q: Which intracranial hemorrhage is particularly associated with very premature infants?

A: Intraventricular hemorrhage (IVH) arising from the fragile germinal matrix.

247. Q: Why are premature infants susceptible to germinal-matrix hemorrhage?

A: The germinal matrix contains a highly vascular, fragile capillary network that is particularly vulnerable to fluctuations in cerebral blood flow and pressure.

248. Q: Where does germinal-matrix hemorrhage typically occur?

A: It originates in the subependymal germinal matrix and can extend into the lateral ventricles.

249. Q: What is a major complication of severe intraventricular hemorrhage?

A: Posthemorrhagic hydrocephalus due to impaired CSF circulation/absorption.

250. Q: What imaging modality is commonly used to screen very premature infants for intraventricular hemorrhage?

A: Cranial ultrasonography through the anterior fontanelle is commonly used because it is portable, noninvasive, and can be performed at the bedside.

251. Q: What is necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC)?

A: A potentially life-threatening inflammatory and ischemic disease of the intestinal tract, primarily affecting premature or very-low-birth-weight infants.

252. Q: Which newborns are at greatest risk for NEC?

A: Very premature and very-low-birth-weight infants, especially those receiving enteral feeds.

253. Q: What are major risk factors for NEC?

A:

Prematurity
Very low birth weight
Enteral feeding
Intestinal ischemia/hypoxia
Abnormal intestinal microbiota
Formula feeding

254. Q: Why does prematurity predispose to NEC?

A: Premature intestines have:

Immature mucosal defenses
Abnormal motility
Immature immune responses
Increased susceptibility to ischemia and bacterial invasion

255. Q: What is the classic early clinical presentation of NEC?

A: Feeding intolerance, abdominal distension, and bloody stools in a premature infant.

256. Q: What abdominal finding is particularly concerning for NEC?

A: Abdominal distension with tenderness and feeding intolerance.

257. Q: What stool finding strongly suggests intestinal injury from NEC?

A: Grossly bloody stools.

258. Q: What is the classic abdominal x-ray finding in NEC?

A: Pneumatosis intestinalis — gas within the bowel wall.

259. Q: What does pneumatosis intestinalis represent in NEC?

A: Gas produced by bacteria enters the intestinal wall, reflecting mucosal injury and bacterial translocation.

260. Q: What is another important radiographic finding in severe NEC?

A: Portal venous gas.

261. Q: What radiographic finding suggests intestinal perforation in NEC?

A: Pneumoperitoneum/free intraperitoneal air.

262. Q: A 28-week premature infant develops abdominal distension and bloody stools. X-ray shows pneumatosis intestinalis. What is the diagnosis?

A: Necrotizing enterocolitis.

263. Q: What is the initial management of suspected NEC?

A:

Stop enteral feeds
Gastric decompression with an NG/OG tube
IV fluids
Broad-spectrum antibiotics
Close clinical and radiographic monitoring

264. Q: Why are enteral feeds stopped in NEC?

A: Feeding can worsen intestinal injury and bacterial proliferation while the bowel is inflamed and ischemic.

265. Q: Why are broad-spectrum antibiotics used in NEC?

A: They treat/prevent progression of intestinal bacterial translocation and systemic infection.

266. Q: When is surgery indicated in NEC?

A: Major indications include:

Intestinal perforation
Pneumoperitoneum
Peritonitis
Clinical deterioration despite maximal medical therapy
Necrotic bowel requiring removal

267. Q: What is a major long-term complication of NEC?

A: Intestinal stricture, particularly colonic stricture.

268. Q: What other major long-term complication can follow severe NEC?

A: Short bowel syndrome can occur after extensive intestinal resection.

Patent Ductus Arteriosus

269. Q: Why is patent ductus arteriosus (PDA) common in premature infants?

A: Prematurity is associated with immaturity of ductal smooth muscle and increased sensitivity to prostaglandins, delaying ductal closure.

270. Q: What direction does blood flow through a PDA after pulmonary vascular resistance falls?

A: From:

Aorta→Pulmonary artery

This produces a left-to-right shunt.

271. Q: What are the hemodynamic consequences of a significant PDA?

A: Increased pulmonary blood flow can cause:

Pulmonary edema
Increased respiratory support requirements
Left atrial/ventricular volume overload
Systemic hypoperfusion

272. Q: What is the classic murmur of a PDA?

A: A continuous "machine-like" murmur, often heard best at the left upper sternal border.

273. Q: What pulse finding may occur with a significant PDA?

A: Bounding peripheral pulses and widened pulse pressure can occur due to runoff of blood from the aorta into the pulmonary circulation.

274. Q: Why can a significant PDA worsen respiratory status in a premature infant?

A: Excessive left-to-right shunting increases pulmonary blood flow, potentially causing pulmonary edema and increased respiratory support requirements.

275. Q: Which medication can promote closure of a hemodynamically significant PDA?

A: Indomethacin or ibuprofen, both of which inhibit prostaglandin synthesis.

276. Q: What newer medication is also used in some settings to facilitate PDA closure?

A: Acetaminophen (paracetamol) may be used in selected premature infants when appropriate.

277. Q: Why do NSAIDs promote PDA closure?

A: They inhibit cyclooxygenase, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis:

COX inhibition→PGE
2
	​

↓→ductal constriction

278. Q: What medication keeps the ductus arteriosus open?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil).

279. Q: In which clinical situation is prostaglandin E₁ particularly important?

A: In a neonate with suspected ductal-dependent congenital heart disease, where maintaining ductal patency is necessary for pulmonary or systemic blood flow.

280. Q: What important adverse effect can occur with prostaglandin E₁?

A: Apnea is a major adverse effect, so respiratory support and close monitoring may be required.

Neonatal Feeding & Nutrition

281. Q: Why do premature infants have difficulty establishing oral feeding?

A: Coordination of suck, swallow, and breathing is immature, particularly in very premature infants.

282. Q: At approximately what gestational age does coordinated oral feeding typically become more reliable?

A: Suck-swallow-breathe coordination generally matures around 34–36 weeks gestation, although individual infants vary.

283. Q: What is the preferred enteral nutrition for most newborns when available?

A: Mother's own breast milk.

284. Q: Why is human milk particularly beneficial for premature infants?

A: It is associated with lower risk of:

NEC
Late-onset infection
Feeding intolerance

It also provides immunologic and developmental benefits.

285. Q: What is donor human milk used for?

A: It may be used when mother's own milk is unavailable or insufficient, particularly for very-low-birth-weight or very preterm infants.

286. Q: Why may human milk require fortification in very premature infants?

A: Human milk alone may not provide sufficient protein, calcium, phosphorus, and calories to meet the high nutritional requirements of very premature infants.

287. Q: What is parenteral nutrition?

A: Nutritional support delivered intravenously, providing combinations of:

Glucose
Amino acids
Lipids
Electrolytes
Vitamins
Trace elements

288. Q: Why may premature infants require parenteral nutrition?

A: They may be unable to tolerate sufficient enteral nutrition because of extreme prematurity, intestinal disease, or critical illness.

Anemia of Prematurity

289. Q: What is anemia of prematurity?

A: A physiologic anemia that is more pronounced and occurs earlier in premature infants because of reduced erythropoietin production, shorter RBC lifespan, rapid growth, and frequent blood sampling.

290. Q: Why does hemoglobin fall after birth in all infants?

A: After birth, oxygenation improves, reducing erythropoietin production and therefore decreasing RBC production.

This is the physiologic anemia of infancy.

291. Q: Why is anemia of prematurity more severe than physiologic anemia in term infants?

A: Premature infants have:

Lower erythropoietin response
Shorter RBC lifespan
Rapid growth
Lower iron stores
Frequent phlebotomy

292. Q: What is the major treatment approach for anemia of prematurity?

A: Minimize blood loss, provide adequate iron/nutrition, and use RBC transfusion when clinically indicated.

293. Q: What hormone stimulates erythropoiesis?

A: Erythropoietin (EPO), produced primarily by the kidneys.

294. Q: Why might exogenous erythropoietin be considered in some premature infants?

A: It can stimulate RBC production and potentially reduce transfusion requirements, although routine use is not universally indicated.

Retinopathy of Prematurity

295. Q: What is retinopathy of prematurity (ROP)?

A: A disorder of abnormal retinal vascular development in premature infants, potentially leading to retinal detachment and blindness.

296. Q: Why are premature infants susceptible to ROP?

A: Retinal vascularization is incomplete at premature birth. Exposure to abnormal oxygen levels disrupts normal vascular development and can trigger pathologic retinal neovascularization.

297. Q: What are the two major risk factors for ROP?

A: Low gestational age and low birth weight.

Prolonged or poorly controlled oxygen exposure is an additional important risk factor.

298. Q: Why can excessive oxygen exposure worsen ROP?

A: Hyperoxia suppresses normal retinal vascular growth. Subsequent relative retinal hypoxia stimulates VEGF-mediated pathologic neovascularization.

299. Q: What is a major late complication of severe untreated ROP?

A: Retinal detachment, potentially resulting in permanent blindness.

300. Q: Which premature infants require screening for retinopathy of prematurity?

A: Screening is based primarily on gestational age and birth weight, with additional consideration of clinical risk factors and NICU course. Very premature and very-low-birth-weight infants are the highest-risk group.

301. Q: What is caput succedaneum?

A: Diffuse, soft scalp edema caused by pressure during labor. It crosses suture lines and is usually present at birth.

302. Q: What is the key distinction between caput succedaneum and cephalohematoma?

A:
Caput: edema → crosses suture lines → present at birth → resolves rapidly.

Cephalohematoma: subperiosteal blood → does not cross suture lines → often becomes apparent hours after birth.

303. Q: What is a cephalohematoma?

A: A collection of blood between the skull and periosteum, usually caused by birth trauma.

304. Q: Why does a cephalohematoma not cross suture lines?

A: The periosteum is attached at the cranial sutures, limiting the hematoma to a single bone.

305. Q: What complication can occur with a large cephalohematoma?

A: Unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia can develop because the accumulated blood is gradually broken down.

306. Q: Should a neonatal cephalohematoma generally be aspirated?

A: No. It usually resolves spontaneously. Aspiration increases the risk of infection and is generally unnecessary.

307. Q: What is subgaleal hemorrhage?

A: Bleeding into the potential space between the galea aponeurotica and periosteum.

It can cause massive blood loss and hypovolemic shock.

308. Q: Why is subgaleal hemorrhage a neonatal emergency?

A: The subgaleal space can accommodate a large volume of blood, potentially causing severe anemia, hypovolemic shock, and death.

309. Q: What physical finding suggests subgaleal hemorrhage?

A: A diffuse, fluctuant scalp swelling that crosses suture lines, often with increasing head circumference and systemic signs of blood loss.

310. Q: A newborn develops progressive scalp swelling, pallor, tachycardia, and hypotension several hours after vacuum-assisted delivery. What is the diagnosis?

A: Subgaleal hemorrhage.

311. Q: What neonatal fracture is most commonly associated with difficult delivery?

A: Clavicular fracture.

312. Q: What clinical finding suggests a neonatal clavicular fracture?

A: Decreased movement of the affected arm, tenderness, swelling, or crepitus over the clavicle.

313. Q: What nerve injury is classically associated with shoulder dystocia?

A: Brachial plexus injury, particularly Erb palsy involving C5–C6.

314. Q: What is the classic posture of an infant with Erb palsy?

A: The "waiter's tip" position:

Arm adducted
Shoulder internally rotated
Elbow extended
Forearm pronated

315. Q: Which movements are impaired in Erb palsy?

A: Primarily:

Shoulder abduction
Shoulder external rotation
Elbow flexion

316. Q: What nerve roots are typically involved in Erb palsy?

A: C5–C6.

317. Q: What is Klumpke palsy?

A: A lower brachial plexus injury involving primarily C8–T1, causing weakness of the intrinsic muscles of the hand.

318. Q: What characteristic hand posture can occur with Klumpke palsy?

A: An intrinsic-minus/claw hand due to involvement of the lower brachial plexus.

319. Q: What other neurologic finding can occur with an extensive lower brachial plexus injury?

A: Horner syndrome if the sympathetic fibers at T1 are involved.

320. Q: What is neonatal facial nerve palsy?

A: Weakness of facial muscles due to injury/compression of cranial nerve VII, often associated with difficult or instrument-assisted delivery.

321. Q: How does peripheral facial nerve palsy differ from central facial weakness?

A: Peripheral CN VII injury causes weakness of both the upper and lower face on the affected side.

322. Q: What is a common cause of neonatal facial nerve palsy?

A: Compression of the facial nerve during labor, particularly with forceps-assisted delivery.

Congenital Heart Disease in the Newborn

323. Q: What congenital heart lesions are considered ductal-dependent?

A: Lesions in which adequate circulation depends on a patent ductus arteriosus, including:

Critical coarctation of the aorta
Interrupted aortic arch
Critical aortic stenosis
Pulmonary atresia
Critical pulmonary stenosis
Some forms of transposition physiology

324. Q: A newborn becomes profoundly cyanotic shortly after birth and does not improve substantially with oxygen. What congenital cardiac diagnosis should be considered?

A: Cyanotic congenital heart disease, particularly transposition of the great arteries (TGA) or another lesion causing inadequate mixing.

325. Q: What is the fundamental anatomic abnormality in transposition of the great arteries?

A: The ventriculoarterial connections are reversed:

RV→Aorta
LV→Pulmonary artery

This creates parallel rather than series circulation.

326. Q: Why is mixing essential for survival in TGA?

A: Without mixing, oxygenated pulmonary venous blood repeatedly returns to the lungs while systemic venous blood repeatedly returns to the body.

Adequate mixing through an ASD, VSD, or PDA is therefore essential.

327. Q: What medication is urgently used to maintain ductal patency in a neonate with suspected ductal-dependent congenital heart disease?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil).

328. Q: A newborn with suspected critical congenital heart disease develops worsening cyanosis after ductal closure. What is the immediate pharmacologic intervention?

A: Start prostaglandin E₁ while urgent cardiac evaluation is performed.

329. Q: Why can prostaglandin E₁ cause apnea in neonates?

A: It can suppress respiratory drive and cause dose-related apnea, requiring close respiratory monitoring.

330. Q: What is the classic presentation of critical coarctation of the aorta after ductal closure?

A: Poor feeding, tachypnea, lethargy, metabolic acidosis, weak/absent femoral pulses, and shock.

331. Q: Why can critical coarctation initially be asymptomatic?

A: Before ductal closure, the ductus arteriosus can supply blood to the descending aorta, temporarily bypassing the obstructed segment.

332. Q: What bedside finding strongly suggests coarctation of the aorta?

A: Weak femoral pulses compared with upper-extremity pulses.

333. Q: What is critical pulmonary stenosis or pulmonary atresia dependent on?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus may be required to provide pulmonary blood flow.

334. Q: What happens to a ductal-dependent pulmonary circulation when the ductus arteriosus closes?

A: Pulmonary blood flow falls dramatically, producing severe cyanosis and hypoxemia.

Critical Congenital Heart Disease Screening

335. Q: What is the purpose of newborn pulse-oximetry screening for critical congenital heart disease?

A: To identify otherwise asymptomatic newborns with hypoxemia from critical congenital heart disease before discharge.

The AAP recommends screening well-appearing newborns around 24 hours of life or shortly before discharge.

336. Q: Which two sites are used for CCHD pulse-oximetry screening?

A:

Right hand — preductal
Either foot — postductal

337. Q: Under the updated AAP algorithm, what oxygen saturation is considered a passing result?

A: ≥95% in both the right hand and foot, with an acceptable difference between the two measurements.

338. Q: Why is the right hand used for the preductal measurement?

A: It represents oxygen saturation before potential ductal mixing, whereas the lower extremity represents postductal oxygenation.

339. Q: What should be done when a newborn does not pass the CCHD screen?

A: The infant requires further evaluation, including assessment for congenital heart disease and other causes of hypoxemia; screening should not simply be assumed to represent a cardiac diagnosis.

340. Q: Can a newborn with a normal CCHD pulse-oximetry screen still have congenital heart disease?

A: Yes.

Pulse oximetry does not detect every congenital heart defect, particularly lesions that do not cause significant hypoxemia before discharge.

Newborn Screening & Metabolic Disorders

341. Q: What is the purpose of newborn screening?

A: To identify infants with potentially serious congenital or metabolic disorders before irreversible disease develops, allowing early treatment.

342. Q: What is the classic newborn-screening disorder caused by phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Phenylketonuria (PKU).

343. Q: What accumulates in untreated PKU?

A: Phenylalanine accumulates because it cannot be adequately converted to tyrosine.

Phenylalanine
phenylalanine hydroxylase
	​

Tyrosine

344. Q: What is the major treatment for classic PKU?

A: Lifelong restriction of dietary phenylalanine with appropriate nutritional supplementation; tyrosine becomes an essential amino acid.

345. Q: What neonatal metabolic disease results from galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase deficiency?

A: Classic galactosemia.

346. Q: What findings suggest classic galactosemia in a newborn?

A: After exposure to lactose/galactose:

Poor feeding
Vomiting
Jaundice
Hepatomegaly
Hypoglycemia
E. coli sepsis
Cataracts

347. Q: Why can galactosemia cause cataracts?

A: Excess galactose is converted to galactitol, which accumulates in the lens and causes osmotic injury.

348. Q: What is the treatment for classic galactosemia?

A: Immediate elimination of lactose and galactose from the diet, including avoidance of standard cow's-milk-based formulas.

349. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes classic congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH)?

A: 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

It accounts for the vast majority of classic CAH cases.

350. Q: What is the classic neonatal presentation of salt-wasting 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Affected infants can develop:

Hyponatremia
Hyperkalemia
Hypotension/dehydration
Hypoglycemia
Elevated 17-hydroxyprogesterone
Virilization of genetically female infants

The newborn-screening test is based primarily on 17-hydroxyprogesterone (17-OHP) measurement.

=============================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Genetic/Congenital Disorders

1. Q: What are the three major patterns of Mendelian inheritance?

A:

Autosomal dominant
Autosomal recessive
X-linked inheritance, which may be dominant or recessive.

2. Q: What is the characteristic pedigree pattern of autosomal dominant inheritance?

A:

Affects both sexes equally
Usually appears in every generation
An affected individual usually has an affected parent
Male-to-male transmission can occur
An affected heterozygous parent has a 50% chance of transmitting the disorder to each child.

3. Q: What is the recurrence risk when one parent is heterozygous for an autosomal dominant disorder?

A: 50% for each pregnancy, regardless of the child's sex.

P(affected child)=
2
1
	​

=50%

4. Q: What is the characteristic pedigree pattern of autosomal recessive inheritance?

A:

Usually skips generations
Affects males and females equally
Parents are often unaffected carriers
Consanguinity may be present
Disease may occur among siblings.

5. Q: What is the recurrence risk when both parents are heterozygous carriers of the same autosomal recessive disorder?

A: For each pregnancy:

4
1
	​

=25% affected
2
1
	​

=50% carrier
4
1
	​

=25% unaffected, noncarrier

6. Q: What is the classic pedigree pattern of X-linked recessive inheritance?

A:

Primarily affects males
Often skips generations
No male-to-male transmission
Carrier females can transmit the disease to sons
An affected male transmits the pathogenic X chromosome to all daughters but no sons.

7. Q: What is the probability that a carrier woman will have an affected son with an X-linked recessive disorder?

A: 50% of her sons, or 25% of all children if sex is not predetermined.

8. Q: What is the probability that a carrier woman will have a carrier daughter for an X-linked recessive disorder?

A: 50% of her daughters will be carriers, assuming the father is unaffected.

9. Q: Can an affected father transmit an X-linked disorder to his son?

A: No.

A father gives his Y chromosome to sons and his X chromosome to daughters.

10. Q: What is characteristic of X-linked dominant inheritance?

A:

Affects both sexes
No male-to-male transmission
An affected father transmits the disorder to all daughters
An affected heterozygous mother has a 50% chance of transmitting it to each child.

11. Q: What is mitochondrial inheritance?

A: Mitochondrial disorders are transmitted through the maternal lineage because the embryo receives essentially all of its mitochondria from the oocyte.

12. Q: Can an affected father transmit a mitochondrial disorder to his children?

A: No.

Affected mothers can transmit mitochondrial disease to both sons and daughters.

13. Q: What is anticipation?

A: Earlier onset and/or increased severity of a genetic disorder in successive generations, usually due to expansion of unstable nucleotide repeats.

14. Q: Which genetic disorders are classic examples of anticipation?

A:

Huntington disease
Myotonic dystrophy
Fragile X syndrome

15. Q: What is genomic imprinting?

A: A phenomenon in which gene expression depends on whether the gene was inherited from the mother or father.

Epigenetic mechanisms such as DNA methylation determine which parental allele is expressed.

16. Q: What is uniparental disomy?

A: Inheritance of both copies of a chromosome or chromosome region from one parent and none from the other.

It can cause disease when genomic imprinting or recessive mutations are involved.

17. Q: What is mosaicism?

A: Presence of two or more genetically distinct cell populations derived from a single zygote.

It usually results from a postzygotic mitotic error.

18. Q: How does mosaicism affect disease severity?

A: The clinical phenotype may be milder or variable depending on:

The proportion of abnormal cells
Which tissues contain abnormal cells
The specific genetic abnormality

19. Q: What is nondisjunction?

A: Failure of homologous chromosomes or sister chromatids to separate properly during cell division, resulting in aneuploidy.

20. Q: What is aneuploidy?

A: An abnormal number of individual chromosomes, such as:

2n+1=Trisomy
2n−1=Monosomy

21. Q: What is the most common viable autosomal trisomy?

A: Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome).

22. Q: What is the most common mechanism causing Down syndrome?

A: Meiotic nondisjunction, producing an extra chromosome 21.

Most cases have a karyotype of:

47,XX,+21

or

47,XY,+21

23. Q: What are the major physical features of Down syndrome?

A:

Hypotonia
Flat facial profile
Upward-slanting palpebral fissures
Epicanthal folds
Short neck
Single transverse palmar crease
Fifth-finger clinodactyly
Developmental delay/intellectual disability

24. Q: Which congenital heart defect is strongly associated with Down syndrome?

A: Atrioventricular septal defect (AVSD), particularly an endocardial cushion defect.

25. Q: What gastrointestinal abnormality is strongly associated with Down syndrome?

A: Duodenal atresia.

A newborn may have bilious vomiting and the classic "double-bubble" sign on abdominal x-ray.

26. Q: What hematologic malignancy is increased in children with Down syndrome?

A: Acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) and particularly acute megakaryoblastic leukemia (AMKL) are increased.

27. Q: What transient neonatal hematologic condition is associated with Down syndrome?

A: Transient abnormal myelopoiesis (transient myeloproliferative disorder) can occur in newborns with Down syndrome.

It may spontaneously resolve but increases the risk of later myeloid leukemia.

28. Q: What endocrine disorder is particularly common in Down syndrome?

A: Autoimmune hypothyroidism.

Routine thyroid screening is therefore important.

29. Q: What neurologic complication is associated with Down syndrome?

A: Atlantoaxial instability can occur because of ligamentous laxity, increasing the risk of spinal cord injury.

30. Q: What major ophthalmologic problems are increased in Down syndrome?

A:

Refractive errors
Strabismus
Cataracts
Keratoconus

31. Q: What is the classic karyotype of Turner syndrome?

A:

45,X

32. Q: What is the characteristic phenotype of Turner syndrome?

A:

Short stature
Webbed neck
Broad chest with widely spaced nipples
Lymphedema of hands/feet in infancy
Gonadal dysgenesis
Primary amenorrhea
Infertility

33. Q: What cardiac abnormalities are classically associated with Turner syndrome?

A:

Coarctation of the aorta
Bicuspid aortic valve

34. Q: What renal abnormality is associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Horseshoe kidney and other renal structural abnormalities can occur.

35. Q: Why do patients with Turner syndrome have delayed or absent puberty?

A: They have streak ovaries due to gonadal dysgenesis, resulting in inadequate estrogen production.

36. Q: What is the typical karyotype of Klinefelter syndrome?

A:

47,XXY

37. Q: What are classic clinical features of Klinefelter syndrome?

A:

Tall stature
Small firm testes
Infertility
Gynecomastia
Sparse facial/body hair
Learning/language difficulties

38. Q: What is the hormonal pattern in Klinefelter syndrome?

A:

Testosterone↓
FSH↑
LH↑

This reflects primary testicular failure.

39. Q: What is the characteristic karyotype of Edwards syndrome?

A:

47,XX,+18

or

47,XY,+18

This is trisomy 18.

40. Q: What physical findings are classic for trisomy 18?

A:

Severe developmental impairment
Growth restriction
Prominent occiput
Low-set ears
Clenched fists with overlapping fingers
Rocker-bottom feet

41. Q: What congenital heart defects are common in trisomy 18?

A: Common defects include:

VSD
ASD
PDA

42. Q: What is the characteristic karyotype of Patau syndrome?

A:

47,XX,+13

or

47,XY,+13

This is trisomy 13.

43. Q: What physical abnormalities are characteristic of trisomy 13?

A:

Cleft lip/palate
Holoprosencephaly
Microphthalmia
Polydactyly
Scalp defects
Severe developmental impairment
