44. Q: What is the classic neurologic malformation associated with trisomy 13?

A: Holoprosencephaly — failure of the embryonic forebrain to properly divide into cerebral hemispheres.

45. Q: Which trisomy is strongly associated with polydactyly?

A: Trisomy 13.

46. Q: What congenital disorder results from deletion of the short arm of chromosome 5?

A: Cri-du-chat syndrome.

5p−

47. Q: What is the classic neonatal finding in cri-du-chat syndrome?

A: A characteristic high-pitched, cat-like cry, particularly in infancy.

48. Q: What other findings occur in cri-du-chat syndrome?

A:

Microcephaly
Developmental delay
Intellectual disability
Hypotonia
Facial abnormalities
Growth restriction

49. Q: What is the most common microdeletion syndrome associated with congenital heart disease, hypocalcemia, and immune dysfunction?

A: 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge syndrome).

50. Q: What are the major features of 22q11.2 deletion syndrome?

A: Think CATCH-22:

C — Cardiac defects, especially conotruncal defects
A — Abnormal facies
T — Thymic hypoplasia → T-cell deficiency
C — Cleft palate/velopharyngeal abnormalities
H — Hypocalcemia from hypoparathyroidism
22 — Chromosome 22q11.2 deletion


51. Q: What genetic defect causes Williams syndrome?

A: A microdeletion of chromosome 7q11.23, including the ELN (elastin) gene.

52. Q: What is the characteristic facial appearance of Williams syndrome?

A: A characteristic "elfin" facial appearance, often with:

Broad forehead
Full lips
Periorbital fullness
Short upturned nose

53. Q: What cardiovascular abnormality is classic for Williams syndrome?

A: Supravalvular aortic stenosis.

54. Q: What electrolyte abnormality can occur in Williams syndrome?

A: Hypercalcemia, particularly in infancy.

55. Q: What neurodevelopmental phenotype is characteristic of Williams syndrome?

A: Mild-to-moderate intellectual disability with a characteristic socially outgoing personality, relative strength in verbal abilities, and weakness in visuospatial skills.

56. Q: A child has an "elfin" face, hypercalcemia, and supravalvular aortic stenosis. What is the diagnosis?

A: Williams syndrome.

57. Q: What chromosome abnormality causes Prader-Willi syndrome?

A: Loss of expression of paternally derived genes in chromosome 15q11-q13.

Common mechanisms include paternal deletion, maternal uniparental disomy, and imprinting defects.

58. Q: What is the classic infant presentation of Prader-Willi syndrome?

A: Severe hypotonia, poor suck, feeding difficulty, and poor weight gain during infancy.

59. Q: What characteristic behavioral change develops later in Prader-Willi syndrome?

A: Hyperphagia, leading to severe obesity if food intake is not controlled.

60. Q: What endocrine abnormality is characteristic of Prader-Willi syndrome?

A: Hypogonadism, resulting in genital hypoplasia and delayed/incomplete puberty.

61. Q: What is the inheritance/imprinting defect in Angelman syndrome?

A: Loss of function or expression of the maternally inherited UBE3A allele in the chromosome 15q11-q13 region.

62. Q: What neurologic phenotype is classic for Angelman syndrome?

A:

Severe developmental delay/intellectual disability
Severe speech impairment
Ataxic gait
Tremulous movements
Seizures
Characteristically happy/excitable demeanor

63. Q: What is the key clinical distinction between Prader-Willi and Angelman syndromes?

A:

Prader-Willi:

Paternal gene expression lost

→ infantile hypotonia → hyperphagia/obesity.

Angelman:

Maternal UBE3A expression lost

→ severe developmental delay + seizures + ataxia + happy demeanor.

64. Q: A child has severe intellectual disability, minimal speech, seizures, ataxia, and frequent inappropriate laughter. What syndrome should be suspected?

A: Angelman syndrome.

Fragile X Syndrome

65. Q: What is the genetic mechanism underlying Fragile X syndrome?

A: CGG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the FMR1 gene on the X chromosome.

66. Q: What molecular mechanism causes Fragile X syndrome?

A: Expansion of the CGG repeat causes hypermethylation and silencing of FMR1, reducing production of fragile X messenger ribonucleoprotein (FMRP).

67. Q: What inheritance pattern is characteristic of Fragile X syndrome?

A: X-linked dominant with reduced penetrance, complicated by anticipation due to trinucleotide-repeat expansion.

68. Q: What physical features suggest Fragile X syndrome?

A:

Long/narrow face
Large protruding ears
Macroorchidism after puberty
Joint laxity
Large jaw

69. Q: What neurodevelopmental disorder is strongly associated with Fragile X syndrome?

A: Intellectual disability, often accompanied by autism-spectrum features, ADHD, anxiety, and behavioral problems.

70. Q: A boy has intellectual disability, large ears, a long face, and macroorchidism. What diagnosis is most likely?

A: Fragile X syndrome.

Neurofibromatosis

71. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1)?

A: Autosomal dominant, caused by pathogenic variants involving the NF1 gene on chromosome 17.

72. Q: What are café-au-lait macules in NF1?

A: Flat, uniformly pigmented, light-brown skin lesions.

Multiple café-au-lait macules are a major diagnostic clue for NF1.

73. Q: What characteristic benign tumors occur in NF1?

A: Neurofibromas, which may be cutaneous or plexiform.

74. Q: What ophthalmologic tumor is strongly associated with NF1?

A: Optic pathway glioma, particularly involving the optic nerve.

75. Q: What iris finding is characteristic of NF1?

A: Lisch nodules — benign iris hamartomas.

76. Q: What skeletal abnormality can occur in NF1?

A: Tibial bowing/pseudarthrosis and sphenoid wing dysplasia can occur.

77. Q: A child has multiple café-au-lait spots, axillary freckling, and multiple neurofibromas. What is the diagnosis?

A: Neurofibromatosis type 1.

78. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of neurofibromatosis type 2?

A: Autosomal dominant.

79. Q: What tumor is the hallmark of NF2?

A: Bilateral vestibular schwannomas.

80. Q: What other tumors are associated with NF2?

A:

Meningiomas
Ependymomas
Other schwannomas
Tuberous Sclerosis

81. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of tuberous sclerosis complex?

A: Autosomal dominant, involving pathogenic variants in TSC1 or TSC2.

82. Q: What is the molecular pathway affected in tuberous sclerosis?

A: Dysregulation of the mTOR pathway, causing abnormal cell growth and hamartoma formation.

83. Q: What characteristic skin lesions occur in tuberous sclerosis?

A:

Facial angiofibromas
Hypomelanotic macules
Shagreen patches
Periungual fibromas

84. Q: What CNS lesion is strongly associated with tuberous sclerosis?

A: Cortical tubers, which can cause seizures and developmental impairment.

85. Q: What renal lesion is commonly associated with tuberous sclerosis?

A: Renal angiomyolipomas.

86. Q: What cardiac tumor is associated with tuberous sclerosis?

A: Cardiac rhabdomyomas, especially in infants.

87. Q: What brain tumor can occur in tuberous sclerosis near the foramen of Monro?

A: Subependymal giant cell astrocytoma (SEGA).

88. Q: An infant has seizures, hypomelanotic skin macules, and cardiac rhabdomyomas. What syndrome should be suspected?

A: Tuberous sclerosis complex.

Marfan Syndrome

89. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of Marfan syndrome?

A: Autosomal dominant, usually caused by a pathogenic variant in FBN1, encoding fibrillin-1.

90. Q: What cardiovascular complication is the most important cause of morbidity in Marfan syndrome?

A: Aortic root dilation, which can progress to aortic aneurysm and dissection.

91. Q: What ocular abnormality is characteristic of Marfan syndrome?

A: Ectopia lentis — displacement of the lens, classically superotemporally.

92. Q: What skeletal features suggest Marfan syndrome?

A:

Tall stature
Long limbs
Arachnodactyly
Pectus deformity
Scoliosis
Joint laxity

93. Q: What is the molecular pathway associated with Marfan syndrome?

A: Abnormal fibrillin-1 affects regulation of transforming growth factor-β (TGF-β) signaling.

94. Q: A tall adolescent has arachnodactyly, pectus deformity, ectopia lentis, and progressive aortic root dilation. What is the diagnosis?

A: Marfan syndrome.

Achondroplasia

95. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of achondroplasia?

A: Autosomal dominant, caused by an activating mutation in FGFR3.

Most cases result from a de novo mutation.

96. Q: What is the major skeletal abnormality in achondroplasia?

A: Disproportionate short stature due to impaired endochondral ossification, particularly affecting the proximal limbs.

97. Q: What characteristic craniofacial finding occurs in achondroplasia?

A: Macrocephaly with frontal bossing and midface hypoplasia.

98. Q: What hand abnormality is characteristic of achondroplasia?

A: Trident hand, with separation of the middle and ring fingers.

99. Q: What type of bone growth is primarily impaired in achondroplasia?

A: Endochondral ossification is impaired, while intramembranous ossification is relatively preserved.

100. Q: What respiratory complication can occur in infants with achondroplasia?

A: Obstructive sleep apnea, which can result from craniofacial anatomy and upper-airway obstruction.

101. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of osteogenesis imperfecta?

A: Most cases are autosomal dominant, commonly involving mutations in COL1A1 or COL1A2, which encode type I collagen.

102. Q: What is the hallmark clinical feature of osteogenesis imperfecta?

A: Multiple fractures with minimal trauma due to defective type I collagen and abnormal bone strength.

103. Q: What characteristic eye finding can occur in osteogenesis imperfecta?

A: Blue sclerae due to thinning of the connective tissue over the choroid.

104. Q: What auditory complication can develop in osteogenesis imperfecta?

A: Conductive or sensorineural hearing loss, often due to abnormalities of the ossicles or inner ear.

105. Q: What dental abnormality is associated with osteogenesis imperfecta?

A: Dentinogenesis imperfecta, causing fragile, discolored teeth.

106. Q: A child has recurrent fractures, blue sclerae, and hearing loss. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Osteogenesis imperfecta.

Ehlers-Danlos Syndromes

107. Q: What is the major underlying defect in many forms of Ehlers-Danlos syndrome?

A: Abnormal collagen synthesis or processing, although the precise genetic defect varies among subtypes.

108. Q: What are the classic clinical features of Ehlers-Danlos syndrome?

A:

Joint hypermobility
Skin hyperextensibility
Easy bruising
Tissue fragility

109. Q: Which Ehlers-Danlos subtype is particularly associated with arterial and organ rupture?

A: Vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, commonly associated with COL3A1 abnormalities.

110. Q: What is the major life-threatening complication of vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndrome?

A: Spontaneous arterial rupture/dissection or rupture of hollow organs due to severe connective-tissue fragility.

111. Q: A child has extreme joint hypermobility, stretchy skin, and easy bruising. What disorder should be considered?

A: Ehlers-Danlos syndrome.

Homocystinuria

112. Q: What enzyme deficiency classically causes homocystinuria?

A: Cystathionine β-synthase deficiency.

113. Q: What amino acid accumulates in classic homocystinuria?

A: Homocysteine.

Methionine is also typically elevated.

114. Q: What are major clinical features of classic homocystinuria?

A:

Developmental delay/intellectual disability
Ectopia lentis
Osteoporosis
Marfanoid habitus
Thromboembolism

115. Q: How does the direction of lens displacement distinguish homocystinuria from Marfan syndrome?

A:

Homocystinuria: lens tends to dislocate inferonasally.

Marfan syndrome: lens tends to dislocate superotemporally.

116. Q: What vascular complication is particularly important in homocystinuria?

A: Arterial and venous thrombosis.

117. Q: What vitamins may be useful in treating homocystinuria?

A: Depending on the specific defect and biochemical response:

Pyridoxine (vitamin B6)
Folate
Vitamin B12

Betaine may also be used in selected patients.

Duchenne & Becker Muscular Dystrophy

118. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: X-linked recessive.

119. Q: What protein is deficient in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Dystrophin.

120. Q: What is the primary molecular problem in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Usually a mutation causing absent or severely deficient dystrophin, resulting in progressive muscle fiber degeneration.

121. Q: What is the typical age of presentation of Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Usually early childhood, commonly around 2–5 years of age.

122. Q: What early motor finding is characteristic of Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Delayed motor milestones, difficulty running/jumping, frequent falls, and difficulty climbing stairs.

123. Q: What is Gowers sign?

A: A child uses the arms to "walk up" the thighs to rise from the floor because of proximal muscle weakness.

124. Q: What type of muscle weakness occurs in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Predominantly proximal muscle weakness, especially involving the pelvic girdle.

125. Q: What happens to serum creatine kinase in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Markedly elevated CK, often many times above the normal range.

126. Q: Why is creatine kinase elevated in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Muscle fiber membrane damage allows intracellular CK to leak into the bloodstream.

127. Q: What causes calf enlargement in Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Pseudohypertrophy caused by replacement of muscle fibers with fat and connective tissue.

128. Q: What cardiac complication is associated with Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Dilated cardiomyopathy due to progressive myocardial involvement.

129. Q: What respiratory complication is a major cause of morbidity in advanced Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Respiratory muscle weakness, resulting in hypoventilation and recurrent respiratory infections.

130. Q: How does Becker muscular dystrophy differ from Duchenne muscular dystrophy?

A: Becker muscular dystrophy usually has:

Later onset
Slower progression
Partially functional dystrophin

Duchenne generally causes more severe and earlier disease.

131. Q: A 4-year-old boy has frequent falls, difficulty climbing stairs, calf pseudohypertrophy, Gowers sign, and very high CK. What is the diagnosis?

A: Duchenne muscular dystrophy.

Spinal Muscular Atrophy

132. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of spinal muscular atrophy (SMA)?

A: Autosomal recessive.

133. Q: What gene is most commonly affected in SMA?

A: SMN1 (survival motor neuron 1).

134. Q: What type of neurons degenerate in SMA?

A: Anterior horn cells of the spinal cord, causing lower motor neuron weakness.

135. Q: What is the characteristic weakness pattern in SMA?

A: Symmetric proximal muscle weakness, generally greater in the lower limbs.

136. Q: What is a classic physical finding in severe infantile SMA?

A: Hypotonia with a weak cry and tongue fasciculations.

137. Q: How are reflexes affected in SMA?

A: Deep tendon reflexes are decreased or absent due to lower motor neuron dysfunction.

138. Q: What is the major respiratory problem in severe SMA?

A: Respiratory muscle weakness, which can lead to respiratory failure.

139. Q: A floppy infant has tongue fasciculations, absent reflexes, and progressive symmetric weakness. What diagnosis should be suspected?

A: Spinal muscular atrophy.

Congenital Malformation Syndromes

140. Q: What is the most common cause of neural tube defects?

A: Neural tube defects are multifactorial, with folate deficiency being a major preventable risk factor.

141. Q: What are the two major forms of open neural tube defects?

A:

Myelomeningocele
Myelocele

Myelomeningocele is the more common clinically recognized severe form.

142. Q: What neurologic deficits can occur with myelomeningocele?

A: Depending on lesion level:

Lower-extremity weakness/paralysis
Sensory loss
Bladder dysfunction
Bowel dysfunction

143. Q: What CNS abnormality is strongly associated with myelomeningocele?

A: Chiari II malformation, involving downward displacement of cerebellar and brainstem structures.

144. Q: What is the major preventive measure for neural tube defects?

A: Periconceptional folic acid supplementation.

Adequate folate should ideally begin before conception and continue through early pregnancy.

145. Q: What congenital defect results from failure of fusion of the cranial neural tube?

A: Anencephaly.

146. Q: What is the typical outcome of anencephaly?

A: It is lethal, with death occurring before or shortly after birth.

Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia

147. Q: What is the most common type of congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: Bochdalek hernia, usually occurring posterolaterally and more commonly on the left side.

148. Q: What is the major pathophysiologic consequence of congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: Herniation of abdominal organs into the thorax causes pulmonary hypoplasia and pulmonary hypertension.

149. Q: What physical examination finding suggests congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: Scaphoid abdomen with respiratory distress and decreased breath sounds on the affected side.

150. Q: What should be avoided in the immediate management of a newborn with severe congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: Bag-mask ventilation should generally be avoided because it can inflate the herniated bowel and worsen pulmonary compression.

Instead, endotracheal intubation and gastric decompression are used to minimize bowel distension and support ventilation.

151. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of cystic fibrosis (CF)?

A: Autosomal recessive.

152. Q: What gene is mutated in cystic fibrosis?

A: The CFTR gene on chromosome 7.

CFTR encodes an epithelial chloride channel/regulator.

153. Q: What is the most common CFTR mutation causing cystic fibrosis?

A: ΔF508 (F508del), a deletion of phenylalanine at position 508.

154. Q: What is the basic defect in cystic fibrosis?

A: Abnormal CFTR function causes defective epithelial ion and water transport, producing dehydrated, thick secretions in multiple organs.

155. Q: What pulmonary finding is characteristic of cystic fibrosis?

A: Recurrent sinopulmonary infections with chronic productive cough and bronchiectasis.

156. Q: Which organism is classically associated with chronic pulmonary infection in older children with CF?

A: Pseudomonas aeruginosa.

157. Q: Which organism is particularly important in young children with cystic fibrosis?

A: Staphylococcus aureus is an important early respiratory pathogen.

158. Q: What characteristic pulmonary imaging finding can develop in cystic fibrosis?

A: Bronchiectasis, with permanent dilation and structural destruction of the bronchi.

159. Q: What gastrointestinal manifestation is characteristic of cystic fibrosis?

A: Pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, causing malabsorption and poor growth.

160. Q: Why does cystic fibrosis cause pancreatic insufficiency?

A: Thick secretions obstruct pancreatic ducts, causing progressive pancreatic destruction and inadequate digestive enzyme delivery to the intestine.

161. Q: What type of stool can occur in pancreatic insufficiency from CF?

A: Bulky, greasy, foul-smelling stools (steatorrhea) due to fat malabsorption.

162. Q: What vitamin deficiencies are particularly likely in pancreatic-insufficient CF?

A: Fat-soluble vitamins:

A, D, E, K

163. Q: What is meconium ileus?

A: Intestinal obstruction in a newborn caused by abnormally thick, sticky meconium.

It is strongly associated with cystic fibrosis.

164. Q: A newborn develops abdominal distension and failure to pass meconium. Imaging suggests distal small-bowel obstruction. What genetic disorder should be considered?

A: Cystic fibrosis with meconium ileus.

165. Q: What diagnostic test measures sweat chloride in suspected cystic fibrosis?

A: The quantitative pilocarpine iontophoresis sweat test.

166. Q: What sweat chloride concentration is diagnostic of cystic fibrosis in an appropriately tested patient?

A: A sweat chloride concentration of ≥60 mmol/L supports the diagnosis when interpreted in the appropriate clinical context.

167. Q: Why is sweat chloride elevated in cystic fibrosis?

A: Defective CFTR function reduces chloride reabsorption in sweat ducts, resulting in abnormally salty sweat.

168. Q: What reproductive abnormality is common in males with cystic fibrosis?

A: Congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens (CBAVD), causing obstructive azoospermia and infertility.

169. Q: What is the usual mechanism of infertility in males with cystic fibrosis?

A: Obstructive azoospermia due to congenital absence/abnormal development of the vas deferens, rather than failure of sperm production.

Disorders of Sex Development

170. Q: What chromosomal pattern is typical of a phenotypic female with Turner syndrome?

A:

45,X

Turner syndrome was covered earlier; the key DSD distinction here is that Turner syndrome involves gonadal dysgenesis, not androgen excess.

171. Q: What is the most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in a 46,XX newborn?

A: Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH), particularly 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

172. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes the classic form of 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Deficiency of 21-hydroxylase (CYP21A2).

173. Q: What happens to cortisol in classic 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Cortisol decreases because conversion of 17-hydroxyprogesterone toward cortisol is impaired.

174. Q: What happens to adrenal androgens in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Androgen production increases because steroid precursors are diverted toward androgen synthesis.

175. Q: What laboratory marker is elevated in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: 17-hydroxyprogesterone (17-OHP).

176. Q: What causes salt wasting in the classic form of 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Reduced aldosterone synthesis, causing:

Na
+
↓
K
+
↑
Volume↓

177. Q: What acid-base abnormality can occur in salt-wasting CAH?

A: Metabolic acidosis, related to impaired renal potassium and hydrogen ion excretion in the setting of mineralocorticoid deficiency.

178. Q: What is the typical sex-development presentation of a 46,XX infant with severe 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: Virilized external genitalia due to excess adrenal androgens, while internal female reproductive structures are present.

179. Q: Why are Müllerian structures preserved in a 46,XX infant with 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

A: There is no anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) from testes because the infant has ovaries rather than testes.

180. Q: What is the key hormone responsible for Müllerian duct regression in an XY fetus?

A: Anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) produced by Sertoli cells.

181. Q: What is androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS)?

A: A disorder in which an 46,XY individual has impaired androgen receptor function, resulting in reduced or absent response to testosterone and dihydrotestosterone.

182. Q: What is the typical phenotype of complete androgen insensitivity syndrome?

A: Phenotypic female with:

Normal female external genitalia
Absent uterus
Intra-abdominal/inguinal testes
Sparse or absent pubic/axillary hair

183. Q: Why is the uterus absent in complete androgen insensitivity syndrome?

A: The testes produce AMH, causing Müllerian duct regression.

184. Q: Why are pubic and axillary hairs sparse in complete AIS?

A: Hair growth is androgen dependent, and the defective androgen receptor prevents effective androgen action.

185. Q: What is the karyotype in complete androgen insensitivity syndrome?

A:

46,XY

186. Q: A phenotypic female adolescent has primary amenorrhea, absent uterus, sparse pubic hair, and an inguinal mass. What is the diagnosis?

A: Complete androgen insensitivity syndrome.

187. Q: What enzyme converts testosterone to dihydrotestosterone (DHT)?

A: 5-α-reductase.

Testosterone
5-α-reductase
	​

DHT

188. Q: What is the role of DHT in fetal development?

A: DHT is essential for development of the male external genitalia and prostate.

189. Q: What is the typical presentation of 5-α-reductase deficiency?

A: An 46,XY infant may have undervirilized or ambiguous external genitalia at birth, with increased virilization at puberty due to rising testosterone.

190. Q: How can 5-α-reductase deficiency be distinguished from complete AIS?

A: Both can have undervirilized external genitalia, but in 5-α-reductase deficiency, testosterone production is present and virilization may increase at puberty.

Inborn Errors of Metabolism

191. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes maple syrup urine disease (MSUD)?

A: Deficiency of the branched-chain α-ketoacid dehydrogenase complex.

192. Q: Which amino acids accumulate in maple syrup urine disease?

A: The branched-chain amino acids:

Leucine, Isoleucine, Valine

and their corresponding ketoacids.

193. Q: What is the classic neonatal presentation of maple syrup urine disease?

A: After protein feeding:

Poor feeding
Vomiting
Lethargy
Abnormal tone
Neurologic deterioration
Characteristic maple syrup odor of urine

194. Q: Why does MSUD cause neurologic injury?

A: Accumulation of branched-chain amino acids and ketoacids causes neurotoxicity and cerebral dysfunction.

195. Q: What is the dietary treatment for maple syrup urine disease?

A: Restriction of leucine, isoleucine, and valine while maintaining adequate nutrition.

196. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes hereditary fructose intolerance?

A: Aldolase B deficiency.

197. Q: What metabolite accumulates in hereditary fructose intolerance?

A: Fructose-1-phosphate accumulates.

198. Q: What happens when an infant with hereditary fructose intolerance consumes fructose?

A: After introduction of fructose-containing foods:

Vomiting
Abdominal pain
Hypoglycemia
Lethargy
Hepatic dysfunction

may occur.

199. Q: What is the treatment for hereditary fructose intolerance?

A: Avoid:

Fructose
Sucrose
Sorbitol

200. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes essential fructosuria?

A: Fructokinase deficiency.

It is generally benign and asymptomatic, unlike hereditary fructose intolerance.

201. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes von Gierke disease (Glycogen Storage Disease type I)?

A: Glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency.

202. Q: What are the major clinical findings in von Gierke disease?

A:

Severe fasting hypoglycemia
Lactic acidosis
Hyperuricemia
Hyperlipidemia
Hepatomegaly
Growth retardation

203. Q: Why does von Gierke disease cause severe fasting hypoglycemia?

A: The liver cannot convert glucose-6-phosphate into free glucose during glycogenolysis or gluconeogenesis.

204. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Pompe disease?

A: Lysosomal acid α-glucosidase (acid maltase) deficiency.

205. Q: What is the major organ affected in infantile Pompe disease?

A: The heart, causing marked hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

206. Q: What other major manifestation occurs in infantile Pompe disease?

A: Severe generalized hypotonia and muscle weakness.

207. Q: What differentiates Pompe disease from many other glycogen storage diseases?

A: Glycogen accumulates within lysosomes, particularly affecting cardiac and skeletal muscle.

208. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease type V)?

A: Muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase) deficiency.

209. Q: What is the classic presentation of McArdle disease?

A: Exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria after strenuous exercise.

210. Q: What is the "second-wind" phenomenon in McArdle disease?

A: After brief rest during exercise, patients may experience improved exercise tolerance because muscles increase their use of alternative energy sources.

Lysosomal Storage Disorders

211. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Tay-Sachs disease?

A: Hexosaminidase A deficiency.

212. Q: What substance accumulates in Tay-Sachs disease?

A: GM2 ganglioside accumulates within lysosomes, particularly in neurons.

213. Q: What is the classic neurologic progression of Tay-Sachs disease?

A: Progressive:

Developmental regression
Hypotonia
Weakness
Loss of motor skills
Seizures
Severe neurologic deterioration

214. Q: What eye finding is characteristic of Tay-Sachs disease?

A: A cherry-red spot on the macula.

215. Q: What important physical finding is typically absent in Tay-Sachs disease?

A: Hepatosplenomegaly is usually absent.

This helps distinguish it from some other lysosomal storage disorders.

216. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Niemann-Pick disease type A?

A: Acid sphingomyelinase deficiency.

217. Q: What lipid accumulates in Niemann-Pick disease type A?

A: Sphingomyelin accumulates within lysosomes.

218. Q: What major clinical findings suggest Niemann-Pick disease type A?

A:

Neurodegeneration
Developmental regression
Hypotonia
Hepatosplenomegaly
Cherry-red macula

219. Q: How can Niemann-Pick disease type A be distinguished from Tay-Sachs disease?

A: Both can cause neurodegeneration and a cherry-red macula, but Niemann-Pick typically causes prominent hepatosplenomegaly, whereas Tay-Sachs generally does not.

220. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Gaucher disease?

A: β-glucocerebrosidase deficiency.

221. Q: What substance accumulates in Gaucher disease?

A: Glucocerebroside (glucosylceramide) accumulates in macrophages.

222. Q: What are classic clinical findings of Gaucher disease?

A:

Hepatosplenomegaly
Anemia
Thrombocytopenia
Bone pain
Pathologic fractures

223. Q: What are Gaucher cells?

A: Lipid-laden macrophages with "crumpled tissue paper" cytoplasm.

224. Q: What skeletal complication is particularly characteristic of Gaucher disease?

A: Erlenmeyer flask deformity of the long bones.

225. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Fabry disease?

A: α-galactosidase A deficiency.

226. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of Fabry disease?

A: X-linked.

227. Q: What substance accumulates in Fabry disease?

A: Globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) accumulates in vascular endothelial and other cells.

228. Q: What skin lesion is characteristic of Fabry disease?

A: Angiokeratomas, particularly in the distribution of the lower trunk and genital region.

229. Q: What neurologic symptom is characteristic of Fabry disease?

A: Severe episodic burning pain in the hands and feet (acroparesthesias).

230. Q: What renal complication occurs in Fabry disease?

A: Progressive proteinuria and chronic kidney disease due to glomerular involvement.

Urea-Cycle Disorders

231. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of ornithine transcarbamylase deficiency?

A: X-linked recessive.

232. Q: What biochemical abnormality is characteristic of ornithine transcarbamylase deficiency?

A: Hyperammonemia with elevated orotic acid.

233. Q: Why is orotic acid elevated in OTC deficiency?

A: Excess mitochondrial carbamoyl phosphate enters the cytosolic pyrimidine pathway, increasing orotic acid production.

234. Q: What happens to blood urea nitrogen (BUN) in severe urea-cycle disorders?

A: BUN is decreased because ammonia cannot be efficiently incorporated into urea.

235. Q: What is the typical presentation of a neonate with a severe urea-cycle disorder?

A: After an initial symptom-free period:

Poor feeding
Vomiting
Irritability
Lethargy
Hyperventilation
Progressive encephalopathy
Seizures/coma

236. Q: Why can urea-cycle disorders cause respiratory alkalosis?

A: Hyperammonemia stimulates the CNS respiratory center, producing hyperventilation and respiratory alkalosis.

237. Q: What major metabolic distinction separates urea-cycle disorders from many organic acidemias?

A: Urea-cycle disorders generally cause hyperammonemia without a primary high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis.

238. Q: What is an important emergency treatment for severe hyperammonemia?

A: Rapid reduction of ammonia using:

Nitrogen-scavenging agents
Appropriate metabolic therapy
Hemodialysis for severe/refractory hyperammonemia
Organic Acidemias

239. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes methylmalonic acidemia?

A: Most commonly methylmalonyl-CoA mutase deficiency or impaired vitamin B12 metabolism.

240. Q: What metabolic abnormalities are characteristic of methylmalonic acidemia?

A:

Methylmalonic acid ↑
Metabolic acidosis
Ketosis
Hyperammonemia may occur
Hypoglycemia can occur

241. Q: What is the treatment principle for methylmalonic acidemia?

A: Restriction of problematic amino acid substrates with specialized nutritional management; vitamin B12 is particularly important in B12-responsive forms.

242. Q: What enzyme complex is deficient in propionic acidemia?

A: Propionyl-CoA carboxylase.

243. Q: What are typical biochemical findings in propionic acidemia?

A:

High-anion-gap metabolic acidosis
Ketosis
Hyperammonemia
Lactic acidosis
Hypoglycemia

244. Q: Why can organic acidemias cause hyperammonemia?

A: Accumulated organic acids can inhibit urea-cycle function, impairing ammonia detoxification.

Mitochondrial Disorders

245. Q: What inheritance pattern is characteristic of mitochondrial DNA disorders?

A: Maternal inheritance.

Affected mothers can transmit mitochondrial disease to both sons and daughters.

246. Q: Why can mitochondrial disorders affect multiple seemingly unrelated organs?

A: Mitochondria are required for ATP production in virtually all tissues, so high-energy organs such as the brain, skeletal muscle, heart, and retina are particularly vulnerable.

247. Q: What is heteroplasmy?

A: The presence of a mixture of normal and mutant mitochondrial DNA within cells.

248. Q: Why can mitochondrial disorders have variable severity among family members?

A: The proportion and tissue distribution of mutant mitochondrial DNA can differ among individuals because of heteroplasmy and mitochondrial segregation.

Congenital Renal/Genitourinary Disorders

249. Q: What is the most common congenital renal anomaly detected in children?

A: Congenital anomalies of kidney and urinary tract (CAKUT) encompass a broad group; among structural renal findings, renal agenesis, hypoplasia, dysplasia, and ectopia are important congenital abnormalities encountered clinically.

250. Q: What is the classic complication of bilateral renal agenesis?

A: Severe oligohydramnios → pulmonary hypoplasia → Potter sequence, which includes characteristic facial and limb findings and is usually fatal because of pulmonary hypoplasia.

251. Q: What is the classic association of unilateral renal agenesis with genital tract abnormalities?

A: In females, unilateral renal agenesis is associated with Müllerian duct anomalies, because the urinary and reproductive systems develop closely together embryologically.

252. Q: What is the usual clinical significance of unilateral renal agenesis?

A: It may be asymptomatic because the contralateral kidney undergoes compensatory hypertrophy, but associated urinary/genital anomalies should be evaluated.

253. Q: What is a horseshoe kidney?

A: Fusion of the lower poles of the kidneys, usually occurring before ascent, leaving the fused kidneys below the inferior mesenteric artery.

254. Q: What major vascular structure prevents a horseshoe kidney from ascending normally?

A: The inferior mesenteric artery.

255. Q: Which chromosomal disorder is classically associated with horseshoe kidney?

A: Turner syndrome.

256. Q: What congenital anomaly results from failure of normal kidney ascent?

A: Ectopic kidney, most commonly a pelvic kidney.

257. Q: What is vesicoureteral reflux (VUR)?

A: Retrograde flow of urine from the bladder into the ureters and potentially the renal collecting system.

258. Q: Why is vesicoureteral reflux clinically important in children?

A: It predisposes to recurrent febrile urinary tract infections and renal scarring, potentially leading to hypertension or chronic kidney disease.

259. Q: What embryologic abnormality commonly causes congenital vesicoureteral reflux?

A: An abnormally short intramural ureter, which reduces the normal flap-valve mechanism at the ureterovesical junction.

260. Q: What is posterior urethral valve disease?

A: A congenital obstructing membrane in the posterior urethra of males, causing bladder outlet obstruction.

261. Q: What prenatal ultrasound finding can suggest posterior urethral valves?

A: Bilateral hydronephrosis with a distended bladder, sometimes accompanied by oligohydramnios.

262. Q: What is the classic ultrasound appearance of posterior urethral valves?

A: A distended bladder with thickened walls and dilation of the posterior urethra, producing the "keyhole" sign.

263. Q: What is the major neonatal complication of severe posterior urethral valves?

A: Obstructive uropathy with renal dysplasia and impaired renal function.

264. Q: What is hypospadias?

A: A congenital abnormality in which the urethral meatus opens on the ventral surface of the penis rather than at the tip.

265. Q: What additional penile abnormality commonly accompanies hypospadias?

A: Chordee, an abnormal ventral curvature of the penis.

266. Q: What is the key management principle for significant hypospadias?

A: Surgical repair, typically during infancy; circumcision should generally be deferred because the foreskin may be needed for reconstruction.

267. Q: What is epispadias?

A: A congenital abnormality in which the urethral opening is located on the dorsal surface of the penis.

268. Q: What congenital condition is classically associated with epispadias?

A: Bladder exstrophy, in which the bladder is exposed through an anterior abdominal wall defect.

269. Q: What is cryptorchidism?

A: Failure of one or both testes to descend into the scrotum.

270. Q: Why is persistent cryptorchidism clinically important?

A: It increases the risk of:

Testicular germ-cell tumor
Infertility/subfertility
Testicular torsion
Inguinal hernia
Chromosomal Structural Abnormalities

271. Q: What is a chromosomal deletion?

A: Loss of a segment of a chromosome, resulting in loss of genetic material.

Notation may include:

46,XX,del(5p)

272. Q: What is a chromosomal duplication?

A: An extra copy of a chromosomal segment, producing increased dosage of genes within that region.

273. Q: What is a chromosomal inversion?

A: A chromosome segment breaks, rotates 180°, and reinserts into the chromosome.

274. Q: What is a balanced translocation?

A: Rearrangement of genetic material between chromosomes without a net loss or gain of DNA.

The carrier may be clinically normal but can have reproductive problems.

275. Q: Why can a parent with a balanced translocation have a child with a chromosomal disorder?

A: Abnormal segregation during meiosis can produce unbalanced gametes, resulting in partial monosomy or trisomy in the offspring.

276. Q: What is a Robertsonian translocation?

A: A fusion involving the long arms of two acrocentric chromosomes, with loss of their short arms.

277. Q: Which chromosomes commonly participate in Robertsonian translocations?

A: The acrocentric chromosomes:

13, 14, 15, 21, 22

278. Q: Why is a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 21 clinically important?

A: A parent carrying a balanced translocation involving chromosome 21 can have offspring with translocation Down syndrome.

279. Q: Why is genetic counseling important after a child is diagnosed with translocation Down syndrome?

A: The recurrence risk may be substantially higher than with meiotic nondisjunction, depending on the exact parental karyotype.

Teratogenic & Congenital Syndromes

280. Q: What congenital syndrome is classically associated with maternal alcohol exposure?

A: Fetal alcohol spectrum disorder (FASD).

281. Q: What are the classic facial features of fetal alcohol syndrome?

A:

Short palpebral fissures
Smooth philtrum
Thin upper vermilion border

282. Q: What neurologic/developmental abnormalities can occur with fetal alcohol exposure?

A:

Growth restriction
Microcephaly
Developmental delay
Intellectual disability
Behavioral and learning problems

283. Q: What congenital heart defect is classically associated with maternal alcohol exposure?

A: Septal defects, including atrial and ventricular septal defects, can occur.

284. Q: What congenital syndrome is associated with maternal isotretinoin exposure?

A: Retinoic acid embryopathy, which can cause craniofacial, cardiac, thymic, and CNS abnormalities.

285. Q: Why is isotretinoin highly important in pregnancy?

A: It is a potent teratogen associated with severe congenital malformations and therefore requires strict pregnancy-prevention measures.

286. Q: What congenital heart defect is associated with maternal lithium exposure?

A: Ebstein anomaly.

287. Q: What is Ebstein anomaly?

A: Inferior displacement of the tricuspid valve, causing part of the right ventricle to become functionally incorporated into the right atrium ("atrialization").

288. Q: What neonatal cardiac manifestation can occur with severe Ebstein anomaly?

A: Cyanosis, often due to right-to-left shunting through an atrial-level communication.

289. Q: What congenital syndrome is associated with maternal warfarin exposure?

A: Fetal warfarin syndrome, which can include:

Nasal hypoplasia
Skeletal abnormalities
Growth restriction
CNS abnormalities

290. Q: What fetal abnormalities are associated with maternal valproate exposure?

A: Increased risk of neural tube defects, particularly spina bifida, as well as other congenital and neurodevelopmental abnormalities.

Additional Inborn Errors of Metabolism

291. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes classic tyrosinemia type I?

A: Fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase deficiency.

292. Q: What are major features of tyrosinemia type I?

A:

Progressive liver disease
Renal tubular dysfunction
Growth failure
Rickets
Increased risk of hepatocellular carcinoma

293. Q: What medication is used to treat tyrosinemia type I?

A: Nitisinone (NTBC), combined with dietary restriction of tyrosine and phenylalanine.

294. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes alkaptonuria?

A: Homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase deficiency.

295. Q: What are classic features of alkaptonuria?

A:

Dark/black urine after standing
Ochronosis
Progressive degenerative arthritis

296. Q: What is the biochemical basis of the dark urine in alkaptonuria?

A: Accumulation and oxidation of homogentisic acid produces dark pigment.

297. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes biotinidase deficiency?

A: Deficiency of biotinidase, impairing recycling of biotin.

298. Q: What clinical findings can occur in untreated biotinidase deficiency?

A:

Seizures
Hypotonia
Developmental delay
Alopecia
Dermatitis
Metabolic acidosis

299. Q: What is the treatment for biotinidase deficiency?

A: Biotin supplementation, which can prevent or substantially improve manifestations when started early.

300. Q: What is the classic congenital disorder caused by deficiency of α-L-iduronidase?

A: Mucopolysaccharidosis type I (Hurler syndrome).

Features can include:

Coarse facial features
Hepatosplenomegaly
Skeletal abnormalities
Corneal clouding
Developmental impairment

301. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Hurler syndrome (MPS I)?

A: α-L-iduronidase deficiency, causing accumulation of dermatan sulfate and heparan sulfate.

302. Q: What finding helps distinguish Hurler syndrome from many other mucopolysaccharidoses?

A: Corneal clouding is characteristic of Hurler syndrome.

303. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of mucopolysaccharidosis type I?

A: Autosomal recessive.

304. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Hunter syndrome (MPS II)?

A: Iduronate-2-sulfatase deficiency.

305. Q: What important feature distinguishes Hunter syndrome from Hurler syndrome?

A: Hunter syndrome is X-linked recessive, whereas Hurler syndrome is autosomal recessive.

Also, corneal clouding is absent in Hunter syndrome.

306. Q: What is the classic clinical presentation of Hunter syndrome?

A:

Coarse facial features
Hepatosplenomegaly
Skeletal abnormalities
Developmental impairment
Airway obstruction

307. Q: What enzyme deficiency causes Pompe disease?

A: Acid α-glucosidase (acid maltase) deficiency, resulting in lysosomal glycogen accumulation.

308. Q: What distinguishes infantile Pompe disease from McArdle disease?

A: Infantile Pompe disease prominently causes hypotonia and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, whereas McArdle disease causes exercise-induced muscle cramps and myoglobinuria.

Additional Genetic Neurologic Disorders

309. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of Huntington disease?

A: Autosomal dominant with CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the HTT gene.

310. Q: What is anticipation in Huntington disease?

A: Progressive earlier onset in successive generations, particularly when the expanded allele is transmitted through the father.

311. Q: What are the major clinical manifestations of Huntington disease?

A:

Chorea
Psychiatric/behavioral abnormalities
Progressive cognitive decline

312. Q: Why is Huntington disease uncommon in young children despite being genetic?

A: It is typically an adult-onset neurodegenerative disorder, although juvenile-onset disease can occur with larger repeat expansions.

313. Q: What is the inheritance pattern of Rett syndrome?

A: Usually X-linked dominant, most commonly caused by pathogenic variants in MECP2.

314. Q: What is the classic clinical course of Rett syndrome?

A: Affected girls typically have:

Initially apparently normal development
Developmental regression
Loss of purposeful hand use
Hand-wringing/stereotypic movements
Loss of language
Gait abnormalities

315. Q: What sex is predominantly affected by classic Rett syndrome?

A: Girls, because severe MECP2 abnormalities are generally lethal in males.

316. Q: A previously normally developing girl loses purposeful hand movements and language and develops repetitive hand-wringing movements. What is the diagnosis?

A: Rett syndrome.

Congenital Craniofacial Disorders

317. Q: What is craniosynostosis?

A: Premature fusion of one or more cranial sutures, restricting normal skull growth.

318. Q: What is the major consequence of severe craniosynostosis?

A: Abnormal skull shape and, in severe/multisuture disease, increased intracranial pressure and impaired brain growth.

319. Q: What syndrome results from a mutation in FGFR2 and causes craniosynostosis with midface hypoplasia?

A: Apert syndrome.

320. Q: What additional feature is characteristic of Apert syndrome?

A: Syndactyly, particularly involving the hands and feet.

321. Q: What syndrome is associated with craniosynostosis, midface hypoplasia, and proptosis?

A: Crouzon syndrome.

322. Q: How can Apert syndrome be distinguished from Crouzon syndrome?

A:

Apert:

Craniosynostosis + syndactyly

Crouzon:

Craniosynostosis + characteristic craniofacial abnormalities

but syndactyly is not a defining feature.

323. Q: What is Treacher Collins syndrome?

A: A congenital craniofacial disorder characterized by mandibulofacial dysostosis, often involving abnormalities of the zygomatic bones, mandible, ears, and eyelids.

324. Q: What is the major genetic association with Treacher Collins syndrome?

A: Most cases involve TCOF1 or related genes affecting craniofacial development.

325. Q: What functional problem is common in infants with severe Treacher Collins syndrome?

A: Airway obstruction and feeding difficulties due to mandibular and upper-airway abnormalities.

Congenital Heart & Genetic Syndromes

326. Q: What genetic syndrome is strongly associated with multiple café-au-lait macules and congenital cardiovascular abnormalities?

A: Neurofibromatosis type 1, which can be associated with renal artery stenosis, coarctation of the aorta, and other vascular abnormalities.

327. Q: What genetic syndrome is associated with a high risk of aortic root dilation and mitral valve prolapse?

A: Marfan syndrome.

328. Q: What congenital heart lesion is particularly associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta, especially when associated with a bicuspid aortic valve.

329. Q: What congenital heart defects are characteristic of 22q11.2 deletion syndrome?

A: Conotruncal defects, including:

Tetralogy of Fallot
Truncus arteriosus
Interrupted aortic arch

330. Q: Why does 22q11.2 deletion syndrome cause hypocalcemia?

A: Abnormal development of the parathyroid glands causes hypoparathyroidism and reduced PTH secretion.

Genetic Testing & Diagnosis

331. Q: What is a karyotype used to detect?

A: Large-scale chromosomal abnormalities such as:

Aneuploidy
Large deletions
Large duplications
Some translocations

332. Q: What is fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH)?

A: A technique using fluorescent DNA probes to detect specific chromosome regions or genetic abnormalities.

333. Q: What is chromosomal microarray analysis?

A: A genome-wide test that detects copy-number changes, including clinically significant microdeletions and microduplications that may not be visible on a conventional karyotype.

334. Q: What is the major limitation of chromosomal microarray compared with karyotyping?

A: Microarray generally does not detect balanced translocations or inversions, because these rearrangements do not necessarily change DNA copy number.

335. Q: What is PCR commonly used for in genetic medicine?

A: To amplify specific DNA sequences, allowing detection of targeted genetic variants or infectious/genetic sequences.

336. Q: What is DNA sequencing used for?

A: To determine the nucleotide sequence of DNA and identify pathogenic variants within genes or broader genomic regions.

337. Q: What is whole-exome sequencing?

A: Sequencing of the protein-coding regions (exons) of most genes.

It is useful when a patient has an unexplained suspected genetic disorder without a clear single-gene diagnosis.

338. Q: What is whole-genome sequencing?

A: Sequencing of essentially the entire genome, including coding and noncoding regions.

339. Q: What test is generally preferred when Down syndrome is suspected immediately after birth?

A: Chromosomal analysis/karyotype can confirm trisomy 21 and determine whether it resulted from nondisjunction, mosaicism, or a translocation.

340. Q: Why is determining the mechanism of Down syndrome important for the family?

A: Translocation Down syndrome may have a significantly different recurrence risk than standard meiotic nondisjunction, making parental karyotyping and genetic counseling important.

Prenatal Genetic Diagnosis

341. Q: What is chorionic villus sampling (CVS)?

A: An invasive prenatal diagnostic procedure that obtains placental chorionic villi for genetic testing.

342. Q: When is chorionic villus sampling generally performed?

A: Approximately 10–13 weeks of gestation.

343. Q: What is the major advantage of CVS compared with amniocentesis?

A: It provides earlier definitive prenatal genetic diagnosis.

344. Q: What is amniocentesis?

A: An invasive procedure in which amniotic fluid is sampled for fetal genetic and other diagnostic testing.

345. Q: When is amniocentesis typically performed for genetic diagnosis?

A: Generally after 15 weeks of gestation, commonly around 15–20 weeks.

346. Q: What is the major difference between CVS and amniocentesis?

A:

CVS:

Chorionic villi

→ earlier diagnosis.

Amniocentesis:

Amniotic fluid

→ later diagnosis but provides fetal cells and other analytes.

347. Q: What is noninvasive prenatal testing (NIPT)?

A: Maternal blood testing that analyzes cell-free fetal DNA, which is predominantly derived from the placenta, to screen for common fetal aneuploidies.

348. Q: Is NIPT a diagnostic or screening test?

A: Screening test.

A positive NIPT result should generally be followed by diagnostic testing such as CVS or amniocentesis before irreversible decisions are made.

349. Q: What is the difference between a congenital anomaly and a genetic disorder?

A: A congenital anomaly is a structural or functional abnormality present at birth, whereas a genetic disorder results from a pathogenic change in genetic material.

They can overlap, but not every congenital anomaly is genetic.

350. Q: A newborn has multiple congenital anomalies, developmental abnormalities, and an unclear diagnosis despite routine testing. What is an appropriate next genetic investigation?

A: If a chromosomal microarray is nondiagnostic and a genetic etiology remains strongly suspected, exome sequencing or genome sequencing may be appropriate, often guided by genetics specialists.

=============================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Infectious Disease in Children

1. Q: What is the most common cause of acute otitis media (AOM) in children?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a leading bacterial cause of AOM. Other important pathogens include Haemophilus influenzae and Moraxella catarrhalis.

2. Q: What clinical findings support a diagnosis of acute otitis media?

A: AOM is suggested by moderate-to-severe bulging of the tympanic membrane, new-onset otorrhea not caused by otitis externa, or mild tympanic-membrane bulging with recent ear pain/intense erythema.

3. Q: What is the preferred first-line antibiotic for uncomplicated acute otitis media when antibiotics are indicated?

A: High-dose amoxicillin is first-line in most children.

Typical dosing:

80−90 mg/kg/day

divided into 2 doses.

4. Q: When should amoxicillin-clavulanate be used instead of amoxicillin for acute otitis media?

A: Consider amoxicillin-clavulanate when there is:

Amoxicillin use within the previous 30 days
Concurrent purulent conjunctivitis
Treatment failure with amoxicillin

5. Q: What organism is strongly associated with acute otitis media accompanied by purulent conjunctivitis?

A: Nontypeable Haemophilus influenzae.

6. Q: What is otitis media with effusion?

A: Middle-ear fluid without signs or symptoms of acute infection.

It commonly follows an episode of AOM or occurs with eustachian-tube dysfunction.

7. Q: Should antibiotics routinely be given for otitis media with effusion?

A: No. Most cases resolve spontaneously, so routine antibiotic therapy is not indicated.

8. Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of acute bacterial sinusitis in children?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is an important cause; nontypeable H. influenzae is also common.

9. Q: What clinical course suggests acute bacterial sinusitis rather than an uncomplicated viral URI?

A: Any of the following:

Persistent nasal discharge or daytime cough >10 days without improvement
Severe symptoms with high fever and purulent nasal discharge for ≥3 consecutive days
Worsening symptoms after initial improvement ("double sickening")

10. Q: What is the first-line treatment for uncomplicated acute bacterial sinusitis in children?

A: Amoxicillin-clavulanate is commonly recommended as first-line therapy.

Streptococcal Pharyngitis

11. Q: What organism causes classic streptococcal pharyngitis?

A: Group A Streptococcus (GAS), Streptococcus pyogenes.

12. Q: What age group is most commonly affected by group A streptococcal pharyngitis?

A: School-aged children, particularly approximately 5–15 years.

13. Q: What clinical findings favor GAS pharyngitis?

A:

Sudden sore throat
Fever
Tonsillar exudates
Tender anterior cervical lymphadenopathy
Palatal petechiae
Absence of cough

14. Q: Which finding strongly favors a viral cause rather than GAS pharyngitis?

A: Cough, rhinorrhea, hoarseness, conjunctivitis, or oral ulcers strongly suggest a viral infection.

15. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for suspected GAS pharyngitis in children?

A: Rapid antigen detection testing (RADT) followed by throat culture if the RADT is negative in children/adolescents when clinically indicated.

16. Q: Why is treatment of GAS pharyngitis important?

A: Antibiotic therapy reduces symptom duration modestly, decreases transmission, and most importantly reduces the risk of acute rheumatic fever.

17. Q: What is the first-line treatment for confirmed GAS pharyngitis?

A: Penicillin or amoxicillin.

18. Q: What is the classic rash associated with GAS infection?

A: Scarlet fever, characterized by a diffuse, fine, rough "sandpaper" rash caused by streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins.

19. Q: What is the typical distribution of the scarlet fever rash?

A: It often begins on the trunk and spreads outward, with prominent involvement of skin folds.

20. Q: What is Pastia lines?

A: Linear areas of increased erythema in skin folds, particularly in the antecubital and inguinal regions, seen with scarlet fever.

Pneumonia

21. Q: What is the most common cause of bacterial community-acquired pneumonia in children?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

22. Q: What is the most common cause of pneumonia in infants and young children overall?

A: Viruses are the most common causes, particularly respiratory viruses.

23. Q: Which pathogen should be suspected in a school-aged child with pneumonia and a prominent dry cough?

A: Mycoplasma pneumoniae.

24. Q: What is a classic clinical clue for Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection?

A: Gradually developing respiratory symptoms with a persistent dry cough, often with relatively mild findings on physical examination compared with the degree of cough.

25. Q: What antibiotic class is commonly used for Mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia in children?

A: A macrolide, such as azithromycin, may be used when treatment is indicated.

26. Q: Why are β-lactam antibiotics ineffective against Mycoplasma pneumoniae?

A: Mycoplasma lacks a cell wall, so β-lactam antibiotics have no target.

27. Q: What organism is an important cause of severe pneumonia in an unimmunized child?

A: Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) can cause severe invasive pneumonia, although routine Hib vaccination has dramatically reduced disease.

28. Q: What pathogen should be considered in an infant with pneumonia, conjunctivitis, and a history of exposure during delivery?

A: Chlamydia trachomatis.

29. Q: What is the classic respiratory presentation of neonatal Chlamydia trachomatis infection?

A: Afebrile pneumonia with a repetitive staccato cough, usually occurring several weeks after birth.

30. Q: What ocular infection can occur in neonates exposed to Chlamydia trachomatis?

A: Conjunctivitis, typically developing during the first several weeks of life.

Pertussis

31. Q: What organism causes pertussis?

A: Bordetella pertussis, a gram-negative coccobacillus.

32. Q: What are the three classic stages of pertussis?

A:

Catarrhal stage — rhinorrhea and mild cough
Paroxysmal stage — severe coughing fits
Convalescent stage — gradual resolution of cough

33. Q: What is the characteristic cough of pertussis?

A: Recurrent paroxysms of coughing, often followed by an inspiratory "whoop."

34. Q: Why may infants with pertussis not have a classic whoop?

A: Young infants may instead develop apnea, cyanosis, poor feeding, or respiratory distress.

35. Q: What hematologic finding is classically associated with pertussis?

A: Marked lymphocytosis, particularly in infants and young children.

36. Q: What toxin is primarily responsible for the lymphocytosis of pertussis?

A: Pertussis toxin, which interferes with G-protein signaling and contributes to lymphocytosis.

37. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for pertussis during the early symptomatic period?

A: Nasopharyngeal PCR is commonly used because it provides rapid and sensitive detection.

38. Q: What is the preferred antibiotic for treatment of pertussis?

A: A macrolide, typically azithromycin, is preferred.

39. Q: Why is antibiotic therapy given for pertussis even though it may not rapidly resolve the cough?

A: It helps reduce transmission and is most effective when started early.

40. Q: What is the most important severe complication of pertussis in young infants?

A: Apnea and respiratory compromise are major complications; severe disease can be life-threatening.

Diphtheria

41. Q: What organism causes diphtheria?

A: Corynebacterium diphtheriae, a gram-positive bacillus.

42. Q: What is the characteristic oropharyngeal finding in respiratory diphtheria?

A: A thick, adherent gray pseudomembrane over the tonsils/pharynx.

43. Q: Why should the pseudomembrane of diphtheria not be forcibly removed?

A: Removal can cause bleeding because the membrane is firmly attached to inflamed tissue.

44. Q: What toxin causes the systemic manifestations of diphtheria?

A: Diphtheria toxin, which inhibits protein synthesis by ADP-ribosylating elongation factor-2 (EF-2).

45. Q: What major cardiac complication can occur with diphtheria?

A: Myocarditis, which can cause conduction abnormalities and arrhythmias.

46. Q: What neurologic complication can occur after diphtheria?

A: Peripheral neuropathy, resulting from toxin-mediated nerve injury.

47. Q: What is the major preventive measure against diphtheria?

A: Diphtheria toxoid vaccination, administered as part of combination vaccines such as DTaP/Tdap.

Tetanus

48. Q: What organism causes tetanus?

A: Clostridium tetani, an anaerobic, gram-positive, spore-forming bacillus.

49. Q: What toxin causes the manifestations of tetanus?

A: Tetanospasmin, which blocks release of inhibitory neurotransmitters, particularly GABA and glycine, from inhibitory interneurons.

50. Q: What is the classic clinical presentation of generalized tetanus?

A: Trismus (lockjaw), risus sardonicus, generalized muscle rigidity, and painful muscle spasms, often triggered by external stimuli.

51. Q: What are the most important causes of bacterial meningitis in children?

A: The major pathogens depend on age and immunization status. Important causes include:

Neisseria meningitidis
Streptococcus pneumoniae
Haemophilus influenzae type b in inadequately immunized children
Group B Streptococcus and gram-negative organisms in neonates

52. Q: What organism is an important cause of bacterial meningitis in adolescents?

A: Neisseria meningitidis.

53. Q: What clinical features suggest meningitis in an older child?

A:

Fever
Headache
Neck stiffness
Photophobia
Altered mental status
Nausea/vomiting

The classic triad is fever, neck stiffness, and altered mental status, although not all patients have all three.

54. Q: What organism causes meningococcal meningitis?

A: Neisseria meningitidis, a gram-negative diplococcus.

55. Q: What skin finding strongly suggests meningococcemia?

A: A rapidly progressive petechial or purpuric, nonblanching rash.

56. Q: What severe complication can occur in fulminant meningococcemia?

A: Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) with septic shock.

57. Q: What adrenal complication is classically associated with fulminant meningococcemia?

A: Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome — bilateral adrenal hemorrhage causing acute adrenal insufficiency.

58. Q: What is the initial treatment for suspected bacterial meningitis in a seriously ill child?

A: Empiric IV antibiotics should be started immediately after obtaining blood cultures when this does not cause a clinically important delay.

The exact regimen depends on age and risk factors.

59. Q: Why should treatment not be delayed while waiting for lumbar puncture in a critically ill child?

A: Delaying antibiotics in suspected bacterial meningitis can worsen outcomes. Stabilization and prompt empiric antimicrobial therapy take priority.

60. Q: When should lumbar puncture be delayed in a child with suspected meningitis?

A: When there are contraindications such as:

Signs of impending cerebral herniation
Significant hemodynamic or respiratory instability
Certain focal neurologic findings or markedly altered consciousness suggesting increased intracranial pressure

Antibiotics should not be delayed for an unsafe LP.

61. Q: What CSF pattern is typical of bacterial meningitis?

A:

WBC↑(neutrophils)
Protein↑
Glucose↓

62. Q: Why does CSF glucose decrease during bacterial meningitis?

A: Glucose is consumed by bacteria and inflammatory cells, and transport across the blood-CSF barrier can also be impaired.

63. Q: What CSF pattern is typical of viral meningitis?

A:

WBC↑(lymphocyte predominant)
Protein mildly↑
Glucose usually normal

64. Q: What is the most common overall cause of viral meningitis in children?

A: Enteroviruses, particularly coxsackieviruses and echoviruses.

65. Q: What is the most common cause of encephalitis in children?

A: Viruses are the predominant causes; HSV is an especially important treatable cause of severe encephalitis.

66. Q: What clinical feature distinguishes encephalitis from uncomplicated meningitis?

A: Altered mental status or brain dysfunction, such as confusion, personality change, seizures, or focal neurologic deficits, is prominent in encephalitis.

67. Q: What virus should be urgently considered in a child with severe encephalitis and temporal-lobe abnormalities?

A: Herpes simplex virus (HSV).

68. Q: What is the empiric antiviral treatment for suspected HSV encephalitis?

A: IV acyclovir should be started promptly when HSV encephalitis is suspected.

69. Q: What MRI finding is classically associated with HSV encephalitis?

A: Abnormalities involving the temporal lobes, often with hemorrhagic features.

70. Q: What CSF finding may suggest HSV encephalitis?

A: Lymphocytic pleocytosis with elevated RBCs can occur because HSV encephalitis may cause hemorrhagic necrosis.

Pediatric Sepsis

71. Q: What is sepsis in a child?

A: A life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated host response to infection.

72. Q: What are important clinical signs of septic shock in children?

A: Possible findings include:

Altered mental status
Tachycardia
Abnormal capillary refill
Hypotension
Oliguria
Abnormal peripheral temperature
Elevated or depressed respiratory rate

73. Q: Why can hypotension be a late sign of pediatric septic shock?

A: Children can maintain blood pressure through increased systemic vascular resistance and compensatory tachycardia until cardiovascular compensation fails.

74. Q: What is the initial fluid strategy for a child with septic shock?

A: Rapid IV/IO isotonic crystalloid boluses with frequent reassessment, while avoiding fluid overload, especially in settings where cardiac or renal dysfunction is present.

75. Q: What should be done if septic shock persists despite appropriate fluid resuscitation?

A: Start vasoactive support promptly rather than continuing unlimited fluid administration.

76. Q: What is the preferred route for emergency vascular access when IV access cannot rapidly be obtained in a critically ill child?

A: Intraosseous (IO) access.

77. Q: Why is intraosseous access useful in pediatric emergencies?

A: The medullary cavity provides a rapid route for fluids, blood products, and medications when peripheral IV access is difficult.

78. Q: What laboratory finding may occur in severe bacterial sepsis?

A: Possible findings include:

Leukocytosis or leukopenia
Elevated inflammatory markers
Metabolic acidosis
Elevated lactate
Thrombocytopenia
Coagulopathy

79. Q: What does an elevated serum lactate suggest in a child with shock?

A: It suggests impaired tissue perfusion and/or increased anaerobic metabolism, although lactate is not specific for sepsis.

80. Q: What is an important principle when treating suspected pediatric septic shock?

A: Do not wait for culture results before starting appropriate empiric antibiotics when bacterial sepsis is strongly suspected.

Neonatal Infections

81. Q: What are the major pathogens causing early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Important organisms include:

Group B Streptococcus
Escherichia coli
Other gram-negative enteric organisms

82. Q: How does a neonate commonly acquire early-onset group B streptococcal infection?

A: Through vertical transmission from a colonized mother during labor or delivery.

83. Q: What are important risk factors for neonatal group B streptococcal disease?

A: Examples include:

Maternal GBS colonization
Prolonged rupture of membranes
Maternal fever during labor
Prematurity
Previous infant with invasive GBS disease

84. Q: What clinical manifestations can occur with early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A:

Respiratory distress
Temperature instability
Poor feeding
Lethargy
Apnea
Cardiovascular instability

85. Q: What is a common empiric antibiotic regimen for suspected early-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Ampicillin plus gentamicin is a classic empiric regimen, adjusted according to local resistance patterns and the clinical situation.

86. Q: Why is ampicillin included in empiric neonatal sepsis therapy?

A: It provides coverage against important neonatal pathogens including GBS and Listeria monocytogenes.

87. Q: What organism is particularly important in late-onset neonatal meningitis?

A: Group B Streptococcus remains an important cause; gram-negative organisms and other pathogens also occur depending on the clinical setting.

88. Q: What is a major risk factor for late-onset neonatal sepsis?

A: Prematurity and prolonged hospitalization, particularly with invasive devices.

89. Q: What congenital infection is classically associated with chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus, and intracranial calcifications?

A: Congenital toxoplasmosis.

90. Q: What is the classic intracranial calcification pattern in congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Diffuse intracranial calcifications, often involving the brain parenchyma.

91. Q: What is the classic triad of congenital toxoplasmosis?

A:

Chorioretinitis+Hydrocephalus+Intracranial calcifications

92. Q: What organism causes congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Toxoplasma gondii, an intracellular protozoan parasite.

93. Q: How can a pregnant woman acquire Toxoplasma gondii?

A: Important sources include:

Undercooked meat
Cat feces/contaminated soil
Contaminated food or water

94. Q: What congenital infection is associated with periventricular calcifications and sensorineural hearing loss?

A: Congenital cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection.

95. Q: What is a characteristic neuroimaging finding in congenital CMV?

A: Periventricular calcifications.

96. Q: What is the most common long-term neurologic complication of congenital CMV?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss.

97. Q: What skin finding can occur in congenital CMV infection?

A: Petechiae/purpura, sometimes described as a "blueberry muffin" appearance due to extramedullary hematopoiesis.

98. Q: What congenital infection classically causes cataracts, sensorineural deafness, and congenital heart disease?

A: Congenital rubella syndrome.

99. Q: What cardiac lesion is classically associated with congenital rubella?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA), particularly when associated with pulmonary artery stenosis.

100. Q: What is the classic triad of congenital rubella syndrome?

A:

Cataracts+Sensorineural deafness+PDA

Additional findings can include microcephaly, developmental impairment, and "blueberry muffin" lesions.

101. Q: What congenital infection is classically associated with a triad of sensorineural hearing loss, cataracts, and PDA?

A: Congenital rubella syndrome. This was covered previously; for the next concepts, focus on its additional high-yield manifestations.

102. Q: What fetal complication is particularly associated with maternal primary rubella infection early in pregnancy?

A: Multiple congenital abnormalities, with the risk greatest when maternal infection occurs during the first trimester.

103. Q: What is the most important preventive measure against congenital rubella infection?

A: MMR vaccination before pregnancy. The live attenuated MMR vaccine is contraindicated during pregnancy.

104. Q: Why is MMR vaccine contraindicated during pregnancy?

A: It is a live attenuated vaccine, so it is avoided during pregnancy as a precaution against fetal infection.

105. Q: What congenital infection can cause hydrops fetalis through severe fetal anemia?

A: Parvovirus B19.

106. Q: How does Parvovirus B19 cause fetal anemia?

A: It infects erythroid precursor cells in the bone marrow, temporarily suppressing erythropoiesis.

107. Q: What is the characteristic fetal complication of severe congenital Parvovirus B19 infection?

A: Nonimmune hydrops fetalis due to severe fetal anemia and high-output cardiac failure.

108. Q: What is the characteristic rash of Parvovirus B19 infection in children?

A: "Slapped-cheek" facial erythema, followed by a lacy, reticular rash on the trunk and extremities.

109. Q: Why is Parvovirus B19 particularly dangerous in children with sickle cell disease?

A: It can cause an aplastic crisis by temporarily shutting down erythropoiesis.

110. Q: What congenital infection can cause a "blueberry muffin" rash, hepatosplenomegaly, and bone abnormalities?

A: Congenital syphilis.

111. Q: What organism causes syphilis?

A: Treponema pallidum, a spirochete.

112. Q: What are important early manifestations of congenital syphilis?

A:

Rhinitis ("snuffles")
Rash
Hepatosplenomegaly
Jaundice
Lymphadenopathy
Bone abnormalities

113. Q: What skeletal finding is characteristic of congenital syphilis?

A: Osteochondritis/periostitis, which can cause painful pseudoparalysis of an extremity.

114. Q: What classic dental abnormality can occur in late congenital syphilis?

A: Hutchinson teeth — notched, widely spaced permanent incisors.

115. Q: What eye finding is classically associated with late congenital syphilis?

A: Interstitial keratitis.

116. Q: What hearing abnormality can occur in late congenital syphilis?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss.

Neonatal HSV

117. Q: How is neonatal herpes simplex virus infection most commonly acquired?

A: Through contact with infected genital secretions during vaginal delivery.

118. Q: What maternal factor increases the risk of neonatal HSV transmission?

A: Primary maternal genital HSV infection near the time of delivery carries a particularly high transmission risk.

119. Q: What are the three major clinical forms of neonatal HSV infection?

A:

Skin, eye, and mouth disease
Central nervous system disease
Disseminated disease

120. Q: When does neonatal HSV typically present?

A: Usually during the first several weeks of life, with timing depending on the disease manifestation.

121. Q: What skin finding should raise suspicion for neonatal HSV?

A: Grouped vesicular lesions on the skin, eyes, or mouth.

122. Q: Can neonatal HSV occur without skin lesions?

A: Yes. CNS or disseminated neonatal HSV can occur without obvious vesicular lesions.

123. Q: What is the treatment for suspected neonatal HSV infection?

A: IV acyclovir should be started promptly when neonatal HSV is suspected.

124. Q: What is a major complication of neonatal HSV encephalitis?

A: Seizures and permanent neurologic impairment, including developmental delay and cognitive deficits.

Hepatitis & HIV

125. Q: What is the major route of perinatal hepatitis B transmission?

A: Perinatal exposure to infected maternal blood and genital secretions during delivery.

126. Q: What should a newborn receive if the mother is positive for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg)?

A: As soon as possible after birth:

Hepatitis B vaccine
Hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG)

at separate injection sites.

127. Q: Why is prevention of perinatal hepatitis B especially important?

A: Infants who acquire HBV perinatally have a very high risk of developing chronic infection, which can later lead to cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma.

128. Q: Which hepatitis B marker indicates current infection?

A: HBsAg indicates current HBV infection.

129. Q: Which hepatitis B antibody indicates immunity from vaccination?

A: Anti-HBs.

130. Q: What does hepatitis B core IgM antibody indicate?

A: Acute or recently acquired HBV infection.

131. Q: What is the significance of HBeAg positivity?

A: It generally indicates active viral replication and increased infectivity.

132. Q: What is the major concern regarding untreated HIV infection in infants?

A: Progressive immune deficiency with opportunistic infections, developmental problems, and failure to thrive.

133. Q: How is HIV transmitted from mother to child?

A: Transmission can occur:

In utero
During labor/delivery
Through breastfeeding

134. Q: What is the most important strategy for reducing perinatal HIV transmission?

A: Effective maternal antiretroviral therapy and appropriate infant prophylaxis, with delivery and feeding management based on maternal viral load and current guidelines.

135. Q: Why is standard antibody testing unreliable for diagnosing HIV in young infants?

A: Maternal IgG antibodies cross the placenta and can remain in the infant for many months.

136. Q: What type of test is used to diagnose HIV infection in an exposed infant?

A: Virologic nucleic-acid testing, such as HIV RNA or DNA testing, rather than relying on antibody testing alone.

Tuberculosis

137. Q: What organism causes tuberculosis?

A: Mycobacterium tuberculosis, an acid-fast bacillus.

138. Q: How is tuberculosis most commonly transmitted?

A: By airborne respiratory droplets/aerosols from an infectious person with pulmonary or laryngeal TB.

139. Q: What is the difference between latent TB infection and active TB disease?

A:

Latent TB:

Immune response to TB
No clinical disease
Not contagious

Active TB:

Clinical/radiographic disease
May be contagious if pulmonary/laryngeal

140. Q: What test detects immune sensitization to Mycobacterium tuberculosis using a skin reaction?

A: Tuberculin skin test (TST/PPD).

141. Q: What is interferon-γ release assay (IGRA)?

A: A blood test measuring T-cell release of interferon-γ in response to TB-specific antigens.

142. Q: What is an important advantage of IGRA over the tuberculin skin test?

A: IGRA is not affected by prior BCG vaccination and requires only one patient visit for testing.

143. Q: What chest radiographic finding is classically associated with primary TB in children?

A: Hilar or mediastinal lymphadenopathy, often with a parenchymal focus.

144. Q: Why are young children at increased risk of severe TB complications?

A: Their immature immune systems increase the risk of disseminated TB and TB meningitis.

145. Q: What is the most serious extrapulmonary complication of pediatric TB?

A: Tuberculous meningitis.

146. Q: What CSF pattern is typical of tuberculous meningitis?

A: Typically:

Lymphocytes↑
Protein↑
Glucose↓
Measles & Mumps

147. Q: What virus causes measles?

A: Measles virus, a highly contagious paramyxovirus.

148. Q: What is the classic sequence of clinical findings in measles?

A:

Prodrome:

Fever
Cough
Coryza
Conjunctivitis

followed by a maculopapular rash beginning on the face/hairline and spreading downward.

149. Q: What oral finding is highly characteristic of measles?

A: Koplik spots — tiny bluish-white lesions on an erythematous background, typically on the buccal mucosa.

150. Q: What is the major vaccine used to prevent measles?

A: MMR vaccine — measles, mumps, and rubella — a live attenuated vaccine.

151. Q: What virus causes mumps?

A: Mumps virus, a paramyxovirus.

152. Q: What is the classic clinical manifestation of mumps?

A: Painful swelling of the parotid glands, often with fever, headache, and malaise.

153. Q: What finding helps distinguish mumps parotitis from bacterial parotitis?

A: Mumps typically causes bilateral or sequential parotid swelling, whereas bacterial parotitis is more often unilateral and associated with purulent drainage from Stensen duct.

154. Q: What major gonadal complication can occur in postpubertal males with mumps?

A: Orchitis, which causes painful testicular swelling and can rarely contribute to testicular atrophy and impaired fertility.

155. Q: What CNS complications can occur with mumps?

A: Aseptic meningitis and encephalitis.

156. Q: What other important complication is associated with mumps?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss, which may be permanent.

Varicella & Herpes Zoster

157. Q: What virus causes chickenpox (varicella)?

A: Varicella-zoster virus (VZV), a herpesvirus.

158. Q: What is the characteristic rash of varicella?
