A: A pruritic, generalized vesicular rash with lesions appearing in successive crops, so lesions are present at different stages simultaneously.

159. Q: Where does the varicella rash typically begin?

A: It often begins on the trunk and face and then spreads to other areas.

160. Q: What is a characteristic complication of varicella in children?

A: Secondary bacterial infection of skin lesions, commonly caused by Streptococcus pyogenes or Staphylococcus aureus.

161. Q: What serious neurologic complication can occur after varicella?

A: Acute cerebellar ataxia, particularly in children.

162. Q: What is the relationship between varicella and herpes zoster?

A: After primary varicella infection, VZV becomes latent in sensory dorsal-root or cranial nerve ganglia and may reactivate later as herpes zoster.

163. Q: What is the typical rash pattern of herpes zoster?

A: Painful grouped vesicles in a unilateral dermatomal distribution, usually not crossing the midline.

164. Q: Why can herpes zoster occur in a child?

A: It can occur after previous VZV infection, particularly in children with impaired cellular immunity or those infected very early in life.

165. Q: What is an important complication of ophthalmic-division VZV reactivation?

A: Herpes zoster ophthalmicus, which can cause keratitis and other ocular complications.

Poliomyelitis

166. Q: What virus causes poliomyelitis?

A: Poliovirus, an enterovirus.

167. Q: What is the usual route of poliovirus transmission?

A: Fecal-oral transmission.

168. Q: What neurologic structure is primarily damaged in paralytic poliomyelitis?

A: Anterior horn motor neurons of the spinal cord.

169. Q: What type of paralysis occurs in paralytic poliomyelitis?

A: Acute asymmetric flaccid paralysis with decreased reflexes.

170. Q: How does poliomyelitis differ from Guillain-Barré syndrome clinically?

A: Poliomyelitis classically causes asymmetric flaccid weakness, whereas Guillain-Barré syndrome typically causes symmetric ascending weakness.

171. Q: What respiratory complication can occur in severe paralytic poliomyelitis?

A: Respiratory muscle paralysis, potentially causing respiratory failure.

172. Q: What vaccine is used to prevent poliomyelitis in routine childhood immunization?

A: Inactivated poliovirus vaccine (IPV).

Haemophilus influenzae Type b

173. Q: What serious pediatric infections can Hib cause?

A: Particularly:

Epiglottitis
Meningitis
Bacteremia/sepsis
Pneumonia
Cellulitis

174. Q: What clinical finding strongly suggests bacterial epiglottitis?

A: A toxic child with:

High fever
Severe sore throat
Drooling
Dysphagia
Muffled voice
Tripod positioning
Respiratory distress

175. Q: What should be avoided when epiglottitis is suspected?

A: Do not forcefully examine the pharynx with a tongue depressor in an unstable child because this can precipitate complete airway obstruction.

176. Q: What is the most important immediate priority in severe epiglottitis?

A: Secure the airway in a controlled setting with experienced airway personnel.

177. Q: What imaging finding can be seen in epiglottitis?

A: A lateral neck radiograph may show the "thumbprint sign" from an enlarged epiglottis, although imaging should not delay airway management.

178. Q: What vaccine has dramatically reduced invasive Hib disease?

A: The Hib conjugate vaccine.

Pneumococcal Disease

179. Q: What encapsulated bacterium is a major cause of invasive bacterial disease in children?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

180. Q: What pediatric infections can Streptococcus pneumoniae cause?

A:

Otitis media
Sinusitis
Pneumonia
Meningitis
Bacteremia/sepsis

181. Q: Which children are particularly vulnerable to invasive pneumococcal infection?

A: Children with:

Asplenia
Sickle cell disease
Immunodeficiency
Certain chronic illnesses

182. Q: Why are children with functional asplenia at increased risk for pneumococcal sepsis?

A: The spleen is important for clearing encapsulated organisms from the bloodstream.

183. Q: What other encapsulated bacteria are particularly important in asplenic children?

A:

Streptococcus pneumoniae
Neisseria meningitidis
Haemophilus influenzae

184. Q: What vaccine is used routinely to prevent invasive pneumococcal disease in children?

A: Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (PCV).

Meningococcal Disease

185. Q: What organism causes meningococcal disease?

A: Neisseria meningitidis, an encapsulated gram-negative diplococcus.

186. Q: What is a classic presentation of meningococcemia?

A: Abrupt fever, toxic appearance, hypotension, and a rapidly developing petechial or purpuric rash.

187. Q: What is the mechanism of shock in fulminant meningococcemia?

A: Severe systemic inflammation triggered by lipooligosaccharide (LOS) endotoxin, leading to vasodilation, capillary leak, coagulopathy, and shock.

188. Q: What close contacts of a patient with invasive meningococcal disease require chemoprophylaxis?

A: Close contacts with significant exposure to oral/respiratory secretions, such as household contacts or those directly exposed to oral secretions.

189. Q: What drugs can be used for meningococcal chemoprophylaxis?

A: Common options include:

Rifampin
Ciprofloxacin when appropriate based on resistance and age/pregnancy considerations
Ceftriaxone

190. Q: What vaccines protect against meningococcal disease?

A: MenACWY vaccines protect against serogroups A, C, W, and Y; MenB vaccines protect against serogroup B.

Rotavirus & Viral Gastroenteritis

191. Q: What virus is a major cause of severe dehydrating gastroenteritis in infants and young children?

A: Rotavirus.

192. Q: How is rotavirus primarily transmitted?

A: Fecal-oral transmission.

193. Q: What is the major clinical concern in severe rotavirus gastroenteritis?

A: Dehydration, particularly in infants and young children.

194. Q: What is the main treatment for uncomplicated viral gastroenteritis in children?

A: Oral rehydration therapy with appropriate replacement of water and electrolytes.

195. Q: When is IV fluid therapy indicated for pediatric gastroenteritis?

A: When there is severe dehydration, shock, altered mental status, or inability to tolerate oral/enteral rehydration.

196. Q: What vaccine prevents rotavirus gastroenteritis?

A: Oral live attenuated rotavirus vaccine.

197. Q: Why are rotavirus vaccines administered at specific early ages?

A: They have strict upper age limits for initiation/completion because of the small but important risk of intussusception and because efficacy/safety data are age-specific.

Kawasaki Disease

198. Q: What is Kawasaki disease?

A: An acute systemic vasculitis of childhood, predominantly affecting medium-sized arteries, especially the coronary arteries.

199. Q: What are the principal clinical features of Kawasaki disease?

A: Fever for ≥5 days plus characteristic findings such as:

Bilateral nonexudative conjunctival injection
Oral mucosal changes
Polymorphous rash
Changes in the hands/feet
Cervical lymphadenopathy

200. Q: What is the most important cardiovascular complication of Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery aneurysm, which can lead to thrombosis, myocardial ischemia, or myocardial infarction.

201. Q: What is the first-line treatment for Kawasaki disease?

A: IVIG + aspirin.

IVIG is given promptly to reduce the risk of coronary artery complications.

202. Q: What is the standard initial IVIG dose for Kawasaki disease?

A:

IVIG 2 g/kg

given as a single infusion, typically with aspirin therapy.

203. Q: Why is aspirin used in Kawasaki disease?

A: It provides anti-inflammatory and antiplatelet effects, particularly important for reducing thrombosis risk when coronary artery abnormalities are present.

204. Q: What should be done if fever persists or recurs after initial IVIG treatment?

A: The child should be reassessed for IVIG-resistant Kawasaki disease and may require additional IVIG and/or additional immunomodulatory therapy such as corticosteroids or infliximab, depending on risk and clinical response.

205. Q: What echocardiographic abnormality is monitored in Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery dilation or aneurysm, along with ventricular function and other cardiac abnormalities.

206. Q: What long-term complication can occur after a large coronary artery aneurysm?

A: Coronary thrombosis and myocardial infarction.

Acute Rheumatic Fever

207. Q: What organism triggers acute rheumatic fever?

A: Group A Streptococcus (Streptococcus pyogenes) following pharyngeal infection.

208. Q: What is the underlying mechanism of acute rheumatic fever?

A: An immune-mediated reaction following GAS infection, involving antibodies and T cells that cross-react with host tissues.

209. Q: What criteria are used to diagnose acute rheumatic fever?

A: The Jones criteria, requiring evidence of preceding GAS infection plus:

2 major criteria, or
1 major + 2 minor criteria.

210. Q: What are the major manifestations of acute rheumatic fever?

A:

Migratory polyarthritis
Carditis
Sydenham chorea
Erythema marginatum
Subcutaneous nodules

211. Q: What type of arthritis is characteristic of acute rheumatic fever?

A: Migratory polyarthritis, classically affecting large joints such as the knees, ankles, elbows, and wrists.

212. Q: What cardiac lesion is classically associated with rheumatic carditis?

A: Mitral regurgitation is the characteristic acute valvular lesion.

213. Q: What is Sydenham chorea?

A: A delayed neurologic manifestation of acute rheumatic fever characterized by involuntary, purposeless, irregular movements, often accompanied by emotional lability.

214. Q: What is the major long-term cardiac consequence of recurrent rheumatic fever?

A: Chronic rheumatic heart disease, particularly mitral stenosis.

215. Q: How is secondary prevention of rheumatic fever achieved?

A: Long-term antibiotic prophylaxis, usually with periodic intramuscular benzathine penicillin G or an appropriate oral alternative.

Skin & Soft-Tissue Infections

216. Q: What is impetigo?

A: A superficial bacterial skin infection, usually caused by Staphylococcus aureus or GAS.

217. Q: What is the characteristic lesion of nonbullous impetigo?

A: Honey-colored crusted erosions, commonly around the nose and mouth.

218. Q: What organism commonly causes bullous impetigo?

A: Staphylococcus aureus producing exfoliative toxin.

219. Q: What is the characteristic lesion of bullous impetigo?

A: Flaccid, superficial bullae containing clear or yellow fluid.

220. Q: What is ecthyma?

A: A deeper form of impetigo involving the dermis and producing ulcerative lesions with thick crusts.

221. Q: What is cellulitis?

A: An infection of the deeper dermis and subcutaneous tissues, typically causing erythema, warmth, swelling, and tenderness.

222. Q: What is erysipelas?

A: A more superficial infection involving the upper dermis and superficial lymphatics, producing a well-demarcated, raised, bright-red plaque.

223. Q: What are common bacterial causes of cellulitis in children?

A: Streptococcus pyogenes and Staphylococcus aureus are major causes.

224. Q: What clinical finding suggests necrotizing fasciitis rather than uncomplicated cellulitis?

A: Severe pain out of proportion to the visible skin findings, rapidly progressive swelling, systemic toxicity, or skin anesthesia/bullae.

225. Q: What is the most important management principle for suspected necrotizing fasciitis?

A: Immediate surgical consultation and urgent surgical debridement, along with broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and supportive care.

Osteomyelitis & Septic Arthritis

226. Q: What is the most common cause of acute hematogenous osteomyelitis in children?

A: Staphylococcus aureus.

227. Q: Which bone is commonly affected by acute hematogenous osteomyelitis in children?

A: The metaphysis of long bones, particularly the femur and tibia.

228. Q: Why is the metaphysis particularly susceptible to hematogenous osteomyelitis?

A: The metaphyseal circulation contains slow-flowing vascular loops, facilitating bacterial deposition.

229. Q: What pathogen should be especially considered in osteomyelitis of children with sickle cell disease?

A: Salmonella species, although S. aureus remains an important cause.

230. Q: What imaging study is most sensitive for early osteomyelitis?

A: MRI is highly sensitive for detecting early bone and surrounding soft-tissue infection.

231. Q: What laboratory marker can be useful for monitoring response to osteomyelitis treatment?

A: C-reactive protein (CRP) is useful because it generally changes more rapidly than ESR.

232. Q: What is septic arthritis?

A: Infection of a synovial joint, usually caused by hematogenous bacterial spread.

233. Q: What is the most common cause of septic arthritis in children?

A: Staphylococcus aureus.

234. Q: What joint is commonly involved in pediatric septic arthritis?

A: The knee is common; the hip is also clinically important because infection can rapidly damage the joint.

235. Q: What is the definitive diagnostic procedure for suspected septic arthritis?

A: Arthrocentesis with synovial-fluid analysis and culture.

236. Q: What synovial-fluid findings support septic arthritis?

A: Typically:

WBC↑↑

with neutrophil predominance, along with positive Gram stain/culture in some cases.

237. Q: Why is septic arthritis of the hip considered an orthopedic emergency?

A: Increased intra-articular pressure can compromise blood supply to the femoral head, causing avascular necrosis and permanent joint damage.

Pediatric Infectious Diarrhea

238. Q: What is the most important initial treatment for most acute infectious diarrhea in children?

A: Oral rehydration therapy and continued age-appropriate nutrition.

239. Q: What stool finding suggests inflammatory/invasive bacterial diarrhea?

A: Blood and leukocytes in stool, particularly with fever and abdominal pain.

240. Q: What pathogen causes bloody diarrhea followed by hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS)?

A: Shiga toxin-producing Escherichia coli (STEC), especially E. coli O157:H7.

241. Q: What are the three major features of hemolytic uremic syndrome?

A:

Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia+Thrombocytopenia+Acute kidney injury

242. Q: What peripheral blood finding is characteristic of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia in HUS?

A: Schistocytes.

243. Q: Why should antibiotics generally be avoided in suspected STEC infection?

A: Antibiotics may increase Shiga toxin release and potentially increase the risk of HUS.

244. Q: Why should antimotility agents generally be avoided in suspected invasive bacterial diarrhea?

A: They can slow intestinal clearance of pathogens/toxins and potentially worsen complications.

245. Q: What organism is strongly associated with antibiotic-associated diarrhea and pseudomembranous colitis?

A: Clostridioides difficile.

246. Q: What is the major risk factor for C. difficile infection?

A: Recent antibiotic exposure, particularly broad-spectrum antibiotics.

Parasitic Infections

247. Q: What parasite commonly causes intense nocturnal perianal itching in school-aged children?

A: Enterobius vermicularis (pinworm).

248. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for Enterobius vermicularis?

A: The morning "tape test", performed before bathing or defecation.

249. Q: What parasite commonly causes foul-smelling, greasy diarrhea after exposure to contaminated water?

A: Giardia duodenalis (Giardia lamblia).

250. Q: What is the characteristic stool description in giardiasis?

A: Foul-smelling, greasy, nonbloody diarrhea due to malabsorption.

251. Q: What is the first-line treatment for symptomatic giardiasis?

A: Tinidazole or metronidazole are commonly used. Treatment should be accompanied by attention to hydration and prevention of reinfection.

252. Q: How is Giardia commonly acquired?

A: By ingestion of cysts, particularly through contaminated water, food, or person-to-person transmission.

253. Q: What parasite commonly causes abdominal pain, diarrhea, and growth impairment in children living in areas with poor sanitation?

A: Ascaris lumbricoides.

254. Q: How is Ascaris infection acquired?

A: By ingestion of embryonated eggs from fecally contaminated soil or food.

255. Q: What pulmonary manifestation can occur during Ascaris migration?

A: A transient eosinophilic pneumonitis (Löffler syndrome) with cough and pulmonary infiltrates.

256. Q: What parasite can cause iron-deficiency anemia through chronic intestinal blood loss?

A: Hookworms, particularly Ancylostoma duodenale and Necator americanus.

257. Q: How are hookworms commonly acquired?

A: Larvae in contaminated soil can penetrate the skin, often through bare feet.

258. Q: What intestinal parasite is associated with abdominal pain, diarrhea, and rectal prolapse in heavily infected children?

A: Trichuris trichiura (whipworm).

259. Q: What parasite causes cystic lesions in tissues after ingestion of eggs from contaminated food?

A: Taenia solium, which can cause cysticercosis.

260. Q: What CNS manifestation is characteristic of neurocysticercosis?

A: Seizures are the most common clinical presentation.

EBV & Infectious Mononucleosis

261. Q: What virus causes infectious mononucleosis?

A: Epstein-Barr virus (EBV).

262. Q: What are the classic clinical features of infectious mononucleosis?

A:

Fever
Pharyngitis
Posterior cervical lymphadenopathy
Fatigue

263. Q: What peripheral blood finding is characteristic of EBV infection?

A: Atypical lymphocytes, representing reactive cytotoxic T cells.

264. Q: What complication should be considered in a child with infectious mononucleosis who develops severe left-upper-quadrant abdominal pain?

A: Splenic rupture.

265. Q: What activity restriction is important in infectious mononucleosis?

A: Avoid contact sports and activities with splenic-trauma risk during the period of increased splenic vulnerability.

266. Q: What antibiotic-associated rash is classically associated with EBV infection?

A: A diffuse maculopapular rash after administration of aminopenicillins, particularly ampicillin or amoxicillin.

267. Q: Why should amoxicillin not be routinely prescribed for uncomplicated infectious mononucleosis?

A: It can precipitate a characteristic diffuse maculopapular eruption, and antibiotics do not treat EBV itself.

268. Q: How can EBV infection cause airway compromise?

A: Marked tonsillar and lymphoid tissue enlargement can cause significant upper-airway obstruction in severe cases.

Enteroviral Diseases

269. Q: What viruses commonly cause hand-foot-and-mouth disease?

A: Coxsackievirus A viruses, especially A16; enterovirus 71 is another important cause.

270. Q: What is the characteristic distribution of lesions in hand-foot-and-mouth disease?

A: Vesicular lesions involving the:

Hands
Feet
Oral mucosa

271. Q: What is herpangina?

A: An enteroviral illness characterized by fever and painful vesicular lesions on the posterior oropharynx, particularly the soft palate and tonsillar pillars.

272. Q: Which enterovirus is particularly associated with severe neurologic disease in children?

A: Enterovirus 71 can cause severe neurologic complications, including brainstem encephalitis.

Roseola & Fifth Disease

273. Q: What virus most commonly causes roseola infantum?

A: Human herpesvirus 6 (HHV-6); HHV-7 can also cause a similar illness.

274. Q: What is the classic sequence of roseola?

A: Several days of high fever followed by a maculopapular rash as the fever resolves.

275. Q: Where does the roseola rash typically begin?

A: It usually begins on the trunk and spreads to the neck and extremities.

276. Q: What important complication can occur during the febrile phase of roseola?

A: Febrile seizures.

277. Q: What virus causes fifth disease?

A: Parvovirus B19.

278. Q: What is the classic facial finding of fifth disease?

A: "Slapped-cheek" erythema.

279. Q: What is the typical subsequent body rash in fifth disease?

A: A lacy, reticular rash involving the trunk and extremities.

Respiratory Viral Infections

280. Q: What virus is the most common cause of bronchiolitis?

A: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV).

281. Q: What age group is most commonly affected by bronchiolitis?

A: Infants and children younger than 2 years, especially young infants.

282. Q: What are typical clinical findings in bronchiolitis?

A:

Rhinorrhea
Cough
Tachypnea
Wheezing
Crackles
Increased work of breathing

283. Q: What is the mainstay of treatment for uncomplicated bronchiolitis?

A: Supportive care, including hydration and oxygen when indicated.

Routine antibiotics are not useful because bronchiolitis is usually viral.

284. Q: What oxygen saturation finding generally warrants supplemental oxygen in an infant with bronchiolitis?

A: Persistent hypoxemia, with treatment thresholds guided by clinical status and institutional protocols rather than a single isolated saturation value.

285. Q: What is croup?

A: A viral infection causing inflammation and narrowing of the subglottic upper airway, producing a characteristic barking cough.

286. Q: What is the classic presentation of croup?

A:

Barking cough
Hoarseness
Inspiratory stridor
Symptoms often worse at night

287. Q: What virus is the most common cause of croup?

A: Parainfluenza virus, particularly type 1.

288. Q: What is the first-line treatment for mild croup?

A: A single dose of corticosteroid, typically dexamethasone.

289. Q: What medication is added for moderate-to-severe croup with significant stridor or respiratory distress?

A: Nebulized epinephrine provides rapid temporary reduction in upper-airway edema.

290. Q: What radiographic finding can occur in croup?

A: Steeple sign on an AP neck radiograph due to subglottic narrowing.

Influenza

291. Q: What viruses cause influenza?

A: Influenza A and influenza B viruses are the major causes of seasonal influenza in humans.

292. Q: What clinical features distinguish influenza from an uncomplicated common cold?

A: Influenza often has an abrupt onset of:

Fever
Headache
Myalgias
Malaise
Dry cough

293. Q: What is the major antiviral treatment for influenza?

A: Oseltamivir, a neuraminidase inhibitor, is commonly used for children with severe, complicated, or high-risk influenza and is most effective when started early.

294. Q: Why is aspirin avoided in children with influenza?

A: Aspirin use during certain viral infections, especially influenza and varicella, is associated with Reye syndrome.

295. Q: What is Reye syndrome?

A: A rare but serious illness characterized by acute encephalopathy and hepatic dysfunction, classically associated with aspirin exposure during a viral illness.

296. Q: What is the major preventive strategy against seasonal influenza in children?

A: Annual influenza vaccination for eligible children.

Rabies & Animal Exposure

297. Q: What virus causes rabies?

A: Rabies lyssavirus, a bullet-shaped, enveloped RNA virus in the Rhabdoviridae family.

298. Q: How is rabies usually transmitted?

A: Through saliva from an infected mammal, most commonly via a bite.

299. Q: What is the classic neurologic manifestation of symptomatic rabies?

A: Progressive encephalitis with hydrophobia and aerophobia, often accompanied by agitation and autonomic dysfunction.

300. Q: What should be done after a potentially rabid animal exposure in an unvaccinated child?

A: Immediately perform thorough wound cleansing, then administer:

Rabies vaccine
Rabies immune globulin (RIG) infiltrated around the wound when indicated

Post-exposure prophylaxis should be started promptly when exposure risk warrants it.

301. Q: What organism causes Lyme disease?

A: Borrelia burgdorferi, a spirochete transmitted primarily by Ixodes ticks.

302. Q: What is the characteristic early skin lesion of Lyme disease?

A: Erythema migrans — an expanding erythematous lesion that develops at the site of the tick bite.

303. Q: Does erythema migrans always have a "bull's-eye" appearance?

A: No. The classic target appearance can occur, but many lesions are uniformly erythematous.

304. Q: What neurologic manifestation can occur during early disseminated Lyme disease?

A: Facial nerve palsy, sometimes bilateral, is a classic manifestation.

305. Q: What cardiac complication can occur in Lyme disease?

A: Lyme carditis, which classically causes atrioventricular (AV) conduction block.

306. Q: What is a typical late manifestation of Lyme disease?

A: Lyme arthritis, usually involving intermittent or persistent swelling of one or a few large joints, particularly the knee.

307. Q: What is the usual treatment for uncomplicated erythema migrans?

A: Doxycycline is commonly preferred in appropriate patients; amoxicillin or cefuroxime are alternatives when doxycycline is unsuitable.

308. Q: What is the major USMLE distinction between Lyme disease and Rocky Mountain spotted fever?

A: Lyme disease is associated with erythema migrans and later arthritis/facial palsy, whereas RMSF classically causes fever with a petechial rash involving the wrists/ankles that may spread centrally.

309. Q: What organism causes Rocky Mountain spotted fever (RMSF)?

A: Rickettsia rickettsii, transmitted primarily by Dermacentor ticks.

310. Q: What is the classic rash distribution in RMSF?

A: A petechial/maculopapular rash beginning on the wrists and ankles, often spreading toward the trunk and palms/soles.

311. Q: What is the preferred treatment for suspected RMSF in children?

A: Doxycycline, including in young children when RMSF is suspected.

Treatment should not be delayed while awaiting laboratory confirmation.

312. Q: Why should treatment for suspected RMSF not be delayed?

A: RMSF can progress rapidly to severe vasculitis, shock, neurologic disease, and death.

Bartonella & Animal-Associated Infections

313. Q: What organism causes cat-scratch disease?

A: Bartonella henselae.

314. Q: What is the classic presentation of cat-scratch disease?

A: A small papule at the inoculation site followed by regional lymphadenopathy, usually developing over days to weeks.

315. Q: What animal exposure is classically associated with Bartonella henselae?

A: Cats, particularly kittens.

316. Q: What serious systemic complications can occur with Bartonella infection?

A: In susceptible patients, complications can include hepatosplenic disease, neuroretinitis, encephalopathy, and endocarditis.

317. Q: What is a common treatment approach for uncomplicated cat-scratch disease?

A: Most immunocompetent children have a self-limited illness; azithromycin may be used when treatment is indicated.

Adolescent Sexually Transmitted Infections

318. Q: What organism is the most common bacterial cause of sexually transmitted infection in adolescents?

A: Chlamydia trachomatis is one of the most common bacterial STIs in adolescents.

319. Q: What is a common presentation of genital Chlamydia infection in adolescent females?

A: Often asymptomatic, but possible manifestations include cervicitis, dysuria, abnormal vaginal discharge, and pelvic inflammatory disease.

320. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic method for uncomplicated genital Chlamydia infection?

A: Nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT).

321. Q: What complication can untreated Chlamydia infection cause in adolescent females?

A: Pelvic inflammatory disease (PID), which can subsequently cause infertility, ectopic pregnancy, and chronic pelvic pain.

322. Q: What organism causes gonorrhea?

A: Neisseria gonorrhoeae, a gram-negative diplococcus.

323. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for gonorrhea?

A: NAAT is commonly used.

324. Q: What is a characteristic complication of disseminated gonococcal infection?

A: The classic triad is:

Tenosynovitis+Migratory polyarthralgia+Pustular skin lesions

325. Q: What is the recommended treatment for uncomplicated gonorrhea?

A: Ceftriaxone is the recommended first-line therapy, with management of possible concurrent Chlamydia infection when it has not been excluded.

326. Q: What virus causes genital herpes?

A: HSV-1 or HSV-2, with HSV-2 traditionally more strongly associated with genital disease.

327. Q: What is the characteristic lesion of genital herpes?

A: Painful grouped vesicles or ulcers on an erythematous base.

328. Q: Can genital herpes be cured with antiviral therapy?

A: No. Antivirals such as acyclovir, valacyclovir, and famciclovir suppress viral replication and symptoms but do not eradicate latent HSV.

Congenital Infection — Additional High-Yield Concepts

329. Q: What congenital infection is strongly associated with microcephaly, intracranial calcifications, and severe neurologic abnormalities after maternal infection?

A: Congenital Zika virus infection.

330. Q: What is the most characteristic congenital abnormality associated with Zika virus?

A: Severe microcephaly, often accompanied by other brain abnormalities.

331. Q: How is Zika virus primarily transmitted?

A: Through Aedes mosquito bites; sexual transmission can also occur.

332. Q: What congenital infection can cause limb abnormalities, eye disease, and neurologic abnormalities following maternal infection?

A: Congenital Zika infection can cause a spectrum of fetal abnormalities beyond microcephaly.

333. Q: What congenital infection is particularly associated with hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, thrombocytopenia, and "blueberry muffin" lesions?

A: Several congenital infections can produce this pattern, but CMV, rubella, and congenital syphilis are important considerations.

334. Q: What is the classic hearing-related complication of congenital CMV?

A: Sensorineural hearing loss, which may be progressive or delayed in onset.

335. Q: What is an important long-term neurologic complication of congenital toxoplasmosis?

A: Visual impairment from chorioretinitis, along with potential developmental and neurologic abnormalities.

336. Q: What maternal infection is associated with fetal hydrops and severe anemia rather than classic structural malformations?

A: Parvovirus B19 infection.

Infection in Immunocompromised Children

337. Q: What fungal infection commonly causes oral white plaques in immunocompromised children?

A: Oropharyngeal candidiasis (thrush) caused by Candida species.

338. Q: What is the characteristic appearance of oral candidiasis?

A: White, creamy plaques that can be scraped off, often leaving an erythematous surface underneath.

339. Q: What opportunistic infection should be suspected in an immunocompromised child with diffuse bilateral pulmonary infiltrates and hypoxemia?

A: Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP).

340. Q: What is a characteristic radiographic pattern of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia?

A: Usually bilateral diffuse/interstitial or ground-glass pulmonary opacities.

341. Q: What organism causes cryptococcosis in severely immunocompromised patients?

A: Cryptococcus neoformans.

342. Q: What CNS disease can Cryptococcus cause?

A: Subacute meningitis or meningoencephalitis, particularly in patients with severe cellular immunodeficiency.

Infection Control & Post-Exposure Management

343. Q: What is the most important initial step after a contaminated wound from an animal bite?

A: Immediate, thorough irrigation and cleansing of the wound with soap and water.

344. Q: What is an important indication for tetanus prophylaxis after an injury?

A: Tetanus vaccination status and wound characteristics determine whether a booster and/or tetanus immune globulin is needed.

345. Q: Which wounds are considered higher risk for tetanus?

A: Examples include:

Puncture wounds
Wounds contaminated with soil/feces/saliva
Devitalized tissue
Burns
Crush injuries

346. Q: What should be done after a significant exposure to a patient with measles in a susceptible child?

A: Post-exposure prophylaxis depends on timing and immune status; MMR vaccine can be given promptly to eligible susceptible individuals, while immunoglobulin is used for certain high-risk exposures.

347. Q: What is the key infection-control precaution for measles?

A: Airborne precautions because measles is highly contagious and can remain suspended in the air.

348. Q: What isolation precaution is appropriate for a child with infectious diarrhea caused by a pathogen transmitted by fecal-oral contact?

A: Contact precautions, in addition to standard precautions, when indicated by the pathogen and healthcare setting.

High-Yield Diagnostic Reasoning

349. Q: A child has fever, rash, hypotension, and rapidly progressive purpura. What life-threatening infectious diagnosis must be considered immediately?

A: Fulminant meningococcemia with septic shock and possible DIC.

This requires immediate empiric antimicrobial therapy and aggressive stabilization.

350. Q: A child develops fever, altered mental status, focal neurologic abnormalities, and seizures. What infectious diagnosis should be urgently considered and empirically treated?

A: Encephalitis, particularly HSV encephalitis.

Start IV acyclovir promptly while diagnostic testing, including CSF analysis/PCR when safe, is performed.

351. Q: What is the most important concern in a febrile infant younger than 28 days?

A: Serious bacterial infection (SBI) must be assumed until appropriately evaluated because neonates have immature immune defenses and may have few localizing signs.

352. Q: What serious bacterial infections should be considered in a febrile neonate?

A:

Bacteremia/sepsis
Meningitis
Urinary tract infection
Pneumonia
Osteomyelitis/septic arthritis

353. Q: Why can neonates with serious infections have a normal temperature?

A: Neonates may develop hypothermia or temperature instability rather than fever because thermoregulation and immune responses are immature.

354. Q: What clinical finding in a young infant with fever is particularly concerning for invasive bacterial infection?

A: Ill appearance, poor feeding, lethargy, apnea, respiratory distress, abnormal perfusion, or temperature instability.

355. Q: What is the most common serious bacterial infection in febrile infants?

A: Urinary tract infection is the most common SBI in many young febrile infants.

Pediatric Urinary Tract Infection

356. Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of pediatric urinary tract infection?

A: Escherichia coli.

357. Q: What risk factors predispose children to recurrent UTIs?

A:

Vesicoureteral reflux
Urinary obstruction
Neurogenic bladder
Constipation
Dysfunctional voiding
Female sex

358. Q: What symptoms suggest a UTI in an older child?

A:

Dysuria
Urinary frequency
Urgency
Suprapubic discomfort
Hematuria

Fever and flank pain suggest upper urinary tract involvement.

359. Q: What clinical findings suggest pyelonephritis rather than cystitis?

A: Fever, flank/CVA tenderness, vomiting, and systemic illness suggest pyelonephritis.

360. Q: What is the preferred method of obtaining urine for culture in a non-toilet-trained child when accurate diagnosis is important?

A: Catheterized urine or suprapubic aspiration provides a reliable specimen for culture.

361. Q: Why is a bagged urine specimen unreliable for diagnosing UTI?

A: It has a high rate of contamination, making positive cultures difficult to interpret.

362. Q: What complication can recurrent febrile UTIs cause in children with vesicoureteral reflux?

A: Renal scarring, potentially leading to hypertension and chronic kidney disease.

363. Q: What imaging study is commonly used to evaluate suspected vesicoureteral reflux?

A: Voiding cystourethrography (VCUG).

364. Q: What imaging study is useful for evaluating renal anatomy after certain pediatric UTIs?

A: Renal and bladder ultrasonography can evaluate structural abnormalities and hydronephrosis.

Congenital & Perinatal Infections — Additional Concepts

365. Q: What pathogen can cause congenital or perinatal infection with chorioretinitis, cataracts, or microcephaly?

A: Several congenital pathogens can cause ocular/CNS abnormalities; the specific pattern should guide diagnosis. CMV, Toxoplasma gondii, rubella, and Zika virus are important considerations.

366. Q: What is the most important fetal complication of untreated maternal primary CMV infection?

A: Congenital CMV infection, which can result in neurologic impairment, hearing loss, growth restriction, and intracranial abnormalities.

367. Q: What is the characteristic neuroimaging distinction between congenital CMV and congenital toxoplasmosis?

A:

CMV→Periventricular calcifications
Toxoplasmosis→More diffuse intracranial calcifications

368. Q: What congenital infection classically causes hydrocephalus?

A: Toxoplasmosis is classically associated with hydrocephalus.

369. Q: What congenital infection is strongly associated with sensorineural hearing loss even when the newborn initially appears well?

A: Congenital CMV.

370. Q: Why is congenital CMV particularly important even in asymptomatic newborns?

A: Some apparently asymptomatic infants later develop sensorineural hearing loss or neurodevelopmental problems.

Neonatal Conjunctivitis

371. Q: What organism causes neonatal ophthalmia that typically appears within the first 24 hours after birth and is associated with chemical irritation?

A: Chemical conjunctivitis, often caused by ocular prophylactic agents, typically appears very early and is not an infectious disease.

372. Q: What neonatal conjunctivitis typically presents 2–5 days after birth with copious purulent discharge?

A: Neisseria gonorrhoeae.

373. Q: Why is gonococcal ophthalmia neonatorum an emergency?

A: It can rapidly cause corneal ulceration, perforation, and blindness.

374. Q: What is the treatment for neonatal gonococcal ophthalmia?

A: Systemic ceftriaxone or cefotaxime, with evaluation and treatment of the mother and sexual partners as appropriate.

375. Q: What neonatal conjunctivitis typically presents several days to weeks after birth with mucopurulent discharge?

A: Chlamydia trachomatis conjunctivitis.

376. Q: Why is topical therapy alone inadequate for neonatal Chlamydia conjunctivitis?

A: The infection can involve the nasopharynx and respiratory tract, so systemic therapy is required.

Foodborne & Waterborne Infections

377. Q: What organism is classically associated with bloody diarrhea, fever, and tenesmus after contaminated food or water exposure?

A: Shigella species.

378. Q: What serious neurologic complication can occur with Shigella infection in children?

A: Seizures, particularly in young children.

379. Q: What organism is associated with watery diarrhea following ingestion of undercooked poultry?

A: Campylobacter jejuni is a major cause of bacterial gastroenteritis associated with poultry exposure.

380. Q: What important postinfectious neurologic complication is associated with Campylobacter infection?

A: Guillain-Barré syndrome.

381. Q: What organism is associated with diarrhea after eating undercooked beef and can cause HUS?

A: Shiga toxin-producing E. coli, especially E. coli O157:H7.

382. Q: What foodborne organism can cause fever, diarrhea, and invasive disease particularly in neonates and immunocompromised patients?

A: Listeria monocytogenes.

383. Q: What foods are classically associated with Listeria exposure?

A:

Unpasteurized dairy products
Soft cheeses made from unpasteurized milk
Refrigerated ready-to-eat foods
Certain processed meats

384. Q: Why is Listeria particularly important during pregnancy?

A: Maternal infection can cause placental infection, fetal loss, preterm birth, or neonatal invasive disease.

Botulism

385. Q: What organism causes infant botulism?

A: Clostridium botulinum.

386. Q: How do infants commonly acquire botulism?

A: By ingestion of C. botulinum spores, which can germinate in the infant intestine and produce toxin.

387. Q: What food should generally be avoided in infants younger than 12 months because of botulism risk?

A: Honey.

388. Q: What is the classic presentation of infant botulism?

A:

Constipation
Poor feeding
Weak cry
Hypotonia
Progressive descending weakness
Respiratory difficulty

389. Q: What is the neurologic mechanism of botulinum toxin?

A: It prevents acetylcholine release at presynaptic neuromuscular junctions, causing flaccid paralysis.

390. Q: What treatment is used for infant botulism?

A: Human botulism immune globulin (BIG-IV) should be administered promptly when infant botulism is suspected.

Diphtheria & Toxin-Mediated Disease

391. Q: What is the mechanism of diphtheria toxin?

A: It ADP-ribosylates elongation factor-2 (EF-2), inhibiting protein synthesis.

392. Q: What complication of diphtheria can cause delayed neurologic deficits?

A: Peripheral neuropathy, which can develop weeks after the acute infection.

393. Q: What treatment should be given for suspected respiratory diphtheria?

A: Diphtheria antitoxin plus appropriate antibiotics, along with respiratory isolation and public-health management.

394. Q: Why should treatment of suspected diphtheria begin before laboratory confirmation?

A: Disease progression and toxin-mediated complications can be severe, and treatment should not await definitive culture results.

Tetanus & Wound Management

395. Q: Why does tetanus infection not produce person-to-person transmission?

A: Clostridium tetani spores enter through contaminated wounds; tetanus is not transmitted between people.

396. Q: What type of muscle paralysis is produced by tetanus toxin?

A: Spastic paralysis due to inhibition of GABA and glycine release.

397. Q: How does botulism differ from tetanus?

A:

Botulism→Flaccid paralysis
Tetanus→Spastic paralysis

398. Q: What is an important wound-care measure for preventing tetanus?

A: Thorough wound cleaning and removal of devitalized tissue, combined with vaccination and tetanus immune globulin when indicated.

Clinical Integration

399. Q: A 2-year-old develops fever, dysuria, vomiting, and flank tenderness. What diagnosis should be suspected?

A: Acute pyelonephritis due to a urinary tract infection, most commonly caused by E. coli.

400. Q: A 4-month-old has constipation followed by poor feeding, weak cry, hypotonia, and descending weakness. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A: Infant botulism due to Clostridium botulinum toxin.

401. Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of community-acquired pneumonia in a previously healthy, fully immunized child?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae remains an important cause of typical bacterial pneumonia.

402. Q: What pathogen should be suspected in an adolescent with pneumonia, headache, low-grade fever, and a persistent dry cough?

A: Mycoplasma pneumoniae — a common cause of atypical pneumonia in school-aged children and adolescents.

403. Q: Why is Mycoplasma pneumoniae not treated with penicillin?

A: Mycoplasma lacks a cell wall, so β-lactam antibiotics are ineffective.

404. Q: What antibiotic class is commonly used for symptomatic Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection?

A: A macrolide, such as azithromycin, is commonly used when antibiotic treatment is indicated.

405. Q: What pathogen should be considered in a child with severe pneumonia following influenza?

A: Staphylococcus aureus, including MRSA, can cause severe secondary bacterial pneumonia after influenza.

406. Q: What complication should be suspected when a child with bacterial pneumonia develops persistent fever and a pleural effusion?

A: Parapneumonic effusion or empyema.

407. Q: What imaging modality is useful for characterizing a pleural effusion in a child with pneumonia?

A: Chest ultrasonography can identify and characterize pleural fluid and septations.

408. Q: What is empyema?

A: Infected purulent fluid within the pleural space, usually complicating bacterial pneumonia.

Pertussis

409. Q: What organism causes pertussis?

A: Bordetella pertussis, a gram-negative coccobacillus.

410. Q: What are the classic stages of pertussis?

A:

Catarrhal stage
Paroxysmal stage
Convalescent stage

411. Q: What characterizes the catarrhal stage of pertussis?

A: Rhinorrhea, mild cough, and low-grade fever, resembling a common upper-respiratory infection.

412. Q: What characterizes the paroxysmal stage?

A: Repeated paroxysms of severe coughing, often followed by an inspiratory "whoop" and/or vomiting.

413. Q: Why may infants with pertussis not develop the classic whoop?

A: Infants may instead present with apnea, cyanosis, bradycardia, or respiratory distress.

414. Q: What hematologic finding is classically associated with pertussis?

A: Marked lymphocytosis, particularly in infants and young children.

415. Q: What is the preferred antibiotic for treating pertussis?

A: Azithromycin or another appropriate macrolide.

416. Q: Does antibiotic therapy immediately eliminate the cough of pertussis?

A: No. Antibiotics reduce transmission and eradicate the organism, but the prolonged cough may persist because of airway injury and inflammation.

417. Q: What is the major cause of death from pertussis in young infants?

A: Apnea and respiratory failure, often complicated by severe pulmonary disease.

Diphtheria & Upper-Airway Disease

418. Q: What is the classic pharyngeal finding in respiratory diphtheria?

A: An adherent gray-white pseudomembrane over the pharynx or tonsils.

419. Q: Why should the pseudomembrane in diphtheria not be forcibly removed?

A: Removal can cause bleeding and potentially worsen airway compromise.

420. Q: What cardiac complication can occur with diphtheria toxin?

A: Myocarditis, which may cause arrhythmias and heart failure.

421. Q: What distinguishes diphtheria from uncomplicated viral pharyngitis?

A: Diphtheria may cause a firm adherent pseudomembrane, cervical lymphadenopathy, "bull neck," and systemic toxin-mediated complications.

Acute Otitis Media

422. Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of acute otitis media?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a major cause; Haemophilus influenzae and Moraxella catarrhalis are also important.

423. Q: What otoscopic finding strongly supports acute otitis media?

A: Moderate-to-severe bulging of the tympanic membrane or new-onset otorrhea not caused by otitis externa.

424. Q: Why is a simple erythematous tympanic membrane insufficient to diagnose acute otitis media?

A: Redness alone is nonspecific and can occur with crying, fever, or viral upper-respiratory infection.

425. Q: What is first-line antibiotic therapy for uncomplicated acute bacterial otitis media when antibiotics are indicated?

A: High-dose amoxicillin is generally first-line.

426. Q: When is amoxicillin-clavulanate preferred for acute otitis media?

A: Examples include recent amoxicillin exposure, concurrent purulent conjunctivitis, or treatment failure, because β-lactamase-producing organisms become more likely.

427. Q: What is a major complication of untreated acute otitis media involving the mastoid?

A: Acute mastoiditis.

428. Q: What physical finding suggests mastoiditis?

A: Postauricular erythema, swelling, and tenderness with protrusion of the auricle.

Acute Bacterial Sinusitis

429. Q: What clinical pattern supports acute bacterial sinusitis rather than a viral upper-respiratory infection?

A: One of the following:

Persistent nasal discharge or daytime cough for >10 days without improvement
Severe symptoms with high fever and purulent nasal discharge for several consecutive days
Worsening symptoms after initial improvement

430. Q: What bacteria commonly cause acute bacterial sinusitis in children?

A: S. pneumoniae, nontypeable H. influenzae, and M. catarrhalis.

431. Q: What orbital complication can result from bacterial sinusitis?

A: Orbital cellulitis or orbital abscess, particularly from ethmoid sinus infection.

432. Q: What finding suggests orbital cellulitis rather than uncomplicated preseptal cellulitis?

A: Pain with eye movement, ophthalmoplegia, proptosis, or decreased visual acuity suggests postseptal/orbital involvement.

433. Q: Why is orbital cellulitis an emergency?

A: Infection can spread to the orbit, cavernous sinus, or intracranial structures, threatening vision and life.

Meningitis — Clinical Reasoning

434. Q: What is the classic triad of meningitis?

A:

Fever
Neck stiffness
Altered mental status

However, not all three are necessarily present.

435. Q: What CSF pattern is typical of acute bacterial meningitis?

A:

Opening pressure↑
Neutrophils↑
Protein↑
Glucose↓

436. Q: What CSF pattern is more typical of viral meningitis?

A:

Lymphocytes↑
Protein mildly↑
Glucose usually normal

437. Q: What is the most important principle when bacterial meningitis is strongly suspected?

A: Do not delay empiric antibiotics while waiting for diagnostic testing if obtaining tests would significantly delay treatment.

438. Q: When should neuroimaging generally precede lumbar puncture?

A: When there are features suggesting increased intracranial pressure or a focal intracranial lesion, such as new focal neurologic deficits, papilledema, significantly impaired consciousness, or certain immunocompromised states.

439. Q: Why is dexamethasone sometimes administered before or with the first antibiotic dose in bacterial meningitis?

A: It can reduce inflammatory complications, with the strongest evidence for certain bacterial pathogens/settings.

Encephalitis

440. Q: What clinical features favor encephalitis over isolated meningitis?

A: Altered mental status, behavioral changes, seizures, and focal neurologic deficits suggest brain-parenchymal involvement.

441. Q: What virus is an important treatable cause of sporadic encephalitis in children?

A: Herpes simplex virus (HSV).

442. Q: What MRI finding is classically associated with HSV encephalitis?

A: Abnormalities involving the temporal lobes, often with edema and/or hemorrhagic changes.

443. Q: What CSF finding can occur in HSV encephalitis?

A: Lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated protein, and sometimes red blood cells due to hemorrhagic necrosis.

444. Q: What test confirms HSV encephalitis in most cases?

A: CSF HSV PCR, obtained when lumbar puncture is safe.

445. Q: What treatment should be started immediately when HSV encephalitis is suspected?

A: IV acyclovir.

Brain Abscess & Intracranial Complications

446. Q: What pediatric conditions can predispose to a brain abscess?

A:

Cyanotic congenital heart disease
Chronic otitis/mastoiditis
Sinusitis
Immunodeficiency
Head trauma/neurosurgery

447. Q: What imaging study is preferred for evaluating a suspected brain abscess?

A: Contrast-enhanced MRI of the brain is generally preferred.

448. Q: What classic MRI appearance can suggest a brain abscess?

A: A ring-enhancing lesion with surrounding edema, although ring enhancement has a broad differential.

Infection-Associated Neurologic Syndromes

449. Q: What postinfectious neurologic disorder causes rapidly progressive symmetric ascending weakness and areflexia?

A: Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS).

450. Q: What infection is a classic trigger of Guillain-Barré syndrome in children?

A: Campylobacter jejuni gastroenteritis is a classic trigger; other infections can also precede GBS.

451. Q: What is the most important initial management principle for a child with suspected septic shock?

A: Immediate stabilization and treatment: airway/breathing support, IV/IO access, appropriate fluid resuscitation, cultures when they do not delay therapy, and prompt empiric antibiotics.

452. Q: What clinical findings suggest septic shock in a child?

A: Altered mental status, abnormal perfusion, prolonged capillary refill, weak pulses, hypotension, tachycardia, oliguria, and/or respiratory distress.

453. Q: What is the preferred route for rapidly administering medications and fluids when IV access cannot be obtained promptly in a critically ill child?

A: Intraosseous (IO) access.

454. Q: What is an important difference between compensated and decompensated pediatric shock?

A: Children may maintain normal blood pressure despite significant shock through increased systemic vascular resistance and tachycardia. Hypotension is a late sign.

455. Q: What laboratory marker can help assess tissue hypoperfusion in septic shock?

A: Serum lactate, interpreted in the context of the clinical picture.

Fever Without a Source

456. Q: What is the most important bacterial source to evaluate in a young child with unexplained fever?

A: Urinary tract infection, particularly in infants and young children.

457. Q: Why has occult bacteremia become less common in appropriately vaccinated children?

A: Conjugate vaccines against organisms such as Hib and Streptococcus pneumoniae have substantially reduced invasive disease.

458. Q: What clinical feature makes an unexplained fever more concerning for serious infection?

A: Toxic appearance, including poor interaction, abnormal perfusion, lethargy, respiratory distress, or inability to feed.

Osteomyelitis — Advanced Concepts

459. Q: What organism should be considered in osteomyelitis after a puncture wound through a shoe?

A: Pseudomonas aeruginosa, particularly with puncture wounds involving footwear.

460. Q: What organism is associated with osteomyelitis after an animal bite?

A: Pasteurella multocida is an important consideration, along with other oral flora.

461. Q: What pathogen can cause osteomyelitis in adolescents after sexual exposure or in certain high-risk populations?

A: Neisseria gonorrhoeae can rarely cause disseminated infection involving bones or joints.

462. Q: What is the characteristic symptom of chronic osteomyelitis?

A: Persistent or recurrent localized bone pain, sometimes with drainage through a sinus tract.

Septic Arthritis — Advanced Concepts

463. Q: What organism should be considered in septic arthritis of a sexually active adolescent?

A: Neisseria gonorrhoeae, particularly when accompanied by rash or tenosynovitis.

464. Q: What organism should be considered in septic arthritis following a tick exposure?

A: Borrelia burgdorferi in an appropriate epidemiologic setting.

465. Q: What is the major distinction between transient synovitis and septic arthritis of the hip?

A: Septic arthritis usually causes more severe systemic illness, fever, inability to bear weight, and markedly inflammatory findings, whereas transient synovitis is generally milder.

466. Q: What is the definitive way to distinguish septic arthritis from transient synovitis?

A: Joint aspiration with synovial-fluid analysis and culture.

Bone & Joint Infection After Trauma

467. Q: A child develops fever and localized bone pain after a penetrating injury. What diagnosis should be considered?

A: Acute osteomyelitis, particularly if inflammatory markers are elevated and imaging supports bone infection.

468. Q: Why can plain radiographs be normal early in osteomyelitis?

A: Bony changes may take days to weeks to become radiographically apparent.

Viral Respiratory Infections

469. Q: What virus is strongly associated with severe bronchiolitis and apnea in young infants?

A: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV).

470. Q: What host factors increase the risk of severe RSV disease?

A:

Prematurity
Chronic lung disease
Significant congenital heart disease
Young age
Certain immunocompromising conditions

471. Q: What preventive antibody strategy is available for RSV in infants and young children?

A: Long-acting monoclonal antibody prophylaxis, such as nirsevimab, can provide passive protection to eligible infants according to current recommendations.

472. Q: What maternal strategy can reduce RSV hospitalization risk in young infants?

A: Maternal RSV vaccination during the recommended gestational window can provide passive antibody protection to the infant.

473. Q: What is the typical radiographic finding in viral bronchiolitis?

A: Imaging is usually unnecessary, but if obtained it may show hyperinflation and peribronchial thickening.

Viral Croup — Advanced Concepts

474. Q: What finding indicates severe croup requiring urgent treatment?

A: Stridor at rest, significant retractions, hypoxemia, agitation/lethargy, or impending respiratory failure.

475. Q: What should be considered when a child has recurrent or unusually severe croup?

A: An anatomic airway abnormality, foreign body, or other nonviral cause should be considered.

476. Q: How can bacterial tracheitis be distinguished from uncomplicated viral croup?

A: Bacterial tracheitis often presents with high fever, toxic appearance, worsening respiratory distress, and thick purulent airway secretions, frequently after a viral prodrome.

477. Q: What is the management priority in suspected bacterial tracheitis?

A: Airway stabilization and IV antibiotics, with early involvement of experienced airway personnel.

Respiratory Infection Differentials

478. Q: What clinical feature favors foreign-body aspiration over viral bronchiolitis?

A: Sudden onset of cough, wheezing, or respiratory distress, particularly with unilateral decreased breath sounds.

479. Q: What finding may suggest an aspirated foreign body?

A: Unilateral hyperinflation, focal atelectasis, or asymmetric breath sounds.

480. Q: What is the definitive diagnostic and therapeutic procedure for a suspected airway foreign body?

A: Bronchoscopy.

Gastrointestinal Infections

481. Q: What organism is associated with watery diarrhea following consumption of contaminated shellfish?

A: Vibrio species, depending on the exposure and geographic setting.

482. Q: What organism is classically associated with severe watery diarrhea after ingestion of contaminated water in endemic regions?

A: Vibrio cholerae.

483. Q: What is the major physiologic danger of cholera?

A: Massive isotonic fluid loss causing severe dehydration, hypovolemic shock, and electrolyte abnormalities.

484. Q: What is the cornerstone of treatment for cholera?

A: Rapid oral or IV fluid and electrolyte replacement; antibiotics may shorten illness in selected patients.

485. Q: What organism is associated with diarrhea after undercooked poultry and can cause bacteremia in vulnerable patients?

A: Salmonella species.

486. Q: Which children are at increased risk of invasive nontyphoidal Salmonella infection?

A: Children with sickle cell disease, immunodeficiency, or other significant comorbidities, as well as very young infants.

487. Q: What gastrointestinal infection can cause prolonged diarrhea after contaminated water exposure and is particularly important in immunocompromised children?

A: Cryptosporidium infection.

488. Q: What is the characteristic stool pattern of cryptosporidiosis?

A: Watery, nonbloody diarrhea, which can be prolonged or severe in immunocompromised patients.

Hepatitis

489. Q: How is hepatitis A primarily transmitted?

A: Fecal-oral transmission, commonly through contaminated food or water or close person-to-person contact.

490. Q: Does hepatitis A cause chronic hepatitis?

A: No. Hepatitis A does not typically cause chronic infection.

491. Q: What is the main preventive strategy against hepatitis A?

A: Hepatitis A vaccination.

492. Q: What is the major difference between hepatitis A and hepatitis B regarding chronic infection?

A:

HAV→No chronic infection
HBV→Can become chronic
Pediatric HIV — Advanced Concepts

493. Q: What is the most common presenting manifestation of untreated pediatric HIV infection?

A: Recurrent or severe infections, failure to thrive, persistent lymphadenopathy, and other signs of progressive immune dysfunction may occur.

494. Q: What opportunistic infection is particularly associated with severe cellular immunodeficiency in children with HIV?

A: Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP).

495. Q: What fungal infection can cause persistent esophageal disease in severely immunocompromised children?

A: Candida esophagitis.

496. Q: What viral infection can cause retinitis in children with severe immunosuppression?

A: Cytomegalovirus (CMV).

Infection Prevention

497. Q: What is the most effective general strategy for preventing transmission of infectious diseases in healthcare settings?

A: Hand hygiene, combined with appropriate standard and transmission-based precautions.

498. Q: Why are conjugate vaccines particularly effective in young children?

A: They convert poorly immunogenic polysaccharide antigens into T-cell–dependent immune responses, producing better immunologic memory in young children.

499. Q: Which pediatric infections have been dramatically reduced through routine vaccination?

A: Examples include:

Hib disease
Invasive pneumococcal disease
Measles
Mumps
Rubella
Polio
Pertussis
Diphtheria
Varicella

500. Q: A child with fever, rapidly progressive purpura, hypotension, and altered mental status arrives in the emergency department. What is the immediate clinical priority?

A: Treat as fulminant meningococcal sepsis until proven otherwise: immediately stabilize the child and administer appropriate empiric IV antibiotics without waiting for culture results.

=======================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Immunizations

1. Q: What is the basic principle of a live attenuated vaccine?

A: It contains a weakened form of a living pathogen that can replicate to produce a strong, durable immune response.

Examples: MMR, varicella, rotavirus, and live attenuated influenza vaccine (LAIV).

2. Q: What is the major advantage of live attenuated vaccines?

A: They generally produce a strong and long-lasting immune response, often resembling natural infection.

3. Q: What is the major contraindication shared by most live vaccines?

A: Severe immunocompromise is a major contraindication because the attenuated organism may cause disease.

4. Q: Are live vaccines generally given during pregnancy?

A: No. Live vaccines such as MMR and varicella are generally contraindicated during pregnancy.

5. Q: Can a live vaccine accidentally given during pregnancy be considered an indication for pregnancy termination?

A: No. Accidental administration of a live vaccine is not an indication for termination of pregnancy.

6. Q: What is the key principle when a child falls behind on routine vaccinations?

A: Do not restart the vaccine series. Continue using the appropriate catch-up schedule.

7. Q: What determines the interval between vaccine doses?

A: The child's age, previous doses, minimum intervals, and vaccine-specific recommendations determine the appropriate interval.

8. Q: Can multiple vaccines be administered during the same visit?

A: Yes. Multiple recommended vaccines can generally be administered during the same visit at different injection sites.

9. Q: Why are combination vaccines useful?

A: They reduce the number of injections while providing protection against multiple pathogens.

10. Q: What is the purpose of a vaccine contraindication?

A: A contraindication identifies a situation in which vaccination poses a significant risk of serious adverse effects and the vaccine should generally not be administered.

Hepatitis B

11. Q: When should the first dose of hepatitis B vaccine generally be given to a healthy newborn?

A: At birth, ideally within the first 24 hours.

12. Q: What should a newborn receive if the mother is HBsAg-positive?

A: The newborn should receive:

Hepatitis B vaccine
Hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG)

as soon as possible after birth, using separate injection sites.

13. Q: Why is prevention of perinatal hepatitis B transmission particularly important?

A: Infection acquired during infancy has a very high probability of becoming chronic, increasing the lifetime risk of cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma.

14. Q: What antibody indicates immunity after hepatitis B vaccination?

A: Anti-HBs.

Rotavirus

15. Q: What type of vaccine is the rotavirus vaccine?

A: An oral live attenuated vaccine.

16. Q: What is an important age-related rule for rotavirus vaccination?

A: Rotavirus vaccination has strict age limits for starting and completing the series; the series should not be initiated beyond the recommended maximum age.

17. Q: What serious adverse event is associated with rotavirus vaccination?

A: A small increased risk of intussusception.

18. Q: What is a major contraindication to rotavirus vaccination?

A: Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) and a history of intussusception are major contraindications.

DTaP / Tdap

19. Q: What does DTaP protect against?

A:

D=Diphtheria
T=Tetanus
aP=acellular Pertussis

20. Q: What is the major difference between DTaP and Tdap?

A: DTaP is the pediatric formulation used in younger children, whereas Tdap contains reduced quantities of diphtheria and pertussis antigens and is used for older children/adolescents and adults.

21. Q: What is the routine adolescent pertussis booster?

A: Tdap, routinely given at age 11–12 years.

22. Q: What vaccine should be given during every pregnancy to protect the newborn against pertussis?

A: Tdap during each pregnancy, ideally during 27–36 weeks' gestation.

23. Q: Why is maternal Tdap given during pregnancy rather than waiting until after delivery?

A: Maternal antibodies cross the placenta and provide passive protection to the newborn during the first months of life, before the infant has completed the primary pertussis series.

24. Q: What is a major contraindication to additional pertussis-containing vaccine after a previous dose?

A: Encephalopathy within 7 days after a previous pertussis-containing vaccine, without another identifiable cause, is a contraindication to further pertussis-containing vaccination.

25. Q: Does a simple fever after a previous DTaP dose contraindicate future DTaP vaccination?

A: No. Mild fever and other common local/systemic reactions are generally not contraindications.

Haemophilus influenzae type b

26. Q: What type of vaccine is the Hib vaccine?

A: A conjugate vaccine containing Hib capsular polysaccharide linked to a protein carrier.

27. Q: Why is conjugation important for Hib vaccination in infants?

A: Conjugation converts the polysaccharide antigen into a T-cell–dependent response, producing better immunologic memory in young children.

28. Q: Is routine Hib vaccination recommended for healthy adults?

A: No. Routine Hib vaccination is primarily directed at young children, with special indications for certain high-risk patients.

Pneumococcal Vaccination

29. Q: What vaccines are used to prevent invasive pneumococcal disease?

A: Pneumococcal conjugate vaccines (PCVs) are used routinely in children; specific products and schedules depend on age and risk factors.

30. Q: Which children have increased risk of invasive pneumococcal disease?

A: Examples include children with:

Sickle cell disease
Asplenia
CSF leaks
Cochlear implants
Certain immunocompromising conditions

31. Q: Why are asplenic children particularly vulnerable to pneumococcal infection?

A: The spleen is critical for clearing encapsulated bacteria from the bloodstream.

32. Q: What major encapsulated organisms are particularly dangerous in an asplenic child?

A:

S. pneumoniae
N. meningitidis
H. influenzae
Meningococcal Vaccines

33. Q: What does the MenACWY vaccine protect against?

A: Meningococcal serogroups:

A, C, W, Y

34. Q: What does the MenB vaccine protect against?

A: Neisseria meningitidis serogroup B.

35. Q: Which adolescent age group routinely receives MenACWY vaccination?

A: A routine dose is given at 11–12 years, with a booster at 16 years.

36. Q: Which adolescents may benefit from MenB vaccination?

A: MenB vaccination is considered for adolescents and young adults based on shared clinical decision-making, and it is recommended for certain people at increased risk.

37. Q: Which medical conditions increase the indication for meningococcal vaccination?

A: Important examples include:

Anatomic or functional asplenia
Persistent complement deficiency
Complement inhibitor therapy
Certain microbiologists with occupational exposure
Outbreak exposure
MMR

38. Q: What does MMR protect against?

A:

M=Measles
M=Mumps
R=Rubella

39. Q: What type of vaccine is MMR?

A: Live attenuated vaccine.

40. Q: What is a major contraindication to MMR vaccination?

A: Severe immunodeficiency and pregnancy are major contraindications.

41. Q: Can MMR cause measles, mumps, or rubella infection in an immunocompetent child?

A: No. The vaccine contains attenuated viruses and does not cause the natural diseases in immunocompetent individuals.

42. Q: What should be done after a significant measles exposure in an unvaccinated susceptible child?

A: Depending on age, timing, and risk factors, MMR vaccine may be given promptly; immune globulin is used for certain high-risk exposed individuals.

Varicella

43. Q: What type of vaccine is the varicella vaccine?

A: Live attenuated vaccine.

44. Q: What is a major contraindication to varicella vaccination?

A: Severe immunodeficiency and pregnancy.

45. Q: What should be done after significant varicella exposure in a susceptible high-risk child who cannot receive live vaccine?

A: Varicella-zoster immune globulin (VariZIG) may be indicated when given within the recommended post-exposure window.

Influenza

46. Q: What are the two major types of influenza vaccine formulations used in children?

A:

Inactivated influenza vaccine (IIV) — injectable
Live attenuated influenza vaccine (LAIV) — intranasal, for eligible age groups

47. Q: Which influenza vaccine should generally be avoided in a severely immunocompromised child?

A: Live attenuated influenza vaccine (LAIV) should be avoided because it contains a live attenuated virus.

48. Q: Why is annual influenza vaccination recommended?

A: Because influenza strains evolve over time, and vaccine composition is updated periodically to improve protection against circulating strains.

HPV

49. Q: What cancer-causing infections is the HPV vaccine designed to prevent?

A: Infection with oncogenic HPV types that can cause:

Cervical cancer
Anal cancer
Penile cancer
Vulvar/vaginal cancer
Oropharyngeal cancer
Other HPV-associated cancers

50. Q: What is the preferred age for routine HPV vaccination?

A: Age 11–12 years, although vaccination can begin at age 9 years. Catch-up vaccination is recommended through age 26 years for those not adequately vaccinated; vaccination ages 27–45 are based on shared clinical decision-making.

51. Q: What is the difference between a vaccine contraindication and a precaution?

A: A contraindication means the vaccine should not be given because the risk of a serious adverse reaction outweighs benefit. A precaution means vaccination may be appropriate if benefits outweigh the potential risk.

52. Q: Is a mild acute illness a contraindication to routine vaccination?

A: No. Mild illness, with or without low-grade fever, generally is not a contraindication to vaccination.

53. Q: Is moderate or severe acute illness a contraindication to every vaccine?

A: No. It is generally a precaution, meaning vaccination may be deferred depending on the clinical situation.

54. Q: What is the classic contraindication involving a previous vaccine reaction?

A: A severe allergic reaction (eg, anaphylaxis) to a previous dose or vaccine component is a contraindication to subsequent doses of that vaccine.

55. Q: Can a child with a history of anaphylaxis to eggs receive MMR vaccine?

A: Yes. Egg allergy is not a contraindication to MMR vaccination.

56. Q: What should be done if a child received a vaccine dose earlier than the minimum recommended interval?

A: The dose may be considered invalid and may need to be repeated according to the applicable minimum-interval rules.

57. Q: What is the "grace period" for vaccine minimum ages and intervals?

A: In U.S. immunization guidance, a dose administered ≤4 days before the minimum age or minimum interval is generally considered valid; a dose given ≥5 days too early is generally invalid and must be repeated.

58. Q: If a child has a prolonged gap between vaccine doses, should the series be restarted?

A: No. Vaccine series generally do not need to be restarted, regardless of how long the interval has been.

Polio

59. Q: What type of vaccine is the routine U.S. polio vaccine?

A: Inactivated poliovirus vaccine (IPV).

60. Q: Why is IPV preferred over oral live poliovirus vaccine in the United States?

A: IPV cannot cause vaccine-associated paralytic poliomyelitis because it contains inactivated virus.

61. Q: What is the major advantage of oral poliovirus vaccine (OPV) in settings where it is used?

A: OPV produces strong intestinal immunity, which can reduce transmission of poliovirus.

62. Q: What is the major disadvantage of OPV?

A: Rarely, live vaccine virus can cause vaccine-associated paralytic poliomyelitis or contribute to circulating vaccine-derived poliovirus.

63. Q: A child received only 2 IPV doses and is now older than the recommended routine vaccination age. What should be done?

A: Complete the IPV catch-up series using the appropriate minimum intervals; do not restart the series.

Hepatitis A

64. Q: At what age does routine hepatitis A vaccination begin?

A: 12 months of age. The routine series consists of 2 doses separated by at least 6 months.

65. Q: Can an unvaccinated older child receive catch-up hepatitis A vaccination?

A: Yes. Unvaccinated children/adolescents through age 18 years should receive the 2-dose series, with at least 6 months between doses.

66. Q: What type of vaccine is hepatitis A vaccine?

A: Inactivated vaccine.

67. Q: Is hepatitis A vaccine routinely given before 12 months of age?

A: Routine vaccination begins at 12 months. An infant aged 6–11 months may receive a dose for certain travel situations, but that travel dose does not replace the routine series beginning at ≥12 months.

Influenza

68. Q: At what age can children begin receiving routine influenza vaccination?

A: 6 months of age.

69. Q: How often should influenza vaccination generally be given?

A: Annually, because vaccine composition is updated as circulating influenza viruses evolve.

70. Q: A 7-month-old is receiving influenza vaccine for the first time. How many doses are generally required during that season?

A: 2 doses, administered at least 4 weeks apart, when the child is receiving influenza vaccination for the first time or has an incomplete vaccination history.

71. Q: What is an important distinction between injectable inactivated influenza vaccine and intranasal LAIV?

A: IIV is inactivated, whereas LAIV contains live attenuated influenza virus and therefore has additional contraindications.

72. Q: Why should LAIV generally be avoided in severely immunocompromised children?

A: Because it contains live attenuated virus, creating a potential risk in severe immunodeficiency.

MMR — Special Situations

73. Q: What should be done with MMR vaccination for a child traveling internationally?

A: Children 6–11 months traveling internationally should receive an early MMR dose; this dose does not replace the routine doses given after 12 months.

74. Q: How many MMR doses are routinely required during childhood?

A: 2 doses are routinely recommended.

75. Q: What is an important reason for the second MMR dose?

A: It provides immunity to children who failed to respond to the first dose; it is not simply a conventional "booster" for waning immunity.

76. Q: Can MMR be administered to a child with a history of febrile seizures?

A: Yes. A history of febrile seizures is not an absolute contraindication to MMR.

77. Q: What is a recognized adverse effect after MMR vaccination?

A: Fever and a mild transient rash can occur approximately 1–2 weeks after vaccination.

Varicella

78. Q: How many routine doses of varicella vaccine are recommended?

A: 2 doses are recommended for children who lack evidence of immunity.

79. Q: What is the minimum interval between the 2 routine varicella doses in young children?

A: For children younger than 13 years, the minimum interval is generally 3 months; a dose given at least 4 weeks apart may be considered valid in certain circumstances.

80. Q: What is the preferred approach if a child develops a mild rash after varicella vaccination?

A: Usually observation and supportive care are sufficient; a mild vaccine-associated rash is generally self-limited.

81. Q: Can a child with a history of severe allergy to gelatin receive varicella vaccine?

A: A severe allergic reaction to a vaccine component is a contraindication; vaccine components should therefore be reviewed carefully before administration.

MMRV

82. Q: What does MMRV combine?

A:

MMRV=Measles+Mumps+Rubella+Varicella

83. Q: What is an important adverse-event consideration when MMRV is used as the first measles-containing dose in young children?

A: Febrile seizures occur somewhat more frequently after the first MMRV dose than when MMR and varicella are administered separately in certain young children.

84. Q: In a child younger than 4 years receiving the first measles-containing vaccine dose, what strategy may reduce febrile-seizure risk?

A: Separate MMR and varicella vaccines may be preferred over combination MMRV for the first dose in the relevant age group.

HPV

85. Q: Does HPV vaccination prevent an existing HPV infection?

A: No. HPV vaccine is preventive and does not treat established HPV infection or HPV-associated disease.

86. Q: Why is HPV vaccination ideally administered before sexual exposure?

A: The vaccine is most effective before exposure to HPV, because it prevents new infection rather than eliminating established infection.

87. Q: How many HPV vaccine doses are required when the series is initiated before the 15th birthday?

A: 2 doses, generally at 0 and 6–12 months, with a minimum interval of 5 months.

88. Q: How many HPV vaccine doses are required when vaccination begins at age 15 years or older?

A: 3 doses at approximately:

0, 1–2, 6 months

with the required minimum intervals between doses.

89. Q: What happens if the second HPV dose is accidentally administered only 4 months after the first in a patient who started before age 15?

A: The interval is too short to count as a valid 2-dose series; another dose should be administered according to the appropriate minimum interval.

Meningococcal Vaccination

90. Q: What is the routine MenACWY schedule for adolescents?

A: 1 dose at 11–12 years and a booster at 16 years.

91. Q: A healthy adolescent receives MenACWY at age 13. When should the booster generally be given?

A: A booster is recommended at 16–18 years, with the minimum interval between doses taken into account.

92. Q: What is a major risk factor that makes meningococcal vaccination particularly important?

A: Complement deficiency or complement-inhibitor therapy, because terminal complement is important for defense against N. meningitidis.

93. Q: Why are patients receiving complement inhibitors at increased risk of meningococcal disease?

A: Drugs that inhibit terminal complement, such as eculizumab/ravulizumab, impair complement-mediated killing of N. meningitidis.

Pneumococcal & High-Risk Children

94. Q: Why does a child with sickle cell disease require special attention to pneumococcal vaccination?

A: Functional asplenia increases susceptibility to invasive encapsulated bacterial infections, especially S. pneumoniae.

95. Q: What is the major vaccine-related principle for children with anatomic or functional asplenia?

A: Ensure appropriate protection against pneumococcus, meningococcus, and Hib, according to age and current recommendations.

Immunocompromised Children

96. Q: Should a child receiving intensive immunosuppressive chemotherapy receive live vaccines?

A: Generally no. Live vaccines are contraindicated during significant immunosuppression because of the risk of vaccine-associated disease.

97. Q: Can an immunocompromised child receive inactivated vaccines?

A: Yes, when indicated. However, the immune response may be weaker than in immunocompetent individuals.

98. Q: What is an important vaccination principle for children with HIV?
