A: Vaccination should be individualized according to immune status, age, vaccine type, and current recommendations; certain live vaccines may be contraindicated in severe immunosuppression.

RSV Prevention

99. Q: What is nirsevimab?

A: Nirsevimab is a long-acting monoclonal antibody against RSV, providing passive protection rather than active vaccine-induced immunity. Current U.S. recommendations use it for eligible infants and certain high-risk young children.

100. Q: How does nirsevimab differ mechanistically from a vaccine?

A:

Nirsevimab→Passive immunity
Vaccine→Active immune response

Nirsevimab supplies preformed RSV-specific antibodies rather than stimulating the infant's immune system to generate its own antibodies.

101. Q: What is the most serious immediate allergic reaction to a vaccine?

A: Anaphylaxis, typically occurring within minutes to hours after vaccination. It requires immediate intramuscular epinephrine and emergency management.

102. Q: What is the first-line treatment for vaccine-associated anaphylaxis?

A: Intramuscular epinephrine in the anterolateral thigh.

103. Q: Does a history of anaphylaxis to one vaccine automatically contraindicate all other vaccines?

A: No. The contraindication applies to the specific vaccine or specific component responsible for the reaction.

104. Q: What is a common local reaction after vaccination?

A: Pain, redness, and swelling at the injection site.

105. Q: Are local injection-site reactions generally a reason to stop future doses?

A: No. Mild local reactions are expected and generally do not contraindicate subsequent doses.

106. Q: What is a hypotonic-hyporesponsive episode (HHE)?

A: A rare reaction after vaccination characterized by sudden limpness, decreased responsiveness, and pallor or cyanosis.

107. Q: Does a previous HHE after a pertussis-containing vaccine generally contraindicate future vaccination?

A: No. HHE is generally not considered a contraindication to subsequent pertussis-containing vaccination.

108. Q: What neurologic event is a contraindication to further pertussis-containing vaccine?

A: Encephalopathy occurring within 7 days after a previous pertussis-containing vaccine, when no alternative cause is identified.

Febrile Seizures & Vaccination

109. Q: Which routine childhood vaccines can transiently increase the risk of febrile seizures?

A: MMR/MMRV and some influenza-containing vaccine combinations can transiently increase febrile-seizure risk.

110. Q: Are febrile seizures after vaccination usually associated with long-term neurologic injury?

A: No. Vaccine-associated febrile seizures are generally brief and have an excellent neurologic prognosis.

111. Q: Does a previous simple febrile seizure contraindicate future routine vaccination?

A: No.

Pregnancy & Vaccination

112. Q: Which vaccines are routinely recommended during every pregnancy to protect both mother and infant?

A: Influenza vaccine and Tdap are key pregnancy vaccines.

113. Q: Which type of influenza vaccine should pregnant patients receive?

A: An inactivated or recombinant influenza vaccine; live attenuated intranasal influenza vaccine should not be used during pregnancy.

114. Q: Why is influenza vaccination particularly important during pregnancy?

A: Pregnancy increases the risk of severe influenza complications, and maternal antibodies also provide passive protection to the newborn.

115. Q: Which routine childhood live vaccines are generally contraindicated during pregnancy?

A: MMR, varicella, and live attenuated influenza vaccine (LAIV).

116. Q: What should be done if a nonimmune woman is identified during pregnancy who needs MMR or varicella vaccination?

A: Defer the live vaccine until after pregnancy, then vaccinate postpartum when appropriate.

Breastfeeding & Vaccination

117. Q: Can most vaccines be administered to breastfeeding mothers?

A: Yes. Breastfeeding is generally not a contraindication to routine vaccination.

118. Q: Can a breastfeeding mother receive MMR vaccine if she is susceptible?

A: Yes. MMR vaccination is compatible with breastfeeding.

119. Q: Can a breastfeeding mother receive varicella vaccine?

A: Yes. Varicella vaccination can be administered postpartum to susceptible breastfeeding mothers.

Travel Vaccination

120. Q: What vaccine should be considered for an eligible child traveling to a region with substantial yellow fever transmission?

A: Yellow fever vaccine, when indicated based on destination and individual risk.

121. Q: What type of vaccine is yellow fever vaccine?

A: Live attenuated vaccine.

122. Q: Why is yellow fever vaccine particularly important for travelers to endemic regions?

A: Yellow fever can cause severe hemorrhagic disease, hepatic failure, and death, and vaccination provides highly effective prevention.

123. Q: What should be considered before administering yellow fever vaccine to an immunocompromised child?

A: Because it is a live vaccine, significant immunosuppression may contraindicate vaccination; travel risk and alternative preventive strategies should be assessed.

Rabies Post-Exposure Vaccination

124. Q: What is the key principle of rabies post-exposure prophylaxis?

A: It combines wound cleansing, rabies vaccine, and rabies immune globulin (RIG) when indicated for a previously unvaccinated person.

125. Q: Where should rabies immune globulin be administered?

A: As much as anatomically feasible should be infiltrated into and around the wound, with any remaining volume administered at a site distant from the vaccine.

126. Q: Why should rabies vaccine and rabies immune globulin not be administered at the same anatomical site?

A: Passive antibody could interfere with the active immune response generated by the vaccine.

127. Q: Does a previously vaccinated person generally require rabies immune globulin after an exposure?

A: No. Previously vaccinated individuals generally receive rabies vaccine boosters without RIG.

Tetanus Post-Exposure Prevention

128. Q: What determines whether a child needs tetanus vaccination after a wound?

A: The decision depends on:

Wound type
Previous tetanus vaccination history
Time since the last dose

129. Q: What is the purpose of tetanus immune globulin (TIG)?

A: TIG provides immediate passive antibodies against tetanus toxin when vaccination alone is insufficient in high-risk exposures.

130. Q: When is TIG particularly important after an injury?

A: In patients with dirty/major wounds and inadequate or unknown vaccination history.

Hepatitis B Post-Exposure

131. Q: What should be given to an unvaccinated newborn born to an HBsAg-positive mother?

A: Hepatitis B vaccine + HBIG, administered as soon as possible after birth at separate sites.

132. Q: What is the major purpose of HBIG in a newborn exposed to maternal hepatitis B?

A: It provides immediate passive anti-HBs antibodies while active immunity develops from vaccination.

133. Q: Why should hepatitis B vaccination begin at birth?

A: It provides early protection against perinatal and household exposure, which can otherwise result in chronic infection.

Passive vs Active Immunity

134. Q: What is the difference between active and passive immunity?

A:

Active immunity=patient produces immune response
Passive immunity=preformed antibodies are administered

135. Q: What is an example of naturally acquired passive immunity in a newborn?

A: Maternal IgG transferred across the placenta.

136. Q: What is an example of vaccine-induced active immunity?

A: Production of antibodies and memory cells after receiving a vaccine such as MMR or hepatitis B vaccine.

137. Q: What is the major disadvantage of passive immunity?

A: Protection is temporary because the administered antibodies eventually disappear.

Vaccine Timing & Immunoglobulin

138. Q: Why can administration of antibody-containing blood products interfere with some live vaccines?

A: Passively acquired antibodies can neutralize the attenuated vaccine virus, reducing the immune response.

139. Q: Which live vaccine is particularly affected by antibody-containing blood products?

A: Measles- and varicella-containing vaccines can be affected.

140. Q: If a child recently received an antibody-containing blood product, what should be considered before giving MMR?

A: Determine whether the blood product could interfere with vaccine response and follow recommended spacing intervals when applicable.

Immunization in Premature Infants

141. Q: Should most premature infants receive routine vaccines according to chronological age?

A: Yes. Vaccination is generally based on chronologic age rather than corrected gestational age.

142. Q: Does prematurity itself reduce the need for routine childhood immunization?

A: No. Premature infants remain vulnerable to vaccine-preventable diseases and should generally follow the routine schedule.

143. Q: What special consideration applies to hepatitis B vaccination in very low birth-weight infants?

A: Hepatitis B vaccination at birth depends on birth weight and maternal HBsAg status; infants <2,000 g born to HBsAg-negative mothers may have their first dose deferred until approximately 1 month of age or hospital discharge.

Immunization in Asplenia

144. Q: Why is vaccination particularly important in a child with functional asplenia?

A: Asplenia causes impaired clearance of encapsulated bacteria, increasing the risk of rapidly fatal sepsis.

145. Q: What clinical syndrome is classically feared in an asplenic child?

A: Overwhelming postsplenectomy infection (OPSI) — rapidly progressive sepsis, especially from encapsulated organisms.

146. Q: Which vaccine-preventable organism is especially important in children with asplenia?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

Immunization in Cochlear Implant / CSF Leak

147. Q: Why are children with cochlear implants at increased risk of invasive pneumococcal disease?

A: Cochlear implants can increase susceptibility to pneumococcal meningitis, particularly in the presence of anatomic communication with the CNS.

148. Q: What vaccine-preventable infection is especially important in a child with a CSF leak?

A: Invasive pneumococcal disease, particularly pneumococcal meningitis.

Vaccine Failure

149. Q: What is a primary vaccine failure?

A: Failure to develop an adequate immune response after receiving the vaccine, despite appropriate administration.

150. Q: What is secondary vaccine failure?

A: Loss of previously acquired immunity over time, potentially allowing infection despite prior vaccination.

151. Q: A 6-year-old has never received DTaP. Should the entire infant DTaP series simply be started from the beginning?

A: No. Use the CDC catch-up schedule based on the child's current age and previous doses. Older children may transition to Tdap/Td-containing products rather than receiving the routine infant DTaP schedule.

152. Q: What is the key principle when determining whether a delayed vaccine dose is valid?

A: Check the minimum age and minimum interval requirements. If those requirements are met, the dose generally counts regardless of how late it was given.

153. Q: A child received a valid first dose of a vaccine several years ago. What should be done?

A: Continue the series using the catch-up schedule; do not restart it.

154. Q: Why are minimum intervals between vaccine doses important?

A: They allow sufficient time for the immune system to develop an effective primary or secondary response.

155. Q: What is the general rule for vaccines requiring multiple doses?

A: The first dose primes the immune system, while subsequent doses generally improve the magnitude and durability of protection.

DTaP / Tdap — Advanced

156. Q: What vaccine should be used for routine tetanus, diphtheria, and pertussis protection in an adolescent who has completed the childhood DTaP series?

A: Tdap, followed by Td or Tdap boosters every 10 years according to current recommendations.

157. Q: Can Tdap be administered more than once?

A: Yes. Tdap can be administered when indicated, including during each pregnancy and for certain wound-management situations.

158. Q: A 13-year-old never received Tdap. What is the appropriate approach?

A: Give Tdap now, then complete any remaining tetanus/diphtheria-containing doses according to the age-appropriate catch-up schedule.

159. Q: Why does pertussis vaccination not provide lifelong complete protection?

A: Immunity wanes over time, which is why booster vaccination is recommended.

Pneumococcal Vaccination — Advanced

160. Q: What is the major difference between pneumococcal conjugate vaccines and pneumococcal polysaccharide vaccine?

A: Conjugate vaccines generate a strong T-cell–dependent response and immunologic memory, whereas polysaccharide vaccine produces a more limited T-cell–independent response.

161. Q: Why is a pure polysaccharide vaccine less effective in children younger than 2 years?

A: Young children have a relatively poor immune response to T-cell–independent polysaccharide antigens.

162. Q: Why is pneumococcal vaccination particularly important in children with nephrotic syndrome?

A: Nephrotic syndrome causes urinary loss of immunoglobulins and complement, increasing susceptibility to serious infections, including encapsulated bacteria.

163. Q: What other encapsulated organism is particularly important in children with nephrotic syndrome?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is the major vaccine-preventable concern.

Hib — Special Populations

164. Q: Should a healthy 7-year-old who never received Hib vaccine routinely begin a Hib series?

A: Generally no. Routine Hib vaccination is directed primarily at children younger than 5 years, except for certain high-risk conditions.

165. Q: Which children older than the routine Hib vaccination age may require Hib vaccination?

A: Certain high-risk children, including those with anatomic or functional asplenia or undergoing hematopoietic stem-cell transplantation, depending on circumstances.

Meningococcal Vaccination — Special Situations

166. Q: What vaccine strategy is important for a child with functional or anatomic asplenia regarding meningococcal disease?

A: Ensure appropriate MenACWY vaccination and, when indicated, MenB vaccination, according to age and risk-specific recommendations.

167. Q: Why does terminal complement deficiency increase meningococcal risk?

A: Terminal complement components form the membrane attack complex (MAC), which is important for killing Neisseria species.

168. Q: What is the membrane attack complex?

A:

C5b−C9

forms the membrane attack complex, which creates pores in susceptible bacterial membranes.

169. Q: A teenager taking eculizumab has not received meningococcal vaccination. What is the major concern?

A: Invasive meningococcal disease, because eculizumab inhibits terminal complement activity.

HPV — Clinical Application

170. Q: Does HPV vaccination eliminate the need for cervical cancer screening?

A: No. Vaccination does not eliminate the need for recommended cervical cancer screening.

171. Q: Why can a vaccinated adolescent still develop an HPV-associated cancer?

A: Vaccination does not protect against every oncogenic HPV type and is not therapeutic for preexisting infection.

172. Q: Can HPV vaccine be administered to someone who has already become sexually active?

A: Yes. Vaccination remains appropriate for eligible individuals who have not completed the recommended series.

173. Q: What is an important contraindication to HPV vaccination?

A: Severe allergic reaction to a previous dose or vaccine component.

174. Q: Should HPV vaccination be initiated during pregnancy?

A: No. HPV vaccination should be deferred during pregnancy; inadvertent administration is not an indication for intervention or pregnancy termination.

Rotavirus — Clinical Application

175. Q: Why does rotavirus vaccination have strict upper age limits?

A: The vaccine is administered only during early infancy because of age-specific safety considerations, including the risk of intussusception.

176. Q: What should be done if an infant develops intussusception after a previous rotavirus vaccine dose?

A: Do not administer further rotavirus vaccine doses.

177. Q: Can a child with SCID receive live rotavirus vaccine?

A: No. Severe immunodeficiency such as SCID is a contraindication to rotavirus vaccination.

Live Vaccine Principles

178. Q: What happens if two injectable or intranasal live vaccines are not administered on the same day?

A: They should generally be separated by at least 28 days to reduce interference between vaccine viruses.

179. Q: Does the 28-day spacing rule apply to inactivated vaccines?

A: No. Inactivated vaccines can generally be administered at any interval relative to other vaccines.

180. Q: Can an inactivated vaccine and a live vaccine be given on the same day?

A: Yes.

181. Q: Why can live vaccines interfere with each other when administered too close together?

A: Replication of one vaccine virus can alter the immune response to another live vaccine.

Immunocompromised Patients

182. Q: Why are live vaccines generally avoided in patients with severe cellular immunodeficiency?

A: The attenuated organism may replicate uncontrollably and cause serious disease.

183. Q: What type of immune defect is particularly concerning when considering live viral vaccines?

A: Severe T-cell/cellular immunodeficiency.

184. Q: What is a key vaccination consideration after hematopoietic stem-cell transplantation?

A: Previous vaccine immunity may not persist adequately, so patients generally require a re-immunization strategy once clinically appropriate.

185. Q: Why might vaccines be less effective immediately after transplantation?

A: Immunosuppressive therapy and impaired immune reconstitution can result in poor vaccine responses.

Splenectomy

186. Q: When should vaccines ideally be administered before an elective splenectomy?

A: Ideally before splenectomy, allowing time for the patient to develop an immune response.

187. Q: Which vaccine-preventable organisms are particularly important before/after splenectomy?

A:

S. pneumoniae
N. meningitidis
H. influenzae type b
Vaccine Storage & Administration

188. Q: Why is proper vaccine storage important?

A: Improper temperature or handling can reduce vaccine potency, resulting in inadequate protection.

189. Q: What is the general principle of the vaccine cold chain?

A: Vaccines must be stored and transported within their recommended temperature ranges from manufacture through administration.

190. Q: Should vaccines be stored in the refrigerator door?

A: Generally no. Refrigerator doors experience greater temperature variation and are inappropriate for most vaccines.

Vaccine Components & Reactions

191. Q: What is an adjuvant?

A: A substance added to some vaccines to enhance the immune response to the antigen.

192. Q: Why can aluminum salts be present in some vaccines?

A: They can act as adjuvants, enhancing immune responses.

193. Q: What type of hypersensitivity reaction causes immediate vaccine anaphylaxis?

A: IgE-mediated type I hypersensitivity.

194. Q: What is the mechanism of a type I hypersensitivity reaction?

A:

Allergen→IgE→mast-cell activation

leading to mediator release such as histamine, causing urticaria, bronchospasm, and/or hypotension.

Herd Immunity

195. Q: What is herd immunity?

A: Indirect protection that occurs when a sufficiently large proportion of a population is immune, reducing transmission and protecting susceptible individuals.

196. Q: Why is herd immunity particularly important for infants who are too young to receive certain vaccines?

A: Reduced circulation of the pathogen decreases their probability of exposure and infection.

197. Q: Why can declining vaccination rates lead to outbreaks of measles?

A: Measles is extremely contagious, so a high population immunity level is required to prevent sustained transmission.

Vaccine Effectiveness

198. Q: What is vaccine efficacy?

A: The relative reduction in disease among vaccinated individuals compared with an appropriate unvaccinated group under controlled conditions.

199. Q: What is vaccine effectiveness?

A: The protection produced by a vaccine under real-world conditions, where adherence, population characteristics, and circulating strains may vary.

200. Q: Why can vaccinated children occasionally still develop the disease?

A: No vaccine provides 100% protection. Primary vaccine failure, waning immunity, pathogen variation, incomplete vaccination, or host factors can contribute to breakthrough infection.

201. Q: A child developed anaphylaxis after a previous dose of MMR. Should another MMR dose be administered?

A: No. A severe allergic reaction to a previous dose or a vaccine component is a contraindication to further doses of that vaccine.

202. Q: Does a family history of seizures contraindicate routine childhood vaccination?

A: No. A family history of seizures is not a contraindication to vaccination.

203. Q: Does a family history of vaccine-related adverse events generally contraindicate vaccination?

A: No. Contraindications are based primarily on the patient's own medical history, not a family member's reaction.

204. Q: Is a history of Guillain-Barré syndrome an absolute contraindication to all vaccines?

A: No. Vaccine-specific precautions apply. For example, GBS occurring within 6 weeks after a previous influenza vaccine is a precaution for subsequent influenza vaccination.

205. Q: A child had GBS 2 months after an influenza vaccine. What should be considered before another influenza vaccine?

A: The prior GBS episode is a precaution, and the risks and benefits of further influenza vaccination should be assessed.

Vaccination During Acute Illness

206. Q: A child has mild diarrhea and a low-grade fever. Should routine vaccination necessarily be postponed?

A: No. Mild acute illness generally is not a contraindication.

207. Q: Why can unnecessary vaccine delays be harmful?

A: Delaying vaccination leaves the child susceptible to vaccine-preventable disease without providing a meaningful safety advantage in most mild illnesses.

208. Q: A child is hospitalized with a severe acute illness. Should routine vaccination usually be given immediately?

A: Vaccination may be deferred until the acute illness improves, because moderate/severe acute illness is generally a precaution.

DTaP Adverse Events

209. Q: What common local reaction occurs after DTaP?

A: Pain, redness, and swelling at the injection site.

210. Q: What is an uncommon but dramatic reaction involving extensive swelling after DTaP?

A: An extensive limb swelling reaction can occur and usually resolves without permanent injury.

211. Q: Does an extensive local reaction after DTaP necessarily contraindicate future doses?

A: No. It is generally not a contraindication to subsequent vaccination.

Tetanus Wound Management

212. Q: A fully immunized child sustains a clean minor wound. When is tetanus vaccination generally unnecessary?

A: If the child has completed the primary series and the most recent tetanus-containing vaccine was within the recommended interval for that wound category.

213. Q: A child with an unknown vaccination history has a dirty wound. What should be considered?

A: Tetanus-containing vaccine plus tetanus immune globulin (TIG) when indicated.

214. Q: Why is TIG different from tetanus vaccination?

A: TIG provides immediate passive neutralization of tetanus toxin, whereas vaccination stimulates active antibody production.

Rabies Vaccination

215. Q: What is the first step after a potentially rabid animal bite?

A: Immediately wash and thoroughly irrigate the wound with soap and water.

216. Q: Why is wound irrigation important after a potential rabies exposure?

A: It mechanically removes virus from the wound and can substantially reduce infection risk.

217. Q: A child previously completed rabies vaccination and is exposed again. Is rabies immune globulin indicated?

A: No. Previously vaccinated individuals generally receive 2 booster vaccine doses and do not receive RIG.

Hepatitis B — Postexposure & Maternal Transmission

218. Q: What serologic marker indicates current hepatitis B infection in the mother and determines neonatal prophylaxis?

A: HBsAg.

219. Q: What should be done if a pregnant woman is found to be HBsAg-positive?

A: Ensure appropriate newborn hepatitis B vaccination and HBIG prophylaxis at birth, followed by completion of the vaccine series.

220. Q: Why is hepatitis B vaccination particularly effective at preventing chronic infection when given to newborns?

A: Early vaccination prevents perinatal transmission, which has a much higher risk of chronic infection than infection acquired later in life.

Hepatitis A — Postexposure

221. Q: What can be used for postexposure prevention of hepatitis A in susceptible individuals?

A: Hepatitis A vaccine is commonly used; immune globulin may be indicated for certain high-risk individuals or specific circumstances.

222. Q: Why is hepatitis A vaccination useful after recent exposure?

A: The vaccine can induce protective immunity before clinical disease develops, because the incubation period is relatively long.

MMR — Postexposure

223. Q: What is the preferred postexposure strategy for a susceptible immunocompetent child exposed to measles?

A: MMR vaccine administered promptly, when the child is eligible and within the recommended postexposure window.

224. Q: What is used for measles postexposure prophylaxis in certain high-risk patients who cannot receive MMR?

A: Immune globulin.

225. Q: Why might an immunocompromised child exposed to measles receive immune globulin rather than MMR?

A: Severe immunocompromise may contraindicate live MMR, while passive antibody can provide immediate protection.

Varicella — Postexposure

226. Q: What is the preferred postexposure strategy for a healthy susceptible child after significant varicella exposure?

A: Varicella vaccine, administered as soon as possible within the recommended postexposure window.

227. Q: Which exposed children may require varicella-zoster immune globulin instead of live vaccine?

A: Certain high-risk susceptible individuals who cannot receive varicella vaccine, such as significantly immunocompromised patients.

228. Q: Why is varicella-zoster immune globulin useful after exposure?

A: It provides immediate passive antibodies that can reduce the severity of infection.

Pertussis Exposure

229. Q: What is the major preventive strategy for pertussis?

A: Routine vaccination with pertussis-containing vaccines, plus appropriate management of close contacts when indicated.

230. Q: Which antibiotic is classically used for pertussis postexposure prophylaxis?

A: A macrolide, typically azithromycin.

231. Q: Why are household contacts of a child with pertussis often treated prophylactically?

A: Household exposure carries a high transmission risk, and prophylaxis can reduce secondary cases, especially in vulnerable contacts.

Meningococcal Postexposure Prevention

232. Q: What should close household contacts of a child with invasive meningococcal disease receive?

A: Chemoprophylactic antibiotics, regardless of vaccination status, when they meet criteria for close contact.

233. Q: Why is meningococcal chemoprophylaxis given to close contacts?

A: To eradicate nasopharyngeal carriage and prevent secondary invasive disease.

234. Q: Does vaccination replace antibiotic prophylaxis for close contacts of meningococcal disease?

A: No. Appropriate close contacts should receive chemoprophylaxis, even if vaccinated.

Immunization & Immunodeficiency

235. Q: Which vaccine type is generally preferred when vaccinating a severely immunocompromised child?

A: Inactivated or non-live vaccines, when indicated.

236. Q: Does an inactivated vaccine cause the corresponding infectious disease?

A: No. It cannot replicate because the pathogen is inactivated or the vaccine uses nonreplicating components.

237. Q: Why might an inactivated vaccine be less effective during intensive chemotherapy?

A: The child's immune system may be unable to mount an adequate antibody and cellular immune response.

238. Q: Should household members of an immunocompromised child receive routine vaccines?

A: Yes. Vaccinating household contacts helps create a protective cocoon around the immunocompromised child, with specific precautions for certain live vaccines.

239. Q: Can household contacts generally receive MMR vaccine when living with an immunocompromised child?

A: Yes. MMR vaccination of household contacts is recommended when indicated and helps reduce transmission risk.

240. Q: What special consideration applies to a household contact who develops a rash after varicella vaccination?

A: Avoiding direct contact between the vaccine-rash lesions and the severely immunocompromised individual is prudent until the rash resolves.

Vaccine-Induced Immunity

241. Q: Why does a vaccine booster sometimes produce a faster antibody response than the first dose?

A: Immunologic memory allows memory B and T cells to respond rapidly to repeat antigen exposure.

242. Q: What antibody class is most important for long-term systemic vaccine-induced humoral immunity?

A: IgG is the major long-term circulating antibody class.

243. Q: What is the role of memory B cells after vaccination?

A: They allow a rapid and stronger antibody response when the antigen is encountered again.

Conjugate Vaccines

244. Q: What is the basic immunologic mechanism of a conjugate vaccine?

A: A bacterial polysaccharide is linked to a protein carrier, converting the response into a T-cell–dependent response.

245. Q: Why do conjugate vaccines generate better immune memory than unconjugated polysaccharide vaccines?

A: Protein-linked polysaccharide antigen can recruit T-helper cells, promoting germinal-center reactions, class switching, affinity maturation, and memory.

246. Q: Name important pediatric conjugate vaccines.

A: Examples include:

Hib
Pneumococcal conjugate vaccines
Meningococcal conjugate vaccines
Vaccine Platforms

247. Q: What is a toxoid vaccine?

A: A vaccine containing an inactivated bacterial toxin that stimulates antibodies against the toxin.

Examples:

Diphtheria toxoid
Tetanus toxoid

248. Q: What is the immune target of tetanus vaccination?

A: Tetanus toxin (tetanospasmin) rather than the bacterial organism itself.

249. Q: What is the immune target of diphtheria vaccination?

A: Diphtheria toxin, allowing antibodies to neutralize the toxin produced by Corynebacterium diphtheriae.

250. Q: Why can toxoid vaccines prevent disease even though they do not necessarily prevent bacterial colonization?

A: They induce neutralizing antibodies against the toxin, preventing toxin-mediated disease.

251. Q: What is the routine age for the first dose of MMR in the United States?

A: 12–15 months.

252. Q: What is the routine age for the second MMR dose?

A: 4–6 years. The second dose can be given earlier if the required minimum interval is met.

253. Q: What is the routine age range for the first varicella vaccine dose?

A: 12–15 months.

254. Q: When is the second routine varicella dose given?

A: 4–6 years, although it may be administered earlier if the appropriate minimum interval is satisfied.

255. Q: Why is the first dose of MMR or varicella generally not given before 12 months for routine vaccination?

A: Transplacental maternal antibodies can interfere with the immune response to live viral vaccines in young infants.

256. Q: Does an MMR dose given before 12 months generally count toward the routine series?

A: No. An early dose given at 6–11 months for situations such as international travel does not replace the routine doses given after 12 months.

Hepatitis B

257. Q: What is the standard childhood hepatitis B vaccination schedule?

A: A 3-dose primary series is commonly used, beginning with the birth dose, followed by subsequent doses according to the recommended schedule and minimum intervals.

258. Q: What is the major reason for giving hepatitis B vaccine at birth rather than waiting until infancy?

A: To prevent perinatal transmission and early household-acquired infection.

259. Q: A child completed the hepatitis B vaccine series but later has an undetectable anti-HBs level. Does this automatically mean the child is no longer immune?

A: No. In immunocompetent individuals, declining measurable antibody does not necessarily mean loss of immune memory or protection.

Hepatitis A

260. Q: What type of immune response does hepatitis A vaccination generate?

A: Active immunity against hepatitis A virus through development of protective antibodies.

261. Q: Why is hepatitis A vaccine particularly important for children traveling to endemic regions?

A: It prevents fecal-oral acquisition of HAV through contaminated food, water, or close contact.

Polio

262. Q: What type of pathogen causes poliomyelitis?

A: Poliovirus, an enterovirus in the Picornaviridae family.

263. Q: Why does IPV not cause vaccine-associated paralytic poliomyelitis?

A: IPV contains inactivated poliovirus, which cannot replicate.

264. Q: What is the major route of poliovirus transmission?

A: Primarily the fecal-oral route.

265. Q: What is the major clinical complication vaccination aims to prevent in poliovirus infection?

A: Acute flaccid paralysis caused by destruction of anterior horn motor neurons.

Hib

266. Q: What serious disease does Hib vaccination primarily prevent?

A: Invasive Haemophilus influenzae type b disease, especially meningitis, epiglottitis, bacteremia, and pneumonia.

267. Q: Why is Hib disease most concerning in young children?

A: Young children have immature immune responses to polysaccharide capsules, making them particularly susceptible to invasive disease without vaccination.

268. Q: Does Hib vaccination protect against all strains of Haemophilus influenzae?

A: No. It specifically targets type b (Hib) and does not provide universal protection against nontypeable or other encapsulated strains.

Pneumococcal Vaccination

269. Q: What major diseases does pneumococcal vaccination prevent?

A: Invasive pneumococcal disease, including meningitis and bacteremia, as well as pneumococcal pneumonia and other disease caused by vaccine-covered serotypes.

270. Q: Why is pneumococcal vaccination particularly important in a child with a cochlear implant?

A: Cochlear implantation is associated with increased risk of pneumococcal meningitis.

271. Q: Why is a child with a CSF leak at increased risk of pneumococcal meningitis?

A: The leak can provide a pathway for nasopharyngeal bacteria to reach the CNS.

Meningococcal Vaccination

272. Q: What organism causes meningococcal disease?

A: Neisseria meningitidis, a gram-negative diplococcus.

273. Q: What is the major clinical syndrome prevented by meningococcal vaccination?

A: Meningococcemia and meningococcal meningitis.

274. Q: What distinctive rash should raise concern for meningococcemia?

A: A rapidly developing petechial or purpuric, nonblanching rash, especially with fever and toxicity.

275. Q: Why is meningococcal disease particularly dangerous?

A: It can progress extremely rapidly to septic shock, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), limb ischemia, and death.

Pertussis

276. Q: What organism causes pertussis?

A: Bordetella pertussis.

277. Q: Why is pertussis especially dangerous in young infants?

A: Infants can develop apnea, cyanosis, pneumonia, and severe respiratory compromise, sometimes without the classic prolonged cough.

278. Q: Why is maternal Tdap vaccination important for newborn protection?

A: Maternal IgG antibodies cross the placenta, providing passive protection during the vulnerable first months of life.

279. Q: What is the classic cough pattern of pertussis?

A: Paroxysmal coughing, sometimes followed by an inspiratory "whoop" and/or posttussive vomiting.

Influenza

280. Q: Why can influenza vaccination reduce severe pediatric respiratory disease?

A: It reduces the risk of influenza infection and its complications, including pneumonia and hospitalization.

281. Q: What severe complication of influenza can occur in children?

A: Viral pneumonia, secondary bacterial pneumonia, encephalopathy, myocarditis, and other systemic complications can occur.

282. Q: Which bacterial pathogen is classically associated with secondary bacterial pneumonia after influenza?

A: Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pneumoniae are important causes.

Rotavirus

283. Q: What is the major disease prevented by rotavirus vaccination?

A: Severe rotavirus gastroenteritis, particularly dehydration requiring hospitalization.

284. Q: How is rotavirus vaccine administered?

A: Orally.

285. Q: Why is rotavirus vaccine not administered by injection?

A: It is an oral live attenuated vaccine designed to stimulate intestinal mucosal immunity.

286. Q: What is the classic complication associated with rotavirus vaccination that is tested on exams?

A: Intussusception.

HPV — Advanced Concepts

287. Q: What type of vaccine is the current HPV vaccine used in the United States?

A: Recombinant, non-live vaccine based on virus-like particles.

288. Q: Can HPV vaccine cause HPV infection?

A: No. It contains noninfectious virus-like particles, not live HPV.

289. Q: What viral protein forms the basis of HPV vaccine virus-like particles?

A: The L1 major capsid protein.

290. Q: What is the primary immunologic effect of HPV vaccination?

A: It induces neutralizing antibodies against vaccine-covered HPV types, preventing initial infection.

Live vs Non-Live Vaccines

291. Q: Which major routine pediatric vaccines are live attenuated?

A: High-yield examples include:

MMR
Varicella
Rotavirus
LAIV

292. Q: Which major routine pediatric vaccines are inactivated or non-live?

A: Examples include:

IPV
Hepatitis A
Hepatitis B
HPV
Inactivated influenza vaccine

293. Q: Why are live vaccines generally more effective at generating cellular immunity?

A: Because limited replication of the vaccine organism more closely mimics natural infection and stimulates both cell-mediated and humoral immunity.

294. Q: Why are inactivated vaccines generally safer in severe immunocompromise?

A: They cannot replicate, so they cannot cause infection from the vaccine organism.

Vaccine Adverse Effects

295. Q: What type of adverse effect typically occurs within minutes after vaccination in anaphylaxis?

A: Immediate hypersensitivity, including urticaria, angioedema, wheezing, hypotension, or cardiovascular collapse.

296. Q: What is the immediate medication of choice for vaccine-associated anaphylaxis?

A: Epinephrine IM.

297. Q: What common systemic reaction can occur after MMR vaccination?

A: Fever, sometimes accompanied by a mild rash.

298. Q: Why can fever occur approximately 1–2 weeks after MMR vaccination?

A: The attenuated vaccine viruses undergo limited replication and stimulate an immune response, producing transient fever.

Special Populations

299. Q: Should children with stable chronic illnesses generally receive routine vaccines?

A: Yes. Chronic disease itself is generally not a reason to omit routine vaccination, unless a specific contraindication exists.

300. Q: Why is vaccination particularly important in children with chronic cardiopulmonary disease?

A: They may have a higher risk of severe complications from vaccine-preventable respiratory infections such as influenza and pneumococcal disease.

=============================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Pediatric GI/Cardio/Pulm


1. Q: What is the most common cause of acute diarrhea in children?

A: Viral gastroenteritis, especially rotavirus and norovirus. Rotavirus is now less common in vaccinated populations.

2. Q: What is the most important initial treatment for a child with acute gastroenteritis and mild-to-moderate dehydration?

A: Oral rehydration solution (ORS) containing appropriate concentrations of glucose and electrolytes.

3. Q: Why is glucose included in oral rehydration solution?

A: Glucose facilitates sodium and water absorption through the intestinal sodium-glucose cotransporter.

Na
+
+Glucose→intestinal absorption→water absorption

4. Q: What clinical finding is most suggestive of severe dehydration in an infant?

A: Lethargy, very dry mucous membranes, absent tears, markedly decreased urine output, weak pulses, prolonged capillary refill, and/or hypotension.

5. Q: What is the preferred IV fluid for a child with severe hypovolemic dehydration?

A: Isotonic crystalloid, such as 0.9% normal saline or lactated Ringer solution, with reassessment after each bolus.

6. Q: What is the classic presentation of hypertrophic pyloric stenosis?

A: A young infant with progressive projectile, nonbilious vomiting, persistent hunger after vomiting, and possible visible peristaltic waves.

7. Q: What acid-base abnormality is classically associated with prolonged vomiting from pyloric stenosis?

A: Hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis.

↓Cl
−
+↓H
+
→metabolic alkalosis

8. Q: What imaging study is preferred for suspected hypertrophic pyloric stenosis?

A: Abdominal ultrasound.

9. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception?

A: Intermittent severe colicky abdominal pain, drawing up of the legs, vomiting, and later currant-jelly stool.

10. Q: What is the classic ultrasound finding in intussusception?

A: A target/doughnut sign on transverse imaging.

11. Q: What is the preferred treatment for uncomplicated ileocolic intussusception?

A: Air or contrast enema, which is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

12. Q: What is a major contraindication to pneumatic or contrast enema reduction of intussusception?

A: Perforation or peritonitis, which requires surgical management.

13. Q: What is the typical presentation of Hirschsprung disease?

A: Failure to pass meconium within the first 24–48 hours, abdominal distention, constipation, and possible explosive stool after rectal examination.

14. Q: What is the underlying defect in Hirschsprung disease?

A: Absence of enteric ganglion cells in the distal bowel due to failed neural crest migration.

15. Q: What is the diagnostic test for Hirschsprung disease?

A: Rectal suction biopsy demonstrating absence of ganglion cells.

16. Q: What is the classic presentation of celiac disease in a child?

A: Chronic diarrhea, abdominal distention, poor weight gain, and malabsorption, often after introduction of gluten-containing foods.

17. Q: What antibodies are most useful for initial screening for celiac disease?

A: IgA anti-tissue transglutaminase (tTG), with total IgA measured to identify IgA deficiency.

18. Q: What is the definitive treatment for celiac disease?

A: A strict lifelong gluten-free diet.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

19. Q: What is the most common congenital heart disease?

A: Ventricular septal defect (VSD).

20. Q: What murmur is characteristic of a VSD?

A: A harsh, holosystolic murmur best heard at the lower left sternal border.

21. Q: Why can a large VSD initially be asymptomatic at birth but cause heart failure later?

A: Pulmonary vascular resistance is initially high after birth. As it falls, left-to-right shunting increases, increasing pulmonary blood flow and causing heart failure.

22. Q: What is the most common cyanotic congenital heart disease presenting during the neonatal period?

A: Transposition of the great arteries (TGA) is a major cause of severe neonatal cyanosis.

23. Q: What is the fundamental anatomical defect in TGA?

A: The aorta arises from the right ventricle and the pulmonary artery arises from the left ventricle, creating parallel circulations.

24. Q: Why is an atrial or ventricular communication important in TGA?

A: It permits mixing of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood, which is necessary for survival.

25. Q: What medication is used to maintain ductal patency in a critically cyanotic newborn with suspected ductal-dependent congenital heart disease?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil).

26. Q: What important adverse effect can occur with prostaglandin E₁ infusion?

A: Apnea, so respiratory support should be immediately available.

27. Q: What are the four components of Tetralogy of Fallot?

A:

Ventricular septal defect
Right ventricular outflow obstruction
Overriding aorta
Right ventricular hypertrophy

28. Q: What maneuver can temporarily improve a hypercyanotic "tet spell"?

A: Place the child in the knee-chest position.

29. Q: Why does the knee-chest position improve a tet spell?

A: It increases systemic vascular resistance, reducing right-to-left shunting across the VSD and improving pulmonary blood flow.

30. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of Tetralogy of Fallot?

A: A "boot-shaped" heart, due primarily to right ventricular hypertrophy.

31. Q: What is the classic murmur of pulmonary stenosis?

A: A harsh systolic ejection murmur best heard at the upper left sternal border, often with an ejection click.

32. Q: What congenital heart lesion is associated with a continuous "machine-like" murmur?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA).

33. Q: What medication can close a PDA in a premature infant?

A: Indomethacin or ibuprofen, which inhibit prostaglandin synthesis.

34. Q: What medication can maintain PDA patency when systemic or pulmonary blood flow is ductal-dependent?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (PGE₁).

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

35. Q: What is the most common cause of bronchiolitis in infants?

A: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV).

36. Q: What age group is most commonly affected by bronchiolitis?

A: Infants and young children, particularly those younger than 2 years.

37. Q: What is the classic presentation of bronchiolitis?

A: Viral prodrome followed by tachypnea, wheezing, crackles, increased work of breathing, and feeding difficulty.

38. Q: What is the main treatment for uncomplicated bronchiolitis?

A: Supportive care, including hydration and oxygen when indicated.

39. Q: What is the most common chronic respiratory disease in children?

A: Asthma.

40. Q: What is the characteristic physiologic abnormality in asthma?

A: Variable, reversible airflow obstruction caused by bronchoconstriction, airway inflammation, and increased airway responsiveness.

41. Q: What spirometry finding supports asthma in an older cooperative child?

A: Obstructive airflow limitation with significant reversibility after bronchodilator administration.

42. Q: What is the classic auscultatory finding in asthma?

A: Expiratory wheezing, although severe airflow obstruction can produce a silent chest.

43. Q: What does a "silent chest" indicate in a child with severe asthma?

A: Very severe airflow obstruction with minimal air movement; it is a medical emergency.

44. Q: What is the first-line rescue medication for acute bronchospasm in pediatric asthma?

A: Inhaled short-acting β₂-agonist (SABA) such as albuterol.

45. Q: What is the most important long-term controller medication for persistent pediatric asthma?

A: Inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) are the preferred first-line controller therapy.

46. Q: What clinical finding suggests foreign-body aspiration rather than viral wheezing?

A: Sudden onset of coughing or respiratory distress, especially with unilateral wheezing or decreased breath sounds.

47. Q: What is the most common location for an aspirated foreign body in a child?

A: The right main bronchus is commonly involved because it is relatively wider and more vertically oriented.

48. Q: What is the definitive procedure for suspected airway foreign-body aspiration?

A: Bronchoscopy, which allows both diagnosis and removal.

49. Q: What is the classic presentation of cystic fibrosis in infancy?

A: Meconium ileus, followed later by recurrent sinopulmonary infections, pancreatic insufficiency, and poor growth.

50. Q: What is the diagnostic test for cystic fibrosis?

A: Sweat chloride testing demonstrating elevated chloride concentration, confirmed according to current diagnostic criteria.

CFTR dysfunction→↑Cl
−
 in sweat

51. Q: What is the most common cause of constipation in children?

A: Functional constipation, usually related to stool withholding, painful defecation, dietary factors, or toilet-training difficulties.

52. Q: What finding suggests functional constipation rather than an organic disorder?

A: A child with stool withholding, large painful stools, fecal soiling, and otherwise normal growth and development.

53. Q: What is the first-line treatment for fecal impaction due to functional constipation?

A: Disimpaction, commonly with oral polyethylene glycol (PEG), followed by maintenance therapy.

54. Q: What is the goal of maintenance treatment after fecal disimpaction?

A: To maintain soft, painless stools and prevent recurrent stool withholding.

55. Q: What is encopresis?

A: Involuntary passage of stool in inappropriate places in a child who is developmentally old enough for bowel control, often secondary to chronic constipation.

56. Q: What is the classic presentation of gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) in an infant?

A: Regurgitation or vomiting associated with complications such as poor weight gain, feeding difficulty, esophagitis, or respiratory symptoms.

57. Q: How does physiologic infant gastroesophageal reflux differ from GERD?

A: Physiologic reflux is common and generally does not impair growth or cause complications; GERD causes troublesome symptoms or complications.

58. Q: What is the first management step for uncomplicated infant reflux?

A: Conservative measures, including avoiding overfeeding and appropriate feeding adjustments.

59. Q: What is the classic presentation of malrotation with midgut volvulus in an infant?

A: Sudden bilious vomiting, abdominal distention, pain, and potentially rapid cardiovascular deterioration.

60. Q: Why is bilious vomiting in a neonate or young infant considered a surgical emergency until proven otherwise?

A: It may indicate intestinal obstruction distal to the ampulla, particularly malrotation with volvulus, which can rapidly cause bowel ischemia.

61. Q: What is the preferred initial imaging study for suspected malrotation in a symptomatic infant?

A: An upper gastrointestinal contrast study is classically used to evaluate abnormal positioning of the duodenojejunal junction.

62. Q: What is the treatment for midgut volvulus?

A: Urgent surgical reduction, typically with a Ladd procedure.

63. Q: What is the classic presentation of Meckel diverticulum?

A: Painless lower gastrointestinal bleeding in a child, although obstruction or inflammation can also occur.

64. Q: Why can Meckel diverticulum cause gastrointestinal bleeding?

A: It may contain ectopic gastric mucosa, which secretes acid and causes adjacent ileal ulceration.

65. Q: What diagnostic study is classically used when bleeding from Meckel diverticulum is suspected?

A: A technetium-99m pertechnetate scan (Meckel scan), which detects ectopic gastric mucosa.

66. Q: What is the classic presentation of appendicitis in an older child?

A: Periumbilical pain migrating to the right lower quadrant, anorexia, nausea/vomiting, and fever.

67. Q: Why can appendicitis present atypically in young children?

A: Young children may have nonspecific symptoms and difficulty localizing pain, increasing the risk of delayed diagnosis and perforation.

68. Q: What is the most common complication of untreated appendicitis in children?

A: Perforation, potentially resulting in an intra-abdominal abscess or generalized peritonitis.

69. Q: What is the typical presentation of inflammatory bowel disease in a child?

A: Chronic diarrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss, growth failure, and/or rectal bleeding, depending on the disease.

70. Q: Which inflammatory bowel disease commonly causes bloody diarrhea and continuous colonic inflammation?

A: Ulcerative colitis.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

71. Q: What is the characteristic murmur of an atrial septal defect (ASD)?

A: A fixed, wide split S₂ with a systolic ejection murmur at the upper left sternal border.

72. Q: Why does an ASD produce fixed splitting of S₂?

A: The left-to-right shunt causes chronically increased right ventricular volume, delaying pulmonic valve closure.

73. Q: What is the usual direction of blood flow through an uncomplicated ASD?

A: Left-to-right, because left atrial pressure exceeds right atrial pressure.

74. Q: What is a potential long-term complication of a large unrepaired ASD?

A: Pulmonary hypertension, which can eventually cause reversal of the shunt and cyanosis (Eisenmenger syndrome).

75. Q: What is the characteristic murmur of a coarctation of the aorta?

A: A systolic murmur, often best heard over the left upper sternal border or back, with diminished lower-extremity pulses.

76. Q: What blood pressure pattern suggests coarctation of the aorta?

A: Higher blood pressure in the upper extremities than the lower extremities, with reduced or delayed femoral pulses.

77. Q: What is a classic physical finding in older children with coarctation?

A: Radiofemoral delay and, in longstanding disease, collateral vessels causing rib notching.

78. Q: Why can a neonate with critical coarctation become severely ill when the ductus arteriosus closes?

A: Lower-body systemic perfusion becomes critically dependent on the ductus arteriosus, so closure can cause shock and organ hypoperfusion.

79. Q: What medication should be started in a critically ill neonate with suspected ductal-dependent systemic blood flow?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (PGE₁) to maintain ductal patency.

80. Q: What congenital cardiac lesion is associated with an "egg-on-a-string" appearance on chest x-ray?

A: Transposition of the great arteries.

81. Q: What congenital heart defect is associated with maternal diabetes?

A: Transposition of the great arteries is an important association.

82. Q: What congenital heart lesion is associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta and bicuspid aortic valve are important associations.

83. Q: What congenital heart defect is strongly associated with Down syndrome?

A: Atrioventricular septal defect (AVSD).

84. Q: Why are AV septal defects common in Down syndrome?

A: Abnormal development of the endocardial cushions causes defects involving the atrial and ventricular septa and atrioventricular valves.

85. Q: What murmur is characteristic of a complete AV septal defect?

A: A combination of findings from ASD/VSD physiology and atrioventricular valve regurgitation, often producing a prominent systolic murmur.

86. Q: What is Eisenmenger syndrome?

A: Pulmonary vascular disease from a longstanding left-to-right shunt eventually causes pulmonary hypertension and reversal to a right-to-left shunt with cyanosis.

87. Q: Why should a significant left-to-right shunt generally be repaired before irreversible pulmonary vascular disease develops?

A: Persistent high pulmonary blood flow and pressure can cause pulmonary vascular remodeling, eventually making the shunt irreversible.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

88. Q: What is the most common cause of stridor in an otherwise healthy infant?

A: Laryngomalacia.

89. Q: What is the typical presentation of laryngomalacia?

A: Inspiratory stridor beginning during early infancy, often worse with feeding, crying, or supine positioning.

90. Q: What is the usual management of uncomplicated laryngomalacia?

A: Observation and supportive care, because most cases resolve spontaneously as the airway matures.

91. Q: What is the classic presentation of croup?

A: Barking cough, hoarseness, and inspiratory stridor, often following an upper respiratory infection.

92. Q: Which virus is the classic cause of croup?

A: Parainfluenza virus, particularly type 1.

93. Q: What is the preferred treatment for mild-to-moderate croup?

A: Dexamethasone is the key therapy.

94. Q: When is nebulized epinephrine indicated in croup?

A: For moderate-to-severe croup with significant stridor or respiratory distress.

95. Q: What is the classic presentation of epiglottitis?

A: Abrupt high fever, toxic appearance, drooling, dysphagia, muffled voice, and respiratory distress.

96. Q: What is the most important airway principle in suspected epiglottitis?

A: Do not agitate the child or attempt an unnecessary oropharyngeal examination. Secure the airway in a controlled setting with appropriate expertise.

97. Q: Which organism historically caused most childhood epiglottitis before widespread Hib vaccination?

A: Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib).

98. Q: What is the classic presentation of bacterial pneumonia in a child?

A: Fever, cough, tachypnea, respiratory distress, and focal crackles or decreased breath sounds, sometimes with pleuritic pain.

99. Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of community-acquired pneumonia in a previously healthy child?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is the classic bacterial cause.

100. Q: What clinical finding is particularly useful for identifying pneumonia in a young child?

A: Tachypnea, especially when accompanied by fever and respiratory symptoms, is an important clinical indicator.

101. Q: What is the classic presentation of biliary atresia?

A: Persistent jaundice, direct hyperbilirubinemia, dark urine, pale/acholic stools, and hepatomegaly in an infant.

102. Q: What stool finding is particularly concerning for biliary obstruction in an infant?

A: Acholic (pale/white) stools, indicating absent bile pigment reaching the intestine.

103. Q: What is the definitive treatment for biliary atresia?

A: Kasai portoenterostomy, ideally performed early, followed by liver transplantation if progressive liver disease develops.

104. Q: What type of bilirubin is elevated in biliary atresia?

A: Conjugated (direct) bilirubin.

105. Q: What is the classic presentation of neonatal hepatitis?

A: Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia, hepatomegaly, poor feeding, and variable liver dysfunction, with no extrahepatic biliary obstruction.

106. Q: What is a major difference between biliary atresia and physiologic neonatal jaundice?

A: Physiologic jaundice is predominantly unconjugated, whereas biliary atresia causes conjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

107. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute pancreatitis in an older child?

A: Epigastric abdominal pain, often radiating to the back, with nausea/vomiting and elevated pancreatic enzymes.

108. Q: What serum enzyme is commonly elevated in acute pancreatitis?

A: Lipase is preferred because it is more specific and remains elevated longer than amylase.

109. Q: What is an important cause of pancreatitis in children?

A: Causes include trauma, medications, infections, gallstones, and metabolic/genetic disorders.

110. Q: What is the classic clinical pattern of lactose intolerance?

A: Bloating, abdominal cramps, flatulence, and watery diarrhea after lactose ingestion.

111. Q: What type of diarrhea occurs in carbohydrate malabsorption such as lactose intolerance?

A: Osmotic diarrhea, caused by unabsorbed carbohydrate retaining water in the intestinal lumen.

112. Q: What stool finding can support carbohydrate malabsorption?

A: Acidic stool pH due to bacterial fermentation of unabsorbed carbohydrates.

113. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception-associated obstruction?

A: Intermittent colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, abdominal mass, and eventually bloody/mucous stool.

114. Q: What is the "sausage-shaped" abdominal mass associated with?

A: Intussusception.

115. Q: What complication should be suspected when intussusception is untreated?

A: Bowel ischemia, necrosis, perforation, and peritonitis.

116. Q: What is the classic presentation of an incarcerated inguinal hernia in an infant?

A: A painful, irreducible groin/scrotal mass, often accompanied by vomiting or signs of bowel obstruction.

117. Q: Why are inguinal hernias more common in premature infants?

A: The processus vaginalis may remain patent because closure has not yet occurred.

118. Q: What is the major complication of an incarcerated inguinal hernia?

A: Bowel strangulation and ischemia.

119. Q: What is the classic presentation of anal fissure in a child?

A: Painful defecation with bright-red blood coating the stool or toilet paper, commonly associated with constipation.

120. Q: What is the first-line treatment for a typical anal fissure caused by constipation?

A: Stool softening, adequate dietary fiber/fluid intake, and treatment of the underlying constipation.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

121. Q: What is the characteristic murmur of a patent ductus arteriosus?

A: A continuous, machine-like murmur best heard at the upper left sternal border.

122. Q: What happens to pulmonary blood flow in a significant PDA?

A: There is left-to-right shunting, producing increased pulmonary blood flow.

123. Q: What physical finding may occur with a significant PDA?

A: Bounding peripheral pulses and widened pulse pressure.

124. Q: Which prostaglandin promotes closure of the ductus arteriosus?

A: Prostaglandin inhibition promotes closure; NSAIDs such as ibuprofen or indomethacin reduce prostaglandin synthesis.

125. Q: Which prostaglandin maintains ductal patency?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (PGE₁).

126. Q: What congenital heart lesion is associated with a harsh systolic murmur and a thrill at the left lower sternal border?

A: A large ventricular septal defect (VSD).

127. Q: Why can a small VSD produce a louder murmur than a large VSD?

A: A small defect creates higher-velocity turbulent flow, producing a louder murmur.

128. Q: What happens to a large VSD if pulmonary vascular resistance becomes severely elevated?

A: The left-to-right shunt can decrease and eventually reverse, producing Eisenmenger physiology.

129. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute rheumatic fever after streptococcal pharyngitis?

A: Migratory polyarthritis, carditis, chorea, erythema marginatum, and subcutaneous nodules.

130. Q: What cardiac structure is most commonly affected by rheumatic carditis?

A: The mitral valve is most commonly affected.

131. Q: What type of valve disease is classically associated with chronic rheumatic heart disease?

A: Mitral stenosis is the classic late complication.

132. Q: What is the underlying mechanism of Kawasaki disease?

A: It is an acute systemic vasculitis, particularly affecting medium-sized arteries and the coronary arteries.

133. Q: What are the major clinical features of Kawasaki disease?

A: Fever ≥5 days, bilateral nonexudative conjunctivitis, oral mucosal changes, polymorphous rash, extremity changes, and cervical lymphadenopathy.

134. Q: What is the most important cardiac complication of Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery aneurysms.

135. Q: What is the standard initial treatment for Kawasaki disease?

A: IVIG plus aspirin, with additional therapy for IVIG-resistant or high-risk disease when indicated.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

136. Q: What is the classic presentation of bacterial tracheitis?

A: High fever, toxic appearance, stridor, respiratory distress, and thick purulent tracheal secretions, often following a viral upper respiratory infection.

137. Q: How does bacterial tracheitis differ from uncomplicated croup?

A: Bacterial tracheitis tends to produce a more toxic appearance, higher fever, purulent secretions, and potentially severe airway obstruction.

138. Q: What is an important bacterial cause of bacterial tracheitis?

A: Staphylococcus aureus is a common pathogen.

139. Q: What is the classic presentation of obstructive sleep apnea in a child?

A: Loud snoring, witnessed apneas, restless sleep, daytime behavioral problems, and/or excessive daytime sleepiness.

140. Q: What is the most common cause of obstructive sleep apnea in otherwise healthy children?

A: Adenotonsillar hypertrophy.

141. Q: What is the first-line treatment for significant pediatric obstructive sleep apnea caused by adenotonsillar hypertrophy?

A: Adenotonsillectomy when appropriate.

142. Q: What pulmonary complication is particularly associated with neuromuscular weakness?

A: Hypoventilation due to inadequate respiratory muscle strength, potentially leading to hypercapnia.

143. Q: What acid-base abnormality develops with chronic hypoventilation?

A: Respiratory acidosis with renal compensation.

↑PaCO
2
	​

→↑H
+
→respiratory acidosis

144. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding in foreign-body aspiration?

A: Unilateral hyperinflation due to a ball-valve obstruction may occur; atelectasis can occur with complete obstruction.

145. Q: Why can an aspirated foreign body cause unilateral hyperinflation?

A: Air enters during inspiration but becomes trapped during expiration because the foreign body acts as a one-way valve.

146. Q: What is the classic presentation of pneumothorax?

A: Sudden chest pain, dyspnea, decreased breath sounds, and hyperresonance on the affected side.

147. Q: What is the immediate treatment for a tension pneumothorax?

A: Immediate needle or catheter decompression, followed by definitive chest-tube drainage.

148. Q: What distinguishes tension pneumothorax from a simple pneumothorax?

A: Tension pneumothorax causes hemodynamic compromise due to increased intrathoracic pressure, potentially producing obstructive shock.

149. Q: What is the classic presentation of aspiration pneumonia in a child with swallowing dysfunction?

A: Coughing or choking with feeds, recurrent respiratory infections, and infiltrates in dependent lung regions.

150. Q: Which lung regions are commonly affected by aspiration when the child is upright?

A: The basal segments of the lower lobes, depending on the child's position and anatomy.

151. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception in an older infant or toddler?

A: Intermittent severe colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, lethargy, and sometimes bloody/mucoid stool. A sausage-shaped mass may be palpable.

152. Q: What is the most common lead point for intussusception in young children?

A: Lymphoid hyperplasia, often following a viral infection.

153. Q: What is an important pathologic lead point for recurrent intussusception?

A: Conditions such as Meckel diverticulum, intestinal polyps, or tumors can serve as lead points.

154. Q: What is the classic presentation of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC)?

A: A premature infant develops feeding intolerance, abdominal distention, bloody stools, temperature instability, and systemic illness.

155. Q: What is the major risk factor for necrotizing enterocolitis?

A: Prematurity, particularly very-low-birth-weight infants.

156. Q: What abdominal radiographic finding is characteristic of NEC?

A: Pneumatosis intestinalis—gas within the intestinal wall.

157. Q: What does portal venous gas indicate in NEC?

A: It indicates severe intestinal injury and is an important marker of disease severity.

158. Q: What is an important initial management step in suspected NEC?

A: Stop enteral feeds, decompress the stomach with an orogastric tube, provide IV fluids/nutrition, and begin broad-spectrum antibiotics.

159. Q: What is the classic presentation of infantile hypertrophic pyloric stenosis in terms of timing?

A: It usually presents at approximately 2–8 weeks of age with progressively worsening projectile, nonbilious vomiting.

160. Q: What electrolyte abnormality can occur in severe pyloric stenosis?

A: Hypokalemia and hypochloremia, along with metabolic alkalosis.

161. Q: What is the initial treatment before surgical pyloromyotomy in hypertrophic pyloric stenosis?

A: Correct dehydration and electrolyte abnormalities first, particularly the metabolic alkalosis.

162. Q: What is the typical presentation of duodenal atresia?

A: A neonate develops bilious vomiting shortly after birth, often with minimal abdominal distention.

163. Q: What is the classic x-ray finding in duodenal atresia?

A: The double-bubble sign, representing air in the stomach and proximal duodenum.

164. Q: Which chromosomal disorder is strongly associated with duodenal atresia?

A: Down syndrome (trisomy 21).

165. Q: What is the classic presentation of esophageal atresia with tracheoesophageal fistula?

A: Excessive drooling, choking, coughing, cyanosis with feeding, and inability to pass an orogastric tube into the stomach.

166. Q: What prenatal finding is associated with esophageal atresia?

A: Polyhydramnios, due to impaired fetal swallowing.

167. Q: What is the most common type of tracheoesophageal fistula?

A: Esophageal atresia with a distal tracheoesophageal fistula.

168. Q: What is the classic chest/abdominal x-ray finding in esophageal atresia with distal TE fistula?

A: The tube coils in the proximal esophageal pouch while air is present in the stomach and intestines through the distal fistula.

169. Q: What is the classic presentation of appendicitis in a young child?

A: Poorly localized abdominal pain, vomiting, fever, and progressive abdominal tenderness, with a relatively high risk of perforation.

170. Q: What is the preferred imaging approach for suspected appendicitis in children?

A: Ultrasound is generally preferred as initial imaging, especially to avoid radiation exposure; CT or MRI may follow if the diagnosis remains uncertain.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

171. Q: What is the most common congenital cardiac lesion associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta.

172. Q: What congenital cardiac abnormality is particularly associated with bicuspid aortic valve?

A: Coarctation of the aorta is a major associated congenital lesion.

173. Q: What is the classic presentation of critical aortic stenosis in a neonate?

A: Poor feeding, tachypnea, weak pulses, heart failure, and shock as ductal flow decreases.

174. Q: Why can critical aortic stenosis be ductal dependent?

A: Severe obstruction prevents adequate left ventricular output; systemic perfusion may depend partly on ductal blood flow.

175. Q: What medication should be given while preparing definitive treatment for ductal-dependent critical aortic stenosis?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (PGE₁).

176. Q: What is the classic murmur of aortic stenosis?

A: A crescendo-decrescendo systolic ejection murmur, usually best heard at the upper right sternal border and radiating toward the neck.

177. Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis in a child?

A: Fatigue, tachypnea, exercise intolerance, chest discomfort, arrhythmias, or signs of congestive heart failure, often following a viral illness.

178. Q: What cardiac biomarker may be elevated in myocarditis?

A: Cardiac troponin, reflecting myocardial injury.

179. Q: What imaging study is particularly useful for assessing ventricular function in suspected pediatric myocarditis?

A: Echocardiography.

180. Q: What is the classic ECG finding in acute pericarditis?

A: Diffuse ST-segment elevation with PR-segment depression.

181. Q: What symptom classically distinguishes pericarditis from myocardial ischemia?

A: Sharp, pleuritic chest pain that improves when sitting forward.

182. Q: What is cardiac tamponade?

A: Accumulation of fluid in the pericardial space causing impaired ventricular filling and reduced cardiac output.

183. Q: What are classic clinical findings of cardiac tamponade?

A: Hypotension, elevated jugular venous pressure, and muffled heart sounds (Beck triad), although all three may not be present in children.

184. Q: What is pulsus paradoxus?

A: An exaggerated inspiratory decrease in systolic blood pressure, classically:

ΔSBP>10 mmHg

185. Q: Why can cardiac tamponade cause pulsus paradoxus?

A: Increased ventricular interdependence within a constrained pericardial space causes inspiratory reduction in left ventricular filling and stroke volume.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

186. Q: What is the classic presentation of pneumonia caused by Mycoplasma pneumoniae in a school-aged child?

A: Gradual onset fever, headache, malaise, and persistent dry cough, often with relatively mild examination findings.

187. Q: Which age group is most commonly affected by Mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia?

A: School-aged children and adolescents.

188. Q: What is a common bacterial cause of pneumonia in infants and young children?

A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is an important typical bacterial cause.

189. Q: What is a common cause of pneumonia in neonates shortly after birth?

A: Depending on timing and circumstances, important pathogens include Group B Streptococcus, gram-negative enteric organisms, and other perinatal pathogens.

190. Q: What is the classic presentation of pertussis-associated pneumonia in an infant?

A: Paroxysmal cough, apnea, cyanosis, poor feeding, and respiratory distress may occur, sometimes without a classic inspiratory whoop.

191. Q: What is the most important clinical sign of respiratory distress in an infant?

A: Increased work of breathing, manifested by findings such as nasal flaring, grunting, retractions, and tachypnea.

192. Q: What does expiratory grunting signify in a newborn?

A: The infant is attempting to maintain positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) and prevent alveolar collapse.

193. Q: What is the classic cause of respiratory distress syndrome in a premature infant?

A: Surfactant deficiency due to immature type II pneumocytes.

194. Q: What is the primary function of pulmonary surfactant?

A: It reduces alveolar surface tension, preventing alveolar collapse.

P=
r
2T
	​


where P is collapsing pressure, T is surface tension, and r is alveolar radius.

195. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of neonatal respiratory distress syndrome?

A: Diffuse reticulogranular ("ground-glass") opacities with air bronchograms and low lung volumes.

196. Q: What medication can accelerate fetal lung maturation when preterm delivery is anticipated?

A: Antenatal corticosteroids, such as betamethasone, when indicated by gestational age and clinical circumstances.

197. Q: What is the classic presentation of meconium aspiration syndrome?

A: A newborn with respiratory distress, often with meconium-stained amniotic fluid, and patchy pulmonary opacities.

198. Q: What serious complication can occur with meconium aspiration?

A: Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN).

199. Q: What is the underlying problem in persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?

A: Persistently elevated pulmonary vascular resistance causes right-to-left shunting through fetal circulatory pathways, producing hypoxemia.

200. Q: What is a characteristic finding in PPHN?

A: Differential oxygen saturation between the preductal and postductal circulations may occur because of right-to-left ductal shunting.

201. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception-associated bowel ischemia?

A: Increasing abdominal pain, abdominal distention, bloody stool, systemic toxicity, and eventually peritonitis or shock.

202. Q: What is the most common cause of acute abdominal pain requiring surgery in children?

A: Appendicitis is the most common surgical cause of acute abdominal pain in children.

203. Q: What is the classic laboratory pattern in acute appendicitis?

A: Leukocytosis with neutrophilia may occur, although normal laboratory values do not exclude appendicitis.

204. Q: What is the classic ultrasound finding of appendicitis?

A: A noncompressible, enlarged appendix, typically >6 mm in diameter, with associated inflammatory findings.

205. Q: What is the typical presentation of viral hepatitis A in an older child?

A: Fever, malaise, anorexia, nausea, abdominal discomfort, followed by jaundice and dark urine.

206. Q: What laboratory pattern is characteristic of acute hepatitis?

A: Markedly elevated AST and ALT, often with increased bilirubin.

207. Q: What is the major route of transmission of hepatitis A?

A: Fecal-oral transmission.

208. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute hepatitis B infection?

A: It may be asymptomatic or cause fatigue, anorexia, nausea, jaundice, and elevated aminotransferases.

209. Q: Which pediatric liver disease is strongly associated with Wilson disease?

A: Chronic hepatitis, cirrhosis, acute liver failure, and neurologic disease can occur because of copper accumulation.

210. Q: What is the characteristic laboratory clue to Wilson disease?

A: Low ceruloplasmin with increased tissue copper; 24-hour urinary copper is often elevated.

211. Q: What eye finding is associated with Wilson disease?

A: Kayser-Fleischer rings, caused by copper deposition in the cornea.

212. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception caused by IgA vasculitis?

A: A child with palpable purpura, abdominal pain, arthralgia, and renal findings may develop intussusception due to bowel-wall edema.

213. Q: What is the classic gastrointestinal manifestation of IgA vasculitis?

A: Colicky abdominal pain and gastrointestinal bleeding due to intestinal vasculitis and edema.

214. Q: What is the classic presentation of Meckel diverticulum inflammation?

A: It can mimic acute appendicitis, causing right lower quadrant abdominal pain, fever, nausea, and vomiting.

215. Q: What embryologic remnant forms Meckel diverticulum?

A: The vitelline (omphalomesenteric) duct.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

216. Q: What is the most common arrhythmia in children?

A: Sinus tachycardia is common; among clinically significant supraventricular tachyarrhythmias, SVT is particularly important.

217. Q: What ECG finding distinguishes SVT from sinus tachycardia in a child?

A: SVT often has a very rapid, regular narrow-complex rhythm with abrupt onset and termination, whereas sinus tachycardia usually has gradual onset and a normal P-wave relationship.

218. Q: What is the first treatment for a hemodynamically stable infant with SVT?

A: Vagal maneuvers, followed by adenosine if the rhythm persists.

219. Q: Why should carotid sinus massage generally not be used in infants?

A: It is less appropriate and potentially hazardous; safer vagal techniques such as applying a cold stimulus to the face are preferred in infants.

220. Q: What is the treatment for unstable pediatric SVT?

A: Synchronized cardioversion.

221. Q: What is the classic ECG feature of Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome?

A: Short PR interval, delta wave, and widened QRS due to ventricular preexcitation.

222. Q: What is the underlying abnormality in Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome?

A: An accessory atrioventricular pathway bypasses the AV node.

223. Q: What is a major concern if a child with WPW develops atrial fibrillation?

A: Rapid conduction through the accessory pathway can cause extremely rapid ventricular rates and potentially ventricular fibrillation.

224. Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis progressing to dilated cardiomyopathy?

A: Exercise intolerance, tachypnea, hepatomegaly, edema, gallop rhythm, and other signs of congestive heart failure.

225. Q: What is the characteristic structural abnormality in dilated cardiomyopathy?

A: Dilated ventricular chambers with impaired systolic function.

226. Q: What is the classic presentation of congestive heart failure in an infant?

A: Tachypnea, feeding difficulty, diaphoresis with feeds, poor weight gain, hepatomegaly, and recurrent respiratory symptoms.

227. Q: Why do infants with heart failure often sweat during feeding?

A: Feeding is an exertional activity that increases cardiac workload and sympathetic activation.

228. Q: What is a gallop rhythm in pediatric heart failure?

A: An S₃ or S₄ heart sound indicating abnormal ventricular filling or compliance.

229. Q: What is the classic cause of heart failure in a young infant with a large left-to-right shunt?

A: Increasing pulmonary blood flow as pulmonary vascular resistance falls after birth.

230. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding in significant left-to-right shunting?
