A: Cardiomegaly and increased pulmonary vascular markings due to increased pulmonary blood flow.

231. Q: What is the physiologic reason newborns with cyanotic congenital heart disease may deteriorate after birth?

A: Closure of the ductus arteriosus can eliminate a critical route for pulmonary or systemic blood flow.

232. Q: What are the major categories of ductal-dependent congenital heart disease?

A: Lesions requiring the ductus for systemic blood flow or pulmonary blood flow, such as critical left-sided obstruction or severe pulmonary outflow obstruction.

233. Q: What is the classic congenital heart lesion with "single-ventricle" physiology that requires staged palliation?

A: Hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS).

234. Q: What is the initial stabilization of a neonate with suspected HLHS?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ infusion to maintain ductal patency, along with stabilization of oxygenation, ventilation, perfusion, and acid-base status.

235. Q: Why can excessive oxygen administration be problematic in certain ductal-dependent systemic lesions?

A: Oxygen can lower pulmonary vascular resistance, potentially increasing pulmonary blood flow at the expense of systemic perfusion in some lesions.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

236. Q: What is the most common cause of wheezing in an infant?

A: Viral bronchiolitis, particularly due to RSV, is a common cause.

237. Q: What is the classic auscultatory finding in bronchiolitis?

A: Diffuse wheezing and crackles, often accompanied by prolonged expiration.

238. Q: Why can bronchiolitis cause feeding difficulty?

A: Tachypnea and nasal congestion interfere with suck-swallow-breathe coordination.

239. Q: What is an important risk factor for severe RSV disease?

A: Prematurity, especially significant prematurity, is a major risk factor; congenital heart disease and chronic lung disease also increase risk.

240. Q: What is the mechanism of airway obstruction in bronchiolitis?

A: Small-airway edema, mucus production, epithelial necrosis, and cellular debris narrow the bronchioles.

241. Q: What is the classic presentation of foreign-body aspiration in a toddler?

A: Sudden coughing, choking, wheezing, or respiratory distress, often during eating or playing.

242. Q: What should be suspected when a previously healthy child develops sudden unilateral wheezing?

A: Foreign-body aspiration until proven otherwise.

243. Q: What is the treatment for complete airway obstruction in a conscious infant?

A: Five back blows followed by five chest thrusts, repeated until the obstruction is relieved or the infant becomes unresponsive.

244. Q: What is the treatment for severe foreign-body airway obstruction in a conscious child older than 1 year?

A: Abdominal thrusts, repeated until the object is expelled or the child becomes unresponsive.

245. Q: What is the classic presentation of an asthma exacerbation?

A: Dyspnea, cough, wheezing, chest tightness, prolonged expiration, and increased work of breathing.

246. Q: What finding indicates a potentially life-threatening asthma exacerbation?

A: Altered mental status, exhaustion, severe hypoxemia, rising PaCO₂, or a silent chest indicates impending or established respiratory failure.

247. Q: Why can a normal or elevated PaCO₂ be alarming during a severe asthma attack?

A: A severely obstructed child initially hyperventilates and develops low PaCO₂. A normalizing or rising PaCO₂ can indicate respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.

248. Q: What is the expected blood-gas pattern early in a severe asthma exacerbation?

A: Respiratory alkalosis due to hyperventilation:

↓PaCO
2
	​

→↓H
+
→↑pH

249. Q: What is the most important long-term environmental intervention for a child with asthma who is exposed to tobacco smoke?

A: Eliminate exposure to tobacco smoke, including secondhand and thirdhand smoke.

250. Q: What is the major pulmonary consequence of poorly controlled persistent asthma in childhood?

A: Recurrent exacerbations and airway inflammation can lead to airway remodeling and persistent impairment of lung function.

251. Q: What is the classic presentation of celiac disease in a child?

A: Chronic diarrhea, abdominal distention, poor weight gain, iron-deficiency anemia, and other manifestations of malabsorption after exposure to gluten.

252. Q: Which intestinal portion is primarily affected in celiac disease?

A: The proximal small intestine, particularly the duodenum and jejunum.

253. Q: What histologic finding is characteristic of celiac disease?

A: Villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes.

254. Q: Which antibody is preferred for initial screening of suspected celiac disease?

A: IgA anti-tissue transglutaminase (tTG) with measurement of total IgA.

255. Q: Why should total IgA be checked when screening for celiac disease?

A: Selective IgA deficiency can cause a falsely negative IgA-based celiac serology.

256. Q: What is the definitive treatment for celiac disease?

A: A lifelong strict gluten-free diet avoiding wheat, barley, and rye.

257. Q: What nutritional deficiency is particularly common in untreated celiac disease?

A: Iron deficiency, which may cause microcytic anemia.

258. Q: What is the classic presentation of lactose intolerance after gastroenteritis?

A: Temporary bloating, abdominal cramps, flatulence, and watery diarrhea after consuming dairy, due to secondary lactase deficiency.

259. Q: What is the mechanism of osmotic diarrhea?

A: Poorly absorbed solutes remain in the intestinal lumen and retain water, increasing stool water content.

260. Q: What happens to diarrhea caused by an osmotic substance when the patient stops ingesting that substance?

A: The diarrhea improves or resolves.

261. Q: What is the classic presentation of secretory diarrhea?

A: Large-volume watery diarrhea that persists despite fasting, caused by active intestinal secretion or impaired absorption.

262. Q: What is a common cause of secretory diarrhea in children?

A: Enterotoxin-producing infections, including certain strains of E. coli.

263. Q: What is the classic presentation of Shiga toxin-producing E. coli infection?

A: Abdominal cramps and bloody diarrhea, typically without high fever.

264. Q: What serious complication can follow Shiga toxin-producing E. coli infection?

A: Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS).

265. Q: What are the classic features of HUS?

A: Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury.

266. Q: What peripheral blood smear finding is characteristic of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia?

A: Schistocytes (fragmented RBCs).

267. Q: Why should antibiotics generally be avoided in suspected STEC infection?

A: They may increase Shiga toxin release and potentially increase the risk of HUS.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

268. Q: What is the most common cause of acquired heart disease in children in developed countries?

A: Kawasaki disease is an important acquired cardiovascular disease of childhood.

269. Q: What laboratory findings commonly accompany Kawasaki disease?

A: Elevated inflammatory markers, leukocytosis, thrombocytosis during the subacute phase, and sometimes sterile pyuria or elevated liver enzymes.

270. Q: What is the characteristic hand/foot finding in Kawasaki disease?

A: Erythema and edema of the hands and feet, followed later by periungual desquamation.

271. Q: What oral finding is characteristic of Kawasaki disease?

A: Strawberry tongue and erythematous, cracked lips.

272. Q: What is the preferred cardiac imaging study for monitoring Kawasaki disease?

A: Echocardiography, particularly for assessing coronary artery abnormalities and ventricular function.

273. Q: What is the major long-term cardiac concern after Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery aneurysm and thrombosis, which can cause myocardial ischemia or infarction.

274. Q: What is the classic presentation of an innocent Still murmur?

A: A soft, vibratory/ musical systolic ejection murmur, usually heard along the lower left sternal border, without other abnormal cardiac findings.

275. Q: What features suggest a pathologic rather than innocent murmur?

A: Diastolic murmurs, harsh or loud murmurs, holosystolic murmurs, abnormal S₂, cyanosis, syncope, abnormal pulses, or signs of heart failure.

276. Q: What is the classic murmur of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: A harsh systolic ejection murmur that increases with decreased preload, such as during standing or Valsalva.

277. Q: Why does the murmur of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy increase with Valsalva?

A: Reduced venous return decreases LV volume, bringing the hypertrophied septum closer to the mitral valve and worsening LV outflow obstruction.

278. Q: What symptom in an adolescent athlete should raise concern for hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: Exertional syncope, particularly with a family history of sudden cardiac death.

279. Q: What is the most common cause of sudden cardiac death in young competitive athletes?

A: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is a major cause, although other structural and electrical disorders can also cause sudden death.

280. Q: What is the classic ECG finding associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: Left ventricular hypertrophy, often with repolarization abnormalities and sometimes deep narrow Q waves.

281. Q: What congenital cardiac lesion causes right ventricular volume overload?

A: A significant atrial septal defect (ASD) causes chronic left-to-right flow into the right atrium and ventricle.

282. Q: What is the characteristic ECG finding in an ostium secundum ASD?

A: Incomplete right bundle branch block with right ventricular conduction/volume-overload findings may occur.

283. Q: What is the most common type of ASD?

A: Ostium secundum ASD.

284. Q: What is the classic complication of an unrepaired large ASD in adulthood?

A: Pulmonary hypertension and eventual Eisenmenger syndrome, although this typically develops later than with some other large shunts.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

285. Q: What is the most common cause of respiratory distress syndrome in premature infants?

A: Pulmonary surfactant deficiency due to immature type II pneumocytes.

286. Q: What is the main function of surfactant protein and phospholipid in the alveoli?

A: They reduce surface tension, increasing lung compliance and preventing alveolar collapse.

287. Q: Which fetal hormone accelerates pulmonary surfactant production?

A: Cortisol promotes fetal lung maturation and surfactant production.

288. Q: What antenatal medication is used to reduce neonatal respiratory morbidity when preterm delivery is anticipated?

A: Betamethasone or dexamethasone given antenatally when indicated.

289. Q: What is a major complication of respiratory distress syndrome in premature infants?

A: Bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD), especially in extremely premature infants requiring prolonged respiratory support.

290. Q: What is the classic clinical definition of bronchopulmonary dysplasia?

A: Chronic respiratory disease in a premature infant requiring ongoing oxygen or respiratory support beyond the expected period of acute respiratory illness, with definitions varying by gestational age and contemporary criteria.

291. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of bronchopulmonary dysplasia?

A: Heterogeneous areas of hyperinflation and atelectasis with interstitial changes in established disease.

292. Q: What is a major long-term respiratory complication of extreme prematurity?

A: Chronic lung disease/bronchopulmonary dysplasia, with increased susceptibility to respiratory infections and wheezing.

293. Q: What is the classic presentation of pulmonary edema due to congenital heart disease?

A: Tachypnea, increased work of breathing, feeding difficulty, crackles, hepatomegaly, and poor weight gain.

294. Q: Why does pulmonary edema occur with a significant left-to-right cardiac shunt?

A: Excessive pulmonary blood flow increases pulmonary capillary hydrostatic pressure, promoting fluid movement into the interstitium and alveoli.

295. Q: What is the classic presentation of pleural effusion associated with bacterial pneumonia?

A: Persistent fever, respiratory distress, pleuritic chest pain, and decreased breath sounds despite appropriate treatment.

296. Q: What is the preferred imaging study for detecting a complicated parapneumonic effusion?

A: Chest ultrasound is highly useful for identifying and characterizing pleural fluid and septations.

297. Q: What is an empyema?

A: Infected purulent fluid within the pleural space, usually occurring as a complication of bacterial pneumonia.

298. Q: What is the classic presentation of a pulmonary abscess in a child?

A: Persistent fever, cough, systemic illness, and a cavitary lung lesion, often following aspiration or severe bacterial infection.

299. Q: What is the most important initial management principle for a child with severe respiratory distress?

A: Rapidly assess and support airway, breathing, and circulation, while providing supplemental oxygen and escalating respiratory support as needed.

300. Q: What clinical finding indicates impending respiratory failure in a child?

A: Altered mental status, exhaustion, apnea, severe hypoxemia despite oxygen, poor respiratory effort, or bradycardia indicates impending respiratory failure and requires immediate escalation of support.

301. Q: What is the classic presentation of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC) on abdominal examination?

A: Abdominal distention, tenderness, decreased bowel sounds, and sometimes abdominal wall erythema or discoloration in a sick premature infant.

302. Q: What radiographic finding in NEC suggests intestinal perforation?

A: Pneumoperitoneum (free intraperitoneal air).

303. Q: What is the major surgical indication in necrotizing enterocolitis?

A: Intestinal perforation, or clinical deterioration despite maximal medical therapy with evidence of necrotic bowel.

304. Q: What is the classic presentation of spontaneous intestinal perforation in a very premature infant?

A: Sudden abdominal distention and clinical deterioration, often with free intraperitoneal air, but without the classic pneumatosis of NEC.

305. Q: What is the classic presentation of congenital diaphragmatic hernia (CDH)?

A: A newborn with severe respiratory distress, scaphoid abdomen, decreased breath sounds on the affected side, and bowel sounds in the chest.

306. Q: Which side is most commonly affected by congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: The left side (Bochdalek hernia) is most common.

307. Q: What is the primary respiratory problem in congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

A: Pulmonary hypoplasia with associated pulmonary hypertension due to compression of developing lungs.

308. Q: What is an important initial management principle for a newborn with CDH?

A: Avoid bag-mask ventilation because it can inflate herniated bowel and worsen pulmonary compression; use endotracheal intubation and gastric decompression.

309. Q: What is the classic presentation of esophageal atresia without a distal tracheoesophageal fistula?

A: Excessive secretions, choking/cyanosis, and inability to pass an orogastric tube, with little or no gas in the abdomen.

310. Q: What prenatal finding strongly suggests esophageal atresia?

A: Polyhydramnios due to impaired fetal swallowing.

311. Q: What is the classic presentation of congenital diaphragmatic hernia on prenatal ultrasound?

A: Abdominal organs, particularly bowel, visualized within the thoracic cavity, often with mediastinal shift.

312. Q: What is the major vascular complication of severe portal hypertension in a child?

A: Esophageal varices, which can cause life-threatening upper gastrointestinal bleeding.

313. Q: What is the classic presentation of esophageal variceal bleeding?

A: Sudden, painless hematemesis in a child with portal hypertension or chronic liver disease.

314. Q: What is the initial priority in a child with massive variceal hemorrhage?

A: Airway protection and hemodynamic stabilization, followed by urgent endoscopic therapy.

315. Q: What is the preferred endoscopic treatment for esophageal variceal bleeding?

A: Endoscopic variceal ligation when technically feasible.

316. Q: What is a major cause of portal hypertension in children worldwide?

A: Chronic liver disease/cirrhosis is an important cause; extrahepatic portal vein obstruction is also a major pediatric cause in some settings.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

317. Q: What is the most common cause of acquired heart disease following an upper respiratory infection in children?

A: Acute rheumatic fever can follow untreated group A streptococcal pharyngitis.

318. Q: How long after streptococcal pharyngitis does acute rheumatic fever typically occur?

A: Usually 2–4 weeks after the infection.

319. Q: What diagnostic framework is used for acute rheumatic fever?

A: The Jones criteria, requiring evidence of preceding group A streptococcal infection plus the appropriate combination of major and minor criteria.

320. Q: What are the major Jones criteria?

A: Carditis, migratory polyarthritis, Sydenham chorea, erythema marginatum, and subcutaneous nodules.

321. Q: What is Sydenham chorea?

A: A delayed neurologic manifestation of rheumatic fever characterized by involuntary, purposeless movements, emotional lability, and motor incoordination.

322. Q: What type of arthritis is characteristic of acute rheumatic fever?

A: Migratory polyarthritis, typically involving large joints.

323. Q: What is the classic skin lesion of acute rheumatic fever?

A: Erythema marginatum—a nonpruritic, serpiginous, erythematous rash mainly on the trunk and proximal extremities.

324. Q: What is the classic congenital cardiac lesion associated with fetal alcohol exposure?

A: Atrial septal defect, ventricular septal defect, and other congenital heart abnormalities can occur; no single cardiac lesion is uniquely diagnostic.

325. Q: What congenital heart lesion is particularly associated with maternal rubella infection?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus, along with pulmonary artery stenosis and other congenital abnormalities.

326. Q: What congenital cardiac lesion is associated with DiGeorge syndrome?

A: Conotruncal abnormalities, particularly Tetralogy of Fallot and interrupted aortic arch.

327. Q: What is the classic cardiac association of Noonan syndrome?

A: Pulmonary valve stenosis is the characteristic congenital heart lesion.

328. Q: What is the classic cardiac association of Marfan syndrome?

A: Aortic root dilation with risk of aortic aneurysm/dissection and mitral valve prolapse.

329. Q: What is the classic presentation of aortic dissection in a patient with Marfan syndrome?

A: Sudden severe chest or back pain, potentially accompanied by pulse or blood-pressure asymmetry and hemodynamic instability.

330. Q: What is the characteristic murmur of mitral regurgitation?

A: A holosystolic murmur best heard at the apex, often radiating toward the axilla.

331. Q: What is the characteristic murmur of mitral stenosis?

A: A low-pitched diastolic rumble with an opening snap, best heard at the apex.

332. Q: What is the classic cardiac manifestation of infective endocarditis in a child?

A: Fever with a new or changing murmur, sometimes accompanied by embolic or immune-mediated findings.

333. Q: What is an important risk factor for infective endocarditis in children?

A: Certain congenital heart defects, prosthetic material/valves, or previous infective endocarditis increase risk.

334. Q: What organism commonly causes infective endocarditis in children with congenital heart disease?

A: Viridans group streptococci and Staphylococcus aureus are important causes, depending on the clinical setting.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

335. Q: What is the classic presentation of pneumococcal pneumonia on chest x-ray?

A: Lobar consolidation, although radiographic patterns can overlap among pathogens.

336. Q: What is a major complication of bacterial pneumonia in children?

A: Parapneumonic effusion/empyema, lung abscess, necrotizing pneumonia, or bacteremia.

337. Q: What clinical course suggests complicated bacterial pneumonia?

A: Persistent fever or respiratory distress despite appropriate antibiotics, especially with pleural fluid or worsening radiographic findings.

338. Q: What is the classic presentation of atypical pneumonia caused by Mycoplasma?

A: Gradual-onset dry cough, fever, headache, and malaise in a school-aged child or adolescent.

339. Q: What extrapulmonary manifestation can occur with Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection?

A: Rash, mucositis, neurologic complications, hemolytic anemia, and other extrapulmonary manifestations can occur.

340. Q: What is the classic presentation of viral croup?

A: Barking cough, hoarseness, and inspiratory stridor, often worse at night.

341. Q: What is the characteristic radiographic finding in croup?

A: Steeple sign—subglottic narrowing on an AP neck radiograph, although imaging is usually unnecessary in typical cases.

342. Q: What distinguishes stridor from wheezing?

A: Stridor generally indicates upper-airway obstruction, whereas wheezing usually reflects lower-airway narrowing.

343. Q: What does inspiratory stridor usually indicate?

A: Extrathoracic upper-airway obstruction, such as laryngomalacia, croup, or epiglottitis.

344. Q: What does expiratory stridor or wheezing generally suggest?

A: Intrathoracic or lower-airway obstruction, such as asthma or bronchiolitis.

345. Q: What is the classic presentation of laryngomalacia?

A: Chronic inspiratory stridor beginning during the first weeks of life, often worsened by feeding, crying, or supine positioning.

346. Q: What is the most common congenital cause of chronic stridor in infancy?

A: Laryngomalacia.

347. Q: What is the classic presentation of tracheomalacia?

A: Expiratory or biphasic noisy breathing, cough, recurrent respiratory infections, and symptoms that may worsen with crying or exertion.

348. Q: What is the underlying problem in tracheomalacia?

A: Excessive collapsibility of the tracheal walls due to weakness of the supporting cartilage.

349. Q: What is the classic presentation of obstructive sleep apnea caused by adenotonsillar hypertrophy?

A: Habitual snoring, witnessed apneas, restless sleep, mouth breathing, and daytime behavioral or learning problems.

350. Q: What is the gold-standard diagnostic test for pediatric obstructive sleep apnea when the diagnosis requires objective confirmation?

A: Overnight polysomnography (sleep study).

351. Q: What is the classic presentation of pyloric stenosis on physical examination?

A: A palpable “olive-shaped” mass in the right upper quadrant/epigastrium, sometimes accompanied by visible gastric peristaltic waves.

352. Q: Why is vomiting in pyloric stenosis nonbilious?

A: The obstruction is at the gastric outlet, proximal to the duodenum and ampulla of Vater, so bile does not enter the vomitus.

353. Q: What metabolic abnormality should be corrected before pyloromyotomy?

A: Hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis and volume depletion should be corrected before surgery.

354. Q: What is the characteristic presentation of congenital chloride diarrhea?

A: Profuse watery diarrhea beginning in the neonatal period, abdominal distention, dehydration, and metabolic alkalosis.

355. Q: What is the mechanism of congenital chloride diarrhea?

A: Defective intestinal Cl⁻/HCO₃⁻ exchange causes excessive chloride secretion into the intestinal lumen.

356. Q: What acid-base abnormality is characteristic of congenital chloride diarrhea?

A: Hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis.

357. Q: What is the classic presentation of congenital lactase deficiency?

A: Severe watery diarrhea, abdominal distention, and dehydration beginning with the first exposure to lactose-containing feeds.

358. Q: What is the classic presentation of necrotizing enterocolitis in a premature infant?

A: Feeding intolerance followed by abdominal distention, bloody stools, systemic instability, and possible intestinal perforation.

359. Q: Why does NEC occur predominantly in premature infants?

A: Immature intestinal defenses, abnormal microbial colonization, and intestinal ischemia contribute to intestinal mucosal injury and inflammation.

360. Q: What is the most important nutritional intervention to reduce NEC risk in premature infants?

A: Human breast milk is preferred when available because it is associated with a lower risk of NEC than formula feeding.

361. Q: What is the classic presentation of Meckel diverticulum bleeding?

A: Painless, sometimes substantial lower GI bleeding, often caused by acid-producing ectopic gastric mucosa.

362. Q: What is the "rule of 2s" historically associated with Meckel diverticulum?

A: Approximately 2% of the population, located within 2 feet of the ileocecal valve, about 2 inches long, and may present before age 2 years. These are teaching heuristics rather than exact epidemiologic rules.

363. Q: What is the classic presentation of a Meckel diverticulum causing obstruction?

A: Abdominal pain, vomiting, distention, and intestinal obstruction, potentially from intussusception or a fibrous band.

364. Q: What is the classic presentation of volvulus caused by intestinal malrotation?

A: Sudden bilious vomiting with abdominal pain and rapid clinical deterioration due to possible bowel ischemia.

365. Q: What is the preferred initial management when volvulus is strongly suspected?

A: Immediate surgical consultation and urgent operative management, while simultaneously stabilizing the child.

366. Q: What is the classic presentation of Hirschsprung disease in a neonate?

A: Delayed meconium passage, abdominal distention, vomiting, and constipation, often followed by explosive stool after rectal examination.

367. Q: What is the characteristic rectal examination finding in Hirschsprung disease?

A: An empty rectal vault, followed by explosive passage of stool/gas after withdrawal of the examining finger.

368. Q: What is the definitive treatment for Hirschsprung disease?

A: Surgical resection of the aganglionic segment with pull-through of normally innervated bowel.

369. Q: What is a serious complication of Hirschsprung disease?

A: Hirschsprung-associated enterocolitis, characterized by fever, abdominal distention, diarrhea, and systemic toxicity.

370. Q: What is the classic presentation of functional abdominal pain in children?

A: Chronic or recurrent abdominal pain without evidence of an organic disorder, often with normal growth and physical examination.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

371. Q: What is the classic ECG finding in atrial septal defect?

A: Right-axis deviation and incomplete right bundle branch block may occur, particularly with significant right-sided volume overload.

372. Q: What is the classic auscultatory finding in ASD caused by increased pulmonary blood flow?

A: A systolic ejection murmur at the upper left sternal border due to increased flow across the pulmonic valve.

373. Q: Why is the S₂ fixed and widely split in ASD?

A: Persistent right ventricular volume overload causes delayed pulmonic valve closure that does not vary normally with respiration.

374. Q: What is the classic presentation of a large ventricular septal defect during infancy?

A: Poor feeding, diaphoresis with feeds, tachypnea, recurrent respiratory infections, and failure to thrive as pulmonary vascular resistance falls.

375. Q: What happens to pulmonary vascular resistance immediately after birth?

A: It falls substantially as the lungs expand and pulmonary blood flow increases.

376. Q: Why does a large VSD often become symptomatic several weeks after birth?

A: The fall in pulmonary vascular resistance increases left-to-right shunting and pulmonary blood flow, producing volume overload and heart failure.

377. Q: What is the classic murmur of a PDA?

A: A continuous machine-like murmur, often loudest near the left upper sternal border.

378. Q: What pulse finding is associated with a significant PDA?

A: Bounding pulses with widened pulse pressure.

379. Q: What is the classic murmur of pulmonary valve stenosis?

A: A crescendo-decrescendo systolic ejection murmur at the upper left sternal border.

380. Q: What syndrome is strongly associated with pulmonary valve stenosis?

A: Noonan syndrome.

381. Q: What is the classic physical finding in coarctation of the aorta?

A: Weak or delayed femoral pulses with higher blood pressure in the arms than in the legs.

382. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding in longstanding coarctation?

A: Inferior rib notching from enlarged collateral intercostal arteries; a "figure-3" contour may also be seen.

383. Q: Why does coarctation cause upper-extremity hypertension?

A: The obstruction increases pressure proximal to the narrowed aortic segment, producing hypertension in vessels supplying the upper body.

384. Q: What is the classic presentation of Tetralogy of Fallot?

A: Cyanosis, exertional symptoms, systolic ejection murmur, and episodic hypercyanotic spells in more severe obstruction.

385. Q: What determines the severity of cyanosis in Tetralogy of Fallot?

A: Primarily the degree of right ventricular outflow tract obstruction.

386. Q: Why does squatting improve exercise tolerance in children with Tetralogy of Fallot?

A: Squatting increases systemic vascular resistance, reducing right-to-left shunting and improving pulmonary blood flow.

387. Q: What is the classic x-ray appearance of Tetralogy of Fallot?

A: A boot-shaped cardiac silhouette due largely to right ventricular hypertrophy.

388. Q: What is the classic presentation of transposition of the great arteries?

A: Severe neonatal cyanosis shortly after birth, often with relatively little response to supplemental oxygen.

389. Q: What anatomical arrangement produces TGA?

A: Ventriculoarterial discordance: the right ventricle connects to the aorta and the left ventricle connects to the pulmonary artery.

390. Q: What is the immediate physiologic goal in a severely cyanotic newborn with suspected TGA?

A: Improve mixing of systemic and pulmonary circulations, often by maintaining ductal patency with PGE₁ and, when necessary, performing balloon atrial septostomy.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

391. Q: What is the classic presentation of neonatal transient tachypnea?

A: Early respiratory distress with tachypnea, often beginning shortly after birth and resolving within approximately 1–3 days.

392. Q: What is the major pathophysiologic problem in transient tachypnea of the newborn?

A: Delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid.

393. Q: What is a major risk factor for transient tachypnea of the newborn?

A: Cesarean delivery without labor, particularly at term or near term.

394. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance of transient tachypnea?

A: Hyperinflation with prominent pulmonary vascular markings and fluid in the interlobar fissures.

395. Q: How does neonatal respiratory distress syndrome differ from transient tachypnea radiographically?

A: RDS typically shows low lung volumes and diffuse ground-glass opacities with air bronchograms, whereas TTN often shows hyperinflation and prominent vascular/interstitial markings.

396. Q: What is the classic presentation of neonatal respiratory distress syndrome?

A: Tachypnea, grunting, nasal flaring, retractions, cyanosis, and increasing oxygen requirement shortly after premature birth.

397. Q: What is the pathophysiologic consequence of surfactant deficiency?

A: Increased alveolar surface tension causes alveolar collapse, decreased lung compliance, increased work of breathing, and impaired gas exchange.

398. Q: What is the classic presentation of persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?

A: Severe hypoxemia with relatively clear or nonspecific lung findings, caused by persistently elevated pulmonary vascular resistance and right-to-left shunting.

399. Q: What is an important treatment for severe PPHN?

A: Inhaled nitric oxide can selectively reduce pulmonary vascular resistance in appropriately selected term or near-term infants.

400. Q: What is the mechanism of inhaled nitric oxide in PPHN?

A: It activates guanylate cyclase → increases cGMP → relaxes pulmonary vascular smooth muscle, reducing pulmonary vascular resistance.

NO→↑cGMP→pulmonary vasodilation

401. Q: What is the classic presentation of infantile colic?

A: Recurrent episodes of excessive crying in an otherwise healthy, normally growing infant, often occurring in the evening and resolving spontaneously by about 3–4 months.

402. Q: What finding argues against infantile colic?

A: Poor weight gain, fever, bilious vomiting, bloody stool, persistent abdominal distention, or an abnormal physical examination suggests an underlying disorder.

403. Q: What is the classic presentation of cow's milk protein allergy?

A: Infants may develop blood-streaked stools, vomiting, diarrhea, eczema, or feeding intolerance after exposure to cow's milk protein.

404. Q: What is a typical presentation of food protein-induced allergic proctocolitis?

A: A well-appearing young infant with small amounts of bright-red blood and mucus in the stool, usually without systemic illness.

405. Q: What is the treatment for food protein-induced allergic proctocolitis in a breastfed infant?

A: Maternal elimination of the offending protein, commonly cow's milk, while continuing breastfeeding.

406. Q: What is the classic presentation of food protein-induced enterocolitis syndrome (FPIES)?

A: Profuse repetitive vomiting, lethargy, pallor, and sometimes diarrhea occurring hours after ingestion of a trigger food.

407. Q: How does FPIES differ from IgE-mediated food allergy?

A: FPIES is generally a non-IgE-mediated gastrointestinal hypersensitivity, typically producing delayed vomiting rather than immediate urticaria or anaphylaxis.

408. Q: What is the classic presentation of intussusception after a viral infection?

A: An otherwise healthy child develops episodic severe abdominal pain with vomiting and lethargy, sometimes followed by bloody stool.

409. Q: Why can children with intussusception become lethargic between pain episodes?

A: Intermittent visceral pain and systemic stress can produce marked lethargy, which may be an important clue even when abdominal findings are initially subtle.

410. Q: What is the first priority when a child with intussusception is hemodynamically unstable?

A: Resuscitation with IV fluids and stabilization of airway/breathing/circulation before definitive reduction.

411. Q: What is the classic presentation of bowel obstruction caused by adhesions in a child?

A: Colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, abdominal distention, and decreased passage of stool/flatus, especially in a child with prior abdominal surgery.

412. Q: What type of vomiting strongly suggests intestinal obstruction distal to the ampulla of Vater?

A: Bilious (green) vomiting.

413. Q: What is the classic presentation of incarcerated bowel obstruction?

A: Persistent abdominal pain, vomiting, distention, tenderness, and systemic toxicity, with concern for strangulation and ischemia.

414. Q: What is the classic presentation of gastroenteritis-associated dehydration?

A: Decreased urine output, dry mucous membranes, tachycardia, reduced tears, and delayed capillary refill, with severity depending on the degree of fluid loss.

415. Q: Which finding is most concerning for hypovolemic shock in a dehydrated child?

A: Hypotension, because it is generally a late sign in pediatric hypovolemic shock.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

416. Q: What is the most common congenital heart defect overall?

A: Ventricular septal defect (VSD).

417. Q: What is the most common cyanotic congenital heart disease presenting in the neonatal period?

A: Transposition of the great arteries (TGA) is a major cause of profound neonatal cyanosis.

418. Q: What congenital heart lesion classically produces cyanosis that worsens during feeding or crying?

A: Tetralogy of Fallot, particularly in infants with significant right ventricular outflow obstruction.

419. Q: What is the classic treatment for a hypercyanotic tet spell after positioning and oxygen?

A: Morphine, IV fluids, and β-blockade such as propranolol may be used depending on severity and clinical circumstances; severe refractory cases require escalation.

420. Q: Why can morphine help during a tet spell?

A: It reduces agitation and catecholamine-driven increases in right ventricular outflow obstruction.

421. Q: What is the classic presentation of an atrioventricular septal defect?

A: Congestive heart failure, recurrent respiratory infections, poor growth, and a characteristic murmur, especially in infants with complete AVSD.

422. Q: Which chromosomal abnormality is strongly associated with complete AVSD?

A: Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome).

423. Q: What is the embryologic defect underlying AV septal defects?

A: Abnormal development of the endocardial cushions, resulting in defects of the atrial/ventricular septa and AV valves.

424. Q: What is the classic presentation of Ebstein anomaly?

A: Tricuspid regurgitation, right-sided heart failure, cyanosis, and arrhythmias, particularly supraventricular tachyarrhythmias.

425. Q: What is the anatomical abnormality in Ebstein anomaly?

A: Apical displacement of the tricuspid valve, producing an abnormally large right atrium and "atrialized" portion of the right ventricle.

426. Q: What maternal medication exposure is associated with Ebstein anomaly?

A: Lithium exposure during pregnancy is a classic association.

427. Q: What is the classic presentation of truncus arteriosus?

A: A newborn with cyanosis, heart failure, increased pulmonary blood flow, and a single arterial trunk supplying systemic and pulmonary circulation.

428. Q: Which congenital syndrome is associated with truncus arteriosus?

A: 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge syndrome) is an important association.

429. Q: What is the key anatomical feature of truncus arteriosus?

A: A single arterial trunk arises from the heart and supplies systemic, pulmonary, and coronary circulation, usually accompanied by a VSD.

430. Q: What is the classic presentation of total anomalous pulmonary venous return (TAPVR)?

A: Cyanosis with varying degrees of respiratory distress and heart failure, depending on whether pulmonary venous drainage is obstructed.

431. Q: Why is an atrial septal communication essential in TAPVR?

A: All pulmonary venous blood returns to the right atrium, so an ASD or patent foramen ovale allows oxygenated blood to reach the systemic circulation.

432. Q: What is the classic radiographic appearance of unobstructed supracardiac TAPVR?

A: The "snowman" or "figure-of-8" appearance, usually developing after the first few months rather than immediately after birth.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

433. Q: What is the classic presentation of respiratory syncytial virus bronchiolitis?

A: A viral upper-respiratory prodrome followed by tachypnea, wheezing, crackles, retractions, and feeding difficulty.

434. Q: What is the major reason infants with bronchiolitis may require hospitalization?

A: Hypoxemia, apnea, dehydration, or significant respiratory distress, particularly in high-risk infants.

435. Q: What is the recommended routine therapy for uncomplicated bronchiolitis?

A: Supportive care, including hydration and supplemental oxygen when clinically indicated.

436. Q: Are routine antibiotics indicated for uncomplicated viral bronchiolitis?

A: No. Antibiotics do not treat the viral infection and are reserved for a documented or strongly suspected bacterial co-infection.

437. Q: What is the classic presentation of croup with severe upper-airway obstruction?

A: Stridor at rest, significant retractions, tachypnea, agitation, and potentially hypoxemia or fatigue.

438. Q: What is the immediate medication for severe croup?

A: Nebulized epinephrine plus systemic corticosteroid, with close observation and airway support as needed.

439. Q: Why does nebulized epinephrine rapidly improve severe croup?

A: Its α₁-adrenergic vasoconstrictive effect reduces subglottic mucosal edema.

440. Q: What is the classic presentation of epiglottitis?

A: High fever, toxic appearance, drooling, dysphagia, muffled voice, tripod positioning, and inspiratory distress.

441. Q: Why should a child with suspected epiglottitis not be forced to lie down?

A: Agitation or positioning changes can worsen upper-airway obstruction and precipitate complete airway collapse.

442. Q: What organism caused most classic childhood epiglottitis before widespread vaccination?

A: Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib).

443. Q: What is the classic presentation of bacterial tracheitis?

A: High fever, toxic appearance, stridor, respiratory distress, and thick purulent airway secretions, often following viral croup.

444. Q: What is the key difference between bacterial tracheitis and viral croup?

A: Bacterial tracheitis generally produces a more toxic appearance, higher fever, and purulent secretions, with potentially severe airway obstruction.

445. Q: What is the classic presentation of a severe asthma exacerbation?

A: Marked dyspnea, tachypnea, retractions, wheezing or absent breath sounds, inability to speak normally, and hypoxemia.

446. Q: What is the first-line bronchodilator for an acute asthma exacerbation?

A: Inhaled albuterol, a short-acting β₂-adrenergic agonist.

447. Q: What additional inhaled medication is commonly added for moderate-to-severe pediatric asthma exacerbations?

A: Ipratropium, an inhaled anticholinergic bronchodilator.

448. Q: What systemic medication should be given early in a moderate-to-severe asthma exacerbation?

A: Systemic corticosteroids, such as oral prednisone/prednisolone or IV methylprednisolone when oral therapy is not feasible.

449. Q: What is the classic indication for magnesium sulfate in acute pediatric asthma?

A: Severe asthma with inadequate response to initial bronchodilator and corticosteroid therapy, particularly when significant airflow obstruction persists.

450. Q: What is the most concerning respiratory finding in a child with severe asthma?

A: Decreasing wheezing accompanied by worsening respiratory effort, altered mental status, fatigue, or poor air movement—a "silent chest" may indicate impending respiratory failure.

451. Q: What is the classic presentation of inflammatory bowel disease in an adolescent?

A: Chronic diarrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss, growth delay, and sometimes blood in the stool; extraintestinal manifestations may also occur.

452. Q: What clinical feature favors Crohn disease over ulcerative colitis?

A: Perianal disease, transmural inflammation, skip lesions, and involvement of any part of the GI tract favor Crohn disease.

453. Q: What clinical feature favors ulcerative colitis over Crohn disease?

A: Continuous colonic inflammation beginning in the rectum with bloody diarrhea favors ulcerative colitis.

454. Q: What is the characteristic endoscopic appearance of Crohn disease?

A: Patchy ulceration with a cobblestone appearance, reflecting areas of inflamed mucosa separated by relatively normal tissue.

455. Q: What is the characteristic histologic finding that may occur in Crohn disease?

A: Noncaseating granulomas, although they are not present in every patient.

456. Q: What is the major nutritional concern in pediatric Crohn disease?

A: Poor linear growth and delayed puberty due to chronic inflammation, inadequate intake, and malabsorption.

457. Q: What is the classic complication of ulcerative colitis involving the colon?

A: Toxic megacolon, characterized by severe colitis with systemic toxicity and colonic dilation.

458. Q: What clinical findings suggest toxic megacolon?

A: Fever, tachycardia, abdominal distention, abdominal tenderness, systemic toxicity, and marked colonic dilation.

459. Q: What is the major immediate danger of toxic megacolon?

A: Colonic perforation, sepsis, and hemodynamic instability.

460. Q: What hepatobiliary disease is strongly associated with ulcerative colitis?

A: Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC).

461. Q: What is the classic laboratory pattern of cholestatic liver disease?

A: Predominant elevation of alkaline phosphatase and γ-glutamyl transferase (GGT), often with elevated conjugated bilirubin.

462. Q: What imaging finding is characteristic of primary sclerosing cholangitis?

A: Multifocal strictures and dilation of the intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts, producing a "beaded" appearance on cholangiography.

463. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute pancreatitis in an older child?

A: Acute epigastric abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting, often with elevated serum lipase.

464. Q: Which pancreatic enzyme is generally more useful for diagnosing acute pancreatitis?

A: Serum lipase, because it remains elevated longer and is more specific than amylase.

465. Q: What are important causes of acute pancreatitis in children?

A: Trauma, medications, infections, systemic disease, gallstones, metabolic disorders, and genetic causes.

466. Q: What is the classic presentation of gallstone disease in an adolescent?

A: Right upper quadrant or epigastric pain, often after fatty meals, with possible nausea or vomiting.

467. Q: Which pediatric population has increased risk of cholesterol gallstones?

A: Children with hemolytic disorders, obesity, rapid weight loss, or certain chronic illnesses are at increased risk.

468. Q: What is the classic presentation of biliary atresia?

A: Persistent neonatal jaundice, pale/acholic stools, dark urine, and hepatomegaly.

469. Q: Why are pale stools important in suspected biliary atresia?

A: They indicate lack of bile reaching the intestine, suggesting extrahepatic biliary obstruction.

470. Q: What is the definitive treatment for biliary atresia?

A: Kasai portoenterostomy, preferably performed early, followed by liver transplantation if progressive liver disease develops.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

471. Q: What is the classic presentation of Kawasaki disease in the acute phase?

A: Persistent fever plus bilateral conjunctival injection, oral mucosal changes, polymorphous rash, extremity changes, and cervical lymphadenopathy.

472. Q: What is the preferred initial treatment for Kawasaki disease?

A: IVIG plus aspirin, administered promptly to reduce the risk of coronary artery complications.

473. Q: Why is aspirin used in Kawasaki disease?

A: It provides anti-inflammatory and antiplatelet effects, particularly important during the subacute phase.

474. Q: What is the most important cardiac complication of Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery aneurysm.

475. Q: What should be suspected when a child has fever for several days plus conjunctival injection, rash, oral changes, and extremity edema?

A: Kawasaki disease, particularly if no alternative diagnosis explains the findings.

476. Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis in a child?

A: Fatigue, tachycardia disproportionate to fever, dyspnea, chest pain, arrhythmias, and signs of heart failure.

477. Q: What viral infection is classically associated with myocarditis?

A: Coxsackie B/enteroviruses are classic associations, although many viruses can cause myocarditis.

478. Q: What ECG abnormalities can occur in myocarditis?

A: Sinus tachycardia, nonspecific ST-T changes, conduction abnormalities, and arrhythmias may occur.

479. Q: What echocardiographic finding is typical of myocarditis with ventricular dysfunction?

A: Global reduction in ventricular systolic function, sometimes with ventricular dilation.

480. Q: What is the classic presentation of pericarditis in an adolescent?

A: Sharp, pleuritic chest pain that improves when sitting forward and worsens when supine.

481. Q: What ECG finding is characteristic of acute pericarditis?

A: Diffuse ST-segment elevation with PR-segment depression, typically without a localized coronary distribution.

482. Q: What is the major complication of pericardial effusion?

A: Cardiac tamponade, which impairs ventricular filling and can cause obstructive shock.

483. Q: What clinical finding is classically associated with cardiac tamponade?

A: Hypotension, elevated jugular venous pressure, and muffled heart sounds—Beck triad—although the complete triad is not always present in children.

484. Q: What is pulsus paradoxus?

A: An exaggerated inspiratory fall in systolic blood pressure, classically >10 mm Hg.

485. Q: Why does cardiac tamponade cause pulsus paradoxus?

A: During inspiration, increased right-sided filling shifts the interventricular septum toward the left ventricle within the constrained pericardial space, reducing LV filling and stroke volume.

486. Q: What is the definitive emergency treatment for hemodynamically significant cardiac tamponade?

A: Urgent pericardial drainage, usually by pericardiocentesis or surgical drainage depending on the cause and setting.

487. Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis-induced cardiogenic shock?

A: Hypotension, tachycardia, cool extremities, weak pulses, altered mental status, oliguria, and signs of pulmonary congestion or ventricular dysfunction.

488. Q: How does cardiogenic shock differ from hypovolemic shock?

A: Cardiogenic shock results from pump failure, often with pulmonary congestion and poor cardiac output despite adequate or increased intravascular volume.

489. Q: What is a key echocardiographic feature of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: Asymmetric ventricular septal hypertrophy, often with dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction.

490. Q: What is the major cause of sudden death in children with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: Ventricular arrhythmias are a major mechanism of sudden cardiac death.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

491. Q: What is the classic presentation of cystic fibrosis in infancy?

A: Meconium ileus, failure to thrive, recurrent respiratory symptoms, or pancreatic insufficiency may be presenting features.

492. Q: What is the underlying genetic defect in cystic fibrosis?

A: An autosomal recessive mutation in the CFTR gene, which encodes a chloride channel involved in epithelial ion transport.

493. Q: What is the classic sweat test finding in cystic fibrosis?

A: Elevated sweat chloride concentration on appropriately performed testing.

494. Q: Why is sweat chloride elevated in cystic fibrosis?

A: Dysfunctional CFTR reduces chloride reabsorption in sweat ducts, resulting in abnormally salty sweat.

495. Q: What pulmonary finding is characteristic of established cystic fibrosis?

A: Chronic productive cough, recurrent sinopulmonary infections, bronchiectasis, and persistent airway colonization.

496. Q: Which organism commonly chronically colonizes the airways of patients with cystic fibrosis?

A: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a major pathogen, particularly in older children and adults.

497. Q: What pancreatic complication occurs commonly in cystic fibrosis?

A: Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency, causing malabsorption and deficiency of fat-soluble vitamins.

498. Q: What stool characteristic suggests pancreatic insufficiency in cystic fibrosis?

A: Bulky, greasy, foul-smelling stools (steatorrhea) due to fat malabsorption.

499. Q: Which vitamins are particularly deficient in cystic fibrosis-associated pancreatic insufficiency?

A: Vitamins A, D, E, and K because they are fat-soluble.

500. Q: What is the classic neonatal intestinal complication of cystic fibrosis?

A: Meconium ileus, caused by thick, inspissated meconium obstructing the distal ileum.

501. Q: What is the classic imaging finding in Hirschsprung disease on contrast enema?

A: A transition zone between a distal aganglionic, narrowed segment and a proximally dilated segment.

502. Q: What confirms the diagnosis of Hirschsprung disease?

A: Rectal suction biopsy demonstrating absence of ganglion cells, with associated hypertrophic nerve fibers.

503. Q: What is the underlying defect in Hirschsprung disease?

A: Failure of neural crest cells to migrate into the distal bowel, resulting in absence of enteric ganglion cells.

504. Q: What is the classic presentation of anal fissure in a child?

A: Painful defecation with bright-red blood on the surface of the stool or toilet paper, commonly associated with constipation.

505. Q: Where are typical anal fissures located in children?

A: Usually in the posterior midline.

506. Q: What finding should raise concern for an underlying cause rather than a simple constipation-associated fissure?

A: Multiple fissures, lateral fissures, recurrent fissures, or associated systemic symptoms should prompt evaluation for conditions such as inflammatory bowel disease.

507. Q: What is the most common cause of chronic constipation in otherwise healthy children?

A: Functional constipation, often beginning after painful stool passage and resulting in stool withholding.

508. Q: What behavior commonly perpetuates functional constipation?

A: Voluntary stool withholding, which causes progressive rectal distention and reduced sensation.

509. Q: What is encopresis?

A: Involuntary passage of stool into inappropriate locations, commonly caused by overflow around retained stool in chronic constipation.

510. Q: What is the initial treatment for fecal impaction in functional constipation?

A: Disimpaction, commonly using high-dose oral polyethylene glycol or another appropriate regimen.

511. Q: What is the maintenance treatment after fecal disimpaction?

A: Daily osmotic laxative therapy, adequate fiber/fluid intake, regular toilet sitting, and behavioral interventions.

512. Q: What is the classic presentation of a duodenal obstruction caused by annular pancreas?

A: Bilious vomiting and feeding intolerance, often beginning in infancy or sometimes presenting later.

513. Q: What is the embryologic defect in annular pancreas?

A: Abnormal pancreatic development produces a ring of pancreatic tissue surrounding the second portion of the duodenum.

514. Q: Which chromosomal disorder is associated with duodenal atresia?

A: Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome).

515. Q: What is the classic abdominal x-ray finding in duodenal atresia?

A: The “double-bubble” sign, representing dilation of the stomach and proximal duodenum.

516. Q: What is the classic presentation of duodenal atresia?

A: Bilious vomiting shortly after birth, abdominal distention that is generally limited, and feeding intolerance.

517. Q: What is the classic x-ray finding of distal intestinal obstruction from meconium ileus?

A: Dilated bowel loops with a granular or “soap-bubble” appearance from mixed meconium and gas.

518. Q: What is an important association of meconium ileus?

A: Cystic fibrosis.

519. Q: What is the classic presentation of Meconium plug syndrome?

A: Failure to pass meconium and abdominal distention in a newborn, with passage of meconium after rectal stimulation or contrast enema.

520. Q: How does meconium plug syndrome differ from Hirschsprung disease?

A: Meconium plug syndrome may resolve after passage of the plug, whereas Hirschsprung disease has persistent aganglionosis and requires definitive evaluation and treatment.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

521. Q: What is the classic clinical finding in significant aortic stenosis?

A: A harsh systolic ejection murmur with an ejection click and diminished or delayed carotid upstroke in severe disease.

522. Q: What symptom in a child with aortic stenosis is particularly concerning?

A: Exertional syncope or chest pain, which may indicate severe outflow obstruction.

523. Q: What is the most common congenital valvular abnormality causing aortic stenosis in children?

A: A bicuspid aortic valve.

524. Q: What is the major long-term complication of a bicuspid aortic valve?

A: Progressive aortic stenosis or regurgitation and dilation of the ascending aorta.

525. Q: What is the classic murmur of aortic regurgitation?

A: A high-pitched, early diastolic decrescendo murmur, best heard along the left sternal border.

526. Q: What is the classic presentation of a child with severe aortic coarctation after ductal closure?

A: Poor feeding, tachypnea, metabolic acidosis, weak lower-extremity pulses, and shock due to inadequate systemic perfusion.

527. Q: Why can neonates with critical coarctation deteriorate rapidly after birth?

A: Ductus arteriosus closure removes a major source of blood flow to the descending aorta and lower body.

528. Q: What medication can temporarily stabilize a neonate with critical coarctation?

A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil) to maintain ductal patency.

529. Q: What is the classic association between coarctation and bicuspid aortic valve?

A: Bicuspid aortic valve commonly coexists with coarctation, reflecting associated left-sided obstructive congenital heart disease.

530. Q: What is the classic ECG finding in severe pulmonary hypertension?

A: Right ventricular hypertrophy and right-axis deviation may be present.

531. Q: What is Eisenmenger syndrome?

A: Reversal of a longstanding left-to-right shunt to a right-to-left shunt due to severe pulmonary vascular disease and pulmonary hypertension.

532. Q: What clinical finding indicates development of Eisenmenger physiology?

A: New or worsening cyanosis and clubbing in a patient with a longstanding congenital left-to-right shunt.

533. Q: Why should closure of a large shunt lesion be avoided after irreversible Eisenmenger physiology develops?

A: The pulmonary vascular resistance is severely elevated, and closure can eliminate the compensatory pathway for systemic blood flow.

534. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta and bicuspid aortic valve are important associations.

535. Q: What cardiac abnormality is associated with Williams syndrome?

A: Supravalvular aortic stenosis caused by elastin-related vascular abnormalities.

536. Q: What characteristic facial/behavioral condition is associated with Williams syndrome?

A: A characteristic facial appearance and overly social personality, often with developmental difficulties.

537. Q: What electrolyte abnormality can occur in Williams syndrome?

A: Hypercalcemia, particularly in infancy.

538. Q: What cardiac lesion is associated with Holt-Oram syndrome?

A: Atrial septal defect or ventricular septal defect associated with upper-limb abnormalities.

539. Q: What is the classic clinical clue to Holt-Oram syndrome?

A: Radial-ray/upper-limb abnormalities combined with a congenital cardiac defect.

540. Q: What is the major cardiac manifestation of Friedreich ataxia?

A: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is a major cause of morbidity and mortality.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

541. Q: What is the classic presentation of pulmonary aspiration in a child with swallowing dysfunction?

A: Chronic cough or choking during feeds, recurrent pneumonia, wheezing, and poor weight gain.

542. Q: What lung findings suggest chronic aspiration?

A: Recurrent or persistent infiltrates, often involving dependent lung regions, along with chronic cough or wheezing.

543. Q: Which children are at increased risk for aspiration pneumonia?

A: Children with neuromuscular disorders, impaired swallowing, severe developmental disability, structural abnormalities, or gastroesophageal reflux with aspiration.

544. Q: What is the classic presentation of foreign-body aspiration that is initially missed?

A: A child may have persistent unilateral wheezing, chronic cough, recurrent pneumonia, or asymmetric breath sounds after an unwitnessed aspiration event.

545. Q: Why can a foreign body cause unilateral hyperinflation?

A: A partially obstructing object may function as a ball-valve, allowing air in during inspiration but preventing adequate expiration.

546. Q: What imaging finding can suggest a radiolucent aspirated foreign body?

A: Unilateral hyperinflation or air trapping, particularly on an expiratory or decubitus film.

547. Q: What is the definitive diagnostic and therapeutic procedure for suspected airway foreign body?

A: Bronchoscopy.

548. Q: What is the classic presentation of bronchiectasis?

A: Chronic productive cough, recurrent respiratory infections, hemoptysis, and persistent abnormal lung sounds.

549. Q: What structural change defines bronchiectasis?

A: Permanent dilation and destruction of the bronchial walls caused by chronic inflammation and infection.

550. Q: What imaging study best establishes the diagnosis of bronchiectasis?

A: High-resolution CT of the chest, showing dilated bronchi, lack of normal tapering, and airway-wall abnormalities.

PEDIATRIC GASTROENTEROLOGY

551. Q: What is the classic presentation of gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) in an infant?

A: Regurgitation/vomiting associated with feeding, irritability, feeding refusal, poor weight gain, or respiratory symptoms when reflux is pathologic.

552. Q: How does physiologic infant reflux differ from GERD?

A: Physiologic reflux occurs in otherwise healthy infants with normal growth and no significant complications; GERD causes troublesome symptoms or complications.

553. Q: What is the initial management of uncomplicated infant reflux?

A: Feeding modification and avoidance of overfeeding, with continued appropriate growth monitoring; medications are not routinely indicated.

554. Q: What symptom in an infant with reflux requires evaluation for an alternative diagnosis?

A: Bilious vomiting, hematemesis, projectile vomiting with dehydration, significant abdominal distention, or failure to thrive.

555. Q: What is the classic presentation of eosinophilic esophagitis in a school-aged child?

A: Feeding difficulty, dysphagia, food impaction, abdominal pain, or vomiting, often with a history of atopy.

556. Q: What histologic finding establishes eosinophilic esophagitis?

A: Increased eosinophilic infiltration of the esophageal mucosa with characteristic endoscopic and clinical findings.

557. Q: What is a classic endoscopic appearance of eosinophilic esophagitis?

A: Esophageal rings (“trachealization”), linear furrows, exudates, and mucosal edema may be seen.

558. Q: What is an important complication of untreated eosinophilic esophagitis?

A: Esophageal remodeling and stricture formation, increasing the risk of food impaction.

559. Q: What is the classic presentation of appendicitis in a young child?

A: It may be atypical, with diffuse abdominal pain, vomiting, fever, irritability, and delayed diagnosis compared with older children.

560. Q: What is the typical migration of pain in acute appendicitis?

A: Pain classically migrates from periumbilical discomfort to the right lower quadrant as parietal peritoneal inflammation develops.

561. Q: What is the classic physical examination finding in appendicitis?

A: Right lower quadrant tenderness, sometimes with guarding and rebound tenderness.

562. Q: What is the most important complication of delayed appendicitis diagnosis in children?

A: Appendiceal perforation with abscess formation or generalized peritonitis.

563. Q: What is the classic presentation of an appendiceal abscess?

A: Persistent fever, localized right lower quadrant pain, tenderness, and sometimes a palpable mass after delayed or perforated appendicitis.

564. Q: What is the classic presentation of viral gastroenteritis?

A: Acute vomiting and watery diarrhea, often accompanied by fever, abdominal cramps, and sick contacts.

565. Q: What is the major complication of severe pediatric gastroenteritis?

A: Dehydration with electrolyte abnormalities and hypovolemic shock.

566. Q: What is the preferred fluid for oral rehydration therapy?

A: A properly formulated oral rehydration solution containing glucose and electrolytes.

567. Q: Why does glucose improve intestinal sodium and water absorption during oral rehydration?

A: Glucose and sodium are cotransported across the intestinal epithelium, and water follows osmotically.

568. Q: When is IV fluid resuscitation indicated in pediatric gastroenteritis?

A: For severe dehydration, hypovolemic shock, altered mental status, or inability to tolerate adequate oral rehydration.

569. Q: What is the classic acid-base abnormality from severe diarrhea?

A: Normal-anion-gap metabolic acidosis due to bicarbonate loss.

570. Q: Why can severe diarrhea cause hypokalemia?

A: Gastrointestinal losses cause direct potassium depletion, which can become significant with prolonged diarrhea.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

571. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute rheumatic carditis?

A: Tachycardia, new murmur, cardiomegaly, heart failure, or pericardial involvement following group A streptococcal infection.

572. Q: Which valve is most commonly affected by rheumatic heart disease?

A: The mitral valve is most commonly affected.

573. Q: What is the characteristic chronic valvular lesion of rheumatic heart disease?

A: Mitral stenosis, although mitral regurgitation is common earlier in the disease course.

574. Q: How is prevention of recurrent rheumatic fever achieved?

A: Long-term antibiotic prophylaxis, typically with benzathine penicillin G at recommended intervals.

575. Q: What is the major purpose of secondary rheumatic fever prophylaxis?

A: To prevent recurrent group A streptococcal infection and subsequent progressive valvular damage.

576. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute rheumatic fever-associated chorea?

A: Involuntary, irregular movements, motor incoordination, handwriting deterioration, and emotional lability.

577. Q: What is the classic ECG abnormality that counts as a minor Jones criterion?

A: Prolonged PR interval, adjusted for age.

578. Q: What is the classic congenital lesion producing a continuous left-to-right shunt?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA).

579. Q: What happens to the shunt in PDA when pulmonary vascular resistance becomes very high?

A: The left-to-right shunt can eventually reverse, producing Eisenmenger physiology and cyanosis.

580. Q: What is the characteristic differential cyanosis of PDA-associated Eisenmenger syndrome?

A: Lower-extremity cyanosis and clubbing greater than the upper extremities, because the ductus enters the descending aorta distal to vessels supplying the upper limbs.

581. Q: Which congenital heart lesion is classically associated with differential cyanosis?

A: PDA with Eisenmenger physiology.

582. Q: What is the classic murmur of a VSD?

A: A harsh, holosystolic murmur at the lower left sternal border.

583. Q: Why can a small VSD have a louder murmur than a large VSD?

A: A small defect produces higher-velocity turbulent flow, generating a louder murmur.

584. Q: Why can the murmur of a large VSD become softer as pulmonary vascular resistance rises?

A: Increasing pulmonary vascular resistance reduces the pressure gradient across the VSD and decreases shunt velocity.

585. Q: What is the classic ECG finding in a significant ASD?

A: Right ventricular volume overload, often manifested by incomplete RBBB and right-axis deviation depending on the defect and age.

586. Q: What is the usual direction of blood flow through an uncomplicated ASD?

A: Left-to-right, because left atrial pressure normally exceeds right atrial pressure.

587. Q: What is the classic congenital cardiac lesion associated with an "egg-on-a-string" appearance?

A: Transposition of the great arteries, particularly on older radiographs.

588. Q: Why is TGA incompatible with life without mixing?

A: The systemic and pulmonary circulations run in parallel rather than in series, so oxygenated blood cannot adequately reach the systemic circulation without an effective mixing site.

589. Q: What are important sites of mixing in TGA?

A: Atrial-level communication, VSD, and PDA can permit mixing between the parallel circulations.

590. Q: What is the definitive surgical repair for TGA?

A: Arterial switch operation, generally performed early in life.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

591. Q: What is the classic presentation of pulmonary tuberculosis in a child?

A: Persistent cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss or poor weight gain, with possible lymphadenopathy or pulmonary findings.

592. Q: What is the preferred initial test for TB infection in an immunocompetent child?

A: Tuberculin skin testing or an interferon-γ release assay (IGRA), depending on age, vaccination history, and clinical circumstances.

593. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding of primary pulmonary TB in children?

A: Hilar or mediastinal lymphadenopathy, sometimes with an associated parenchymal focus.

594. Q: Why can TB cause airway obstruction in children?

A: Enlarged hilar or mediastinal lymph nodes can compress adjacent bronchi.

595. Q: What is the classic presentation of pertussis?

A: Paroxysmal coughing fits, inspiratory whoop, and posttussive vomiting; young infants may present primarily with apnea.

596. Q: Which organism causes pertussis?

A: Bordetella pertussis.

597. Q: What is the major danger of pertussis in young infants?

A: Apnea, hypoxemia, pneumonia, and severe respiratory compromise.

598. Q: What is the classic laboratory finding during the catarrhal/paroxysmal phase of pertussis?

A: Marked lymphocytosis, particularly in young infants.

599. Q: What antibiotic is preferred for treatment and transmission reduction in pertussis?

A: A macrolide, usually azithromycin, is preferred.

600. Q: What is the classic presentation of a pulmonary infection caused by Chlamydia trachomatis in an infant?

A: Afebrile pneumonia with a staccato cough, tachypnea, and diffuse interstitial infiltrates, typically occurring at several weeks of age.

601. Q: What is the classic presentation of a choledochal cyst in a child?

A: Abdominal pain, jaundice, and a palpable abdominal mass may occur, although the classic triad is uncommon.

602. Q: What is the major complication of a choledochal cyst?

A: Cholangitis, pancreatitis, biliary obstruction, and increased lifetime risk of biliary malignancy.

603. Q: What imaging study is commonly used to evaluate suspected choledochal cysts?

A: Abdominal ultrasound is a useful initial study; MRCP can further define biliary anatomy.

604. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute cholangitis?

A: Fever, right upper quadrant pain, and jaundice (Charcot triad).

605. Q: What additional findings suggest severe ascending cholangitis?

A: Hypotension and altered mental status indicate severe systemic illness.

606. Q: What is the classic presentation of acute cholecystitis?

A: Right upper quadrant pain, fever, nausea/vomiting, and a positive Murphy sign.

607. Q: What is Murphy sign?

A: Inspiratory arrest caused by pain when the examiner palpates the right upper quadrant over the gallbladder.

608. Q: What is the classic ultrasound finding of acute cholecystitis?

A: Gallbladder wall thickening, pericholecystic fluid, gallstones, and a sonographic Murphy sign may be present.

609. Q: What is the classic presentation of appendicitis-associated perforation?

A: Persistent fever, worsening abdominal pain, guarding, rebound tenderness, and systemic toxicity.

610. Q: Why is perforated appendicitis more common in younger children?

A: Younger children may have nonspecific symptoms and difficulty communicating pain, resulting in delayed diagnosis.

611. Q: What is the classic presentation of mesenteric adenitis?

A: Right lower quadrant abdominal pain, fever, and recent upper respiratory infection, often mimicking appendicitis.

612. Q: What is the usual cause of mesenteric adenitis?

A: Often a viral infection; Yersinia and other bacterial infections can also cause it.

613. Q: What is the classic presentation of Yersinia enterocolitica infection?

A: Fever, abdominal pain, and diarrhea, sometimes with terminal ileitis and mesenteric lymphadenitis mimicking appendicitis.

614. Q: Which food exposure is classically associated with Yersinia enterocolitica?

A: Undercooked pork is a classic source.

615. Q: What is the classic presentation of Clostridioides difficile infection in children?

A: Watery diarrhea, abdominal pain, fever, and leukocytosis, typically after antibiotic exposure or healthcare-associated disruption of gut flora.

616. Q: What is the major complication of severe C. difficile colitis?

A: Toxic megacolon, perforation, sepsis, and severe dehydration can occur.

617. Q: What is the classic presentation of lactose intolerance?

A: Bloating, abdominal cramps, flatulence, and watery diarrhea after lactose ingestion.

618. Q: Why does lactose intolerance cause acidic stools?

A: Unabsorbed lactose is fermented by colonic bacteria, producing organic acids and gases, which lower stool pH.

619. Q: What is the classic presentation of hereditary fructose intolerance?

A: After ingestion of fructose or sucrose, children may develop vomiting, abdominal pain, hypoglycemia, and aversion to sweets.

620. Q: What enzyme is deficient in hereditary fructose intolerance?

A: Aldolase B.

PEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY

621. Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis-associated heart failure in an infant?

A: Tachypnea, feeding intolerance, diaphoresis, hepatomegaly, poor weight gain, and tachycardia disproportionate to fever.

622. Q: What is a key laboratory marker of myocardial injury?

A: Cardiac troponin may be elevated in myocarditis, although the diagnosis relies on the overall clinical and cardiac evaluation.

623. Q: What is the classic echocardiographic finding in dilated cardiomyopathy?

A: Dilated cardiac chambers with reduced systolic function, particularly reduced left ventricular ejection fraction.

624. Q: What is the classic cause of dilated cardiomyopathy in children?

A: Causes include myocarditis, genetic cardiomyopathies, metabolic disease, toxins, and some neuromuscular disorders; many cases are idiopathic.

625. Q: What is the major hemodynamic consequence of dilated cardiomyopathy?

A: Reduced cardiac output due to impaired ventricular systolic contraction.

626. Q: What is the classic presentation of restrictive cardiomyopathy?

A: Impaired ventricular filling with preserved or relatively preserved systolic function, often producing elevated venous pressures and exercise intolerance.

627. Q: What is the major difference between restrictive and dilated cardiomyopathy?

A: Restrictive cardiomyopathy primarily impairs diastolic filling, whereas dilated cardiomyopathy primarily causes systolic dysfunction with ventricular dilation.

628. Q: What is the classic presentation of cardiac tamponade in an infant?

A: Tachycardia, poor perfusion, hypotension, muffled heart sounds, respiratory distress, and hepatomegaly may occur.

629. Q: What echocardiographic finding strongly supports cardiac tamponade?

A: Right atrial or right ventricular diastolic collapse in the appropriate clinical setting.

630. Q: What is the classic cause of a continuous murmur besides PDA?

A: Arteriovenous fistulas and other abnormal vascular communications can produce continuous turbulent flow.

631. Q: What is the classic finding in an arteriovenous fistula?

A: A continuous machinery-like murmur or bruit with a palpable thrill may be present.

632. Q: What is the classic presentation of Kawasaki disease in the subacute phase?

A: Periungual desquamation, thrombocytosis, irritability, and ongoing risk of coronary artery abnormalities.

633. Q: What echocardiographic finding indicates coronary involvement in Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary artery dilation or aneurysm.

634. Q: What is a major complication of large coronary aneurysms in Kawasaki disease?

A: Coronary thrombosis and myocardial infarction.

635. Q: What is the classic congenital cardiac lesion associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta, often associated with a bicuspid aortic valve.

636. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with Down syndrome?

A: Atrioventricular septal defect, particularly in children with complete AV canal.

637. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with 22q11.2 deletion syndrome?

A: Conotruncal defects, including Tetralogy of Fallot and interrupted aortic arch.

638. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with Williams syndrome?

A: Supravalvular aortic stenosis.

639. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with congenital rubella?

A: Patent ductus arteriosus.

640. Q: What is the classic cardiac lesion associated with maternal diabetes?

A: Transposition of the great arteries is an important association; ventricular septal defects and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy may also occur.

PEDIATRIC PULMONOLOGY

641. Q: What is the classic presentation of pulmonary tuberculosis in a young child?

A: Chronic cough, fever, poor weight gain, fatigue, and intrathoracic lymphadenopathy are common manifestations.

642. Q: What is the preferred treatment principle for active tuberculosis in children?

A: Use multidrug therapy, typically an initial combination of several anti-TB agents followed by continuation therapy according to susceptibility and disease site.

643. Q: Why are multiple drugs used to treat tuberculosis?

A: To prevent emergence of drug resistance and effectively treat organisms in different metabolic states.

644. Q: What is the classic presentation of a primary TB infection in an immunocompetent child?

A: It may be asymptomatic or present with mild constitutional symptoms and hilar/mediastinal lymphadenopathy.

645. Q: What is the classic presentation of miliary tuberculosis?

A: Systemic illness with fever, weight loss, respiratory symptoms, and disseminated organ involvement.

646. Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding of miliary TB?

A: Numerous small, uniformly distributed pulmonary nodules producing a "millet seed" appearance.

647. Q: What is the classic presentation of pneumonia caused by Staphylococcus aureus?

A: Rapidly progressive, severe pneumonia that may be complicated by empyema, pneumatoceles, or necrotizing infection.

648. Q: What are pneumatoceles?

A: Thin-walled, air-filled lung cavities that can develop after certain severe bacterial pneumonias, especially S. aureus.

649. Q: What is the classic presentation of pneumonia caused by Chlamydia trachomatis in an infant?

A: Afebrile pneumonia with tachypnea, staccato cough, and diffuse interstitial infiltrates, typically at 1–3 months of age.

650. Q: What clinical clue distinguishes Chlamydia trachomatis pneumonia from typical bacterial pneumonia in infants?

A: The infant is often afebrile, has a characteristic staccato cough, and may have a history of maternal genital infection or conjunctivitis.

651.
Q: What is the most common innocent murmur in children?
A: Still murmur (innocent vibratory/functional murmur), typically a soft midsystolic murmur heard best at the lower left sternal border or apex.

652.
Q: What features suggest that a pediatric murmur is pathologic rather than innocent?
A: Diastolic murmur, holosystolic murmur, loud/harsh murmur, thrill, abnormal S2, cyanosis, syncope, poor growth, abnormal pulses, or symptoms of heart failure.

653.
Q: What is a characteristic feature of an innocent Still murmur?
A: It is usually soft, systolic, vibratory, changes with position, and occurs in an otherwise healthy child without abnormal cardiac findings.

654.
Q: What does a systolic click in a child suggest?
A: Bicuspid aortic valve or valvular pulmonary stenosis, depending on the location and characteristics of the click.

655.
Q: What is the most common congenital valve abnormality associated with aortic stenosis?
A: Bicuspid aortic valve.

656.
Q: What long-term complication can occur in children with bicuspid aortic valve?
A: Progressive aortic stenosis, aortic regurgitation, infective endocarditis, and ascending aortic dilation.

657.
Q: What is the classic murmur of aortic regurgitation?
A: A high-pitched, early diastolic decrescendo murmur along the left sternal border.

658.
