Q: What maneuver generally increases the intensity of aortic regurgitation?
A: Handgrip, because increased systemic vascular resistance increases regurgitant flow.

659.
Q: What congenital lesion produces a continuous murmur caused by blood flow throughout systole and diastole?
A: Patent ductus arteriosus.

660.
Q: What happens to pulmonary vascular resistance in an untreated large left-to-right shunt over time?
A: Chronic pulmonary overcirculation can cause pulmonary vascular remodeling and pulmonary hypertension, eventually producing right-to-left shunting.

661.
Q: What is Eisenmenger syndrome?
A: Irreversible pulmonary vascular disease caused by a longstanding left-to-right shunt, eventually causing pulmonary hypertension and reversal to a right-to-left shunt.

662.
Q: Why can a patient with Eisenmenger syndrome become cyanotic?
A: Severe pulmonary hypertension reverses the original shunt, allowing deoxygenated blood to enter systemic circulation.

663.
Q: Which congenital heart lesions commonly produce Eisenmenger syndrome if untreated?
A: Large VSD, PDA, or ASD, although the timing and likelihood differ among lesions.

664.
Q: Why is surgical closure of a large congenital left-to-right shunt contraindicated after advanced Eisenmenger physiology develops?
A: The pulmonary vascular disease may be irreversible, and closure can eliminate the shunt that has become necessary for maintaining cardiac output.

665.
Q: What congenital heart defect is associated with maternal rubella infection?
A: Patent ductus arteriosus, along with pulmonary artery stenosis and other congenital abnormalities.

666.
Q: What cardiac lesion is classically associated with maternal alcohol exposure?
A: Congenital heart disease can occur, particularly VSD and ASD, as part of fetal alcohol spectrum disorders.

667.
Q: What is the major physiologic problem in tricuspid atresia?
A: Absent tricuspid valve → no direct right atrium-to-right ventricle flow, requiring an atrial-level communication for systemic venous blood to reach the left heart.

668.
Q: What additional defect is usually required for pulmonary blood flow in tricuspid atresia?
A: A VSD and/or PDA, depending on the anatomy and degree of pulmonary outflow obstruction.

669.
Q: What is the characteristic ECG finding in tricuspid atresia?
A: Left axis deviation, often with evidence of right atrial enlargement.

670.
Q: What is the defining anatomical abnormality in truncus arteriosus?
A: Failure of separation of the embryonic outflow tract, resulting in one arterial trunk supplying both systemic and pulmonary circulations.

671.
Q: What is the definitive treatment for truncus arteriosus?
A: Early surgical repair, separating pulmonary and systemic outflow and closing the associated VSD.

672.
Q: What is the defining abnormality in double-outlet right ventricle (DORV)?
A: Both the aorta and pulmonary artery arise predominantly from the right ventricle.

673.
Q: Why does the clinical presentation of DORV vary substantially?
A: Physiology depends on the location of the VSD and presence or absence of pulmonary outflow obstruction.

674.
Q: What is the typical initial test for suspected structural congenital heart disease?
A: Echocardiography is the key diagnostic imaging study.

675.
Q: What is the significance of pulse oximetry screening in newborns?
A: It helps detect critical congenital heart disease, particularly lesions producing significant hypoxemia before discharge.

676.
Q: When comparing preductal and postductal oxygen saturation, where is the preductal measurement obtained?
A: From the right upper extremity, reflecting oxygenation before blood passes through the ductus arteriosus.

677.
Q: What can a significant difference between preductal and postductal oxygen saturation suggest in a newborn?
A: Persistent pulmonary hypertension or certain ductal-dependent cardiovascular lesions causing differential oxygenation.

678.
Q: What is the classic presentation of acute rheumatic fever after streptococcal pharyngitis?
A: Migratory polyarthritis, carditis, chorea, erythema marginatum, and subcutaneous nodules.

679.
Q: What cardiac manifestation is most important in acute rheumatic fever?
A: Pancarditis, particularly valvulitis causing mitral regurgitation.

680.
Q: What is the most commonly affected valve in rheumatic heart disease?
A: Mitral valve.

681.
Q: What is the key preventive treatment after an episode of acute rheumatic fever?
A: Long-term antibiotic prophylaxis, typically with penicillin, to prevent recurrent group A streptococcal infection.

682.
Q: What is a major late cardiac complication of rheumatic heart disease?
A: Chronic mitral stenosis, which can eventually cause pulmonary hypertension and heart failure.

683.
Q: What are the major diagnostic features of Kawasaki disease?
A: Fever ≥5 days plus bilateral nonexudative conjunctivitis, oral mucosal changes, polymorphous rash, cervical lymphadenopathy, and extremity changes.

684.
Q: What physical finding occurs during the subacute phase of Kawasaki disease?
A: Desquamation of the fingers and toes, especially around the periungual region.

685.
Q: Why is IVIG given in Kawasaki disease?
A: It markedly reduces the risk of coronary artery aneurysm formation.

686.
Q: What should be suspected if a child with Kawasaki disease develops new cardiac ischemic symptoms?
A: Coronary artery thrombosis or aneurysm-related myocardial ischemia.

687.
Q: What is the classic ECG finding in acute pericarditis?
A: Diffuse ST-segment elevation with PR-segment depression, rather than a localized coronary distribution.

688.
Q: What symptom strongly suggests pericarditis in a child?
A: Sharp, pleuritic chest pain that improves when sitting forward.

689.
Q: What are the classic findings of cardiac tamponade?
A: Hypotension, elevated jugular venous pressure, and muffled heart sounds (Beck triad), with pulsus paradoxus also possible.

690.
Q: What echocardiographic finding is characteristic of cardiac tamponade?
A: Pericardial effusion with right-sided chamber collapse, particularly during diastole.

691.
Q: What is the definitive emergency treatment for hemodynamically significant cardiac tamponade?
A: Urgent pericardial drainage, usually by pericardiocentesis or surgical drainage depending on the situation.

692.
Q: What is the most common cause of bacterial tracheitis?
A: Staphylococcus aureus is a classic causative organism.

693.
Q: How does bacterial tracheitis typically differ from viral croup?
A: Children often appear more toxic, have high fever and purulent airway secretions, and may have severe airway obstruction despite typical preceding viral symptoms.

694.
Q: What is the initial priority in a child with severe upper-airway obstruction from bacterial tracheitis?
A: Secure the airway, followed by appropriate IV antibiotics.

695.
Q: What is the classic chest x-ray finding in croup?
A: Steeple sign—subglottic airway narrowing on an AP neck radiograph.

696.
Q: What is the classic radiographic finding associated with epiglottitis?
A: Thumbprint sign from an enlarged edematous epiglottis on a lateral neck radiograph.

697.
Q: What is the most common cause of community-acquired atypical pneumonia in school-aged children?
A: Mycoplasma pneumoniae.

698.
Q: What clinical pattern suggests Mycoplasma pneumoniae rather than typical bacterial pneumonia?
A: Gradual onset, persistent dry cough, low-grade fever, headache, and relatively mild examination findings despite radiographic infiltrates.

699.
Q: What is a common complication of severe bacterial pneumonia in children?
A: Parapneumonic effusion or empyema, particularly with complicated bacterial infection.

700.
Q: What is the next diagnostic step when a child with pneumonia has persistent fever and worsening respiratory symptoms despite appropriate antibiotics?
A: Evaluate for complications such as parapneumonic effusion/empyema, lung abscess, necrotizing pneumonia, or an alternative diagnosis, often with chest imaging and ultrasound when pleural disease is suspected.

701.
Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis in a child?
A: Recent viral illness followed by fatigue, tachycardia, dyspnea, exercise intolerance, chest pain, or signs of heart failure.

702.
Q: What ECG abnormality can occur in pediatric myocarditis?
A: Nonspecific ST-T changes, arrhythmias, conduction abnormalities, or diffuse ST-segment changes.

703.
Q: What laboratory marker is commonly elevated with myocardial injury in myocarditis?
A: Cardiac troponin.

704.
Q: What imaging study is central to evaluating ventricular function in suspected myocarditis?
A: Echocardiography, which can demonstrate ventricular dysfunction and reduced ejection fraction.

705.
Q: What is an important cause of sudden cardiac death in young athletes?
A: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM).

706.
Q: What is the characteristic structural abnormality in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?
A: Asymmetric ventricular septal hypertrophy, often causing dynamic left ventricular outflow tract obstruction.

707.
Q: What happens to the HCM murmur with standing or Valsalva?
A: It generally increases because decreased preload worsens dynamic LV outflow obstruction.

708.
Q: What happens to the HCM murmur with squatting?
A: It generally decreases because increased venous return and afterload reduce dynamic outflow obstruction.

709.
Q: What symptoms should raise concern for hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in an adolescent athlete?
A: Exertional syncope, exertional chest pain, dyspnea, palpitations, or a family history of sudden cardiac death.

710.
Q: What is a major treatment for symptomatic hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?
A: β-blockers, which reduce heart rate and improve diastolic filling.

711.
Q: Why should dehydration be avoided in patients with obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?
A: Reduced preload can worsen dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction.

712.
Q: What inherited disorder is strongly associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and sudden cardiac death in children?
A: Familial sarcomeric mutations, commonly involving β-myosin heavy chain or myosin-binding protein C.

713.
Q: What is the classic presentation of myocarditis progressing to dilated cardiomyopathy?
A: Ventricular dilation with reduced systolic function, leading to congestive heart failure.

714.
Q: What is the key difference between systolic and diastolic heart failure?
A: Systolic failure involves impaired ventricular contraction; diastolic failure involves impaired ventricular relaxation/filling.

715.
Q: What is pulsus paradoxus?
A: An exaggerated inspiratory fall in systolic blood pressure, classically >10 mm Hg.

716.
Q: In which pediatric conditions can pulsus paradoxus be prominent?
A: Severe asthma and cardiac tamponade.

717.
Q: What is a gallop rhythm in a child with suspected heart failure?
A: An S3 or S4, suggesting abnormal ventricular filling or reduced ventricular compliance.

718.
Q: What does an S3 generally indicate in pediatric cardiac disease?
A: Rapid ventricular filling associated with volume overload or ventricular dysfunction.

719.
Q: What does hepatomegaly suggest in an infant with respiratory distress?
A: It can indicate congestive heart failure, particularly when accompanied by poor feeding, diaphoresis, and tachycardia.

720.
Q: What is a key distinction between cardiac and pulmonary causes of tachypnea in an infant?
A: Hepatomegaly, diaphoresis with feeds, poor weight gain, and cardiomegaly favor heart failure, whereas isolated wheezing/crackles with an infectious prodrome favors pulmonary disease.

721.
Q: What is bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD)?
A: A chronic lung disease of premature infants, especially those requiring prolonged oxygen or mechanical ventilation.

722.
Q: What is the underlying mechanism of bronchopulmonary dysplasia?
A: Interrupted lung development plus inflammation and injury from prematurity, oxygen exposure, and mechanical ventilation.

723.
Q: What respiratory findings are typical of bronchopulmonary dysplasia?
A: Persistent oxygen requirement, tachypnea, increased work of breathing, wheezing, and impaired pulmonary growth.

724.
Q: What complication of prematurity can produce recurrent apnea and bradycardia?
A: Apnea of prematurity due to immature respiratory control.

725.
Q: What medication is commonly used for clinically significant apnea of prematurity?
A: Caffeine, which stimulates respiratory drive.

726.
Q: Why is apnea in a premature infant clinically important?
A: It can cause hypoxemia and bradycardia and may indicate immature respiratory control or another underlying illness.

727.
Q: What is congenital diaphragmatic hernia (CDH)?
A: A defect in the diaphragm allowing abdominal organs to herniate into the thoracic cavity, impairing lung development.

728.
Q: Which side is most commonly affected by congenital diaphragmatic hernia?
A: Left side, classically due to a posterolateral Bochdalek hernia.

729.
Q: What is the characteristic presentation of severe congenital diaphragmatic hernia immediately after birth?
A: Severe respiratory distress, scaphoid abdomen, decreased breath sounds on the affected side, and displaced heart sounds.

730.
Q: What is the major pulmonary complication of congenital diaphragmatic hernia?
A: Pulmonary hypoplasia with pulmonary hypertension.

731.
Q: What should be avoided in a neonate with suspected congenital diaphragmatic hernia?
A: Bag-mask ventilation, because it can inflate the herniated bowel and further impair lung expansion.

732.
Q: What is the initial airway strategy for a neonate with severe congenital diaphragmatic hernia?
A: Endotracheal intubation with gastric decompression using an orogastric tube.

733.
Q: What is the characteristic presentation of laryngomalacia?
A: Inspiratory stridor beginning in early infancy, often worse with feeding, crying, or supine positioning.

734.
Q: What is the usual course of uncomplicated laryngomalacia?
A: It is usually self-limited, improving as the airway matures.

735.
Q: What is the characteristic respiratory sound of tracheomalacia?
A: Expiratory or biphasic wheezing/stridor caused by dynamic airway collapse.

736.
Q: What is a major risk factor for recurrent aspiration in children?
A: Neurologic impairment or swallowing dysfunction, particularly in children with neuromuscular disease.

737.
Q: What pulmonary complication can result from chronic aspiration?
A: Recurrent pneumonia, chronic cough, wheezing, bronchiectasis, and chemical pneumonitis.

738.
Q: What is the classic presentation of pulmonary sequestration?
A: A congenital mass of nonfunctional lung tissue supplied by an anomalous systemic artery, often presenting with recurrent localized pneumonia.

739.
Q: What is the classic presentation of congenital pulmonary airway malformation (CPAM)?
A: Respiratory distress in a neonate or recurrent respiratory infections in an older child due to abnormal cystic lung development.

740.
Q: What is a key imaging clue for pulmonary sequestration?
A: An anomalous systemic arterial supply, usually arising from the thoracic or abdominal aorta.

741.
Q: What is the most common cause of obstructive sleep apnea in children?
A: Adenotonsillar hypertrophy.

742.
Q: What clinical features suggest pediatric obstructive sleep apnea?
A: Loud snoring, witnessed apneas, restless sleep, mouth breathing, daytime behavioral problems, and poor school performance.

743.
Q: What is the first-line treatment for significant obstructive sleep apnea caused by adenotonsillar hypertrophy in an otherwise appropriate child?
A: Adenotonsillectomy.

744.
Q: What is a serious cardiovascular consequence of untreated severe pediatric obstructive sleep apnea?
A: Pulmonary hypertension and right-sided cardiac strain.

745.
Q: What is the classic pulmonary manifestation of sickle cell disease?
A: Acute chest syndrome.

746.
Q: How is acute chest syndrome defined clinically?
A: A new pulmonary infiltrate accompanied by fever and/or respiratory symptoms such as chest pain, cough, wheezing, tachypnea, or hypoxemia in a patient with sickle cell disease.

747.
Q: What commonly triggers acute chest syndrome in children with sickle cell disease?
A: Infection, pulmonary vaso-occlusion, and fat embolization are important causes.

748.
Q: What is the initial management of acute chest syndrome?
A: Oxygen for hypoxemia, analgesia, incentive spirometry, antibiotics, and supportive care; transfusion is considered for severe disease.

749.
Q: Why is incentive spirometry used in children with sickle cell disease who are hospitalized with chest symptoms?
A: It helps prevent atelectasis, which can worsen hypoxemia and contribute to acute chest syndrome.

750.
Q: What is the classic pulmonary complication of primary ciliary dyskinesia?
A: Chronic sinopulmonary infections with bronchiectasis, often accompanied by chronic wet cough and recurrent otitis/sinusitis.

751.
Q: What is the most common cause of secondary hypertension in young children?
A: Renal disease is the most common secondary cause; other important causes include renovascular disease and coarctation of the aorta.

752.
Q: When should hypertension in a child raise particular concern for a secondary cause?
A: Very young age, severe hypertension, sudden onset, abnormal renal findings, or failure to respond to lifestyle therapy suggest secondary hypertension.

753.
Q: What renal vascular abnormality can cause hypertension in children?
A: Renal artery stenosis, which activates the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system.

754.
Q: What is the mechanism of hypertension caused by renal artery stenosis?
A: Reduced renal perfusion → increased renin → increased angiotensin II and aldosterone → vasoconstriction and sodium retention → hypertension.

755.
Q: What is the preferred initial test for suspected renal artery stenosis in a child?
A: Renal Doppler ultrasonography is commonly used as a noninvasive initial study, with further vascular imaging when necessary.

756.
Q: What is an important cardiovascular manifestation of Marfan syndrome?
A: Aortic root dilation, which increases the risk of aortic aneurysm and dissection.

757.
Q: What medication classes can slow aortic root enlargement in Marfan syndrome?
A: β-blockers or angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs) may be used to reduce aortic wall stress.

758.
Q: What physical finding in Marfan syndrome results from abnormal connective tissue in the cardiovascular system?
A: Aortic root dilation and mitral valve prolapse are important cardiac manifestations.

759.
Q: What is the major cardiovascular concern in Loeys-Dietz syndrome?
A: Aggressive arterial aneurysms and dissections, including aortic root dilation.

760.
Q: What inherited disorder can cause premature coronary artery disease and aortic stenosis?
A: Familial hypercholesterolemia, particularly with markedly elevated LDL cholesterol.

761.
Q: What is the underlying lipid abnormality in familial hypercholesterolemia?
A: Markedly elevated LDL cholesterol due to impaired LDL receptor-mediated clearance.

762.
Q: What physical finding can occur in severe familial hypercholesterolemia?
A: Tendon xanthomas, particularly involving the Achilles tendons.

763.
Q: What is the first-line pharmacologic treatment for significant pediatric familial hypercholesterolemia?
A: Statin therapy, together with dietary and lifestyle modification.

764.
Q: What is an important cause of chest pain in adolescents that is usually noncardiac?
A: Musculoskeletal chest pain, particularly costochondritis, is common.

765.
Q: Which features of pediatric chest pain suggest a potentially serious cardiac cause?
A: Exertional chest pain, exertional syncope, palpitations, abnormal cardiac examination, or a family history of sudden unexplained death.

766.
Q: What is an important cause of syncope during exercise in an adolescent?
A: Cardiac disease, especially hypertrophic cardiomyopathy or an inherited arrhythmia syndrome, must be considered.

767.
Q: What inherited channelopathy causes syncope or sudden death associated with prolonged QT?
A: Long QT syndrome.

768.
Q: What ECG finding defines congenital long QT syndrome?
A: Prolonged corrected QT (QTc) interval.

769.
Q: What is a major treatment for symptomatic congenital long QT syndrome?
A: β-blocker therapy, particularly nonselective β-blockers such as nadolol or propranolol.

770.
Q: What medication should generally be avoided in congenital long QT syndrome?
A: QT-prolonging medications, because they increase the risk of torsades de pointes.

771.
Q: What ventricular arrhythmia is classically associated with prolonged QT?
A: Torsades de pointes, a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia.

772.
Q: What is the acute treatment for unstable torsades de pointes?
A: Immediate defibrillation if pulseless/unstable, with IV magnesium sulfate used to suppress torsades.

773.
Q: What inherited arrhythmia syndrome can cause sudden cardiac death with structurally normal hearts and is associated with sodium-channel dysfunction?
A: Brugada syndrome.

774.
Q: What ECG pattern is characteristic of Brugada syndrome?
A: ST-segment elevation in the right precordial leads, particularly V1–V3, with a characteristic coved pattern.

775.
Q: What can trigger malignant arrhythmias in Brugada syndrome?
A: Fever and certain sodium-channel–blocking medications can precipitate ventricular arrhythmias.

776.
Q: What is the classic presentation of a pulmonary embolism in an adolescent?
A: Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, and hypoxemia, particularly with risk factors for venous thromboembolism.

777.
Q: What pediatric conditions increase the risk of venous thromboembolism?
A: Central venous catheters, immobility, malignancy, nephrotic syndrome, inherited thrombophilia, estrogen-containing contraceptives, and severe obesity are important risk factors.

778.
Q: What is the preferred initial imaging test for suspected pulmonary embolism in a stable child?
A: CT pulmonary angiography is commonly used when imaging is required, although the diagnostic approach depends on clinical probability and radiation considerations.

779.
Q: What is the most common cause of acute cough in children?
A: Viral upper respiratory tract infection.

780.
Q: What clinical feature favors bacterial sinusitis over a simple viral URI?
A: Persistent symptoms >10 days without improvement, severe symptoms with high fever and purulent discharge, or worsening after initial improvement.

781.
Q: What complication of bacterial sinusitis can cause orbital swelling and pain with eye movement?
A: Orbital cellulitis.

782.
Q: Why is orbital cellulitis an emergency?
A: Infection can extend into the orbit, cavernous sinus, or intracranial space, potentially causing vision loss or brain abscess.

783.
Q: What is a classic clinical feature of pleural effusion associated with pneumonia?
A: Decreased breath sounds and dullness to percussion over the affected area.

784.
Q: What imaging modality is particularly useful for identifying and characterizing a pediatric parapneumonic effusion?
A: Chest ultrasonography, especially for determining whether fluid is free-flowing or loculated.

785.
Q: What is empyema?
A: Purulent infected fluid within the pleural space, usually occurring as a complication of bacterial pneumonia.

786.
Q: What is a major management principle for significant pediatric empyema?
A: Antibiotic therapy plus adequate pleural drainage when the effusion is complicated or causing significant respiratory compromise.

787.
Q: What is atelectasis?
A: Collapse or incomplete expansion of alveoli, resulting in reduced ventilation of the affected region.

788.
Q: What physical finding can suggest lobar atelectasis?
A: Reduced breath sounds and decreased chest expansion over the affected area.

789.
Q: What is the typical chest x-ray appearance of atelectasis?
A: Increased opacity with volume loss, often accompanied by displacement of fissures or mediastinal structures toward the affected side.

790.
Q: What is the difference between atelectasis and pneumonia on chest imaging?
A: Atelectasis causes volume loss, whereas pneumonia generally produces air-space opacity without the same degree of volume loss.

791.
Q: What is the classic pulmonary manifestation of alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency?
A: Early-onset emphysema, although clinically significant disease is uncommon in childhood.

792.
Q: What is the mechanism of lung injury in alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency?
A: Reduced α₁-antitrypsin allows neutrophil elastase to damage alveolar elastin, producing emphysema.

793.
Q: What is a classic finding in a child with primary immunodeficiency and recurrent pulmonary infections?
A: Recurrent pneumonia, chronic sinusitis, bronchiectasis, or unusual/opportunistic respiratory infections, depending on the immune defect.

794.
Q: Why can recurrent pneumonia lead to bronchiectasis?
A: Repeated infection and inflammation cause irreversible bronchial wall destruction and dilation.

795.
Q: What is the classic clinical pattern of bronchiectasis?
A: Chronic productive cough, recurrent respiratory infections, purulent sputum, and persistent crackles.

796.
Q: What is the definitive imaging study for diagnosing bronchiectasis?
A: High-resolution CT of the chest.

797.
Q: What is the characteristic CT finding in bronchiectasis?
A: Permanent bronchial dilation, such as bronchi larger than their accompanying pulmonary arteries and lack of normal tapering.

798.
Q: What pulmonary disease should be suspected in a child with recurrent pneumonia, chronic wet cough, and digital clubbing?
A: Bronchiectasis, with cystic fibrosis being an important underlying cause.

799.
Q: What is a major cause of pulmonary hypertension secondary to chronic lung disease in children?
A: Chronic hypoxemia and pulmonary vascular remodeling, as seen in conditions such as bronchopulmonary dysplasia or advanced chronic lung disease.

800.
Q: What is the relationship between chronic hypoxemia and pulmonary vascular resistance?
A: Chronic hypoxemia causes pulmonary vasoconstriction and vascular remodeling, increasing pulmonary vascular resistance and potentially producing right ventricular hypertrophy and failure.

801.
Q: What is the most common cause of syncope in otherwise healthy adolescents?
A: Vasovagal syncope, usually triggered by prolonged standing, heat, dehydration, pain, or emotional stress.

802.
Q: What prodromal symptoms commonly occur with vasovagal syncope?
A: Lightheadedness, nausea, diaphoresis, visual dimming, and pallor before loss of consciousness.

803.
Q: What features make syncope in a child concerning for a cardiac cause?
A: Syncope during exertion, syncope without prodrome, palpitations immediately before syncope, abnormal ECG, structural heart disease, or family history of sudden cardiac death.

804.
Q: What is the initial evaluation of unexplained pediatric syncope?
A: Detailed history, physical examination including orthostatic vital signs, and a 12-lead ECG.

805.
Q: What is the typical ECG finding in Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome between tachycardic episodes?
A: Short PR interval, delta wave, and widened QRS complex.

806.
Q: What is an important danger of atrial fibrillation in a patient with WPW?
A: Rapid conduction through the accessory pathway can produce extremely rapid ventricular rates and ventricular fibrillation.

807.
Q: Which AV-nodal blocking drugs should be avoided in atrial fibrillation with WPW?
A: AV-nodal blockers such as adenosine, β-blockers, verapamil, and diltiazem because they can favor conduction through the accessory pathway.

808.
Q: What is the preferred acute treatment for hemodynamically unstable atrial fibrillation with WPW?
A: Synchronized cardioversion.

809.
Q: What is first-degree AV block on ECG?
A: PR interval >200 ms in an older child/adolescent, with every atrial impulse conducted to the ventricles.

810.
Q: What defines Mobitz type I second-degree AV block?
A: Progressive PR prolongation followed by a dropped QRS complex.

811.
Q: What defines Mobitz type II second-degree AV block?
A: Constant PR intervals with intermittent nonconducted P waves and dropped QRS complexes.

812.
Q: Why is Mobitz type II more concerning than Mobitz type I?
A: It can progress suddenly to complete heart block and generally reflects more significant conduction-system disease.

813.
Q: What ECG finding characterizes complete heart block?
A: AV dissociation—atria and ventricles beat independently.

814.
Q: What congenital condition is associated with complete heart block in a newborn?
A: Maternal anti-Ro/SSA or anti-La/SSB antibodies, often associated with maternal systemic lupus erythematosus or Sjögren syndrome.

815.
Q: What is the definitive treatment for symptomatic congenital complete heart block?
A: Permanent pacemaker placement.

816.
Q: What is the classic ECG finding in atrial septal defect?
A: Right-axis deviation and an incomplete right bundle-branch block pattern may occur, along with the characteristic fixed split S2.

817.
Q: What long-term arrhythmia can develop in adults with an unrepaired ASD?
A: Atrial arrhythmias, including atrial fibrillation and atrial flutter, due to chronic right atrial/ventricular volume overload.

818.
Q: What is the major physiologic difference between an ASD and VSD?
A: ASD primarily causes right-sided volume overload, whereas VSD causes left-sided volume overload from pulmonary venous return.

819.
Q: Why can a small VSD produce a louder murmur than a large VSD?
A: A small defect creates high-velocity turbulent flow, whereas a large defect has less pressure difference between ventricles and may produce a softer murmur.

820.
Q: What does a palpable thrill over a murmur generally indicate?
A: At least a grade 4/6 murmur, suggesting significant turbulent blood flow and increasing concern for structural heart disease.

821.
Q: What is the classic hemodynamic consequence of severe pulmonary valve stenosis?
A: Right ventricular pressure overload, which can cause right ventricular hypertrophy and eventually right-sided failure.

822.
Q: What intervention is preferred for significant isolated congenital pulmonary valve stenosis?
A: Balloon valvuloplasty is generally the preferred intervention.

823.
Q: What intervention is commonly used for significant congenital valvar aortic stenosis in children?
A: Balloon valvuloplasty is commonly used when intervention is indicated.

824.
Q: What is a major complication of severe congenital aortic stenosis?
A: Left ventricular hypertrophy, myocardial ischemia, ventricular dysfunction, syncope, and sudden cardiac death.

825.
Q: What is the classic pulse finding in severe aortic stenosis?
A: Delayed and diminished carotid upstroke (pulsus parvus et tardus).

826.
Q: What congenital cardiac defect produces right ventricular volume overload due to abnormal drainage of pulmonary venous blood?
A: Partial anomalous pulmonary venous return (PAPVR).

827.
Q: What congenital defect is commonly associated with partial anomalous pulmonary venous return?
A: Atrial septal defect, particularly a sinus venosus ASD.

828.
Q: What happens to pulmonary blood flow in an unrepaired significant PAPVR?
A: Pulmonary venous blood is recirculated through the right heart, causing right-sided volume overload and pulmonary overcirculation.

829.
Q: What is the hallmark of pulmonary atresia with intact ventricular septum?
A: Complete obstruction of pulmonary blood flow with no VSD, requiring ductal patency for pulmonary circulation.

830.
Q: What medication is urgently used in pulmonary atresia with intact ventricular septum?
A: Prostaglandin E₁ to maintain ductal patency and provide pulmonary blood flow.

831.
Q: What is the major physiologic problem in hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS)?
A: Severe underdevelopment of the left ventricle, mitral valve, aortic valve, and ascending aorta, making systemic circulation dependent on the ductus arteriosus.

832.
Q: How may a neonate with critical hypoplastic left heart syndrome present after ductal closure?
A: Shock, poor perfusion, metabolic acidosis, tachypnea, and cyanosis as systemic blood flow collapses.

833.
Q: What is the immediate medical treatment for suspected HLHS in a deteriorating neonate?
A: Prostaglandin E₁ infusion to maintain ductal patency.

834.
Q: What is the first-stage surgical procedure traditionally used for HLHS?
A: Norwood procedure, establishing the right ventricle as the systemic ventricle and creating a new systemic outflow pathway.

835.
Q: What is the major long-term issue after Fontan palliation for single-ventricle physiology?
A: Fontan-associated complications, including exercise intolerance, arrhythmias, hepatic congestion/fibrosis, protein-losing enteropathy, and thrombosis.

836.
Q: What is the typical pulmonary function pattern in cystic fibrosis?
A: Progressive obstructive lung disease, characterized by airflow limitation and air trapping.

837.
Q: What is the diagnostic test of choice for cystic fibrosis?
A: Sweat chloride testing is the standard diagnostic test, supported by genetic testing when appropriate.

838.
Q: What sweat chloride concentration strongly supports cystic fibrosis?
A: ≥60 mmol/L on appropriately performed testing supports the diagnosis in the proper clinical context.

839.
Q: Why does cystic fibrosis cause thick airway secretions?
A: Dysfunction of the CFTR chloride channel alters epithelial ion and water transport, producing dehydrated, viscous airway mucus.

840.
Q: What organism commonly chronically colonizes the airways of patients with cystic fibrosis?
A: Pseudomonas aeruginosa, especially as lung disease progresses.

841.
Q: What other organism is particularly important in older patients with cystic fibrosis?
A: Burkholderia cepacia complex, which can cause severe and difficult-to-treat pulmonary infection.

842.
Q: What chronic airway therapy is used to improve mucus clearance in cystic fibrosis?
A: Airway-clearance techniques, often combined with inhaled therapies such as hypertonic saline or dornase alfa.

843.
Q: What does dornase alfa do in cystic fibrosis?
A: It cleaves extracellular DNA in airway secretions, decreasing mucus viscosity and improving clearance.

844.
Q: Why are pancreatic enzymes needed in many patients with cystic fibrosis?
A: CFTR dysfunction can cause exocrine pancreatic insufficiency, resulting in malabsorption and poor growth.

845.
Q: What respiratory complication of cystic fibrosis can present with sudden chest pain and acute dyspnea?
A: Pneumothorax, particularly in patients with advanced structural lung disease.

846.
Q: What is the classic presentation of pertussis in an infant?
A: Paroxysmal coughing, apnea, cyanosis, and feeding difficulty; infants may not develop the classic whoop.

847.
Q: Which organism causes pertussis?
A: Bordetella pertussis.

848.
Q: What antibiotic is used to treat pertussis and reduce transmission?
A: A macrolide, typically azithromycin.

849.
Q: What is an important complication of pertussis in young infants?
A: Apnea, pneumonia, hypoxemia, and potentially pulmonary hypertension or death in severe disease.

850.
Q: What pulmonary finding in a child with prolonged cough should raise suspicion for pertussis even without a classic whoop?
A: Repeated paroxysms of cough with posttussive vomiting or apnea, particularly in an incompletely immunized infant or child.

851.
Q: What is the most common cause of myocarditis in children?
A: Viral infection, with enteroviruses and adenoviruses classically associated; other viruses can also cause myocarditis.

852.
Q: What finding distinguishes myocarditis from an uncomplicated viral upper respiratory infection?
A: New ventricular dysfunction, arrhythmias, unexplained tachycardia, cardiomegaly, elevated troponin, or signs of heart failure after a viral illness.

853.
Q: What is a dangerous complication of acute myocarditis?
A: Malignant ventricular arrhythmias or cardiogenic shock.

854.
Q: What is the typical echocardiographic finding in dilated cardiomyopathy?
A: Dilated ventricles with impaired systolic function and reduced ejection fraction.

855.
Q: What is the primary hemodynamic problem in dilated cardiomyopathy?
A: Reduced myocardial contractility → decreased cardiac output → compensatory neurohormonal activation and heart failure.

856.
Q: What is a major cause of dilated cardiomyopathy in children?
A: Myocarditis, although genetic, metabolic, toxic, and other causes also occur.

857.
Q: What is the key difference between dilated and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?
A: Dilated cardiomyopathy primarily causes systolic dysfunction, whereas hypertrophic cardiomyopathy primarily causes diastolic dysfunction and may cause dynamic outflow obstruction.

858.
Q: What is restrictive cardiomyopathy?
A: A disorder characterized by impaired ventricular relaxation and filling despite relatively preserved systolic function.

859.
Q: What physical finding may suggest restrictive cardiomyopathy in a child?
A: Elevated venous pressure, hepatomegaly, peripheral edema, and exercise intolerance due to impaired ventricular filling.

860.
Q: What is a major long-term complication of pediatric cardiomyopathy?
A: Progressive heart failure and potentially sudden cardiac death from arrhythmia.

861.
Q: What is the difference between central and peripheral cyanosis?
A: Central cyanosis involves the lips/tongue and indicates arterial desaturation; peripheral cyanosis can result from vasoconstriction despite normal arterial oxygenation.

862.
Q: What finding favors central cyanosis in a newborn?
A: Cyanosis of the tongue and oral mucosa, rather than only the hands and feet.

863.
Q: What is acrocyanosis in a newborn?
A: Bluish discoloration of the hands and feet due to peripheral vasoconstriction; it can be physiologic shortly after birth.

864.
Q: What is the hyperoxia test traditionally used for in a cyanotic neonate?
A: It can help distinguish pulmonary disease from cyanotic congenital heart disease, although echocardiography and pulse oximetry are now preferred diagnostic tools.

865.
Q: What does failure to substantially increase arterial oxygenation with 100% oxygen suggest in a neonate?
A: Right-to-left shunting, as occurs with certain cyanotic congenital heart defects or severe persistent pulmonary hypertension.

866.
Q: Why does severe pulmonary hypertension cause right-to-left shunting in newborns?
A: Elevated pulmonary vascular resistance maintains fetal-type shunting through the ductus arteriosus and/or foramen ovale.

867.
Q: What is the normal direction of flow through the fetal foramen ovale?
A: Right atrium → left atrium, allowing blood to bypass the fetal lungs.

868.
Q: What causes functional closure of the foramen ovale after birth?
A: Increased left atrial pressure causes the septum primum to press against the septum secundum.

869.
Q: What is a patent foramen ovale (PFO)?
A: Persistent potential communication between the atria due to incomplete fusion of the fetal atrial septa.

870.
Q: Why is a PFO usually asymptomatic in children?
A: Left atrial pressure normally exceeds right atrial pressure, preventing significant right-to-left shunting.

871.
Q: What is the major concern with an unrepaired significant ventricular septal defect?
A: Pulmonary overcirculation → pulmonary hypertension → pulmonary vascular remodeling → Eisenmenger syndrome.

872.
Q: Why do symptoms of a large VSD often become apparent several weeks after birth rather than immediately?
A: Pulmonary vascular resistance falls progressively after birth, increasing left-to-right shunting.

873.
Q: What is the usual direction of blood flow in an uncomplicated ASD?
A: Left-to-right, from the higher-pressure left atrium to the right atrium.

874.
Q: Why may an ASD remain clinically silent during childhood?
A: The pressure difference between the atria is relatively small, so the shunt may be tolerated for years.

875.
Q: What is the long-term consequence of a large unrepaired ASD?
A: Right-sided volume overload, atrial arrhythmias, pulmonary hypertension, and eventually Eisenmenger physiology.

876.
Q: What type of shunt is produced by an isolated PDA?
A: Left-to-right shunt from the aorta into the pulmonary artery.

877.
Q: What peripheral pulse finding may occur with a significant PDA?
A: Bounding peripheral pulses and widened pulse pressure due to increased diastolic runoff into the pulmonary circulation.

878.
Q: What is the major physiologic consequence of pulmonary overcirculation from a large PDA?
A: Left ventricular volume overload and congestive heart failure.

879.
Q: Why does indomethacin close a PDA?
A: It inhibits cyclooxygenase → decreases prostaglandin synthesis → promotes ductal constriction.

880.
Q: Why should prostaglandin E₁ be used cautiously in neonates?
A: It can cause apnea, hypotension, fever, and flushing, so respiratory support should be readily available.

881.
Q: What is the most common chronic respiratory disease of childhood?
A: Asthma.

882.
Q: What type of pulmonary physiology characterizes asthma?
A: Reversible airflow obstruction with airway hyperresponsiveness and inflammation.

883.
Q: What spirometry finding supports asthma in an older child who can perform pulmonary function testing?
A: Obstructive airflow limitation with significant reversibility after bronchodilator administration.

884.
Q: What is the typical FEV₁/FVC pattern in obstructive lung disease?
A: Decreased FEV₁/FVC ratio.

885.
Q: What happens to residual volume during an asthma exacerbation?
A: It may increase because of air trapping.

886.
Q: What environmental exposure is an important modifiable trigger for pediatric asthma?
A: Tobacco smoke exposure, including secondhand smoke.

887.
Q: What other common environmental trigger should be addressed in children with poorly controlled asthma?
A: Indoor allergens, such as dust mites, cockroaches, mold, and animal dander, depending on the child's sensitization.

888.
Q: What is exercise-induced bronchoconstriction?
A: Transient airway narrowing triggered by exercise, producing cough, wheezing, chest tightness, or dyspnea.

889.
Q: What medication can prevent exercise-induced bronchoconstriction when used shortly before exercise?
A: An inhaled short-acting β₂-agonist, such as albuterol.

890.
Q: What does frequent reliance on a rescue inhaler suggest in a child with asthma?
A: Poor asthma control and the need to reassess adherence, triggers, inhaler technique, and controller therapy.

891.
Q: What is the most important first step when a child presents with severe respiratory distress?
A: Rapid assessment and stabilization of airway, breathing, and circulation, with oxygen and ventilatory support as indicated.

892.
Q: What does nasal flaring indicate in an infant?
A: Increased work of breathing caused by respiratory distress.

893.
Q: What does grunting signify in a newborn with respiratory distress?
A: The infant is generating positive end-expiratory pressure to prevent alveolar collapse.

894.
Q: What does head bobbing in an infant with respiratory distress indicate?
A: Severe increased work of breathing, particularly involving accessory respiratory muscles.

895.
Q: What is a "silent chest" in a child with severe asthma?
A: Markedly reduced or absent wheezing because airflow is so severely limited; it is a sign of impending respiratory failure.

896.
Q: What is the preferred route for oxygen delivery in a child with significant hypoxemia who is spontaneously breathing?
A: Use an age-appropriate supplemental oxygen delivery system such as nasal cannula, face mask, or high-flow nasal cannula depending on severity.

897.
Q: When should noninvasive positive-pressure ventilation be considered in pediatric respiratory failure?
A: When there is significant respiratory distress or hypoxemia/hypercapnia but the child is awake enough to protect the airway and does not require immediate intubation.

898.
Q: What finding indicates that noninvasive ventilation is failing?
A: Worsening hypoxemia, rising CO₂, altered mental status, exhaustion, inability to protect the airway, or hemodynamic instability.

899.
Q: What is a major indication for endotracheal intubation in a child with respiratory disease?
A: Impending or established respiratory failure, especially with apnea, severe hypoxemia, hypercapnia, exhaustion, or inability to protect the airway.

900.
Q: What is the most important principle when ventilating a child with severe obstructive lung disease?
A: Allow adequate expiratory time to reduce dynamic hyperinflation and auto-PEEP; excessively rapid ventilation can worsen air trapping.

901.
Q: What is the most common congenital heart defect in children?
A: Ventricular septal defect (VSD). Small VSDs may close spontaneously; larger defects can cause left-to-right shunting and heart failure.

902.
Q: What murmur is classically heard with a VSD?
A: Harsh, holosystolic murmur best heard at the left lower sternal border, often with a palpable thrill.

903.
Q: Why can a large VSD cause heart failure in infancy?
A: As pulmonary vascular resistance falls after birth, left-to-right shunting increases, causing pulmonary overcirculation and volume overload.

904.
Q: What is the most common acyanotic congenital heart defect?
A: VSD is the most common congenital cardiac defect overall and a major cause of acyanotic congenital heart disease.

905.
Q: What is the classic murmur of an atrial septal defect (ASD)?
A: Fixed, wide splitting of S2 with a systolic ejection murmur at the left upper sternal border due to increased flow across the pulmonic valve.

906.
Q: Why is S2 fixed and widely split in ASD?
A: Increased right-sided volume causes prolonged right ventricular ejection, delaying P2; the split does not significantly vary with respiration.

907.
Q: What is the classic congenital heart defect associated with a continuous "machine-like" murmur?
A: Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA).

908.
Q: Where is the PDA murmur best heard?
A: At the left upper sternal border/infraclavicular area, often radiating toward the back.

909.
Q: What fetal vessel connects the pulmonary artery to the aorta?
A: The ductus arteriosus.

910.
Q: What medication is used to close a hemodynamically significant PDA?
A: Indomethacin or ibuprofen, which inhibit prostaglandin synthesis.

911.
Q: What medication can maintain ductal patency in ductal-dependent congenital heart disease?
A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil).

912.
Q: What major adverse effect must be monitored when giving prostaglandin E₁ to a neonate?
A: Apnea. Respiratory support may be required.

913.
Q: What congenital heart defect classically causes differential cyanosis?
A: PDA with pulmonary hypertension/Eisenmenger physiology, producing cyanosis of the lower extremities more than the upper extremities.

914.
Q: What are the four components of Tetralogy of Fallot?
A: VSD, right ventricular outflow obstruction, overriding aorta, and right ventricular hypertrophy.

915.
Q: What determines the severity of cyanosis in Tetralogy of Fallot?
A: The degree of right ventricular outflow obstruction.

916.
Q: What is a Tet spell?
A: An acute episode of increased right-to-left shunting in Tetralogy of Fallot causing severe cyanosis, tachypnea, and sometimes syncope.

917.
Q: What position should be used during a Tet spell?
A: Knee-chest position in infants; this increases systemic vascular resistance and reduces right-to-left shunting.

918.
Q: What medications can be used for a severe Tet spell?
A: Oxygen, morphine, IV fluids, and a β-blocker such as propranolol; phenylephrine can increase systemic vascular resistance when needed.

919.
Q: What is the classic chest x-ray appearance in Tetralogy of Fallot?
A: A "boot-shaped" heart due to right ventricular hypertrophy and concavity of the pulmonary artery region.

920.
Q: What is the classic presentation of transposition of the great arteries (TGA)?
A: Profound neonatal cyanosis shortly after birth, often with minimal response to oxygen.

921.
Q: Why can an infant with TGA survive after birth?
A: Survival requires mixing between pulmonary and systemic circulations, such as through an ASD, VSD, or PDA.

922.
Q: What is the immediate medication used to maintain circulation in a neonate with suspected ductal-dependent TGA?
A: Prostaglandin E₁ (alprostadil) to maintain PDA patency.

923.
Q: What is the definitive treatment for transposition of the great arteries?
A: Arterial switch operation.

924.
Q: What congenital heart defect is associated with Turner syndrome?
A: Coarctation of the aorta and bicuspid aortic valve.

925.
Q: What physical finding suggests coarctation of the aorta?
A: Higher blood pressure in the upper extremities than lower extremities, with weak or delayed femoral pulses.

926.
Q: What chest x-ray finding can occur in older children with coarctation?
A: Rib notching from collateral intercostal arteries and the "figure 3" sign.

927.
Q: What congenital cardiac lesion is classically associated with Down syndrome?
A: Atrioventricular septal defect (AVSD) due to abnormal endocardial cushion development.

928.
Q: What is the most common cause of heart failure in infants with congenital heart disease?
A: Large left-to-right shunts, especially VSD, PDA, and AVSD, after pulmonary vascular resistance decreases.

929.
Q: What are typical clinical findings of pediatric heart failure?
A: Tachypnea, tachycardia, feeding difficulty, diaphoresis with feeds, poor weight gain, hepatomegaly, and respiratory distress.

930.
Q: What is the most common cause of supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) in infants and children?
A: Reentrant tachycardia, commonly involving an accessory pathway or AV nodal reentry.

931.
Q: How does SVT typically appear on ECG?
A: Regular narrow-complex tachycardia, often with a rate >220/min in infants.

932.
Q: What is the initial treatment for a stable infant with SVT?
A: Vagal maneuvers, followed by adenosine if unsuccessful.

933.
Q: What is the treatment for unstable pediatric SVT?
A: Synchronized cardioversion.

934.
Q: What is the most common cause of bronchiolitis in infants?
A: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV).

935.
Q: What age group is most commonly affected by bronchiolitis?
A: Primarily children <2 years, especially infants.

936.
Q: What is the classic presentation of bronchiolitis?
A: A viral prodrome followed by tachypnea, wheezing, crackles, increased work of breathing, and feeding difficulty.

937.
Q: What is the main treatment for uncomplicated bronchiolitis?
A: Supportive care, including hydration and oxygen when hypoxemia is present.

938.
Q: Are routine antibiotics indicated for uncomplicated bronchiolitis?
A: No. Bronchiolitis is usually viral; antibiotics are reserved for a separate suspected bacterial infection.

939.
Q: What clinical finding strongly suggests severe bronchiolitis requiring escalation of respiratory support?
A: Hypoxemia, apnea, exhaustion, or significant respiratory distress.

940.
Q: What is the classic presentation of viral croup?
A: Barking cough, hoarseness, inspiratory stridor, and symptoms that often worsen at night.

941.
Q: What virus most commonly causes croup?
A: Parainfluenza virus, particularly types 1 and 2.

942.
Q: What is the treatment for mild croup?
A: Dexamethasone, typically given as a single dose.

943.
Q: What is added for moderate-to-severe croup with significant stridor or respiratory distress?
A: Nebulized epinephrine plus corticosteroid therapy.

944.
Q: What distinguishes epiglottitis from croup?
A: Epiglottitis typically presents with high fever, toxic appearance, drooling, dysphagia, muffled voice, and tripod positioning, rather than a barking cough.

945.
Q: What organism classically causes epiglottitis in an unimmunized child?
A: Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib).

946.
Q: What should be avoided when epiglottitis is suspected?
A: Do not forcibly examine the pharynx with a tongue depressor, because this can precipitate airway obstruction.

947.
Q: What is the classic presentation of bacterial pneumonia in a child?
A: Acute fever, cough, tachypnea, respiratory distress, and focal crackles/decreased breath sounds, sometimes with focal infiltrates on chest x-ray.

948.
Q: What is the most common bacterial cause of community-acquired pneumonia in children?
A: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a major cause of typical bacterial pneumonia.

949.
Q: What clinical finding is particularly useful for diagnosing pneumonia in young children?
A: Tachypnea, especially when measured while the child is calm and afebrile.

950.
Q: What is the characteristic respiratory presentation of cystic fibrosis?
A: Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, chronic cough, bronchiectasis, and thick airway secretions due to defective CFTR-mediated chloride transport.

951.
Q: What is the most common cause of wheezing in an infant?
A: Viral bronchiolitis, especially during the first 2 years of life.

952.
Q: What clinical features favor asthma over bronchiolitis in a child with recurrent wheezing?
A: Recurrent episodes, personal/family history of atopy, symptom variability, nighttime symptoms, and improvement with bronchodilators.

953.
Q: What is the first-line controller medication for persistent asthma in children?
A: Inhaled corticosteroids (ICS).

954.
Q: What medication provides rapid relief during an acute asthma exacerbation?
A: Inhaled short-acting β₂-agonist (SABA), such as albuterol.

955.
Q: What systemic medication is commonly added for a moderate-to-severe pediatric asthma exacerbation?
A: Systemic corticosteroids, such as oral prednisone/prednisolone or IV methylprednisolone.

956.
Q: What is the next step if severe asthma remains poorly responsive to inhaled β₂-agonists?
A: Add ipratropium, give systemic corticosteroids, and consider IV magnesium sulfate for severe refractory exacerbations.

957.
Q: What clinical finding indicates impending respiratory failure in a child with asthma?
A: Altered mental status, exhaustion, silent chest, severe hypoxemia, or rising PaCO₂.

958.
Q: Why is a normal or elevated PaCO₂ concerning in severe pediatric asthma?
A: Early asthma causes hyperventilation and low PaCO₂. A normalizing or rising PaCO₂ suggests respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.

959.
Q: What is the most common cause of foreign-body aspiration in young children?
A: Inhalation of food or small objects, particularly in toddlers who are developing chewing and swallowing skills.

960.
Q: What is the classic presentation of an aspirated airway foreign body?
A: Sudden-onset coughing, choking, unilateral wheezing, or asymmetric breath sounds in an otherwise previously healthy child.

961.
Q: What imaging finding can suggest a bronchial foreign body?
A: Unilateral hyperinflation due to a ball-valve obstruction; expiratory films may demonstrate air trapping.

962.
Q: What is the definitive diagnostic and therapeutic procedure for suspected airway foreign body aspiration?
A: Bronchoscopy.

963.
Q: What is the classic presentation of a tension pneumothorax in a child?
A: Sudden respiratory distress, unilateral absent breath sounds, hypotension, tachycardia, and potentially tracheal deviation.

964.
Q: What should be done immediately for a child with suspected tension pneumothorax and hemodynamic instability?
A: Immediate needle or catheter decompression, followed by chest-tube placement; do not delay for imaging.

965.
Q: What is the most common cause of respiratory distress syndrome (RDS) in a premature neonate?
A: Surfactant deficiency due to immature type II pneumocytes.

966.
Q: What are classic findings of neonatal RDS?
A: Tachypnea, grunting, nasal flaring, retractions, hypoxemia, and diffuse reticulogranular/"ground-glass" opacities on chest x-ray.

967.
Q: What medication can reduce the risk of neonatal respiratory distress syndrome when preterm delivery is anticipated?
A: Antenatal corticosteroids, such as betamethasone, accelerate fetal lung maturation.

968.
Q: What therapy is used to treat significant neonatal respiratory distress syndrome due to surfactant deficiency?
A: Exogenous surfactant plus appropriate respiratory support.

969.
Q: What neonatal respiratory disorder is associated with cesarean delivery without labor?
A: Transient tachypnea of the newborn (TTN) due to delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid.

970.
Q: How does TTN differ from neonatal respiratory distress syndrome?
A: TTN generally occurs in term or near-term infants, is usually mild/self-limited, and chest x-ray may show prominent pulmonary vascular markings and fluid in the fissures.

971.
Q: What is meconium aspiration syndrome?
A: Respiratory disease caused by aspiration of meconium-stained amniotic fluid, producing airway obstruction, inflammation, and impaired gas exchange.

972.
Q: What radiographic findings are typical of meconium aspiration syndrome?
A: Patchy, coarse pulmonary opacities with hyperinflation, often with areas of atelectasis.

973.
Q: What major complication is associated with severe meconium aspiration syndrome?
A: Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN).

974.
Q: What is the underlying problem in persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?
A: Failure of normal postnatal reduction in pulmonary vascular resistance, causing right-to-left shunting through fetal circulatory pathways.

975.
Q: What clinical finding suggests persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?
A: Severe hypoxemia that is disproportionate to lung findings, often with differential preductal and postductal oxygen saturation.

976.
Q: What is a major treatment for severe persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn?
A: Inhaled nitric oxide, which selectively decreases pulmonary vascular resistance.

977.
Q: What is the normal physiologic change in pulmonary vascular resistance immediately after birth?
A: Pulmonary vascular resistance decreases as the lungs expand and oxygenation increases.

978.
Q: What happens to the ductus arteriosus after birth in a healthy term infant?
A: Increased oxygen tension and decreased prostaglandins cause functional closure, followed later by anatomic closure.

979.
Q: What is the classic presentation of a critical left-sided obstructive cardiac lesion when the ductus arteriosus closes?
A: Acute cardiovascular collapse, poor perfusion, metabolic acidosis, weak pulses, and shock in a neonate.

980.
Q: Why can prostaglandin E₁ be lifesaving in a neonate with critical coarctation of the aorta?
A: It reopens/maintains the ductus arteriosus, allowing blood flow from the pulmonary artery to the descending aorta and improving systemic perfusion.

981.
Q: What congenital heart lesion causes a harsh systolic ejection murmur with decreased pulses and may cause syncope?
A: Aortic stenosis.

982.
Q: Where is an aortic stenosis murmur best heard?
A: At the right upper sternal border, often radiating to the carotids.

983.
Q: What congenital cardiac lesion produces a systolic ejection murmur at the left upper sternal border with decreased pulmonary blood flow?
A: Pulmonary valve stenosis.

984.
Q: What finding suggests severe pulmonary stenosis in an infant?
A: Cyanosis, particularly when right-to-left atrial shunting occurs because of severe right-sided obstruction.

985.
Q: What is the classic congenital cardiac lesion associated with an interrupted aortic arch?
A: 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge syndrome) is strongly associated.

986.
Q: What other cardiac defects are associated with 22q11.2 deletion syndrome?
A: Conotruncal abnormalities, including truncus arteriosus, Tetralogy of Fallot, and interrupted aortic arch.

987.
Q: What is truncus arteriosus?
A: A congenital defect in which one arterial trunk arises from the heart and receives blood from both ventricles, usually accompanied by a VSD.

988.
Q: Why does truncus arteriosus cause pulmonary overcirculation?
A: After pulmonary vascular resistance falls, blood is preferentially directed into the pulmonary circulation, causing heart failure and pulmonary hypertension.

989.
Q: What is the characteristic finding in total anomalous pulmonary venous return (TAPVR)?
A: All pulmonary veins drain into the systemic venous circulation rather than the left atrium.

990.
Q: What is required for survival in total anomalous pulmonary venous return?
A: An atrial-level communication, allowing oxygenated pulmonary venous blood to reach the systemic circulation.

991.
Q: What is the classic "snowman" or "figure-of-8" radiographic sign associated with?
A: Supracardiac total anomalous pulmonary venous return, typically appearing later in infancy.

992.
Q: What is Ebstein anomaly?
A: A congenital malformation with apical displacement of the tricuspid valve, causing tricuspid regurgitation and right atrial enlargement.

993.
Q: Which medication exposure is classically associated with Ebstein anomaly?
A: Maternal lithium exposure is a classic association.

994.
Q: What is the typical murmur of tricuspid regurgitation?
A: A holosystolic murmur at the lower left sternal border that increases with inspiration.

995.
Q: What is the classic ECG finding in Wolff-Parkinson-White (WPW) syndrome?
A: Short PR interval, delta wave, and widened QRS complex due to an accessory conduction pathway.

996.
Q: Why can WPW cause paroxysmal tachycardia?
A: An accessory atrioventricular pathway permits reentrant electrical circuits between the atria and ventricles.

997.
Q: What is Kawasaki disease's major cardiovascular complication?
A: Coronary artery aneurysms, which can lead to thrombosis or myocardial infarction.

998.
Q: What is the standard treatment for Kawasaki disease?
A: IVIG plus high-dose aspirin during the acute inflammatory phase, followed by lower-dose aspirin for antiplatelet therapy.

999.
Q: Which children with Kawasaki disease require particularly careful coronary artery follow-up?
A: Children with persistent fever, delayed treatment, or coronary artery abnormalities, because they have increased risk of coronary aneurysms and thrombosis.

1000.
Q: What is the most important initial assessment in any critically ill child with respiratory or cardiovascular symptoms?
A: Follow ABCDE: Airway, Breathing, Circulation, Disability, Exposure. Immediately identify and treat life-threatening airway obstruction, respiratory failure, shock, or hypoxemia.

=============================================================

For FlashCards USMLE 2
Section: PEDIATRICS
Topic: Adolescent Medicine

1.
Q: What is the normal sequence of pubertal development in girls?
A: Thelarche (breast development) → pubarche → peak height velocity → menarche. Menarche typically occurs about 2–3 years after thelarche.

2.
Q: What is usually the first visible sign of puberty in girls?
A: Breast budding (thelarche), usually followed by pubic hair development.

3.
Q: What is usually the first sign of puberty in boys?
A: Testicular enlargement, with testicular volume reaching approximately ≥4 mL.

4.
Q: What is the normal sequence of pubertal development in boys?
A: Testicular enlargement → pubic hair development → penile growth → peak height velocity → facial/axillary hair and voice deepening.

5.
Q: What hormone initiates normal puberty?
A: Pulsatile secretion of gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) from the hypothalamus stimulates secretion of LH and FSH from the anterior pituitary.

6.
Q: What are the major hormones secreted by the anterior pituitary during puberty?
A: Luteinizing hormone (LH) and follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH).

7.
Q: What is the role of LH in puberty?
A: In boys, LH stimulates Leydig cells to produce testosterone; in girls, it stimulates theca cells and later supports ovulation and corpus luteum function.

8.
Q: What is the role of FSH during puberty?
A: In boys, FSH stimulates Sertoli cells and spermatogenesis; in girls, it promotes follicular development and estrogen production.

9.
Q: What is precocious puberty?
A: Pubertal development occurring before age 8 in girls or before age 9 in boys.

10.
Q: What is the most common cause of precocious puberty in girls?
A: Central precocious puberty, which is usually idiopathic.

11.
Q: What is the most common cause of precocious puberty in boys?
A: An underlying pathologic cause is more likely than in girls, including CNS lesions or peripheral androgen production.

12.
Q: What distinguishes central from peripheral precocious puberty?
A: Central precocious puberty is GnRH-dependent, with pubertal LH/FSH secretion; peripheral precocious puberty is GnRH-independent and results from excess sex steroids.

13.
Q: What test helps distinguish central from peripheral precocious puberty?
A: A GnRH stimulation test; pubertal LH elevation supports central precocious puberty.

14.
Q: What imaging study should be considered in a boy with central precocious puberty?
A: Brain MRI, because CNS pathology is more common in boys with central precocious puberty.

15.
Q: What is delayed puberty in girls?
A: No breast development by approximately age 13, or no menarche by approximately age 15 or within 3 years of thelarche.

16.
Q: What is delayed puberty in boys?
A: Failure of testicular enlargement by approximately age 14.

17.
Q: What is the most common cause of delayed puberty in otherwise healthy adolescents?
A: Constitutional delay of growth and puberty.

18.
Q: What is constitutional delay of growth and puberty?
A: A normal variant characterized by delayed skeletal maturation and delayed pubertal development, often with a family history of "late bloomers."

19.
Q: What laboratory pattern suggests primary hypogonadism?
A: Low sex hormones with elevated LH and FSH due to loss of negative feedback.

20.
Q: What laboratory pattern suggests hypogonadotropic hypogonadism?
A: Low sex hormones with low or inappropriately normal LH and FSH, indicating hypothalamic or pituitary dysfunction.

21.
Q: What is the most common cause of secondary amenorrhea in an adolescent?
A: Pregnancy until proven otherwise.

22.
Q: What is the first test in an adolescent with secondary amenorrhea?
A: Urine or serum β-hCG pregnancy test.

23.
Q: What is primary amenorrhea?
A: Absence of menarche by approximately age 15, or more than 3 years after thelarche.

24.
Q: What is the most common cause of primary amenorrhea with normal secondary sexual characteristics and absent uterus?
A: Müllerian agenesis or androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS).

25.
Q: How can Müllerian agenesis be distinguished from androgen insensitivity syndrome?
A: Müllerian agenesis has 46,XX karyotype, normal ovaries, absent uterus, and normal pubic/axillary hair. AIS has 46,XY karyotype, testes, absent uterus, and sparse/absent pubic and axillary hair.

26.
Q: What is the typical presentation of complete androgen insensitivity syndrome?
A: A phenotypic female with 46,XY karyotype, normal breast development, absent uterus, and sparse or absent pubic/axillary hair.

27.
Q: What is the most common cause of secondary amenorrhea in adolescents after pregnancy is excluded?
A: Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea, often associated with low energy availability, excessive exercise, psychological stress, or eating disorders.

28.
Q: What is the classic triad of female athlete triad?
A: Low energy availability, menstrual dysfunction, and decreased bone mineral density.

29.
Q: What broader concept has replaced the narrow "female athlete triad" model?
A: Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport (RED-S), which can affect multiple physiologic systems and athletes of any sex.

30.
Q: What is an important consequence of prolonged functional hypothalamic amenorrhea?
A: Low estrogen levels and impaired bone mineralization, increasing the risk of osteopenia and stress fractures.

31.
Q: What is the most common cause of dysmenorrhea in adolescents?
A: Primary dysmenorrhea due to increased endometrial prostaglandin production.

32.
Q: What is the first-line treatment for primary dysmenorrhea?
A: NSAIDs, such as ibuprofen or naproxen, which decrease prostaglandin synthesis.

33.
Q: When should secondary dysmenorrhea be suspected in an adolescent?
A: When pain begins after previously painless cycles, progressively worsens, occurs with abnormal bleeding, dyspareunia, infertility, or fails to respond to NSAIDs/hormonal therapy.

34.
Q: What is the most common cause of secondary dysmenorrhea in adolescents?
A: Endometriosis.

35.
Q: What adolescent symptoms strongly suggest endometriosis?
A: Severe cyclic pelvic pain, dysmenorrhea refractory to NSAIDs, chronic pelvic pain, and pain affecting school or daily activities.

36.
Q: What is the initial treatment for suspected adolescent endometriosis?
A: NSAIDs and hormonal suppression, commonly combined hormonal contraceptives; persistent symptoms warrant further gynecologic evaluation.

37.
Q: What menstrual pattern is common during the first several years after menarche?
A: Irregular, anovulatory cycles are common because the hypothalamic-pituitary-ovarian axis is still maturing.

38.
Q: What is the most common cause of heavy menstrual bleeding in adolescents?
A: Anovulatory cycles, although bleeding disorders must be considered, especially around menarche.

39.
Q: What bleeding disorder should be considered in an adolescent with severe heavy menstrual bleeding from menarche?
A: von Willebrand disease.

40.
Q: What clinical history suggests an underlying bleeding disorder in an adolescent with heavy menstrual bleeding?
A: Heavy bleeding since menarche, easy bruising, frequent epistaxis, prolonged bleeding after dental procedures or surgery, or a family history of abnormal bleeding.

41.
Q: What is the most common eating disorder among adolescents?
A: Binge-eating disorder is common overall, while anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa are particularly important high-yield adolescent diagnoses.

42.
Q: What are the core diagnostic features of anorexia nervosa?
A: Restriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight, intense fear of gaining weight, and disturbance in body-image perception or lack of recognition of the seriousness of low weight.

43.
Q: What is the characteristic physical finding associated with anorexia nervosa?
A: Low body weight, often accompanied by bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, amenorrhea/menstrual dysfunction, and lanugo.

44.
Q: What electrolyte abnormality is classically associated with purging in bulimia nervosa?
A: Hypokalemia, often accompanied by metabolic alkalosis.

45.
Q: What physical finding can result from self-induced vomiting in bulimia nervosa?
A: Dental enamel erosion, parotid enlargement, and calluses on the dorsum of the hand (Russell sign).

46.
Q: What is an important cardiac complication of severe anorexia nervosa?
A: Bradycardia, hypotension, and cardiac arrhythmias, particularly when electrolyte abnormalities or marked malnutrition are present.

47.
Q: What electrolyte abnormality is particularly dangerous during nutritional rehabilitation of severe anorexia nervosa?
A: Hypophosphatemia due to refeeding syndrome, which can cause weakness, respiratory failure, cardiac dysfunction, seizures, and potentially death.

48.
Q: What is the key principle when initiating nutritional rehabilitation in a severely malnourished adolescent?
A: Careful nutritional rehabilitation with monitoring of phosphate, potassium, magnesium, glucose, fluid status, and cardiac function to detect refeeding complications.

49.
Q: What is the most important initial approach when screening an adolescent for depression?
A: Use a validated screening tool such as the PHQ-A, followed by assessment of functional impairment, suicide risk, and other psychiatric symptoms when screening is positive.

50.
Q: What should always be assessed when an adolescent expresses suicidal thoughts?
A: Current suicidal intent, plan, access to lethal means, prior attempts, psychiatric symptoms, substance use, protective factors, and immediate safety. Active suicidal intent or an imminent risk of self-harm requires urgent psychiatric evaluation and safety intervention.

50. Q: What age range generally defines adolescence?

A: Approximately 10–19 years. Puberty usually occurs during early-to-mid adolescence, but timing varies considerably.

51. Q: What are the three developmental stages of adolescence?

A: Early adolescence: ~10–13 years
Middle adolescence: ~14–17 years
Late adolescence: ~18–21 years

52. Q: What is the most important principle when interviewing an adolescent patient?

A: Provide confidential, private time with the adolescent without parents present, while explaining the limits of confidentiality.

53. Q: What are the major exceptions to adolescent confidentiality?

A: Confidentiality must be breached when there is imminent risk of harm, suspected abuse/neglect, or certain legally mandated situations such as reporting specific infectious diseases or serious threats to others.

54. Q: What screening framework is commonly used for adolescent psychosocial assessment?

A: HEADSSS:
Home
Education/Employment
Activities
Drugs
Sexuality
Suicide/Depression
Safety

55. Q: Why should adolescents be interviewed privately?

A: Private interviewing improves disclosure of sensitive issues including sexual activity, substance use, depression, suicidal ideation, abuse, and eating disorders.

56. Q: What is the recommended general approach to adolescent preventive care?

A: Assess growth/puberty, nutrition, physical activity, mental health, substance use, sexual health, safety, immunizations, and psychosocial development.

57. Q: What is the normal sequence of female pubertal development?

A: Thelarche → pubarche → peak height velocity → menarche.

58. Q: What is the usual first sign of true puberty in girls?

A: Breast development (thelarche) due to estrogen exposure.

59. Q: What is usually the first sign of puberty in boys?

A: Testicular enlargement (>4 mL), reflecting activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis.

60. Q: What is the usual sequence of male pubertal development?

A: Testicular enlargement → pubic/axillary hair → penile growth → voice deepening → facial hair.

61. Q: What hormone initiates central puberty?

A: Pulsatile secretion of gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) from the hypothalamus.

62. Q: What hormones increase in response to GnRH during central puberty?

A: LH and FSH, which stimulate gonadal production of sex steroids.

63. Q: What is the typical order of growth during puberty?

A: Hands/feet → limbs → trunk, producing a temporary period of disproportionate growth.

64. Q: What is the most common cause of delayed puberty in an otherwise healthy adolescent?

A: Constitutional delay of growth and puberty.

65. Q: When is puberty considered delayed in girls?

A: No breast development by approximately 13 years or no menarche by 15 years or within ~3 years after thelarche.

66. Q: When is puberty considered delayed in boys?

A: No testicular enlargement by approximately 14 years.

67. Q: What distinguishes constitutional delay of puberty from hypogonadism?

A: Constitutional delay usually has delayed bone age with otherwise normal growth and often a family history of late puberty.

68. Q: What laboratory pattern indicates primary hypogonadism?

A: Low sex hormones + elevated LH/FSH due to loss of negative feedback.

69. Q: What laboratory pattern indicates central hypogonadism?

A: Low sex hormones + low or inappropriately normal LH/FSH.

70. Q: What is precocious puberty in girls?

A: Development of secondary sexual characteristics before age 8.

71. Q: What is precocious puberty in boys?

A: Development of secondary sexual characteristics before age 9.

72. Q: What is the most common cause of precocious puberty in girls?

A: Idiopathic central precocious puberty.

73. Q: What is an important concern when a boy develops precocious puberty?

A: Search carefully for a pathologic CNS or adrenal/testicular source, because pathologic causes are more common in boys.

74. Q: What is the treatment for central precocious puberty?

A: Long-acting GnRH agonists such as leuprolide suppress LH/FSH secretion and halt premature sexual maturation.

75. Q: What is the most common menstrual abnormality during the first years after menarche?

A: Irregular, anovulatory menstrual cycles.

76. Q: What is the most common cause of heavy menstrual bleeding in an adolescent?

A: Anovulatory cycles due to an immature hypothalamic-pituitary-ovarian axis.

77. Q: What important disorder should be considered in an adolescent with severe menorrhagia beginning at menarche?

A: Inherited bleeding disorders, particularly von Willebrand disease.

78. Q: What is the typical first-line treatment for stable adolescents with heavy menstrual bleeding?

A: Combined hormonal contraception or high-dose estrogen-containing therapy when appropriate; NSAIDs can also reduce menstrual blood loss.

79. Q: What is the most common cause of secondary amenorrhea in an adolescent?

A: Pregnancy until proven otherwise.

80. Q: What should be done first in an adolescent with secondary amenorrhea?

A: Obtain a pregnancy test (β-hCG).

81. Q: What is the most common cause of dysmenorrhea in adolescents?

A: Primary dysmenorrhea caused by increased endometrial prostaglandin production.

82. Q: What is first-line treatment for primary dysmenorrhea?

A: NSAIDs, which inhibit cyclooxygenase and decrease prostaglandin synthesis.

83. Q: When should secondary dysmenorrhea be suspected?

A: Severe or progressively worsening pain, abnormal pelvic examination, abnormal bleeding, dyspareunia, or failure to respond to NSAIDs/hormonal therapy.

84. Q: What is the most common cause of secondary dysmenorrhea in adolescents?

A: Endometriosis.

85. Q: What adolescent symptoms strongly suggest endometriosis?

A: Dysmenorrhea refractory to NSAIDs/hormonal therapy, chronic pelvic pain, dyspareunia, or pain causing significant functional impairment.

86. Q: What is the most common contraceptive method used by adolescents?

A: Condoms and hormonal contraception are commonly used; long-acting reversible contraception (LARC) is highly effective and recommended for adolescents who desire contraception.

87. Q: Which contraceptive methods have the highest effectiveness in adolescents?

A: Long-acting reversible contraception (LARC), particularly IUDs and contraceptive implants, because effectiveness does not depend on daily adherence.

88. Q: Does adolescent age alone contraindicate IUD placement?

A: No. IUDs can be safely used by adolescents, including those who have never given birth.

89. Q: What is the major advantage of the contraceptive implant?

A: Extremely effective, long-acting, reversible contraception that does not require daily adherence.

90. Q: What is the emergency contraceptive mechanism of levonorgestrel?

A: Primarily delays or inhibits ovulation; it does not terminate an established pregnancy.

91. Q: Which emergency contraceptive is most effective regardless of timing within the appropriate window?

A: A copper IUD is highly effective emergency contraception and provides ongoing contraception.

92. Q: Can emergency contraception be used by adolescents?

A: Yes. Adolescents can use emergency contraception; age is not a contraindication.

93. Q: What is the most important counseling point regarding condoms?

A: Condoms reduce transmission of STIs and pregnancy, but require correct and consistent use.

94. Q: Which contraceptive method also protects against sexually transmitted infections?

A: Condoms are the principal contraceptive method that also provides meaningful STI protection.

95. Q: What is the most common bacterial STI in adolescents and young adults?

A: Chlamydia trachomatis.

96. Q: What STI screening is recommended for sexually active adolescent females?

A: Routine chlamydia and gonorrhea screening is recommended, with frequency based on age, risk, and current guidelines.

97. Q: What is first-line treatment for uncomplicated chlamydial infection?
