A: Doxycycline is generally preferred for uncomplicated chlamydial infection in nonpregnant adolescents/adults.

98. Q: What is an important alternative treatment for chlamydia during pregnancy?

A: Azithromycin is commonly used during pregnancy because doxycycline is avoided during pregnancy.

99. Q: What is the key USMLE Step 2 principle for adolescent STI management?

A: Treat the patient appropriately, test for other STIs when indicated, counsel regarding abstinence/condom use, and ensure partner evaluation/treatment to prevent reinfection.

100. Q: What is the most important suicide-risk question to ask an adolescent with depression?

A: Ask directly about suicidal thoughts, intent, plan, access to lethal means, and previous attempts. Asking about suicide does not cause suicidal behavior.

101. Q: What finding indicates the highest immediate suicide risk in an adolescent?

A: Active suicidal intent with a specific plan and access to the means, particularly with a previous suicide attempt.

102. Q: What is the appropriate management of an adolescent with active suicidal intent and a specific plan?

A: Immediate psychiatric evaluation and protection in a safe environment, generally requiring emergency/inpatient psychiatric care rather than routine outpatient follow-up.

103. Q: What is the strongest predictor of future suicide attempt?

A: A previous suicide attempt.

104. Q: Which psychiatric condition is particularly important to evaluate when an adolescent presents with irritability, poor school performance, withdrawal, or substance use?

A: Major depressive disorder, because adolescents may present with irritability or behavioral changes rather than classic sadness.

105. Q: What is the minimum duration of symptoms required for diagnosis of major depressive disorder?

A: ≥2 weeks of depressed or irritable mood or loss of interest/pleasure, accompanied by additional symptoms causing functional impairment.

106. Q: What is the first-line treatment for mild-to-moderate depression in adolescents?

A: Psychotherapy, particularly cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) or interpersonal therapy; antidepressant treatment may be added depending on severity and response.

107. Q: Which SSRI has strong evidence for treating adolescent depression?

A: Fluoxetine is a commonly preferred SSRI; escitalopram is another evidence-based option in adolescents.

108. Q: What important counseling point accompanies SSRI treatment in adolescents?

A: Monitor closely for increased suicidal thoughts or behavioral activation, especially early in treatment or after dose changes.

109. Q: What diagnosis should be considered when an adolescent has recurrent episodes of excessive eating followed by compensatory behaviors?

A: Bulimia nervosa.

110. Q: What distinguishes bulimia nervosa from anorexia nervosa?

A: Bulimia involves recurrent binge eating with compensatory behaviors and typically a relatively normal-range body weight; anorexia involves restriction leading to significantly low body weight.

111. Q: What physical findings suggest bulimia nervosa?

A: Dental enamel erosion, parotid enlargement, calluses on the dorsum of the hand (Russell sign), hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis from recurrent vomiting.

112. Q: What electrolyte abnormality is classically associated with recurrent vomiting in bulimia nervosa?

A: Hypokalemia, often accompanied by hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis.

113. Q: What is the major cardiac concern in severe eating disorders with electrolyte abnormalities?

A: Cardiac arrhythmias, particularly ventricular arrhythmias associated with severe electrolyte disturbances and QT prolongation.

114. Q: What is the classic physical finding in anorexia nervosa?

A: Marked weight loss or failure to maintain expected weight, often accompanied by bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, and amenorrhea/menstrual dysfunction.

115. Q: Does anorexia nervosa require an adolescent to appear extremely thin?

A: No. Atypical anorexia nervosa can cause clinically significant restriction and medical instability without the patient being underweight.

116. Q: What laboratory abnormality is particularly concerning in a severely malnourished adolescent beginning nutritional rehabilitation?

A: Hypophosphatemia, indicating refeeding syndrome.

117. Q: What causes the hypophosphatemia of refeeding syndrome?

A: Increased insulin after carbohydrate intake drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium intracellularly, causing potentially severe serum depletion.

118. Q: How is refeeding syndrome prevented or managed?

A: Begin nutritional rehabilitation carefully in high-risk patients, monitor phosphate, potassium, magnesium, glucose, and fluid status, and replace deficiencies promptly.

119. Q: What eating disorder is characterized by recurrent binge eating without regular compensatory behaviors?

A: Binge-eating disorder.

120. Q: What is an important clinical principle regarding eating-disorder screening?

A: Routinely monitor growth trajectory, weight changes, eating behavior, body image, menstrual function, and vital signs, especially when risk factors or warning signs are present.

121. Q: What is the most common substance used by adolescents?

A: Alcohol is among the most commonly used substances, with tobacco/nicotine and cannabis also important sources of adolescent morbidity.

122. Q: What is the best initial approach when an adolescent reports substance use?

A: Use nonjudgmental, confidential screening and motivational interviewing, determine frequency and consequences, assess for dependence and coexisting psychiatric disorders, and counsel regarding risks.

123. Q: What should an adolescent clinician specifically assess after discovering substance use?

A: Determine the substance, amount, frequency, route, context, associated impairment, withdrawal symptoms, risky behaviors, driving, sexual risk, and coexisting psychiatric illness.

124. Q: What is the most important immediate counseling point for an adolescent who uses alcohol or drugs?

A: Do not drive or ride with an impaired driver; assess immediate safety and other high-risk behaviors.

125. Q: What psychiatric condition should be suspected when an adolescent has recurrent behavioral problems, impulsivity, poor school performance, and substance use?

A: ADHD, particularly if symptoms began in childhood and occur across multiple settings.

126. Q: What is required for an ADHD diagnosis?

A: Persistent symptoms of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity, beginning before age 12, occurring in ≥2 settings, and causing functional impairment.

127. Q: What is the preferred initial treatment for ADHD in an adolescent?

A: Stimulant medication is highly effective; behavioral interventions and school accommodations should also be incorporated.

128. Q: What important adverse effect should be monitored with stimulant therapy?

A: Decreased appetite and weight loss, with monitoring of growth, blood pressure, and heart rate.

129. Q: What adolescent presentation should raise suspicion for intimate partner violence?

A: Unexplained injuries, controlling relationships, fear of a partner, repeated emergency visits, sexual coercion, depression, anxiety, or unexplained STI/pregnancy.

130. Q: How should clinicians respond when adolescent abuse is suspected?

A: Ensure immediate safety, interview the adolescent privately, document findings objectively, and follow applicable mandatory reporting laws.

131. Q: What is the most important principle when evaluating suspected sexual abuse?

A: Avoid repeated questioning and unnecessary examinations; use a trauma-informed approach and involve an appropriately trained multidisciplinary team when available.

132. Q: What finding requires urgent evaluation after adolescent sexual assault?

A: Acute injury, significant bleeding, strangulation, altered consciousness, severe abdominal/pelvic pain, or immediate safety concerns require urgent assessment.

133. Q: What preventive interventions should be considered after sexual assault?

A: Depending on circumstances: pregnancy prevention, STI testing/prophylaxis, HIV post-exposure prophylaxis when indicated, hepatitis B vaccination, and psychological support.

134. Q: What is the most common cause of death in adolescents in many high-income countries?

A: Unintentional injuries, particularly motor-vehicle crashes, are major causes of adolescent mortality.

135. Q: What is a high-yield adolescent injury-prevention intervention?

A: Counsel about seat belts, helmets, avoiding distracted/impaired driving, firearm safety, and avoiding riding with impaired drivers.

136. Q: What is the most important screening consideration before an adolescent begins competitive sports?

A: Evaluate for personal/family history of exertional syncope, chest pain, unexplained seizures, palpitations, sudden cardiac death, and known cardiac disease, plus an appropriate physical examination.

137. Q: What cardiac symptom during exercise is particularly concerning in an adolescent athlete?

A: Exertional syncope, especially when associated with palpitations or chest pain, warrants evaluation for potentially life-threatening cardiac disease.

138. Q: What condition should be suspected in an adolescent athlete with exertional syncope and a family history of sudden death?

A: Consider inherited arrhythmogenic disorders such as hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, long-QT syndrome, or catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia.

139. Q: What is the classic murmur of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy?

A: A harsh systolic crescendo-decrescendo murmur that becomes louder with decreased preload, such as during Valsalva or standing.

140. Q: What is the classic ECG clue for long-QT syndrome?

A: Prolonged QT interval, which predisposes to torsades de pointes and sudden cardiac death.

141. Q: What is the recommended initial management of a concussion in an adolescent athlete?

A: Remove the athlete from play and perform clinical assessment; the athlete should not return to play the same day if concussion is suspected.

142. Q: When can an adolescent return to sports after concussion?

A: After symptoms have resolved and the athlete successfully completes a gradual, stepwise return-to-play protocol under appropriate medical guidance.

143. Q: What symptom after a concussion is an emergency warning sign?

A: Progressively worsening headache, repeated vomiting, seizure, focal neurologic deficit, deteriorating consciousness, or significant mental-status change requires urgent evaluation for intracranial injury.

144. Q: What is the most common cause of iron deficiency in menstruating adolescent females?

A: Inadequate dietary iron combined with menstrual blood loss.

145. Q: What dietary finding increases the risk of iron deficiency in adolescents?

A: Diets low in iron-rich foods, particularly restrictive diets without adequate replacement of heme and nonheme iron sources.

146. Q: What is the typical laboratory pattern of iron-deficiency anemia?

A: Low hemoglobin, low MCV, low ferritin, low serum iron, increased TIBC, and increased RDW.

147. Q: What adolescent condition should be suspected when fatigue, poor concentration, and heavy menstrual bleeding occur together?

A: Iron-deficiency anemia secondary to menstrual blood loss, while evaluating the underlying cause of the heavy bleeding.

148. Q: What is the preferred general approach to adolescent obesity?

A: Use family-centered, nonstigmatizing lifestyle intervention, emphasizing nutrition, physical activity, sleep, behavioral strategies, and assessment for obesity-related comorbidities rather than focusing solely on weight.

149. Q: What metabolic complication should be considered in an adolescent with obesity and signs of insulin resistance?

A: Prediabetes/type 2 diabetes mellitus, particularly with acanthosis nigricans, strong family history, hypertension, dyslipidemia, or other metabolic risk factors.

125. Q: What is the preferred screening approach for adolescent substance use?
A: Use a confidential, nonjudgmental screening tool followed by assessment of severity, consequences, and safety risks.

126. Q: What substance is particularly associated with impaired driving and injury in adolescents?
A: Alcohol.

127. Q: What should be assessed when an adolescent reports recreational drug use?
A: Substance type, frequency, amount, route, dependence, withdrawal, impaired driving, sexual risk, overdose risk, and coexisting psychiatric disorders.

128. Q: What is motivational interviewing used for in adolescent medicine?
A: To help adolescents explore ambivalence about changing risky behaviors, such as substance use, without confrontation.

129. Q: What screening question is particularly important when an adolescent uses alcohol or drugs?
A: Ask whether they drive while impaired or ride with an impaired driver.

130. Q: What is the major respiratory consequence of adolescent cigarette smoking?
A: Impaired lung growth and development, with increased risk of chronic respiratory symptoms.

131. Q: What is an important counseling point regarding vaping in adolescents?
A: Vaping is not harmless and can result in nicotine dependence and respiratory injury; adolescents should be counseled to avoid nicotine products.

132. Q: What adolescent behavior is strongly associated with sexually transmitted infections?
A: Multiple or new sexual partners and inconsistent condom use.

133. Q: What is the most important pregnancy-related question when an adolescent presents with abdominal pain and vaginal bleeding?
A: Determine whether she could be pregnant and obtain a pregnancy test.

134. Q: What diagnosis must always be considered in a sexually active adolescent with unilateral pelvic pain?
A: Ectopic pregnancy, after pregnancy is established.

135. Q: What is the typical presentation of pelvic inflammatory disease in an adolescent?
A: Lower abdominal/pelvic pain with cervical motion, uterine, or adnexal tenderness, often associated with abnormal vaginal discharge or bleeding.

136. Q: What is the first-line outpatient treatment for uncomplicated pelvic inflammatory disease?
A: Ceftriaxone + doxycycline + metronidazole for appropriate patients.

137. Q: What complication of pelvic inflammatory disease is particularly important in adolescents?
A: Infertility, chronic pelvic pain, and ectopic pregnancy due to tubal damage.

138. Q: What adolescent STI commonly causes painful genital ulcers?

A: HSV infection.

139. Q: What STI classically causes painless genital ulcers?

A: Primary syphilis caused by Treponema pallidum.

140. Q: What is the characteristic lesion of secondary syphilis?

A: A diffuse rash involving the palms and soles, often with systemic symptoms.

141. Q: What STI commonly causes genital warts?

A: Human papillomavirus (HPV), particularly types 6 and 11.

142. Q: Which HPV types are strongly associated with cervical cancer?

A: HPV 16 and 18 are major high-risk oncogenic types.

143. Q: What is the primary purpose of HPV vaccination?

A: Prevention of HPV infection and HPV-associated cancers and anogenital disease.

144. Q: Can HPV vaccination be given to adolescents who are already sexually active?

A: Yes. Prior sexual activity does not eliminate the benefit of vaccination, although the vaccine does not treat existing HPV infection.

145. Q: What is the most common cause of vaginal discharge in a sexually active adolescent?

A: Common causes include bacterial vaginosis, candidiasis, trichomoniasis, chlamydia, and gonorrhea, depending on symptoms and examination findings.

146. Q: What vaginal discharge finding suggests bacterial vaginosis?

A: Thin, homogeneous, gray-white discharge with a fishy odor, often with vaginal pH >4.5.

147. Q: What finding suggests vulvovaginal candidiasis?

A: Pruritus with thick, white, clumpy discharge and vaginal erythema.

148. Q: What finding is characteristic of trichomoniasis?

A: Frothy yellow-green discharge with a strawberry cervix.

149. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic approach for suspected chlamydia or gonorrhea?

A: Nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT).

150. Q: What is an important reason to screen adolescents for sexually transmitted infections even when asymptomatic?

A: Many STIs, particularly chlamydia and gonorrhea, are frequently asymptomatic but can cause reproductive complications.

151. Q: What is the recommended management principle for sexual partners of an adolescent diagnosed with an STI?

A: Partner notification, evaluation, and treatment when indicated, with counseling to prevent reinfection.

152. Q: What is the major purpose of adolescent confidentiality in sexual-health care?

A: It encourages adolescents to seek care and disclose sensitive information, improving STI detection, contraception counseling, and safety assessment.

153. Q: What is an important exception to confidentiality involving sexual activity?

A: Suspected sexual abuse or exploitation, depending on the circumstances and applicable mandatory-reporting laws.

154. Q: What is the most common cause of primary amenorrhea with normal secondary sexual characteristics and absent uterus?

A: Müllerian agenesis or androgen insensitivity syndrome.

155. Q: How can Müllerian agenesis be distinguished from androgen insensitivity syndrome?

A: Müllerian agenesis: 46,XX, normal ovaries, absent uterus, normal pubic hair.
Androgen insensitivity: 46,XY, testes present, absent uterus, sparse/absent pubic and axillary hair.

156. Q: What is the most common cause of primary amenorrhea with absent secondary sexual characteristics?

A: Gonadal failure or hypothalamic/pituitary dysfunction, depending on the clinical context.

157. Q: What diagnosis should be considered in a short adolescent female with delayed puberty and primary amenorrhea?

A: Turner syndrome, particularly with short stature and characteristic physical findings.

158. Q: What karyotype is classic for Turner syndrome?

A: 45,X or mosaic variants.

159. Q: What important cardiovascular abnormality is associated with Turner syndrome?

A: Coarctation of the aorta and bicuspid aortic valve.

160. Q: What important endocrine problem should be screened for in Turner syndrome?

A: Primary ovarian insufficiency and associated hypothyroidism.

161. Q: What is the most common cause of gynecomastia during adolescence?

A: Physiologic pubertal gynecomastia caused by a temporary imbalance between estrogen and androgen activity.

162. Q: How is physiologic pubertal gynecomastia usually managed?

A: Reassurance and observation, as it usually resolves spontaneously.

163. Q: What findings suggest pathologic gynecomastia?

A: Rapid progression, hard/fixed mass, unilateral suspicious findings, testicular mass, galactorrhea, or signs of endocrine/systemic disease.

164. Q: What is the most common cause of testicular pain in an adolescent requiring emergency treatment?

A: Testicular torsion.

165. Q: What clinical finding strongly suggests testicular torsion?

A: Sudden severe unilateral testicular pain with a high-riding testis and absent cremasteric reflex.

166. Q: What is the next step when testicular torsion is strongly suspected?

A: Immediate urologic consultation and surgical exploration; do not delay definitive treatment for imaging when clinical suspicion is high.

167. Q: What adolescent condition causes gradual scrotal discomfort and a “bag of worms” on examination?

A: Varicocele.

168. Q: On which side is a varicocele most commonly found?

A: Left side, due to the anatomy of left testicular venous drainage.

169. Q: What adolescent condition causes acute scrotal pain with a blue-dot sign?

A: Torsion of the appendix testis.

170. Q: How is torsion of the appendix testis generally managed?

A: Supportive treatment with analgesia and observation, unlike testicular torsion, which requires urgent surgery.

171. Q: What is the most common cause of short stature in an otherwise healthy adolescent?

A: Familial short stature or constitutional growth delay, depending on growth pattern and bone age.

172. Q: What growth pattern suggests an endocrine cause of short stature?

A: Progressive decline in growth velocity, particularly when weight is relatively preserved or increased.

173. Q: What endocrine disorder can cause poor linear growth with relatively preserved or increased weight?

A: Hypothyroidism or growth hormone deficiency; endocrine causes generally impair height more than weight.

174. Q: What is an important red flag in adolescent growth assessment?

A: Crossing downward across growth percentiles or decreased growth velocity, which warrants evaluation rather than relying solely on absolute height.

175. Q: What is the most common cause of secondary hypertension in an adolescent?
A: Primary (essential) hypertension, especially in adolescents with obesity or a family history of hypertension.

176. Q: What finding suggests secondary rather than primary hypertension in an adolescent?
A: Severe hypertension, abnormal renal findings, very young age, or symptoms suggesting an underlying systemic disorder.

177. Q: What lifestyle factors commonly contribute to adolescent hypertension?
A: Obesity, high sodium intake, physical inactivity, poor diet, and substance use.

178. Q: What is the first step in managing uncomplicated adolescent primary hypertension?
A: Lifestyle modification, including weight management, regular physical activity, and a healthy diet.

179. Q: What diet is recommended for adolescents with hypertension?
A: A DASH-style diet rich in fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and low-fat dairy with reduced sodium intake.

180. Q: When should pharmacologic treatment be considered for adolescent hypertension?
A: Persistent hypertension despite lifestyle intervention, symptomatic hypertension, stage 2 hypertension, diabetes, CKD, or evidence of target-organ damage.

181. Q: What is the most common cause of obesity-related insulin resistance in adolescents?
A: Excess adiposity, particularly visceral adiposity, causing impaired insulin signaling.

182. Q: What physical finding is a marker of insulin resistance in an obese adolescent?
A: Acanthosis nigricans, characterized by hyperpigmented, velvety thickening of flexural skin.

183. Q: What metabolic abnormalities commonly accompany adolescent obesity?
A: Insulin resistance, dyslipidemia, hypertension, type 2 diabetes, and metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD).

184. Q: What liver disorder should be suspected in an obese adolescent with persistently elevated ALT?
A: Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD).

185. Q: What is the most important initial intervention for adolescent obesity?
A: Comprehensive lifestyle and behavioral intervention involving the adolescent and family.

186. Q: What is an important sleep-related contributor to adolescent obesity?
A: Insufficient sleep, which is associated with increased appetite and metabolic dysregulation.

187. Q: What sleep disorder should be suspected in an obese adolescent with loud snoring and daytime sleepiness?
A: Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA).

188. Q: What is the preferred diagnostic test for suspected obstructive sleep apnea?
A: Overnight polysomnography.

189. Q: What is a common first-line treatment for obstructive sleep apnea caused by adenotonsillar hypertrophy?
A: Adenotonsillectomy, when clinically appropriate.

190. Q: What adolescent sleep pattern commonly causes excessive daytime sleepiness without another disease?
A: Delayed sleep-wake phase, in which adolescents naturally fall asleep and awaken later.

191. Q: What is delayed sleep-wake phase disorder?
A: A circadian rhythm disorder characterized by difficulty falling asleep at conventional times and difficulty waking at desired times, with normal sleep when allowed the preferred schedule.

192. Q: What is a common consequence of chronic sleep deprivation in adolescents?
A: Poor concentration, impaired academic performance, mood problems, and increased accident risk.

193. Q: What should be assessed in an adolescent with unexplained declining school performance?
A: Sleep, depression, anxiety, ADHD, substance use, learning difficulties, family/social stressors, and medical illness.

194. Q: What is a common presentation of anxiety disorders in adolescents?
A: Excessive worry, avoidance, somatic complaints, sleep disturbance, irritability, or impaired school/social functioning.

195. Q: What disorder involves intense fear of social scrutiny and avoidance of social situations?
A: Social anxiety disorder.

196. Q: What disorder is characterized by recurrent unexpected panic attacks followed by persistent concern or behavioral changes?
A: Panic disorder.

197. Q: What is first-line treatment for many adolescent anxiety disorders?
A: CBT, with an SSRI added for moderate-to-severe symptoms or inadequate response.

198. Q: What adolescent disorder is characterized by recurrent intrusive thoughts and repetitive behaviors performed to reduce anxiety?
A: Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD).

199. Q: What is first-line treatment for adolescent OCD?
A: Exposure and response prevention (ERP), often combined with an SSRI for moderate-to-severe disease.

200. Q: What condition should be suspected when an adolescent has recurrent episodes of intense fear, palpitations, dyspnea, and chest discomfort with normal cardiac evaluation?
A: Panic attacks/panic disorder, after appropriate medical causes have been excluded.

201. Q: What psychiatric disorder should be considered when an adolescent repeatedly restricts food because of fear of gaining weight?
A: Anorexia nervosa.

202. Q: What psychiatric condition involves persistent preoccupation with a perceived physical defect that is not objectively significant or is minimally apparent?
A: Body dysmorphic disorder.

203. Q: What is an important complication of chronic severe body-image disturbance in adolescents?
A: Depression, anxiety, social withdrawal, eating disorders, and suicidal behavior.

204. Q: What is the most common behavioral intervention for adolescent conduct problems?
A: Family-based behavioral interventions and psychosocial therapy, with assessment for comorbid disorders.

205. Q: What diagnosis is characterized by repetitive violation of the rights of others and major age-appropriate social norms?
A: Conduct disorder.

206. Q: What diagnosis involves a persistent pattern of angry/irritable mood, argumentative behavior, and defiance without the severe rights violations of conduct disorder?
A: Oppositional defiant disorder (ODD).

207. Q: What is an important risk factor for adolescent conduct disorder?
A: Childhood-onset behavioral problems, family dysfunction, abuse/neglect, inconsistent discipline, and exposure to violence.

208. Q: What adolescent psychiatric condition is strongly associated with recurrent self-injury without suicidal intent?
A: Nonsuicidal self-injury, often associated with emotional dysregulation, depression, anxiety, or borderline personality traits.

209. Q: What is the appropriate response to an adolescent reporting nonsuicidal self-injury?
A: Assess suicidal ideation and intent, identify underlying psychiatric disorders, ensure safety, and provide appropriate mental-health treatment.

210. Q: What is a major risk factor for adolescent intimate partner violence?
A: History of violence, exposure to family violence, substance use, and unhealthy controlling relationships.

211. Q: What type of adolescent relationship behavior should raise concern for dating violence?
A: Controlling communication, threats, isolation from friends/family, forced sexual activity, or physical aggression.

212. Q: What is the appropriate first response when an adolescent discloses dating violence?
A: Ensure immediate safety, provide private supportive counseling, assess danger, and connect the adolescent with appropriate resources.

213. Q: What is the most common form of bullying experienced by adolescents?
A: Verbal/social bullying, although physical and cyberbullying are also important.

214. Q: What psychiatric problems are associated with persistent bullying or cyberbullying?
A: Depression, anxiety, social withdrawal, self-harm, and suicidal behavior.

215. Q: What should a clinician do when an adolescent reports cyberbullying?
A: Assess mental-health and safety risks, encourage involvement of trusted adults, document serious threats, and develop a safety plan.

216. Q: What is the most common cause of adolescent iron deficiency besides menstrual blood loss?
A: Inadequate dietary iron intake, particularly with restrictive diets.

217. Q: What adolescent population is at increased risk for vitamin B12 deficiency?
A: Adolescents following a strict vegan diet without appropriate B12 supplementation.

218. Q: What nutrient deficiency is particularly important in adolescents with restrictive diets and limited sun exposure?
A: Vitamin D deficiency.

219. Q: What adolescent behavior significantly increases the risk of unintended pregnancy and STIs?
A: Inconsistent or absent barrier contraception, especially with multiple/new sexual partners.

220. Q: What is the most effective reversible contraceptive strategy for an adolescent who has difficulty adhering to daily medication?
A: Long-acting reversible contraception (LARC) such as an implant or IUD.

221. Q: What should be considered when an adolescent repeatedly requests pregnancy tests despite denying sexual activity?
A: Provide a private, nonjudgmental assessment of sexual activity, coercion, contraception, and pregnancy risk rather than assuming the history is complete.

222. Q: What is an important indication for confidential adolescent screening for HIV?
A: Sexual activity or other risk factors, particularly multiple partners, inconsistent condom use, or injection-drug exposure.

223. Q: What is the major prevention strategy for HIV transmission among sexually active adolescents?
A: Condom use, HIV testing, treatment of HIV-positive partners, and HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) when indicated.

224. Q: What is a high-yield principle for adolescent preventive medicine on Step 2 CK?

A: Evaluate the adolescent as a whole person: physical health, puberty, mental health, nutrition, sleep, substance use, sexual/reproductive health, safety, school functioning, family environment, and preventive care.
